41. The Why Behind Violence — And Why America Keeps Burying It

Chapter 1: The Forgotten Science of At-Risk Children

The boy in the folding chair was nine years old, and his feet did not reach the floor of the juvenile intake office. He had thrown a stapler at a teacher. Across the room a woman I will call Denise, who runs an after-school program in a church basement two blocks from his apartment, was arguing with an intake officer in a low, furious voice. She had known the boy since he was four. She knew he startled at loud noises, that he could not sit through a story, that his mother had been drinking hard while she carried him, and that the paint on the window frames in their building flaked into the carpet where he used to crawl. None of that was on the form.

I have sat in rooms like that one more times than I can count, in my years on the crime beat, and the form never has a box for what Denise knew. It has a box for the charge. It has a box for the school. It has a box for prior contacts with police, and in time that box fills up. When I meet these children again, they are usually nineteen or twenty-three, and they are in an orange jumpsuit, and the story in the paper begins on the night of the shooting instead of the day the injury started.

My claim is blunt, and I will defend it with evidence rather than sentiment: a large share of adult crime in America begins as an untreated injury to a child's developing brain or nervous system. The warning signs are visible years in advance, often a decade or more. We have the science to see them. We have programs that have been tested to treat them. And we spend our money, instead, on the arrest, the trial, and the cell, as though the boy in the folding chair had appeared out of nowhere.

Prenatal Exposure and Neurological Injury

The injury can start before birth. Alcohol crosses the placenta freely, and fetal alcohol spectrum disorders, which range from the visible facial features most people associate with the condition to invisible damage to judgment and impulse control, are among the most common preventable causes of developmental disability in the country. Most affected children are never diagnosed. They grow up described as lazy, defiant, or bad, and the adults around them treat a wiring problem as a character problem. Researchers who have screened people in juvenile and adult correctional settings have repeatedly found rates of these disorders far above those in the general population.

Lead is the other quiet poison, and its record is harder to dismiss. Economists and public health researchers spent years tracing the rise and fall of violent crime against the rise and fall of leaded gasoline and lead paint, and they found the curves matched with an uncomfortable lag of about two decades, the time it takes an exposed toddler to become a young adult. Correlation across a whole society can mislead, so the stronger evidence comes from following individual children. The Cincinnati Lead Study did exactly that, measuring blood lead in pregnant mothers and in their children through early childhood, then checking county arrest records once those children were grown. Higher prenatal and early childhood lead levels were associated with higher rates of adult arrest.

Then came Flint. In 2014, to save money, state-appointed managers switched the Michigan city's water source to the Flint River without adequate corrosion control, and lead leached from aging pipes into the taps of families who were told for months that the water was fine. A local pediatrician's analysis of children's blood tests showed the share of kids with elevated lead had jumped after the switch, and officials who had dismissed residents' complaints were forced to reverse themselves. The legal settlement that followed ran to hundreds of millions of dollars. No settlement buys back a child's IQ points, and I think often about where Flint's youngest residents of that era will be when they turn twenty.

Lead and alcohol get the headlines, but they are not the whole list. Drug exposure in the womb, severe malnutrition, premature birth, and head injuries from falls, car crashes, and beatings all leave marks on the brain's executive systems, the parts that weigh consequences and hold back an impulse. Traumatic brain injury shows up in prison populations at rates far higher than in the free population, according to studies that have screened incarcerated men and women. Many of those injuries happened in childhood. Almost none were treated. A child who takes a fist to the head at six and is never seen by a doctor becomes, at sixteen, a kid who cannot stop himself, and we call that a choice.

Developmental Delays and Behavioral Risk

Injury to the body is only half of it. In 1998, physicians at Kaiser Permanente in San Diego and researchers at the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention published findings from a survey of thousands of ordinary, mostly middle-class adults who came in for routine checkups. They asked about adverse childhood experiences: abuse, neglect, a parent in prison, domestic violence in the home, addiction or mental illness in the household. The results upended a lot of assumptions. Such experiences were common, they clustered together, and the more of them a person had suffered, the more likely that person was to struggle decades later with addiction, depression, heart disease, and early death.

Criminologists picked up the thread quickly. Later studies of young people in the juvenile justice system found that high scores on these childhood adversity measures were the rule there, not the exception. The mechanism is not mysterious. A child who lives with chronic fear develops a stress response system tuned for danger, quick to fight, slow to calm, poor at reading neutral faces as neutral. That system kept the child alive in a violent house. In a classroom, on a bus, or at a party where somebody bumps into him, it misfires.

Developmental delays compound the damage. Speech and language problems, in particular, show up at striking rates among young offenders, and they are easy to miss because a kid who cannot follow complex instructions or explain himself under pressure often looks rude rather than impaired. Picture a fourteen-year-old who does not understand a police officer's question, who answers with a shrug or a curse because that is the only tool he has. On paper, that becomes noncompliance. In court, it becomes a defendant who cannot meaningfully assist his own lawyer. Nobody tests his hearing or his language processing, because nobody thought to ask.

School Failure and Early Warning Signs

Schools see these children every day, which is why they are both the best early warning system we have and one of the most wasted. The signals stack up in the records: chronic absence that starts in kindergarten, a reading level that falls behind by third grade and never recovers, a string of suspensions for disruption, a referral for special education testing that sits in a queue for months. Each of those signals alone means little. Together, in the same file, they describe a child at serious risk, and the pattern is often visible to anyone who looks by the end of elementary school.

Denise keeps her own records, in a spiral notebook, because the official ones do not follow children when they change schools. She told me once that she could pick out, among the first graders who come through her door, most of the ones she will later visit in detention. She was not boasting. She was ashamed of it, because she had been right too often and nobody with a budget had ever asked her. What she sees with a notebook and twenty years of attention, a school district could see with the data it already holds, if anyone were assigned to look and given the power to act.

Instead, the typical response to a struggling, injured child is removal. The child who acts out is suspended, and suspension sends him home to the very environment that is hurting him, unsupervised, falling further behind. National civil rights data have shown for years that suspensions and school-based arrests fall unevenly, landing hardest on Black students and on students with disabilities. Whatever the intentions of the adults involved, the practical effect is to push out the children with the deepest injuries just when they most need the structure of a school day.

Missed Intervention Opportunities

The bitterest part of this story is that we know some things work. In the 1960s, in Ypsilanti, Michigan, researchers enrolled a small group of low-income Black preschoolers, judged to be at high risk of school failure, in a high-quality preschool program, and they compared them with a similar group who did not attend. Then they did something rare. They kept following both groups, for decades. By age forty, the adults who had attended Perry Preschool had fewer arrests, higher earnings, and higher rates of high school graduation than those who had not. Economists who later ran the numbers concluded the program returned far more to the public than it cost, much of it in crime avoided.

Perry was small, and skeptics rightly note that small, carefully run pilots often shrink when they are scaled up. I take that point seriously. But Perry is not alone. Home visiting by trained nurses for first-time low-income mothers, rigorously tested in randomized trials, has reduced child abuse and later arrests among the children. Early lead abatement prevents a harm that costs society for a lifetime. Developmental screening during routine pediatric visits catches language delays when therapy is cheap and effective. None of this is exotic. It is simply unglamorous, slow to pay off, and impossible to show off at a press conference.

Every one of these programs has the same political problem. The savings land years later, in a different budget, often under a different mayor or governor. A sheriff can point to arrests made this quarter. A school board member cannot point to a shooting that did not happen in 2041. So the money goes where the credit goes, and the children who could have been reached at three are reached, instead, at seventeen, in handcuffs, by people who never had a chance to help them.

I asked Denise, after the intake officer finally released the boy to his grandmother, how many chances the system had already had with him. She counted on her fingers. A pediatric visit where nobody tested his blood for lead. A preschool screening that noted a speech delay and recommended follow-up that never came. Two calls to child protective services from a neighbor. A kindergarten teacher who wrote, in a report Denise had seen, that he seemed frightened all the time. Five open doors before his ninth birthday, and every one of them closed by an office that assumed somebody else would act.

That is the pattern I have seen in case after case. The failure is rarely ignorance. Somebody almost always noticed. The failure is that noticing carries no duty, and the people who notice, teachers and nurses and coaches and women like Denise, have no power to make a system respond. When the child finally hurts someone, the reporters ask why nobody saw it coming. Somebody did. Nobody was listening.

How Society Criminalizes Childhood Injury

The law itself has begun to admit what the science shows. In Roper v. Simmons in 2005, the Supreme Court held that executing someone for a crime committed before age eighteen violates the Eighth Amendment's ban on cruel and unusual punishment, and the justices leaned on research showing adolescents are more impulsive, more susceptible to pressure, and more capable of change than adults. In Graham v. Florida in 2010, the Court barred life without parole for juveniles convicted of crimes other than homicide. In Miller v. Alabama in 2012, it held that mandatory life without parole for juvenile offenders is unconstitutional, requiring judges to consider youth and its attendant circumstances before imposing that sentence.

Those decisions matter, and I credit the justices who wrote them for listening to the evidence. But notice where they intervene: at sentencing, after a crime has already been committed, after a victim has already been hurt. The Court recognized that a teenager's brain is not an adult's. It did not, because it cannot, order anyone to treat the lead-poisoned toddler, the child with an undiagnosed brain injury, or the girl whose language disorder made every classroom a humiliation. The courtroom is the last stop. By then, everyone in the room has already failed.

What happens in between is criminalization by default. We send police officers into schools to handle behavior that once went to the principal's office, and a tantrum becomes a disorderly conduct charge. We prosecute children for running away from homes that were dangerous. We place traumatized kids in detention facilities that teach them, more efficiently than any street corner, how to be harder. Each step is defended as accountability. In practice, it converts a medical and developmental problem into a criminal record, and the record makes the next failure more likely.

What Cities and Schools Can Do Now

Start with the poison. Newark, New Jersey, faced its own lead-in-water crisis and replaced its lead service lines across the city in roughly three years, faster than nearly anyone thought possible, by paying for the work on private property and moving crews street by street. Any city council can inventory its lead pipes and set a replacement deadline. State legislatures can require universal blood lead testing for young children and fund the abatement of lead paint in rental housing, with the landlord's cost shared rather than ignored.

Next, put money where Perry and the nurse home visiting trials point. State lawmakers can fund high-quality early childhood programs for the highest-risk families and nurse home visiting for first-time mothers who qualify, and they can protect that money in multiyear appropriations so it survives a bad budget cycle. Pediatric practices and public clinics can screen every child for developmental delays and adverse experiences at well-child visits, then connect families with speech therapy, counseling, and parenting support while the problem is still small.

Schools can turn their records into a real warning system. A district that flags children with chronic absence, a slipping reading level, and repeated discipline, and then assigns each flagged child a named adult responsible for a plan, is doing prevention. Superintendents can limit suspensions for young children, require a screening for learning and language disorders before any child is pushed out, and keep police out of routine classroom discipline. Juvenile courts can order neuropsychological evaluations before disposition, so the record shows the injury along with the charge.

Citizens have a part too. Denise did not wait for permission to keep her notebook. Parents can ask pediatricians for lead tests and developmental screening by name. Neighbors can ask their city councils, at a public meeting, how many lead service lines remain in the ground. Voters can ask candidates for sheriff and prosecutor what they will do for a five-year-old, and judge them by the answer. The cheapest crime to solve is the one that never happens, and it is prevented in nurseries and kindergartens, not interrogation rooms.

The takeaway

The boy in the folding chair was not born a criminal; he was injured, visibly and early, and then ignored until his injury became our problem. Find out this week whether your city knows how many lead pipes it still has and whether your local schools flag at-risk children before they are suspended, and if the answer to either is no, put that question to your council member in writing.

Chapter 2: Illiteracy: The Hidden Engine of Criminality

The man who broke into Ray's house could not read the warrant that sent him back to prison. I learned that months after the burglary, sitting beside Ray in the back row of a county courtroom, watching the defendant's lawyer lean over and read the plea agreement aloud to him, line by line, in a whisper. The man nodded at the right places. He signed where her finger pointed. Ray, who had spent two weekends replacing a shattered back door and a year sleeping badly, turned to me and asked the question that has stayed with me ever since. How does a grown man get to thirty-one without learning to read?

He gets there the way millions of Americans do, quietly, one passed-along grade at a time. Federal surveys of adult skills have found for years that tens of millions of adults in this country read at a level too low to handle ordinary printed tasks with confidence: a job application, a lease, the instructions on a prescription bottle. Most of them are not in prison. But in prison the concentration is staggering, and the connection between the inability to read and the life that leads to a cell is one of the most consistent, and most neglected, findings I have encountered in my years covering crime.

Ray is a homeowner, a retired machinist, and a taxpayer with no patience for excuses. He did not want to hear that the man who took his late wife's jewelry was a victim. Neither do I. But as I followed that case and dozens like it, I came to a harder conclusion than either of us wanted. If you want fewer broken back doors, you will have to care, practically and with money, about who learns to read.

Literacy and Economic Survival

Start with the plain economics. Almost every lawful path to a decent income runs through text. A forklift certification has a written test. A commercial driver's license has a written test. Even the jobs that require no diploma usually require an online application, a safety manual, a schedule posted on a break room wall, a text message from a supervisor. A person who cannot read reliably is not just less competitive in that market. For much of it, he is locked out entirely, and he knows it.

What is left is the cash economy, the day-labor corner, and the illegal trade, which has never asked anyone for a résumé. I have interviewed young men who explained their first drug sales in purely practical terms. One of them, years ago, told me he had applied for jobs by having his cousin fill out the forms, and when his cousin moved away, he stopped applying. He was not proud of what he did next. He was candid that it was the only work in his neighborhood that did not start with a sheet of paper he could not decode.

The digital economy has raised the bar again. Twenty years ago a man who could not read might still talk his way into a job at a loading dock. Today the loading dock posts its openings on a website, screens applicants with an online questionnaire, and assigns shifts through a phone app. Every one of those steps assumes a level of reading fluency that a large minority of American adults do not have.

Shame matters here as much as money. Adults who cannot read become experts at hiding it. They say they forgot their glasses. They take the form home and never bring it back. They avoid any office, any classroom, any employer where the secret might come out. That avoidance shrinks a person's world, steadily, until the only places left are the ones where nobody asks him to read anything at all. Many of those places are where crime lives.

Ray did his own arithmetic once the case was over, and it made him angrier than the burglary had. He added up what he had paid out of pocket for the door, the locks, and the cameras, then looked up what his state spends to hold one person in prison for a year, a figure that runs into the tens of thousands of dollars in most states. He realized he would help pay for the man's sentence through his taxes for the next several years. "I paid for him to fail school," he said, "and now I'm paying for him to sit in a cell." It is a crude summary. It is not a wrong one.

The Prison Literacy Crisis

The best modern measurement came in 2014, when the federal government's National Center for Education Statistics extended the international adult skills survey, known as PIAAC, into American prisons. Roughly 1,300 incarcerated men and women in federal, state, and private prisons took the same literacy and numeracy assessments given to a national sample of adults living in households. Their average literacy scores came in well below those of the household population, and their numeracy scores fell further still. A far larger share of the people behind bars scored at the lowest levels, and the overwhelming majority had no more than a high school education.

One finding in that study deserves attention, because it cuts against lazy assumptions. When researchers compared incarcerated adults with free adults of similar education levels, and when they compared Black and Hispanic prisoners with Black and Hispanic adults outside, the literacy gaps largely shrank or disappeared. In other words, prison does not hold a different kind of person. It holds the people our schools failed to teach, drawn disproportionately from the neighborhoods where those schools were weakest. Literacy is not a racial trait. It is an institutional outcome.

I have walked through prison education wings where the waiting list for basic reading classes was longer than the roster. I have talked to corrections teachers who buy their own supplies and who watch students transferred to another facility halfway through a course, losing their progress. Then the state releases those same people with a bus ticket and a stack of paperwork, parole conditions, housing applications, and benefit forms, all of it written down. We hand a man who cannot read a written list of rules and call it supervision. When he violates one he did not understand, we call it recidivism.

Dyslexia and other learning disabilities run through those prison classrooms too, often undiagnosed until a volunteer tutor or a curious teacher notices that a forty-year-old man reverses letters the way a second grader does. Many of these men were simply never tested. They spent their school years labeled slow or lazy, and they absorbed the label. When they finally learn that their brains process text differently, and that methods exist to teach them anyway, the relief can be overwhelming. I have watched a grown man cry over a first-grade reader, not from embarrassment, but because someone finally told him he was not stupid.

How Illiteracy Predicts Crime

The arrow of cause here runs in more than one direction, and I want to be honest about that. Poverty, trauma, learning disabilities, and family chaos all damage reading, and those same forces also push people toward crime. So the inability to read is partly a symptom. But it is also a cause in its own right, because it closes off school, then work, then the ordinary routes to self-respect. A child who falls behind in reading by the end of third grade is far more likely to drop out of high school, and dropouts are far more likely to be arrested and incarcerated than graduates.

Third grade is the hinge. Before it, children learn to read. After it, they read to learn, and every subject, from science to social studies to the word problems in math, assumes the skill is already there. The child who never got it begins to fail everything at once. He acts out to avoid being exposed as stupid, because being in trouble feels better than being humiliated in front of the class. Teachers who have spent decades in high-poverty classrooms told me they could see the turn happen, often in fourth and fifth grade, when a struggling reader decides school is a place that hates him.

Then the discipline file begins. Suspensions, then a transfer to an alternative school, then truancy, then the first contact with police. By the time the courts see him, the reading failure is buried under a stack of behavior reports, and no judge asks the question Ray asked me. The predictor was there the whole time, in the reading scores from second grade, and our institutions keep treating the later behavior as the whole story.

Juvenile detention makes the pattern visible to anyone willing to look. Educators who work in detention centers describe teenagers reading years below grade level, sometimes at a primary-school level, sitting in classrooms that cannot possibly catch them up during a stay of a few weeks or months. The facility's schooling is often thin, interrupted by lockdowns and court dates and transfers. A teenager goes in behind and comes out further behind, with a record that makes his home school less eager to take him back. Each stop on that path is a place where reading could have been taught, and each one passes him along.

The Failure of Schools and Courts

In 2016, a group of students from some of Detroit's lowest-performing public schools sued Michigan's governor and state education officials, arguing that they had been denied access to literacy itself. Their complaint described classrooms without enough books, teachers, or heat, and schools where very few students tested as proficient readers. In April 2020, in Gary B. v. Whitmer, a divided panel of the federal Sixth Circuit Court of Appeals agreed that the Constitution guarantees a fundamental right to a basic minimum education, one that includes the chance to learn to read. It was the first ruling of its kind from a federal appeals court.

It did not last. Weeks later, Governor Gretchen Whitmer's administration settled with the students, agreeing to pay several million dollars toward literacy programs in Detroit, modest payments to the student plaintiffs, and to push legislation for roughly ninety-four million dollars more in literacy funding. The full Sixth Circuit then vacated the panel's opinion and voted to rehear the case, and after the settlement the case was dismissed as moot. The opinion survives only as persuasive language, not binding law. America still has no recognized federal constitutional right to learn to read.

I credit the governor for settling rather than fighting children in court, and I credit the state officials who argued sincerely that schools are a matter for legislatures, not judges. But look at the result. The courts, which will readily send an illiterate adult to prison, declined to hold that the state owed him the skill that might have kept him out. And the criminal courts themselves rarely screen defendants for reading ability, though they hand out written plea forms, written probation terms, and written orders, and then punish people for failing to follow them.

Schools deserve the larger share of blame, and I say that knowing how hard teachers work. For decades many American classrooms taught reading with methods that encouraged children to guess words from pictures and context rather than sound them out, approaches that the research on how the brain learns to read had long called into question. Children from homes full of books often learned anyway. Children without that cushion, disproportionately poor, were the ones left behind. Some districts have begun to reckon with that publicly, retraining teachers and replacing curricula. Many have not, and the children in their classrooms right now are paying for the delay.

What Works, and Who Can Do It

Literacy is one of the few crime-prevention tools with evidence at every stage of life, and the most striking recent case is Mississippi. In 2013 the state's legislature passed a literacy law that trained teachers in reading instruction grounded in phonics and the science of how children learn to decode, placed coaches in struggling schools, screened young students repeatedly, and required intensive help, and in some cases retention, for third graders who could not read at a basic level. A state long ranked near the bottom of national reading tests climbed to around the national average for fourth-grade reading within several years. Other states, from Alabama to Tennessee, have since passed laws built on similar ideas.

Any legislature can copy that model, and any school board can adopt reading curricula backed by evidence rather than fashion. Superintendents can screen every child for reading difficulty and dyslexia in kindergarten through second grade and fund tutoring for those who fall behind before the third-grade hinge. City councils and library boards can fund adult literacy programs in branch libraries, open in the evening, staffed by people trained to work with adults who are ashamed.

Employers belong in this conversation as well. A plant manager or a warehouse owner who partners with a local adult education program, offering paid time for reading classes or hiring graduates, buys loyalty and safety at the same time. Workforce boards that already fund job training can require a literacy screen at intake, so no one is enrolled in a certification course he cannot pass. And churches, unions, and neighborhood associations can host tutoring in places where adults already feel at home, which matters more than any curriculum, because the first obstacle for most adult learners is walking through a door marked education.

Inside prisons, the evidence is just as strong. A large analysis by the RAND Corporation, commissioned by the federal government and published in 2013, pooled decades of studies and found that people who took part in correctional education programs had substantially lower odds of returning to prison, on the order of forty percent, than those who did not. Every dollar spent on that education saved several dollars in reincarceration costs. Congress, which cut prisoners off from federal Pell Grants in the 1994 crime bill, restored that eligibility in 2020 legislation, and it took effect in 2023. State corrections departments can now partner with colleges at little cost to taxpayers, and they should do so at every facility.

Courts can act too. Judges and public defender offices can screen defendants for reading ability at arraignment and explain plea terms and probation conditions orally, in plain language, with the defendant's understanding confirmed on the record. Probation departments can make basic literacy classes a condition of supervision rather than an afterthought, and give credit toward early discharge for progress. Parole boards can treat a reading certificate earned behind bars as evidence of rehabilitation, because it is.

Ray, to his credit, did something I did not expect. A year after the sentencing, he began volunteering as a reading tutor at the public library near his house, one evening a week, working with adults. He told me he had not forgiven the man who broke into his home and did not intend to. He just wanted to make the next one less likely. I can think of no more practical answer to crime than that one, from a man who had every reason to want only punishment.

The takeaway

A person who cannot read has been shut out of nearly every lawful way to earn a living, and our prisons are filled with those people, released with paperwork they cannot use. Ask your school board how it screens young readers and what it does before third grade, and find out whether your local library or jail runs an adult literacy program that needs a volunteer or a vote.

Chapter 3: Addiction, Desperation, and the Customer Side of Crime

Who actually robbed you? Not in the legal sense, with a name on a charging document, but in the deeper sense most victims eventually start to wonder about. When a stranger puts a knife across a convenience store counter at two in the morning, is the person holding it the whole story, or the last link in a chain that started somewhere far away and much higher up?

Marisol asked me that question at her kitchen table, about a year after it happened to her. She had been working the overnight shift at a gas station mini-mart, alone, when a man in a hooded sweatshirt came in, shaking, and told her to empty the register. He got a little over a hundred dollars. She got a scar on her forearm from the blade when she flinched, months of panic attacks, and a new job in daylight. Police caught him within the week. He had pawned a stolen phone the same night, and when officers picked him up he was, by his own account in court, already sick from withdrawal again.

My answer to Marisol, and to every reader who has ever had a car window smashed or a purse snatched, is that a startling share of everyday predatory crime in America happens on the customer side of the drug trade. The man at the counter was not a dealer. He was a buyer, desperate for the next dose, and the robbery was how he paid for it. Our enforcement apparatus is built to chase the people selling drugs. It has never been built to deal with the people buying them, and that is precisely where the theft, the robbery, and much of the violence take place.

The Economics of Addiction

Addiction turns a person into a customer with a fixed daily expense and almost no income. Someone dependent on heroin or fentanyl may need to use several times a day to stave off withdrawal, which is not a mild discomfort but a full-body sickness of vomiting, aching, sweating, and panic. The cost of that habit varies by city and by drug, but over a year it can run into many thousands of dollars. Very few people with a serious addiction hold jobs that pay for that. The money has to come from somewhere.

It comes from shoplifting, car break-ins, stolen catalytic converters, burglaries of garages and sheds, petty fraud, sex work, and robbery. Stolen goods are sold at a steep discount, often a small fraction of their value, which means a user must steal far more than the cost of the drug to cover it. A person who needs a hundred dollars of drugs may need to steal several hundred dollars of merchandise. Multiply that by every day of a long addiction and you begin to see why one desperate person can drive a neighborhood's property crime numbers by himself.

Federal drug-testing programs that sampled people arrested in American cities during the late 1990s and 2000s routinely found that a majority of arrestees tested positive for at least one illegal drug at the time of booking. Not all of those arrests were for drug-motivated crimes, and drug use and crime share many of the same roots. But anyone who has spent time at a booking desk, as I have, knows the pattern. The same faces come back, the same withdrawals play out in the holding cell, and the same thefts begin again within days of release.

The modern American wave of addiction did not start on a street corner. It started, for many people, in a doctor's office. Purdue Pharma marketed its painkiller OxyContin aggressively from the late 1990s, and in 2007 the company and three executives pleaded guilty to federal charges of misbranding the drug, misleading doctors and patients about its risk of addiction, and paid hundreds of millions of dollars. In 2020 Purdue pleaded guilty again, to federal felonies connected to its marketing. Millions of Americans were prescribed opioids in those years. A large number became dependent, and when prescriptions tightened, many turned to heroin and then to illicit fentanyl.

Why Law Enforcement Only Chases Suppliers

Even at the very top of the chain, the instinct is to chase and punish the supplier, and when the supplier is rich, the chase can stall. The Sackler family, which owned Purdue, tried to settle thousands of lawsuits through the company's bankruptcy in a deal that would have shielded family members from future civil claims, though they had not filed for bankruptcy themselves. In Harrington v. Purdue Pharma in 2024, the Supreme Court held, by a five-to-four vote, that the bankruptcy code does not authorize a release that extinguishes victims' claims against third parties like the Sacklers without the victims' consent.

The ruling sent negotiators back to the table, and a new, multibillion-dollar settlement plan followed. Families who had lost children to overdoses had spent years in bankruptcy hearings, many of them traveling to testify about what the drug had done to their homes. I count the decision as a rare moment when the courts refused to let the people at the top of a supply chain buy their way out cheaply.

Street enforcement follows a different logic, aimed at the middle and bottom of the supply chain. The prestige case in American drug policing has always been the seizure: the kilo on the table at a press conference, the cash fanned out next to a pistol, the dealer in handcuffs. Seizures and dealer arrests are countable, they photograph well, and they justify budgets. Federal grants and task force money have for decades rewarded agencies for exactly those numbers. A chief who reports a record seizure gets a headline. A chief who reports that burglaries fell because fewer residents were in active addiction gets a shrug.

I do not doubt the sincerity of officers who work narcotics. Many of the ones I have known hate what the drug trade does to their towns, and taking a violent trafficker off the street is real work that protects people. But decades of seizures have not made drugs meaningfully scarcer or more expensive on American streets for long. Remove one supplier and another fills the gap within weeks, because the demand never left. Enforcement aimed only at supply is like bailing a boat while ignoring the hole.

There is a second, quieter reason the customer gets ignored. He is a hard case, and no agency owns him. Police see him as a medical problem. Hospitals see him as a police problem. Treatment programs, where they exist, often require the kind of stability, a phone number, an address, a clean stretch of days, that a person in active addiction cannot produce. So everyone passes him along, and the only institution that never turns him away is the jail, which is the one least equipped to change anything about him.

The Customer as the Crime Epicenter

When the system does touch the customer, it usually processes him rather than treats him. Arrests for simple drug possession have numbered in the hundreds of thousands to more than a million a year nationally for much of the last few decades. Each arrest produces a booking, a court date, perhaps a few days in jail, and a record that makes legitimate employment even harder. Most jails offer little or no treatment. The person is released in withdrawal, often with lowered tolerance, which makes the first dose after release especially likely to kill him.

The Supreme Court drew a line on this decades ago, then mostly left it alone. In Robinson v. California in 1962, the Court struck down a state law that made it a crime simply to be addicted to narcotics, holding that punishing a person for the status of addiction, as distinct from any act, was cruel and unusual punishment under the Eighth Amendment. Six years later, in Powell v. Texas, a divided Court declined to extend that reasoning to public drunkenness, leaving states free to punish conduct that flows from addiction. The practical result is the system we have: we cannot jail someone for being addicted, but we can jail him for nearly everything addiction drives him to do.

Seen from Marisol's side of the counter, the customer is where the crime lives. Dealers mostly sell to people who come to them. It is the buyer who walks into her store, who breaks into her neighbor's truck, who snatches the purse in the parking lot. And because the system keeps cycling him through jail and back to the street without changing his need, he returns again and again to the same blocks, robbing the same kinds of people. A town can lock up its dealers one after another and still have a theft problem, because the customers are still there and still sick.

The taxpayer pays for that cycle at every turn. Each arrest costs officer hours, each booking costs jail staff time, each court appearance costs a judge, a prosecutor, and a public defender, and each release starts the meter again. Store owners pay through higher insurance premiums and security guards. Shoppers pay through locked display cases and higher prices. Add it up across a mid-sized city and the cost of managing addicted customers through the criminal system easily runs well into the millions of dollars a year, without curing a single one of them.

Desperation-Driven Violence

Most drug-driven crime is theft rather than bloodshed, but desperation changes the math. Withdrawal compresses a person's time horizon to a few hours. Someone who would never consider armed robbery when stable may, at hour twenty of being sick, decide a knife and an empty store are worth the risk. That is what happened to Marisol. The man who cut her did not plan to hurt anyone. He also did not plan anything at all past the next dose, and that is what makes desperate offenders so dangerous to clerks, delivery drivers, and people walking to their cars.

Fentanyl has made the desperation worse. Its effects wear off faster than heroin's for many users, which shortens the cycle between doses and tightens the hunt for money. Its potency also means overdoses come without warning, so the customer lives with the constant possibility of death and with the knowledge that he may lose friends at any time. People in that state are not weighing prison sentences. Deterrence, the whole theory behind longer penalties, assumes a mind that is calculating months ahead. Withdrawal leaves room to calculate only the next few hours.

Violence also runs through the transactions themselves. Disputes over debts, stolen stashes, and robberies of buyers and sellers carry no recourse to police, so they are settled by force. People in active addiction are victimized at high rates too, robbed, beaten, and sexually exploited by others who know they will not report it. When reporters write about the drug trade's violence, they usually picture rival dealers. In my experience, a great deal of it falls on the customers and on the ordinary people who happen to be standing near them.

Missed Treatment and Prevention Models

Other places have treated the customer as the point of intervention, and their results deserve a hearing. In 2001 Portugal decriminalized the personal possession of all drugs, while keeping trafficking a crime. People found with small amounts are referred to regional panels, often called dissuasion commissions, that can steer them toward treatment, levy fines, or impose other administrative sanctions. Portugal paired the change with a major expansion of treatment and harm reduction. In the years that followed, the country saw sharp declines in drug-related HIV infections and overdose deaths, and it did not suffer the surge in drug use that critics predicted, though officials there have more recently warned of strains as funding and attention faded.

Oregon tried to import part of that idea and learned a hard lesson. In 2020, voters passed Measure 110, which decriminalized possession of small amounts of drugs and redirected cannabis tax money toward addiction services. The policy was sold as a humane, health-first answer to a drug war that had filled jails without curing anyone, and many of its supporters were treatment providers and people in recovery who had seen the old system fail up close. The decriminalization took effect quickly, in early 2021.

The treatment system did not, as grant money moved slowly and the new citations, which could be cleared with a phone call to a hotline, were mostly ignored. Public drug use became more visible in Portland and other cities, and overdose deaths kept rising as fentanyl spread, though researchers still argue about how much of that rise to blame on the measure itself. In 2024 the legislature passed House Bill 4002, making possession a misdemeanor again as of that September and encouraging counties to set up deflection programs that send people to treatment instead of prosecution.

Taken together, the two stories point to the same conclusion. Removing the penalty without building the treatment fails. Building the treatment while giving police a reason and a route to send people into it can work. Seattle's Law Enforcement Assisted Diversion program, started in 2011, lets officers send people caught in low-level drug activity to case managers instead of jail. A University of Washington evaluation found participants had markedly lower odds of rearrest than a comparison group, though the study was small and not randomized, and its headline numbers have been disputed. Versions of the model have since spread to dozens of jurisdictions.

What a Town Can Do About the Customer

A police chief can make diversion the default for low-level possession and addiction-driven theft, partnering with a treatment provider so an officer on the street at two in the morning has somewhere to take a person other than a holding cell. County commissioners can fund medication for opioid use disorder, buprenorphine and methadone, in their jails, as a number of states and counties have begun to do, and they can make sure every person leaving jail with an addiction walks out with a prescription, a naloxone kit, and an appointment.

State legislatures can do what Oregon eventually tried: keep a penalty as leverage, but write treatment capacity into the law with deadlines and money attached, and audit the results publicly every year. Hospitals and emergency departments can start buprenorphine for people who come in after an overdose instead of discharging them with a pamphlet. Courts can expand drug courts that use treatment and supervision together, focusing them on the people committing the most thefts.

Citizens, finally, can change what they demand. When a town meeting calls for more police after a rash of car break-ins, someone in the room can ask how many treatment slots exist in the county and how long the wait is. Marisol did. She now sits on a community advisory board for a recovery clinic near her old store. She has not stopped being angry at the man who cut her, and she should not. She simply concluded that the way to protect the next clerk was to reach the next customer before he walked through the door.

The takeaway

The person who robs you to feed an addiction is not a supplier; he is a customer, and no seizure on a press-conference table will make him stop needing the drug. Find out how long the wait is for addiction treatment in your county and whether your local jail offers medication for opioid addiction, and press your sheriff and county board to close that gap.

Chapter 4: Generational Trauma and Crime Families

The first time Walt saw the family's name on a court docket, he was a young man serving on a county grand jury, and the defendant was a father accused of beating his wife so badly she lost hearing in one ear. Thirty-some years later, retired and in the habit of attending county budget hearings because he wanted to know where his property taxes went, Walt heard the same surname read aloud again. This time it belonged to a nineteen-year-old charged with shooting a man outside a bar. The young man was the grandson. In the gallery sat the father's son, the defendant's own father, who had served two prison terms of his own.

Walt told me the story over coffee in the courthouse cafeteria, still a little shaken by it. In the first moment, decades ago, the system had treated the grandfather as a single violent man with a single case. Nobody asked about the children in the house. In the second moment, the system was treating the grandson exactly the same way, as a single violent young man with a single case, as if he had arrived in the world alone. Between those two hearings lay three generations, two more prison sentences, and who knows how many victims. The courthouse had seen all of it and connected none of it.

I have covered courts long enough to know that Walt's story is not unusual. Any veteran prosecutor, public defender, or probation officer can name families whose members have filled their caseloads across decades. They talk about it in hallways, half joking, half despairing. Then they go back into the courtroom and treat each defendant as if he had no family at all. My argument is simple: violence is learned at home and handed down, and a justice system that refuses to see families can never break the chain.

Learned Violence

Children learn violence the way they learn language, by immersion. A boy who watches his father hit his mother learns that anger is settled with fists, that women are targets, and that the person with the most force wins. A girl in the same house learns that love and fear arrive together. None of these lessons are spoken. They are demonstrated, repeatedly, by the people the child depends on for survival, and they sink in deeper than anything a school assembly can teach later.

In 1989 the criminologist Cathy Spatz Widom published research in the journal Science that followed children whose abuse or neglect had been documented by courts in a Midwestern county, then compared their later records with those of similar children who had not been maltreated. Those who had been abused or neglected were significantly more likely to be arrested as juveniles and as adults, and more likely to be arrested for violent crime. Her work helped give the phrase "cycle of violence" its scientific weight. She was careful, and I want to be careful too: most abused children never become violent adults. The risk rises; destiny does not follow.

Witnessing counts as much as being struck. Children who grow up watching domestic violence, even when no adult lays a hand on them, show many of the same emotional and behavioral injuries as children who are beaten. They are hypervigilant. They have trouble sleeping and trouble learning. Many of them, as teenagers, either copy the violence or attach themselves to partners who use it. When police answer a domestic call and arrest the man of the house, the report rarely records the children who hid in the bedroom listening, and nobody follows up with them the next morning.

Walt's grandson fit the pattern almost perfectly. In the first moment of this story, a little boy, his father, was in the house when the grandfather beat the grandmother, and court records from the time mention children present. In the second moment, that little boy had grown into a man with his own record of assault, and his own son had grown up watching him. Each generation learned from the one before. Each generation went to prison. And the lesson passed down anyway, because prison removes a father from the house without removing what he taught.

Family Systems That Breed Crime

Families are systems, and some systems reward exactly the behavior the rest of society punishes. In certain households, fighting is how a child earns respect, stealing is how the family eats, and talking to police is the ultimate betrayal. Loyalty to the family outranks loyalty to any law. Children raised inside those rules are not being lawless. They are following the only code they have ever seen enforced, and they are often punished at home for breaking it.

The forces that hold such a system together are rarely dramatic. Addiction that runs through generations, untreated mental illness, poverty that keeps three generations in the same cramped apartment, and a deep distrust of every institution that has ever come to the door, each of these makes it harder for a child to see another way to live. The adults in these families were usually children in them first. When I have sat with grandmothers raising the children of their incarcerated sons, they often describe their own fathers the same way they describe their grandsons, and they are exhausted by the repetition.

Older siblings and cousins often do the recruiting that outsiders blame on gangs. A twelve-year-old does not need a stranger to introduce him to burglary when his brother already keeps stolen tools in the garage. He learns the trade, the vocabulary, and the attitude toward police at the dinner table. In some families the first arrest is treated almost as a rite of passage, a sign that a boy has become a man. That is not a cultural slur against any group. I have seen the same pattern in rural white counties, in immigrant neighborhoods, and in big-city housing projects, wherever a family has made crime its inheritance.

No case I know shows how a nation can obsess over a family's interior while understanding nothing about it better than the trial of Casey Anthony. Her two-year-old daughter, Caylee, went missing in Florida in 2008, and the child's grandmother reported it to authorities about a month after Caylee was last seen. The girl's remains were later found in woods near the family home.

At the murder trial in 2011, Anthony's defense lawyer told jurors that Caylee had drowned in the family pool and alleged that Anthony had been sexually abused by her father, an allegation her father denied under oath and that was never proved. The jury acquitted her of murder and convicted her of lying to police, and an appeals court later threw out two of those four misdemeanor convictions.

For weeks, millions of Americans watched the inner life of one family dissected on cable television, every lie, every dysfunction, every strange silence. I watched the national frenzy closely and found it revealing for what it never examined. The family's interior became public evidence only after a child was dead, and only as a courtroom tactic. Nobody, then or since, has drawn the lesson that the conditions inside a household are worth understanding before a tragedy rather than after it. The country treated the case as a whodunit. It was also a portrait of how little any agency knows about the families it later prosecutes.

Intergenerational Patterns

Research on families with multiple generations in the justice system keeps arriving at the same finding: having a parent who has been convicted or imprisoned is one of the strongest predictors of a child's own later offending. Long-running studies in Britain and the United States that followed boys from childhood into adulthood found that a small fraction of families accounted for a large share of all convictions in their communities. The pattern held across neighborhoods, decades, and countries. Crime is concentrated not only in places, but in households.

Parental incarceration is itself a wound. Millions of American children have had a parent in prison at some point, a burden that falls hardest on poor families and on Black families. Those children lose income, lose stability, often lose their homes, and carry the stigma to school. Some are relieved to see a violent parent removed. Many are devastated. Almost none are offered help by the system that took the parent away, because the court's file contains the defendant's name and charge, not the names of the children waiting at home.

Trauma also travels in ways that look nothing like a family business. A mother who survived childhood sexual abuse and never received treatment may struggle to protect her own children, not from indifference but because her alarm system was broken long ago. A father raised in a violent home may swear he will never hit his kids and still explode under stress the way his own father did. These patterns are not excuses. They are mechanisms, and anything that can be understood as a mechanism can be interrupted.

Why Agencies Avoid Family-Based Intervention

The Supreme Court made the government's indifference a matter of constitutional law in 1989. In DeShaney v. Winnebago County, the justices considered the case of Joshua DeShaney, a Wisconsin boy whose father beat him so severely that the child was left with permanent brain damage. County social workers had received repeated reports of abuse, had documented suspicious injuries, and had still left the boy in his father's custody. The Court held that the Fourteenth Amendment's Due Process Clause does not require the state to protect people from violence by private actors, so the county could not be held liable under the Constitution for failing to act. Justice Harry Blackmun's dissent, lamenting the boy's fate in unusually raw language, remains one of the most quoted passages in American legal history.

I respect the majority's concern about turning every social worker's mistake into a federal lawsuit, and I understand the argument that such questions belong to state legislatures. But the decision told every agency in the country something they already suspected: there is no legal price for looking away from a family in crisis. Child welfare offices are understaffed and overwhelmed. Police respond to the call, not the household. Courts are organized around individual defendants. Probation officers supervise one person, not the people he goes home to.

Money and privacy fears do the rest. Family-based programs cost more up front, cross agency budgets, and require workers to go into homes where they are often not welcome. Civil libertarians worry, reasonably, about the government reaching into family life, and families with long histories of being policed have every reason to slam the door. The result is a strange equilibrium. Everyone agrees that crime runs in families. No agency is assigned to treat the family.

Even when programs exist, they are the first to go in a budget crunch. I have watched county boards cut family counseling and home visiting while leaving jail budgets untouched, because jail is a mandated expense and prevention is optional. The officials who make those cuts are not villains. They face real deficits and loud voters. But the cut is a false economy, and they know it. Every family program eliminated in a lean year returns as a docket full of that family's children a decade later, at many times the cost.

Community Collapse Through Family Crime Networks

When a handful of families carries a large share of a neighborhood's violence, the whole community bends around them. Neighbors stop calling police because the people they would report on are related to half the block. Feuds between families outlast any single arrest and flare up again when someone comes home from prison. Witnesses go silent, not from loyalty to criminals, but from a realistic fear of the cousins and brothers who will still be living down the street long after the trial.

Retaliation turns family disputes into community violence. A shooting between two young men becomes a feud between their households, and the feud pulls in brothers, cousins, and friends who had nothing to do with the original quarrel. Small businesses close early. Parents keep children indoors. The block's ordinary residents, the majority who want nothing to do with any of it, lose the use of their own sidewalks. When I have reported in neighborhoods caught in these feuds, the people I interviewed rarely asked for more arrests. They asked for someone to sit the families down before the next funeral.

Walt saw what that does to a tax base. The county he lives in has spent, over the decades, enormous sums prosecuting, jailing, and supervising the members of a few families, while the businesses near their homes closed and the houses lost value. He does not romanticize the families. He has been afraid of some of them. But he is a practical man, and he asked me the practical question: if the county had spent even a fraction of that money on the children in those houses in the first moment of his story, would it have needed to spend the rest in the second?

What Agencies Can Do With Families

The evidence suggests it would not. The Nurse-Family Partnership, developed by the researcher David Olds and first tested in a randomized trial in Elmira, New York, sends trained nurses to visit low-income first-time mothers from pregnancy until the child turns two. Long-term follow-ups of that trial found fewer cases of verified child abuse and neglect among participating families, and, when the children reached their teens, fewer arrests. Later trials in Memphis and Denver supported the approach, and the program now operates in many states. Every county commission and state legislature can fund it or similar home-visiting programs for the highest-risk families.

For teenagers already in trouble, there are family therapies with real track records. Multisystemic Therapy and Functional Family Therapy, both tested in controlled studies over decades, put therapists to work with the young person and the whole household together, in the home, on parenting, discipline, school, and peers. Several states have used them as alternatives to juvenile incarceration and have reported lower rates of reoffending and lower costs than locking young people up. Juvenile court judges can order these programs, and state juvenile justice agencies can make them the default for serious young offenders rather than a last resort.

Courts and prosecutors can also simply start counting families. A court system can flag, at sentencing, when a defendant has children at home or parents and siblings with records, and route that information to agencies equipped to help. Prisons can support children's visits and family programs instead of making them harder. Child welfare offices can use those flags to offer services before the next generation reaches the docket. And citizens like Walt can show up at budget hearings and ask, in public, how much the county spends on jail beds compared with how much it spends on helping the families who keep filling them.

The takeaway

Violence is taught in homes and inherited across generations, and every time a court treats an offender as if he had no family, it guarantees the next defendant will share his name. Ask your county or state whether it funds nurse home visiting or proven family therapy for young offenders, and press your representatives to treat the family, not just the defendant.

Chapter 5: Why People Snap: The Psychology We Ignore

"Everybody keeps saying he just snapped," Teresa told me, stirring a cup of diner coffee she never drank. "He didn't just snap. He told us for a year." She was talking about the former coworker who came back to her office with a gun, the man whose bullet passed through the cubicle wall a few inches from her head and killed the colleague at the next desk. She had heard the phrase on the evening news the night it happened, from a neighbor the next morning, and from a detective a week later. Each time it made her angrier. She had reported his threats. Twice.

We talked for two hours. She described his grievances, a disciplinary write-up he considered unfair, a denied transfer, a divorce, a supervisor he believed was out to ruin him. She described the remarks he made in the break room about people who deserved what was coming. She described the human resources meeting where her concerns were thanked and filed. Nothing she told me was secret. Every warning had passed through some official channel and stopped there, the way water stops at a closed valve.

I have heard versions of Teresa's story in many cities, from survivors of workplace attacks, school shootings, and domestic killings that made the front page. The word "snap" is a comfort. It suggests something sudden and unforeseeable, a bolt of lightning nobody could have stopped. It almost never fits the facts. Most so-called sudden violence is a slow burn with predictable breaking points, and the warning signs are usually visible to the people around the attacker for weeks, months, or years. We choose not to read them.

Chronic Stress and Erosion

The erosion begins long before anyone notices danger. A person who is chronically stressed, by debt, by illness, by a failing marriage, by a job that humiliates him, lives with a stress response that never shuts off. Sleep goes. Patience goes. The capacity to see another person's point of view narrows. Friends drift away because he has become exhausting to be around, which means fewer people are close enough to notice when he gets worse. None of this makes violence inevitable. Most people under crushing stress never hurt anyone. But in a small number of cases, erosion is the soil in which a grievance takes root.

Researchers at the Secret Service's National Threat Assessment Center, which studies attacks against public places and schools, have reported again and again that the people who carry out these attacks had almost always experienced significant stressors in the period before the attack, often several at once: job loss, family conflict, legal trouble, financial crisis. Many had histories of mental health symptoms, though most people with mental illness are never violent, and the center's researchers emphasize that diagnosis alone predicts very little. What predicts more is the combination, the piling up of losses with no outlet and no one paying attention.

Teresa watched that piling up happen to the man who later shot at her, though she did not have the vocabulary for it at the time. He stopped eating lunch with anyone. He came in looking as if he had not slept. He talked about money constantly, about lawyers, about a house he was going to lose. His jokes turned bitter, then stopped. Looking back, she said, the change was so gradual that each week seemed only slightly worse than the last, and that is exactly how erosion hides. Nobody around him could point to the day he became dangerous, because there was no such day.

Bureaucratic Abuse and Institutional Harm

Some of the most dangerous grievances are not with a spouse or a coworker. They are with an institution. People who feel crushed by a faceless system, a city hall that keeps fining them, an employer that keeps disciplining them, a court that keeps ruling against them, can come to see that institution as a personal enemy. The bureaucracy, for its part, rarely notices it has become anyone's enemy at all. It is processing paperwork.

I have spent time at the counters where that paperwork changes hands: permit offices, benefits offices, courthouse clerks' windows, unemployment lines. Most of the people behind those counters are decent and overworked, and most of the people in front of them are frustrated but harmless. What struck me was how rarely anyone kept track of the exceptions. The same man might come back a dozen times over a year, more agitated each visit, and each clerk would see him as a fresh, isolated encounter. Systems that log every form in triplicate often keep no record at all of a person's growing rage.

The phrase "going postal" entered American speech after August 20, 1986, when a part-time letter carrier named Patrick Sherrill walked into the post office in Edmond, Oklahoma, and shot fourteen coworkers to death before killing himself. He had been reprimanded by supervisors the day before, and accounts from the time describe him as a troubled employee who believed he was about to be fired and who had a long record of conflicts on the job. A string of later postal workplace shootings followed, and the Postal Service eventually overhauled how it handled discipline, grievances, and threats. Edmond became shorthand for a joke. It should have become a case study in how workplace humiliation, left unmanaged, can curdle.

In Kirkwood, Missouri, on February 7, 2008, a local businessman named Charles Lee Thornton walked into a city council meeting and opened fire, killing two police officers, two council members, and the city's public works director, and critically wounding the mayor, before officers killed him. Thornton had spent years in conflict with the city. He had received a large number of tickets and citations over his business, had been removed from council meetings for disruptive behavior, and had lost a federal lawsuit claiming the city had violated his free speech rights less than two weeks before the shooting. Everyone in Kirkwood government knew who he was and how angry he had become.

None of this excuses a single death, and I will say it plainly for the families of the people killed in Edmond and Kirkwood: the men who pulled the triggers bear full responsibility. But responsibility and prediction are different questions. Both men had broadcast their grievances for years. Both were known to the institutions they attacked. In both cities, the institutions treated the grievance as a nuisance to be managed rather than a risk to be assessed.

The Slow Burn Before Violence

The period between a grievance and an attack has a recognizable shape, and threat assessment professionals have spent decades mapping it. The person fixates on the wrong done to him. He begins to imagine violence as a solution, then to research it, then to talk about it, often obliquely and sometimes directly. He may acquire weapons or practice with them. He may write about previous attackers with admiration. Researchers call the habit of telling others about the plan, intentionally or not, leakage, and it is one of the most consistent findings in the field.

In its 2019 study of targeted attacks on American schools, covering dozens of incidents over the previous decade, the Secret Service found that most of the attackers had shown behavior that worried the people around them, and that in many cases those concerns were never reported or never acted upon. Many attackers had communicated their intentions or made threats in advance. Most had grievances, frequently with classmates. Almost none fit a simple profile, which is exactly why the center argues for assessing behavior rather than hunting for a type.

The shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland, Florida, on February 14, 2018, shows the slow burn in public view. A former student killed seventeen people and wounded seventeen others. He later pleaded guilty to the murders and, after a jury did not unanimously recommend death, was sentenced to life in prison without parole. In the years before the attack, he had alarmed classmates, teachers, neighbors, and relatives. The local sheriff's office had received many calls about him. The FBI acknowledged that a person close to him had called its tip line in January 2018 warning about his guns, his erratic behavior, and his disturbing posts online, and that the tip was never passed along to field agents for investigation.

Florida's state commission, created to investigate the shooting, laid out warning after warning that went nowhere, scattered across a school district, a sheriff's office, mental health providers, and federal agents who never put the pieces together. The commission's work was thorough and, to its credit, unsparing about the failures of the officials who had appointed it. Reading it, I kept thinking of Teresa's word for her own case: a year. Here it was years. The burn had been visible to an entire community, and no single person or office ever owned the job of responding to it.

Predictable Breaking Points

If the slow burn is the fuel, the breaking point is the spark, and breaking points are among the most predictable events in criminal justice. A firing. A final disciplinary meeting. A divorce filing, or the day a protective order is served. A foreclosure or an eviction. A court loss. A suspension or expulsion from school. Threat assessment teams know that these moments, when a person feels his last option has been taken away, are when risk peaks. For domestic violence, separation is the most dangerous period of all, and advocates have known that for decades.

Domestic homicide researchers have identified the danger signs with unusual precision: prior strangulation, threats to kill, escalating jealousy and control, access to a gun, and a partner trying to leave. Some police departments and advocates now use short lethality screening questionnaires at the scene of domestic calls, connecting the highest-risk victims with advocates on the spot. Maryland pioneered one such protocol, and versions of it have spread to many other states. It is threat assessment by another name, applied to the most common kind of predictable killing in America.

What makes these moments so tragic is that institutions usually schedule them. A company decides when to fire someone. A court sets the date for a hearing. A school chooses the day of an expulsion. Those dates are on a calendar weeks in advance, which means there is time to plan: to involve security, to offer counseling or an exit package, to check whether the person has access to weapons, to warn potential targets. In Edmond, the reprimand happened the day before the shooting. In Kirkwood, the court loss came ten days before. The spark was set by the institution, then ignored by it.

Why Society Refuses to Study the Why

The refusal to understand is partly political. For more than two decades after Congress attached a 1996 provision barring federal injury prevention money from being used to advocate or promote gun control, federal research funding on firearm violence largely dried up, as scientists and officials read the language as a warning to stay away. Congress began appropriating dedicated money for that research again only in recent years. Whatever one thinks about gun policy, starving the study of violence of data made the country dumber about its own crisis.

Emotion does the rest. After an attack, the public wants a monster, not a mechanism. Explaining how a man arrived at violence feels, to many grieving people, like excusing him, and I understand why. I have sat with families who could not bear to hear the attacker's history spoken aloud. But understanding is not sympathy. A fire investigator who studies how a blaze started is not defending the arsonist. He is trying to keep the next building standing. Calling every attack a sudden snap is how institutions avoid admitting they were warned.

Liability completes the silence. After an attack, lawyers for the employer, the school district, or the agency often advise officials to say as little as possible, because every admission of a missed warning can become evidence in a lawsuit. Internal reviews get labeled confidential. Personnel files stay sealed. The families of victims, who want above all to know what happened, end up fighting for records in court for years. Florida's decision to create a public commission after Parkland was the exception, and it produced exactly the kind of honest accounting that every community deserves and few get.

The law has long recognized that warning signs create duties. In Tarasoff v. Regents of the University of California in 1976, the California Supreme Court held that a therapist whose patient poses a serious danger of violence to an identifiable person has a duty to take reasonable steps to protect that person. Many states adopted their own versions of the rule. It was a landmark acknowledgment that the people closest to a threat can and must act. Yet outside the therapist's office, most institutions still face no clear duty to assess the threats they hear about every day.

What Institutions Can Do Before the Breaking Point

Employers, schools, and city governments can build threat assessment teams on the model the Secret Service has published for free: a small, trained group, usually drawing on management, security, mental health, and legal staff, that receives every report of concerning behavior, investigates it, and manages the case until the risk passes. Many school districts and universities, including Virginia's public colleges under a state law passed after the 2007 Virginia Tech shooting, now require such teams. Any city council can create one for its own employees and for the residents who come before it angry.

Legislatures can give families and police a legal tool to act during the slow burn. Florida passed a risk protection order law weeks after Parkland, allowing law enforcement to ask a court to temporarily remove firearms from a person shown to pose a significant danger, and a number of other states have similar extreme risk laws. These orders come with due process, a hearing, and a time limit, and they work best when the people who see the warning signs know they exist. Police chiefs can train officers to use them and publicize how families can request help.

Human resources departments and courts can treat breaking points as security events. Before a firing, a final reprimand, a protective order hearing, or an eviction, someone should ask whether the person has made threats, whether he has weapons, and who might be in danger. Citizens matter most of all. Teresa now speaks to workplace groups about reporting threats and, just as important, about demanding a response when they do. Her message is not complicated: if you hear it, say it, and if nobody acts, say it again, louder, to someone higher up.

The takeaway

People rarely snap; they erode, they warn, and then they break at moments institutions can see coming on a calendar. Find out whether your employer, your school district, and your city government have a trained threat assessment team and a clear way to report a warning, and if they do not, ask the person in charge why not.

Chapter 6: Media's Role in National Ignorance

Denise kept the television on mute in her kitchen the week her son was shot. He lived, barely, after a bullet passed through his hip outside a corner store four blocks from her house. She told me later that she watched the screen anyway, waiting for his name, his street, anything that would tell her the city knew what had happened to her family. It never came. What came instead, every hour, was a courtroom in another state, a defendant she had never heard of, and a panel of commentators arguing about whether a young mother had the right face for a killer.

I have sat with a lot of Denises. In my years on the crime beat I learned that the people who pay for crime rarely recognize themselves in the coverage of it. They see a show. They see a story built for ratings, cast with villains and heroes, and wrapped up before the commercial break. Then they see the same country that watched the show vote, fund, and legislate as if the show were the truth. That gap between what crime is and what the public is taught crime looks like is not a side issue. It is one of the reasons we keep choosing the wrong fixes.

Crime as Entertainment

The summer of 2011 belonged to Casey Anthony. Her daughter, Caylee, was last seen in June 2008, and the girl was not reported missing until mid-July, when her grandmother called authorities and said the car smelled of death. That delay, and the string of lies Casey told detectives about a nanny who did not exist, turned an Orlando family tragedy into a national serial. In December 2008 the child's remains were found in woods not far from the family home. By the time the trial opened, cable networks had spent the better part of three years treating the case as a nightly drama.

I watched how the coverage worked, because it was impossible not to. Hosts gave the defendant a nickname. Panels convicted her between segments. Crowds lined up outside the courthouse for seats as if for a concert. The trial was carried gavel to gavel, and viewers came away certain of what had happened. On July 5, 2011, the jury acquitted Casey Anthony of first-degree murder, aggravated child abuse, and aggravated manslaughter. It convicted her only of four misdemeanor counts of giving false information to police. Much of the country reacted as if a crime had been committed in the jury room.

The jurors had seen something the audience had not been shown clearly. The medical examiner ruled the death a homicide but could not determine the cause, so the prosecution's theory of how Caylee died rested on inference. Key forensic evidence, including air samples from the car trunk, was novel and contested. The defense offered its own disputed account.
