Officers learn that the easiest way to satisfy the meeting is to produce stops, summonses, and arrests, whether or not those actions make anyone safer. Supervisors learn that a robbery recorded as lost property improves the map. The tool built to hold commanders accountable turned into a machine for generating the appearance of accountability.

New York was not unique. Audits and newspaper investigations in several other large cities over the years found serious assaults recorded as minor ones, burglaries logged as vandalism, and sexual assault reports left unclassified. Each time, the department involved promised reforms, and each time, the deeper problem survived: an institution grading its own homework will always find a way to pass. When the crime numbers themselves cannot be trusted, every claim built on them, every budget request and every victory lap, rests on sand.

The Absence of Impact Measurement

Ask almost any police department in America a simple question: which of your programs reduced crime last year, and by how much? You will usually get anecdotes, press releases, or a chart of arrests. You will rarely get an evaluation. Federal grants have paid for gang units, saturation patrols, hot-spot initiatives, and youth programs for decades, and only a small fraction of those efforts were ever tested against a comparison group to see whether they worked. Medicine abandoned that way of operating generations ago. Policing never did.

The evidence that does exist is instructive precisely because it is so uneven. Careful studies of focused deterrence, in which police, prosecutors, and community members deliver a direct message to the small number of people driving gun violence, have found meaningful reductions in several cities. Rigorous experiments on hot-spot patrols have generally found that concentrating attention on tiny geographic areas reduces crime there without simply pushing it around the corner. Those are real findings. They were produced by researchers, often against institutional indifference, and many departments that claim to use these strategies never measured whether their own versions worked.

Here is what I find hardest to forgive. Measuring impact is not expensive compared with what we already spend. A city that pours hundreds of millions of dollars a year into its police department could set aside a sliver of that to evaluate whether its major initiatives reduce harm. A few have. Most have not, because an honest evaluation might reveal that a favored unit accomplished nothing, and nobody's promotion has ever depended on finding that out.

Marcus put it to me more simply than any criminologist. If his roofer charged him for a new roof and the ceiling still leaked, he would want his money back. When he pays his property taxes, a large share goes to public safety, and nobody will tell him whether the leak was fixed. He is not asking for perfection. He is asking for a receipt, a plain accounting of whether the money bought fewer break-ins on his street. That is not too much for a taxpayer to want.

Wrongful Arrests & Community Harm

A system that rewards volume punishes the innocent along with the guilty. Between 2004 and 2012, New York police recorded roughly 4.4 million stops under the stop-and-frisk program, and in the large majority of them, close to nine in ten, officers took no further action. No arrest, no summons, nothing found. Most of the people stopped were Black or Latino. Each of those encounters was counted as activity, and each one left behind a person, often a teenager, who had been patted down on the way to school and learned exactly what the police thought of him.

In 2013, in Floyd v. City of New York, federal Judge Shira Scheindlin ruled that the city's stop-and-frisk practices violated the Fourth Amendment's protection against unreasonable searches and the Fourteenth Amendment's guarantee of equal protection. She found that the department had been on notice for years and had pressured officers to make stops while ignoring evidence that the stops were often baseless and racially skewed. She ordered a federal monitor, and the city later dropped its appeal. The ruling matters because it put into law what neighborhoods had been saying for a decade: activity is not safety, and counting stops is not policing.

The arrest-driven mentality reaches its worst outcome in the courtroom. The National Registry of Exonerations, a project run by university researchers, has documented more than 3,600 people in the United States who were convicted of crimes and later cleared since 1989. Together they lost tens of thousands of years behind bars, and states and cities have paid out billions of dollars in compensation and civil settlements. Official misconduct, mistaken eyewitness identification, false confessions, and unreliable forensic claims run through those cases again and again. Every one of them was a cleared case. Every one of them made a clearance rate look better.

I have sat in courtrooms where an exoneree walked out after twenty years, and I have watched the families of the original victims sit a few rows back. They are the forgotten people in these stories. When police arrest the wrong person, the real offender stays free, often to hurt someone else, and the victim's family is told twice that justice has been done. A wrongful conviction is not an unfortunate side effect of a tough approach. It is a failure to solve the crime, dressed up as a success.

Wrongful arrests that never reach trial do quieter damage. A person held for days on a charge that is later dropped can lose a job, an apartment, or custody of a child. The arrest record follows them into background checks long after the case has vanished. Neighborhoods that experience this at scale stop calling the police, stop answering their questions, and stop serving as witnesses. That silence then drives clearance rates down further, and the department responds with more sweeps. The cycle feeds itself, and the people paying for it are the residents who most need protection.

Why Accuracy Is Never Audited

Think about how strange this is. A bank's books are audited. A restaurant's kitchen is inspected. An airline that crashes a plane faces an independent investigation that publishes its findings. A police department that arrests the wrong man, or clears a burglary without evidence, faces nothing comparable. Nobody routinely samples closed cases to check whether the arrest was supported, whether the charge held up, or whether the person arrested was ever convicted of anything.

The reasons are institutional, not mysterious. Prosecutors depend on police for their cases and rarely want to question the quality of the work. Police unions resist outside review. Elected officials fear being seen as soft on crime if they ask hard questions about arrest quality. And the public, having been told for decades that more arrests mean more safety, rarely demands anything else. The result is a profession that measures its accuracy by its own word, in a country that would never accept that standard from a surgeon or a pilot.

An audit would not be complicated. Pull a random sample of a few hundred cleared cases each year. Check whether the arrest was supported by evidence a reasonable reviewer would accept, whether charges were filed, whether they survived to a plea or verdict, and whether the victim was ever told what happened. Publish the error rate. Do the same with crime classification, comparing the original call with the final report. Inspectors in other fields do this routinely, and the public trusts those fields more because of it. Policing has resisted for one reason: the answers would be embarrassing.

I want to be fair to the officers caught inside this system. Most of the detectives I have known went into the work to catch people who hurt others, and many of them hate the numbers game as much as their critics do. A good investigator wants the right person, not just a person. The institution is what fails them, by rewarding speed over accuracy and by never checking which is which.

What Cities and Courts Can Do Now

The fix starts with what gets counted. A city council can require its police department to publish, every quarter, the outcomes that matter to residents: the rate of victimization by neighborhood, the share of cleared cases that ended in conviction, and the number of arrests later dismissed for lack of evidence. Arrest counts can stay on the report, but they should sit below the outcomes they are supposed to produce. When the headline number is harm, commanders will chase harm.

Legislatures can ban the quotas that drive volume for its own sake. New York enacted a law barring arrest and summons quotas in 2010, and other states have followed with their own bans. A ban on paper is not enough, so it should come with an independent auditor, housed outside the department, with authority to sample closed cases, interview officers confidentially, and publish what it finds. Several cities already have inspectors general for their police departments; the useful ones issue reports that make chiefs uncomfortable.

Prosecutors can build conviction integrity units, and many have. Those units, now operating in dozens of counties, have been responsible for a large share of recent exonerations, and the best of them feed lessons back into how cases are investigated. Courts can help by insisting on recorded interrogations, by scrutinizing eyewitness procedures, and by treating forensic claims with the skepticism that the exoneration record says they deserve. Police chiefs can adopt the evaluation habit of medicine: no major program launched without a plan to test whether it worked.

Citizens have a role too, and it is simpler than it sounds. At the next public safety meeting, do what Marcus did. Ask whether fewer people are being hurt. Ask how many cleared cases ended in a conviction. Ask what program was cut last year because it failed. A department that cannot answer those three questions is spending your money on motion, not results.

None of this requires choosing between victims and civil liberties, which is the false choice that keeps the arrest machine running. Accurate policing is tougher on real offenders, because it puts investigative effort into finding them instead of padding a tally with whoever was closest. It is also fairer to everyone else. The families who lose the most to crime and the young men stopped for nothing on the sidewalk want the same thing, and it is the thing the numbers have never measured.

The takeaway

An arrest is a means, never an end, and a department that counts arrests instead of measuring safety will keep closing files while the burglaries continue and the wrong people pay for it. Demand outcome reporting, quota bans backed by an independent auditor, and conviction integrity units, and ask your city the one question Marcus asked: are fewer people getting hurt?

Chapter 12: The Neglect of Proven Technology

Most gunfire in American cities is never reported to anyone. When economists compared years of acoustic sensor data with 911 logs in Washington, D.C., and Oakland, California, they found that only about one gunfire incident in eight produced a call to police. People hear the pops, decide it was fireworks or decide it is not their business, and go back to sleep. The police, the agency responsible for the safety of that block, learn about the shooting only if someone is hit, and sometimes not even then.

That fact alone should change how we think about crime and technology. We tend to imagine that police respond to crime. In truth they respond to reports of crime, which is a much smaller and much later thing. The gap between the moment a crime happens and the moment anyone with a badge hears about it is where victims bleed out, where burglars finish the job, and where evidence disappears. Tools that close that gap already exist. Many have existed for decades. American cities buy them, mostly, after something terrible has already happened.

Renee runs a block club on the South Side of a big Midwestern city, and she has buried more young men than she can name in one breath. When I sat on her porch one summer evening, she pointed at the light pole across the street and told me exactly how many times she had called about shots fired that year and how many times she had seen a squad car afterward. She is a community leader in the oldest sense: she knows everyone, and everyone calls her first. What she wanted from her city was not more speeches. She wanted the city to know what she knew, the moment she knew it.

Sensors & Gunshot Detection

Acoustic gunshot detection is the clearest example of a promising tool caught in a political fight, and it deserves an honest account rather than a sales pitch. The best-known system, ShotSpotter, uses networks of microphones mounted on buildings and poles to pick up the sound of gunfire, triangulate its location, and send an alert to police, often within a minute. Supporters say it lets officers reach wounded people faster and find shell casings that would otherwise be lost. In Chicago, which ran one of the largest deployments in the country, police and many aldermen credited the alerts with saving lives.

Critics had evidence too. In 2021, Chicago's Office of Inspector General reviewed tens of thousands of ShotSpotter alerts over roughly a year and a half and found that only around one in eleven police responses turned up evidence of a gun-related crime. The watchdog also raised concern that officers used the density of alerts in a neighborhood to justify stopping people there. The same year, the police shooting of thirteen-year-old Adam Toledo, which began with a ShotSpotter alert, became a symbol for opponents who argued that the system sent armed officers rushing into neighborhoods on edge.

Mayor Brandon Johnson had campaigned against the contract, and after a negotiated extension, Chicago turned the system off in September 2024. The City Council had voted to rebuke the decision, and the fight split the city along lines that did not map neatly onto left and right. Some residents of the most heavily affected wards wanted the sensors gone. Others, like the people Renee organizes, wanted them kept and improved. Within months some aldermen were already pushing to fund a replacement gunshot detection system.

The research does not settle the argument for either side. Studies of response times generally find that sensors get officers to the scene of gunfire faster than 911 calls do, and some medical researchers have looked at whether that speed reaches wounded victims sooner. Broader studies have been less encouraging. A multi-county analysis published in 2021 found no measurable drop in firearm homicides or in arrests where the system was adopted. Supporters answer that a tool deployed without good follow-up will show weak results no matter how well it hears. Both of those claims can be true at once.

My own view, after years of reporting on shootings that nobody called in, is that Chicago argued about the wrong thing. The inspector general's real finding was not that sensors fail to hear gunfire. It was that the city never decided what officers should do when they arrived, never measured whether the alerts reduced deaths, and never wrote rules about how the data could be used. A tool deployed without a purpose, without evaluation, and without limits will disappoint everyone. The answer to that is to fix the deployment, not to go back to waiting for a phone call that never comes.

Cameras That Identify Weapons & Break-Ins

Of all crime technology, cameras are the most familiar and the most poorly used. After the Boston Marathon bombing in 2013, investigators assembled footage from businesses, phones, and city cameras, and within about three days the FBI released images of the two suspects. That success showed what camera networks can do after the fact. It also showed how dependent investigators were on private footage nobody had planned to share. Most American cities still have no idea where the cameras on their own streets are or who controls them.

Detroit tried to change that. In 2016 the city launched Project Green Light, which began with a handful of gas stations agreeing to install high-definition cameras that streamed to police, marked by a flashing green light outside. The program grew to hundreds of businesses. City officials pointed to drops in violent crime at participating locations, and outside researchers found more mixed effects, with stronger results for some crimes than others. Critics worried about facial recognition being run on the footage. Whatever one concludes, Detroit did something most cities never do: it built the network on purpose, before it needed it, and invited the public to judge the results.

The newer generation of systems claims to do more than record. Software can now flag a visible firearm in a camera feed, alert when a window breaks or a door is forced after hours, or notice a person climbing a fence at a closed car lot. Some school districts have installed visual gun-detection services that send an image to a human reviewer before anyone is alerted. Retailers and warehouses use break-in sensors linked to monitoring centers. When these systems work, they turn a recording device into an early-warning device, and that is a profound difference.

When is the word that matters. The industry is full of extravagant claims, and buyers should be skeptical. In 2024 the Federal Trade Commission took action against Evolv Technology, a maker of so-called AI weapons scanners sold to schools and stadiums, alleging that its marketing overstated what the devices could detect. The company settled without admitting wrongdoing. That same year, a pilot of Evolv scanners in New York City subway stations ran nearly three thousand scans, produced more than a hundred false alarms, and found no guns. Proven technology means tested technology, and much of what is sold has never been tested independently at all.

So the lesson is not to buy everything. It is to buy what has been evaluated, to require vendors to share performance data, and to walk away from products that cannot prove their claims. Break-in sensors, monitored alarms, and well-placed cameras with clear retention rules have long track records. Visual weapon detection is newer and less certain, and it should be piloted with independent measurement before any city spends serious money on it. Cities fail in both directions, ignoring tools that work and overpaying for tools that do not, and the cause is the same in both cases: nobody is measuring.

License-Plate Readers & Real-Time Crime Centers

Automated license-plate readers are cameras that photograph every passing plate and check it against lists of stolen vehicles and wanted cars. Mounted on poles or patrol cars, they can tell an officer within seconds that the sedan ahead was carjacked two hours ago. Departments across the country credit them with recovering stolen cars, finding vehicles tied to shootings, and locating missing people. Private companies now sell networks of these cameras to neighborhoods, homeowners associations, and small towns, and the number in use has exploded over the past decade.

Renee's block has two of them now, paid for by a neighborhood fund rather than the city. She told me the first month they were up, a stolen car used in a drive-by two neighborhoods over was flagged passing the corner, and police picked it up within the hour. I cannot verify every story a resident tells me, and I would not ask anyone to believe a single anecdote. What struck me was her reasoning. For years she had been told that nothing could be done about cars that came through, fired, and vanished. Now something could, and the people who live there had chosen it themselves.

Real-time crime centers pull these streams together. Picture a room where analysts watch camera feeds, plate-reader hits, gunshot alerts, and 911 calls on a single wall of screens, and can direct responding officers with information they would never otherwise have. New Orleans, Chicago, Atlanta, and many smaller cities have built versions of these centers. At their best, they let an officer arrive knowing which way the car fled and what the suspect was wearing, instead of knocking on doors for an hour afterward.

The civil liberties concerns are serious and deserve a straight answer, not a shrug. Plate readers create a record of where millions of law-abiding people drive. Civil liberties groups have documented databases retained for years, shared across jurisdictions with few controls, and searched by officers for personal reasons. A tool that can find a stolen car can also track an ex-partner or a protester. I do not think that danger means we should refuse the tool. I think it means we should write the rules first: short retention periods for plates that match nothing, audit logs of every search, criminal penalties for misuse, and public reports of how the system is used.

Why Technology Is Only Used After Crisis

There is a predictable rhythm to how American governments adopt safety technology. Nothing happens for years. Then a massacre, a viral crime, or a child's death fills the news, and money appears overnight. After the 2018 shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland, Florida, the legislature passed a sweeping school safety law within weeks, funding security measures and threat assessment that advocates had sought for years. After mass shootings elsewhere, districts that had refused requests for door locks and cameras suddenly found grant money.

Crisis buying produces bad purchases. Officials under pressure grab whatever a vendor puts in front of them, sign contracts without performance standards, and skip the evaluation that would tell them whether anything changed. Two years later, when attention has moved on, the system is underused, the maintenance contract lapses, and critics point to the waste as proof that technology does not work. The real failure was the timing. Decisions made in grief are rarely the ones a careful city would have made in calm.

Budgets drive the rest. A new system has an upfront cost that shows up in a single year, while the savings, fewer shootings and fewer burglaries and fewer hours of detective work, are spread over many years and across departments that do not share credit. Elected officials who serve two- or four-year terms have little reason to spend now for a benefit that may arrive after they leave. Meanwhile, the people who pay for that short-term thinking are on Renee's block, calling about gunfire that no one hears.

Renee told me something I have repeated to city officials ever since. She said the city always has money for a funeral detail and never has money for a warning. She did not mean it as a slogan. She meant that she had watched the city spend lavishly on response, on overtime and press conferences and memorial events, after each death, while the requests that might have prevented the next one sat in a committee. Nobody in that system was malicious. They were simply rewarded for reacting and never for preventing.

There is a deeper cultural habit at work too. Americans tend to treat technology in policing as either magic or menace. Vendors promise that their system will end gun violence, and activists warn that every camera is a step toward a police state. Neither picture helps a city make a decision. A gunshot sensor is a smoke detector for violence, no more and no less. Nobody argues that smoke detectors prevent every fire or that they are a plot against homeowners. We install them, test them, replace the batteries, and set rules about where they go. Crime technology deserves the same unglamorous treatment.

Prevention Through Early Adoption

The alternative is to adopt technology deliberately, before the crisis, and to treat it as an experiment with rules. A city council can require that any surveillance or detection technology pass through a public approval process that spells out its purpose, its limits, and how its success will be measured. Several cities, including Oakland and Seattle, have passed surveillance oversight ordinances along these lines. They slow purchases down a little, and they make the purchases that survive far more defensible.

Police chiefs can pilot new tools in a portion of the city and compare results with similar areas that did not get them. That is how medicine tests drugs, and it is how a city can learn whether gunshot sensors reduce deaths or whether weapon-detection cameras catch guns. Publish the results either way. A chief who announces that a pilot failed and the money is going elsewhere earns more public trust than one who quietly renews a contract nobody can justify.

Legislatures can set statewide guardrails so each town does not reinvent them. Rules on how long plate-reader data may be kept, who may search it, and what happens to officers who misuse it belong in state law. Many states have passed some version of these limits, and the ones that combine short retention with real penalties give departments clear permission to use the tool while protecting everyone else. Clarity helps officers too. Nobody wants to build a case on evidence a court might later throw out.

Homeowners and business owners can do their part without waiting for city hall. Registering private cameras with a local voluntary program, which many police departments now offer, lets investigators know where footage might exist without giving anyone live access. Monitored alarms and simple break-in sensors are cheap compared with the cost of a burglary. Block clubs like Renee's can push for a seat on the oversight committees that approve new systems, so that the residents who live with the technology help decide how it is used.

The takeaway

Tools that detect crime as it happens exist now, and the failure is not in the machines but in a government that buys them in panic and never measures them. Insist that your city adopt detection technology before the next crisis, under public rules, with honest evaluation, and with a voice for the people who live on the blocks it is meant to protect.

Chapter 13: Decoys: The Tool America Refuses to Use

If police know which streets lose cars every night, why don't they simply leave one out and wait for the thief? Most people have wondered something like it while standing on a curb staring at an empty parking space, or at a porch where a package used to be. The question sounds naive. It is not. It is the right question, and the honest answer is that some police do exactly that, it works better than almost anything else in their toolbox, and most American departments barely bother.

Linda asked me that question at a neighborhood meeting after her second car in three years was stolen from in front of her house. She is a retired school secretary and a careful taxpayer who reads her property tax bill line by line. She could tell me what her insurance premium had done after each theft, what the rental car cost, and how many hours she spent at the impound lot when the first car turned up stripped. What she could not understand was why the city kept spending money on patrols that never caught anyone, when the thieves were working the same three blocks every week.

The short answer is this. Decoy operations, which include bait cars, bait packages, and officers posing as easy targets, are cheap, precise, and legally sound when they are run correctly. They catch the people already committing a particular crime, rather than sweeping up everyone nearby. Where they have been used at scale and publicized, they have been followed by dramatic drops in the targeted crime. American reluctance to use them has less to do with evidence than with history, habit, and a few well-deserved scandals that the profession never learned from.

International Best Practices

The most famous example sits just across the northern border. In the early 2000s, the Vancouver area had some of the worst auto theft in North America, and police in Surrey were dealing with a volume of stolen cars that had earned the city a grim reputation. Beginning in 2002, police in British Columbia rolled out a bait car program, later run through a provincial auto crime team known as IMPACT and backed by the province's public auto insurer. The cars were wired with cameras, microphones, GPS, and a remote kill switch, and they were parked where thefts clustered.

As reported by the province and the insurer, the results were striking. Auto theft in British Columbia fell by roughly two-thirds or more over the following decade, from well over twenty thousand vehicles a year to well under ten thousand. Thefts from vehicles dropped sharply too. Fairness requires a caveat that the program's own boosters do not always mention: most of those figures come from police and the insurer, and the same period saw engine immobilizers become standard equipment in new Canadian vehicles. Bait cars were not the only reason theft fell. They were a big part of a deliberate, sustained strategy, and the province kept funding them because it believed they paid for themselves many times over.

Britain moved at the same time. Police in Essex launched their own bait car program in 2002, drawing on a model pioneered by the Minneapolis Police Department, which had been running a bait vehicle program since the late 1990s. Australian police in Perth adopted bait cars more recently. What these programs share is not just the technology. It is a decision to treat a high-volume property crime as something that can be targeted surgically, with evidence so complete, video and audio of the theft itself, that guilty pleas follow and court time shrinks.

British Columbia also kept adapting. When thieves shifted from taking whole cars to smashing windows for laptops and bags, the program expanded around 2013 to cover theft from vehicles, using bait items left in plain view. That flexibility is the real best practice. A decoy program is not a single gadget. It is a habit of studying what offenders are doing this season, building a lure that matches it, and moving on when the pattern changes. Departments that treat decoys as a permanent unit with a research mindset get results that one-off holiday stings never match.

Bait Vehicles / Bait Packages / & Decoy Officers

A bait car is an ordinary-looking vehicle parked in an ordinary way, often unlocked, sometimes with the keys inside. When someone drives off, a signal alerts police, who track it in real time. At a safe moment the engine can be shut down remotely and the doors locked, leaving the thief sitting in a stopped car while officers walk up. The entire event is on camera. There is no high-speed pursuit, no dispute about identity, and no question of who was behind the wheel.

Bait packages apply the same logic to the front porch. In December 2018, police in Jersey City, New Jersey, partnered with Amazon to place doorbell cameras and dummy boxes fitted with GPS trackers at homes in areas the department's own crime data had flagged. Police told reporters that one box was taken within minutes of being placed and the suspect was caught. Other departments across the country have run similar holiday stings, and the deterrent value spreads well beyond the boxes themselves. A thief who knows that any package might be watching has to think twice about all of them.

Decoy officers are the oldest version and the most demanding. Officers pose as the people predators target most: an elderly shopper with a purse, a drunk passenger on a late train, a lone worker counting cash at closing. Partners wait nearby. When these operations are run well, they catch robbers and purse snatchers in the act, before the next real victim gets hurt. When they are run badly, they become something else entirely, and that difference sits at the center of every American argument about decoys.

Why Decoys Save Lives

Car theft sounds like a property crime, and on paper it is. In the street it is often a violent one. Stolen cars are used in shootings, robberies, and drive-bys because they cannot be traced to the shooter. They are driven recklessly by teenagers who crash them. And when police spot a stolen car, the traditional response is a pursuit, which is one of the most dangerous things officers do. Federal crash data show that police chases kill hundreds of people in the United States every year, many of them bystanders or passengers who had nothing to do with the theft.

A bait car ends the chase before it starts. Officers do not need to pursue a vehicle they can stop with a keystroke. The thief is caught without anyone running a red light at ninety miles an hour, and the evidence is airtight, which means fewer trials, fewer witness summonses, and fewer days of victims missing work to testify. Every one of those details translates into lives and money that the conventional approach spends without thinking.

Many American departments have quietly recognized the danger of chases and tightened their pursuit policies, limiting officers to chasing suspects in violent felonies. That is a sensible reform, but it leaves a gap. Car thieves figured out quickly that in many cities nobody would follow them. A bait program fills that gap without reopening the danger. The department does not have to choose between letting a stolen car go and risking a fatal crash on a residential street. It can let the thief drive into a trap that closes with nobody hurt.

Decoy officers protect a different kind of victim. The people who rob the elderly on buses or target women walking alone at night tend to repeat the same crime in the same places. A well-run decoy operation removes a repeat predator from a particular corridor before he picks the next victim. I have interviewed survivors of those robberies, people who stopped riding the train or stopped going to evening services because of one attack, and every one of them would have traded anything for a cop in a gray wig to have been sitting in their seat that night.

US Reluctance & Cultural Barriers

The law is not the obstacle, though it is the excuse people reach for. American courts have recognized the defense of entrapment since Sorrells v. United States in 1932, a Prohibition case in which a government agent posing as a fellow war veteran badgered a man repeatedly until he finally agreed to sell liquor. The Supreme Court held that the government may not implant the idea of a crime in an innocent person's mind and then prosecute him for it. In Sherman v. United States in 1958, the Court threw out a conviction where an informant who met a man while both were being treated for addiction pressured him, request after request, into supplying narcotics.

Jacobson v. United States, decided in 1992, drew the modern line. Federal agents spent more than two years sending a Nebraska farmer mailings from fictitious organizations, steadily encouraging him to order illegal material, until he finally did. The Court, in a five-to-four decision, reversed his conviction and held that prosecutors must prove a defendant was predisposed to commit the crime before the government first approached him. That ruling matters for decoys because it draws the boundary clearly. Government may offer an ordinary opportunity to someone already inclined to take it. It may not manufacture a criminal through persistence.

A parked car with a door unlocked is an opportunity, not a campaign of persuasion, and courts in many jurisdictions have treated bait cars that way. The real barriers are cultural. Some American decoy operations crossed the line from opportunity into temptation, and the damage lingered. In New York, a subway program known as Operation Lucky Bag, begun in 2006, left purses and wallets in stations and arrested people who walked off with them. Reports later found that many of those arrested had no criminal record, prosecutors began dismissing cases, and a judge in one case wrote, in effect, that the city had enough real crime without inventing temptation.

Lucky Bag is the cautionary tale American skeptics cite, and they are right to cite it. It tested whether ordinary people would keep found property, not whether active thieves would steal. That is the wrong target. Add the history of plainclothes units accused of aggressive tactics in Black and Latino neighborhoods, and it becomes clear why some community leaders hear the word decoy and think of a trap aimed at them. The fix is not to abandon the tool. It is to aim it precisely at the crimes residents report and keep it away from fishing expeditions.

Television did not help. For several years a cable reality show built around bait cars turned arrests into entertainment, with thieves mocked for laughs as the doors locked around them. Some viewers loved it. Others saw it as proof that decoys were a stunt. Neither reaction had much to do with the serious work of reducing theft. A tool that should be judged by crime trends and court outcomes ended up being judged as spectacle, and police leaders who wanted to be taken seriously kept their distance from it.

Missed Opportunities

Look at the property crimes that have exploded in recent years and you will see how much is being left on the table. Catalytic converter thefts surged across the country for several years, driven by the precious metals inside. Organized retail theft crews hit the same stores repeatedly. Package theft became a national irritation during the boom in home delivery. Each of those crimes is repetitive, location-specific, and committed by a relatively small number of active offenders, which is exactly the profile where decoys work best.

Fraud against older people is another missed opportunity. Telephone scammers who convince a grandparent that a grandson is in jail often send a courier to collect cash at the door. In a number of reported cases, families or banks recognized the scam in time and called police, who waited at the house and arrested the courier when he arrived. Those arrests have sometimes led investigators up the chain to the people running the calls. A planned version of that response, with officers ready to take the place of the intended victim, turns the scammers' own logistics against them.

Linda's neighborhood is a case in point. The thefts on her street followed a pattern any resident could describe: the same kinds of cars, the same overnight hours, the same handful of blocks near a highway ramp. Patrols drove by now and then and saw nothing. A single bait car, placed for a few weeks, would very likely have caught the crew, and a few well-publicized arrests on video would have warned the next one. Instead, the department told residents to lock their doors and buy steering wheel clubs, and the insurers raised everyone's premiums.

The cost comparison is not close. A wired vehicle and its monitoring equipment cost a fraction of an officer's annual salary, and the same car can be used for years. Compare that with the insurance claims, rental cars, lost workdays, crash damage, and pursuit injuries that flow from a single active theft ring. Taxpayers like Linda already pay for car theft. They pay through premiums, through police overtime, through court costs, and through the emergency rooms that treat people hurt in chases. Decoys simply move that money to the front of the problem.

Decoys as Crime Prevention

The biggest benefit of a decoy program is the uncertainty it creates. British Columbia did not just park bait cars; it advertised them, on billboards and signs and in news coverage of thieves caught on camera. The message to anyone considering a theft was simple: the next car might be ours. Deterrence works best when the chance of being caught feels high and immediate, and a well-publicized decoy program creates exactly that feeling, often more cheaply than any amount of visible patrol.

City councils can fund a pilot bait car program aimed at the three or four neighborhoods with the highest theft rates, require monthly public reporting on arrests and theft trends, and partner with insurers, who have every reason to help pay for it. Police chiefs can write clear rules that keep decoys targeted at reported crime patterns, never at random passersby, and that bar the found-property traps that discredited Lucky Bag. Prosecutors can commit to fast-track handling of video-documented cases so that the deterrent message arrives quickly.

Legislatures can help by clarifying in statute that offering an ordinary opportunity is not entrapment, consistent with Jacobson, and by funding statewide auto theft teams modeled on British Columbia's. Retailers and delivery companies can provide bait merchandise and packages, as Amazon did in Jersey City. Residents can ask their local precinct the simple question Linda asked: you know where it happens, so why not wait there with a decoy? Pressure from people who pay for crime has started more good programs than any consultant's report.

The takeaway

Decoys catch the people already committing a crime, end dangerous chases before they start, and deter thieves through uncertainty, and the law already allows them when they target opportunity rather than persuasion. Ask your council for a targeted, publicly reported bait program aimed at your city's worst theft hotspots, with written rules that keep it focused on active offenders and away from ordinary people, and ask your insurer to help fund it.

Chapter 14: Crime's Ripple Effect: The Unseen Victims

On a Saturday in April, Angela's kitchen smelled like fried plantains and the radio was too loud. Her younger brother had stopped by to borrow her ladder, and he was standing in the doorway teasing her eight-year-old son about a missing tooth. Her daughter was doing homework at the table and pretending not to listen. Angela remembers being annoyed that he tracked mud across the floor. It was the most ordinary hour of her life, and she did not know it was the last one of its kind.

Eleven months later, I sat in that same kitchen. The radio was off. The ladder was still in the hallway because nobody could bring themselves to put it away. Her brother had been shot outside a corner store three blocks from the house in the summer, and his case was still open. Her son had started wetting the bed again. Her daughter, who used to be first in her class, had missed nineteen days of school. Angela had lost her job at a medical billing office after running out of leave. None of them had been anywhere near the gunfire. In the official count, there was one victim.

Secondary Trauma

The official count is the lie at the heart of how America measures crime. A homicide statistic records a single death. It does not record a sister who cannot sleep, a mother who stops eating, a father who drinks, or a cousin who decides that the only answer is to go find the shooter. In Angela's first kitchen, the family was a working unit, with a routine, a budget, and a future. In the second kitchen, every person in it was injured, and not one of them would ever appear in a police report.

Psychologists who study traumatic grief, the particular grief that follows a violent death, describe symptoms that look much like those of the people who were physically present: intrusive images, panic, avoidance of the places where it happened, a constant sense of threat. Families of homicide victims report rates of post-traumatic stress far above the general population, and many say the investigation and trial made it worse. Angela told me that the hardest days were not the anniversaries. They were the days a detective did not call back.

The circle of secondary victims is wider than family. Patrol officers who arrive first at a shooting, paramedics who work on a dying teenager, emergency nurses who tell a mother the news, and jurors who sit through weeks of autopsy photographs all carry some of it home. Researchers who study these professions find elevated rates of burnout, depression, and substance abuse. Bystanders who watched the killing from a bus stop are almost never offered help at all. A single act of violence can quietly injure dozens of people, and our systems were designed to notice one.

Of all the cases I know, the most painful example of secondary trauma reaching its endpoint is one the entire country watched. On December 14, 2012, a gunman killed twenty children and six educators at Sandy Hook Elementary School in Newtown, Connecticut. Seven years later, in March 2019, Jeremy Richman, whose six-year-old daughter Avielle was among the children killed, died by suicide. He had spent those years running a foundation devoted to the brain science behind violence. In the same month, two young survivors of the 2018 Parkland school shooting in Florida also died by suicide. Grief this heavy does not stay with the person who was shot.

Community Collapse

What happens to one family happens, more slowly, to a neighborhood. After a killing, people change their routes. Parents stop letting children walk to the park. The older woman who sat on her porch every evening moves inside. Customers avoid the corner store where it happened, and within a year the store closes. A block that once watched itself now looks away, and that loss of eyes on the street makes the next crime more likely. Violence does not just harm a community. It dismantles the habits that kept the community safe.

Newtown faced a version of this in its most visible form. The original Sandy Hook school building, where so many families had walked their children to class, could not be used again. The town tore it down and built a new school on the same site, which opened in 2016, designed with security in mind and with care to make it feel nothing like the old one. A community can rebuild a building with a budget and a vote. Rebuilding the ordinary trust that let parents drop children at a schoolhouse door without a second thought takes far longer.

I have watched this happen in neighborhoods of every kind, from public housing towers to tidy suburban cul-de-sacs, and the sequence is remarkably similar. Those with means leave first. Those who stay withdraw. Churches lose members, block clubs lose volunteers, and the informal network of people who knew which teenager was in trouble and whose grandmother needed a ride frays thread by thread. Sociologists have a name for that network, collective efficacy, and decades of research tie its strength to lower violence. Crime eats it.

Fear finishes the job. Witnesses who saw what happened outside the corner store where Angela's brother died have reasons to stay silent, and those reasons are not cowardice. They live on the same block as the people who might retaliate, and they have watched police come and go without protecting anyone who talked. Each unsolved killing teaches the neighborhood that cooperation carries risk and little reward. That lesson spreads faster than any public service announcement, and it leaves the next family even less likely to see an arrest.

Sandy Hook shows that collapse can come from outside the neighborhood as well. For years after the shooting, conspiracy broadcaster Alex Jones told his audience that the massacre was staged and that grieving parents were actors. Some of his followers harassed the families in person, online, and by phone; several families moved, more than once, to escape. In 2022, juries in Texas and Connecticut found Jones liable for defamation, and the resulting judgments exceeded a billion dollars, though appeals and bankruptcy proceedings followed. Those verdicts mattered because they recognized, in law, that the harm of a violent crime keeps spreading long after the gunfire stops.

Economic Devastation

Crime is also a bill, and the people who pay it are rarely the people who committed it. Angela's household lost her income, then her brother's occasional help with rent, then the money she spent on funeral costs and the gas to drive to court dates. Victim compensation programs, which exist in every state, can cover some funeral expenses and counseling, but many families never hear about them, and the applications often require a police report and paperwork that grieving people cannot face. Some programs have historically denied claims if the victim had a criminal record, punishing the family for the dead person's past.

At the scale of a neighborhood, the losses multiply. Economists who have studied gun violence find that spikes in shootings are followed by lower home values, fewer new businesses, and fewer jobs in the surrounding area. Insurance premiums rise. Commercial lenders hesitate. A homeowner who has paid on a house for twenty years watches its value fall because of crimes she had nothing to do with, and her one real asset shrinks just when she might need to sell and move somewhere safer.

Small business owners feel it first. I once interviewed the owner of a neighborhood diner, a man who had kept his doors open through two recessions, who closed within a year of a shooting in his parking lot. Nobody was hurt inside. His regulars simply stopped coming after dark, and his evening shift, which paid his rent, disappeared. The waitresses and cooks he employed lost their jobs. The city lost his sales taxes. The block lost one more lit window at night. That chain of losses began with a crime that, on paper, had a single victim and no property damage.

Then there are the costs everyone shares. Emergency rooms absorb gunshot victims, many of them uninsured, and the expense flows back through public hospital budgets and private premiums. Police, prosecutors, courts, and prisons run on tax dollars. Lost productivity, from people who are killed, injured, or too traumatized to work, never appears on any government ledger. Credible national estimates put the yearly cost of gun violence alone in the many billions of dollars. The taxpayer in a quiet suburb pays a share of every one of those bills, whether or not anyone on her street has ever heard a shot.

Children as Hidden Victims

Young people absorb violence in ways that adults routinely miss. A federal survey of children's exposure to violence, first conducted for the Justice Department in 2008, found that roughly three in five American children had been exposed to some form of violence, crime, or abuse in the previous year, as victims or witnesses. The researchers cautioned that the headline figure lumps together everything from schoolyard fights to serious assaults. Even the narrower categories were sobering, and a follow-up survey a few years later found similar levels.

The long-term consequences are well documented. The Adverse Childhood Experiences study, a large research collaboration between the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and Kaiser Permanente published in 1998, found that children who experience abuse, household dysfunction, and violence face higher risks of depression, addiction, chronic disease, and early death as adults. Exposure to community violence belongs on the same list. A child who sees a shooting, or loses a parent or uncle to one, carries that injury into the classroom the next morning.

Angela's son is a case study in how schools miss it. His teacher saw a boy who had stopped paying attention and started picking fights, and she sent home behavior notes. Nobody at the school knew his uncle had been killed, because nobody had told them, and the boy did not have words for it. Her daughter's missed days were logged as truancy. Two children carrying a homicide in their backpacks were treated as discipline problems, which is how the cycle that produces the next generation of violence quietly begins.

There is another group of hidden child victims that few people think about: the children of people arrested and imprisoned. Advocacy research has estimated that millions of American children have had a parent incarcerated at some point in their lives. Those children often lose income, housing stability, and a parent's daily presence, and they carry stigma at school. Whatever one thinks about the sentence a parent earned, the child did nothing. A justice system serious about preventing the next crime would track those children's well-being as carefully as it tracks the parent's release date.

Why Society Ignores the Ripple

We ignore the ripple because our institutions are built to see one victim at a time. Police investigate a crime against a person. Prosecutors charge an offense against the state. Statistics count incidents. None of those systems has a category for the eight-year-old who stopped sleeping, so as far as the official record is concerned, he does not exist. What is not counted is not funded, and what is not funded is not treated.

There is also a reason that is harder to admit. Seeing the ripple would require the public to recognize that violence in one neighborhood is a cost to every neighborhood, and that the families of victims are often the same families that later produce offenders. That truth complicates the comfortable division between good people and criminals. It is easier to treat each killing as an isolated event than to accept that every unaddressed injury plants a seed. I say this with real sympathy for those who prefer the simpler story. I preferred it too, before I spent years sitting in kitchens like Angela's.

The media plays its part. A shooting is news on the night it happens, and perhaps again at the trial. The months in between, when the family is falling apart and the children are failing school, are not considered a story. Survivors like Angela learn quickly that public attention is a flash, and that when it passes, they are on their own with injuries nobody can see.

Some of the indifference is simple fatigue. Americans have absorbed so many reports of shootings that each new one barely registers beyond the zip code where it happened. The families on the receiving end notice that numbness. Angela told me she stopped reading the news because every story about another killing reminded her that her brother had once been one of those brief items, read and forgotten by people a few miles away. Her family's year of ruin was, to most of the city, a paragraph.

Repairing the Damage Beyond the Victim

The good news is that the ripple can be interrupted, and several places have shown how. Handle With Care, a program that began in West Virginia in the early 2010s and has spread to many states, asks police who encounter a child at a violent scene to send the child's school a simple confidential notice by the next morning, with no details, just a request to handle that child with care. Teachers who receive the notice can adjust expectations and connect the child with a counselor. It costs almost nothing, and it would have changed Angela's son's year.

Hospital-based violence intervention programs, now operating in dozens of trauma centers, place trained workers at the bedside of gunshot victims and extend support to their families, connecting them with counseling, housing help, and legal advocacy at the moment they are most open to it. Legislatures can broaden victim compensation to cover siblings, children, and grandparents, extend filing deadlines, end the denials based on a victim's past record, and require police to hand every family a plain written notice of their rights and benefits. Several states have already loosened those eligibility rules.

Courts have a role beyond the criminal case. The Sandy Hook defamation verdicts showed that civil law can hold accountable those who prey on the grief of survivors, and judges can move such cases quickly rather than letting them drag on for years. Schools can train every teacher in the basics of trauma-informed practice, so that a child who suddenly falls apart is met with a question before a punishment. Employers can offer bereavement and court-attendance leave that matches the real length of a homicide case, which is measured in years, not days.

City councils can treat neighborhood recovery as part of public safety, funding grief counseling in schools after a homicide, keeping lights on and businesses open near crime scenes, and supporting the block clubs that rebuild collective efficacy. Courts can schedule hearings with the family's work and school lives in mind. And citizens can do the simplest thing of all: when a neighbor loses someone to violence, keep showing up after the funeral. The casseroles stop within a week. Grief does not.

The takeaway

For every person a crime injures directly, a circle of family, neighbors, and children carries damage that no statistic records and no budget treats. Push your schools to adopt Handle With Care, your state to widen victim compensation to the whole family, your employer to offer leave that matches a homicide case's real length, and your neighbors to stay present long after the cameras leave.

Chapter 15: Why Americans Ignore Crime Until It Hits Home

"That's across the highway," Greg said, and flipped a burger. We were standing in his backyard on a warm evening, and a neighbor had just mentioned the string of break-ins in a subdivision a mile or so to the east. Greg is a homeowner in the fullest sense: he coaches Little League, mows in diagonal stripes, and knows the assessed value of his house to the dollar. He said it kindly, without contempt. Those things happen over there. Over here, people leave their garage doors open until bedtime. Everyone at the grill nodded, and the conversation moved on to the baseball standings.

I have heard some version of Greg's sentence in nearly every city I have reported from. It is said in bars and church parking lots, at school pickup and at funerals for other people's children. It is the most natural thing in the world, and it is one of the most expensive. Three months after that barbecue, someone came through Greg's back gate at two in the morning. Nobody was hurt. His tools, his daughter's bicycle, and his wife's car were gone, and for months afterward he slept with a baseball bat beside the bed. When he called me, the first thing he said was that he never thought it would be his street.

Crime as Background Noise

Most Americans experience crime the way they experience weather in another state. It is a scroll of headlines, a local news segment between traffic and sports, a stream of alerts on a neighborhood app that nobody reads closely. The sheer volume turns it into noise. A shooting across town competes with a recipe video and a celebrity divorce, and it usually loses. We are not heartless. We are saturated, and saturation dulls attention the way a constant hum fades out of hearing.

Background noise also distorts. People tend to overestimate the kinds of crime that make dramatic television, stranger abductions and random killings, and underestimate the ordinary crimes that are far more likely to touch them: burglary, fraud, car theft, and violence between people who know each other. National surveys have found for decades that Americans often believe crime is rising even when it is falling, and that they rate crime in their own neighborhoods as far less serious than crime in the country as a whole. Both errors share a root. Crime is real, but it is always somewhere else.

That belief carries a political price. Officials respond to what voters notice, and voters notice what is loud and nearby. Quiet, steady harm, the burglary ring working one zip code, the scammer calling a thousand retirees, the domestic abuser whose neighbors hear the shouting and turn up the television, never produces the pressure that would fund a real response. Prevention requires attention before the damage, and background noise guarantees that attention arrives after.

There is a psychology to it as well. People are wired to believe that bad things are more likely to happen to others than to themselves, a bias researchers have measured in everything from car accidents to illness. It helps us get out of bed in the morning. It also persuades a homeowner that the lock on the back gate can wait until next month, and that the rumor about a neighbor's bruises is none of his business. Optimism is a virtue in most of life. Applied to crime, it is a bet against the odds that most people lose eventually.

The Illusion of Distance

The case that has shaken my own assumptions most in recent years is still unfolding as I write in the fall of 2026. On the evening of January 31, 2026, Nancy Guthrie, the 84-year-old mother of NBC Today anchor Savannah Guthrie, was dropped off at her home in the Tucson area after dinner with family. The next day she failed to join a friend to watch a church service, and she was reported missing. She relied on daily medication. Her home sat in a quiet, comfortable part of the desert foothills, the kind of neighborhood where people like Greg assume nothing of the kind could ever happen.

Led by the Pima County Sheriff's Department with the FBI, the investigation became one of the most closely watched missing-person cases in the country. Investigators released doorbell camera images of a masked, armed figure outside the home on the morning she vanished. Messages claiming to be ransom notes, demanding payment in bitcoin, were sent to news outlets, and authorities have never publicly confirmed whether they are genuine. A combined reward of more than a million dollars was offered, and tens of thousands of tips poured in. Savannah Guthrie made repeated public pleas for her mother's return and for anyone with information to come forward.

As of the most recent reporting I can verify, no suspect has been publicly identified, no motive has been announced, and the case remains open. I will not speculate about who is responsible. What matters here is what the case reveals. A woman with one of the most recognizable family names in American television, living in a safe neighborhood, with every investigative resource the federal government could bring, still disappeared from her own home. If distance protected anyone, it would have protected her. Crime does not check the address, the income, or the fame of the people it reaches.

The case also exposed how vulnerable older Americans living alone can be. An elderly person who goes missing, whether by abduction, a medical crisis, or confusion, faces danger that rises by the hour, especially without medication. Families often do not realize a parent is missing until a scheduled call or visit is missed. Many states run Silver Alert systems for missing seniors, but the protocols vary widely, and the first hours, which are the most important, are often lost to uncertainty about whether anything is wrong at all.

Greg's mother lives alone two states away, and when we talked about the Guthrie case, he went quiet for a moment. He admitted that he calls her on Sundays, and that if she missed a Wednesday he would have no way of knowing. Nothing about her life is unusual. She drives to church, plays cards with friends, and keeps her pills in a weekly organizer on the kitchen counter. Millions of American families share that arrangement, and almost none of them have a plan for the day the phone rings unanswered. Distance, for most of us, is not only geographic. It is the gap we leave between check-ins.

Personal Impact as Awakening

Nearly every advocate I have met in this work came to it the same way. They did not read a report and decide to act. Something happened to them, or to someone they loved. That pattern says something admirable about human beings, who can turn grief into purpose, and something damning about a culture that waits for grief before it pays attention.

Savannah Guthrie had spent much of her career on the other side of these stories, interviewing families of the missing and the murdered and carrying their appeals to a national audience. In 2026 she became the person making the appeal. Her public pleas, delivered with visible anguish, reached millions of viewers in a way few families of the missing ever could. That reach is a privilege most families will never have, and she seemed aware of it. Her experience shows that even people who know crime professionally do not truly know it until it comes through their own door.

Gabby Petito's family became one of the best-known examples. In the summer of 2021, Petito and her fiance, Brian Laundrie, were traveling the West in a camper van. On August 12, police in Moab, Utah, stopped the couple after a caller reported seeing a man strike a woman. Officers treated the episode as an emotional crisis, separated the two for the night, and filed no charges. Weeks later her family reported her missing. Her remains were found in Wyoming that September, and the coroner ruled her death a homicide. Laundrie was later found dead in Florida, and the FBI said he had claimed responsibility in a notebook found nearby.

An independent review commissioned by the city of Moab concluded that the officers had made several unintentional mistakes and that Petito was likely a long-term victim of domestic abuse, and it recommended further training. Her parents sued the city; a judge later dismissed the wrongful-death suit, citing Utah's governmental immunity law. They also did something more lasting. They started a foundation and lobbied the Utah legislature, which in 2023 passed a bill requiring officers to conduct a lethality assessment, a short set of risk questions, on domestic violence calls. Their daughter's death made them experts in a danger they had never thought about.

The Petito case also forced a hard conversation about whose disappearances get noticed. While the nation followed every detail of her case, a state task force report in Wyoming had found earlier that year that hundreds of Indigenous people had been reported missing there over the previous decade, with a small fraction of the press attention. Many families of missing people of color pointed out, with good reason, that their own cases had produced no national search. Attention is itself a form of distance: we see the victims who look like the people we imagine as neighbors, and we look past the rest.

Cultural Detachment

Americans consume more crime stories than ever, and that is part of the problem. True crime podcasts, documentaries, and streaming series have turned real tragedies into entertainment, and the audience can be enormous. The Casey Anthony case showed both sides of this years ago. Her two-year-old daughter, Caylee, was not reported missing until about a month after she was last seen, and when the report finally came it was Casey's own mother who made the call. The 2011 trial was a national spectacle. Anthony was acquitted of murder and convicted only of misdemeanor counts of giving false information to police, and many viewers treated the verdict like the finale of a television season.

The Guthrie case showed the darker edge of the same appetite. Within days of her disappearance, impostors tried to profit from the family's fear. A Southern California man later pleaded guilty in federal court to sending the family fake ransom messages about a bitcoin payment. Livestreamers drawn by the attention showed up in the neighborhood, and deputies arrested some of them during the months that followed. A family desperate for real information had to sort it from hoaxes, and investigators had to spend time chasing noise that a spectator culture produced.

Detachment is what allows millions of people to follow a case like that one minute by minute and still shrug at the break-ins across the highway. A story about someone else, told well, feels like engagement. It is not. Engagement means knowing your neighbors, reporting what you see, showing up at a public safety meeting, and asking why the police took forty minutes to answer a call on the next street. Watching is the opposite of acting, even when it feels like caring.

The Cost of Indifference

Indifference has a bill, and it comes due in predictable ways. A burglary ring that works an area for months before anyone organizes is a ring that could have been stopped after the first few homes. A domestic abuser whose neighbors heard the fights for years is a homicide that might have been prevented. An elderly neighbor who lives alone and goes unchecked for two days is a missing-person case that starts too late. Every one of those costs falls first on a victim, and then on taxpayers who pay for the investigation, the trial, and the aftermath.

Domestic violence is where indifference kills most reliably. Federal public health researchers have found that roughly half of the women murdered in the United States are killed by a current or former intimate partner, and many of those killings follow a long, visible pattern of threats, assaults, and calls to police. Neighbors hear it. Coworkers notice the injuries. Relatives worry quietly. The Moab stop showed how even a police encounter can fail to read the danger. Each of those moments was a chance to intervene, and each was treated as someone else's private business until it became a homicide file.

Greg understands this now in a way no article could have taught him. After the break-in, he learned that two other homes on his street had been entered that spring, and that the people living in them had not mentioned it at the barbecue because it seemed like their own private misfortune. He learned that the police had connected his case to the subdivision across the highway weeks before it reached him. The warning existed. It simply never crossed the road, because nobody on his side was listening for it.

Turning Attention Into Protection

The cure for indifference is not fear. It is structure, the habits and systems that make attention routine instead of reactive. Neighborhoods can form real block associations or watch groups that meet in person, share crime information directly with police, and keep a list of residents who live alone and want a daily check. Some police departments offer voluntary registries for seniors and for people with dementia, so that officers have a photograph and medical information ready in the first hour. Families can set up simple daily check-ins with an elderly parent, so that an absence is noticed within hours rather than days.

Legislatures can standardize missing-senior protocols so that a report triggers immediate action, and they can follow Utah's lead in requiring lethality assessments on domestic violence calls. Police chiefs can publish neighborhood-level crime data that residents can actually read, so that a pattern across the highway reaches the people next in its path. City councils can fund the kind of community liaison officers who attend neighborhood meetings and carry warnings across the invisible boundaries that residents draw.

Courts and prosecutors can do their part by treating hoaxes and exploitation in high-profile cases as the serious crimes they are, as federal prosecutors did with the fake ransom messages in the Guthrie case. Every impostor drains investigative time from the real search. Judges can also make protective orders easier to obtain and faster to serve, so that a victim who finally speaks up is not sent home to wait for weeks. Banks and phone carriers, which see fraud against older customers before anyone else, can be required to flag suspicious patterns and alert a trusted family contact.

Citizens can also change what they consume. Follow the court cases in your own county rather than the sensational trial across the country. Read the police blotter for your own zip code once a week. When a neighbor is hit, treat it as your problem too, because statistically it soon may be. Families like the Petitos did not choose their expertise. The rest of us can choose ours before we have to.

The takeaway

Crime feels distant until it arrives, and the Nancy Guthrie case proves that no fame, wealth, or quiet neighborhood keeps it away, while the Petito case shows how many warnings pass unheeded first. Build the structures now, from daily check-ins and block groups to missing-senior protocols and lethality assessments, so that your attention protects someone before the tragedy rather than mourning them after.

Chapter 16: Government Responses That Become Failures Themselves

Carla still keeps the dispatch printout in a kitchen drawer, folded into quarters, the creases gone soft from handling. It records the minute she called for help when a man with a rifle walked into the warehouse where she worked the night shift, and it records the minute the first officer stepped inside. Between those two lines are forty-one minutes. She spent them under a steel desk with a coworker's blood drying on her sleeve, listening to radios crackle in the parking lot. Nobody came through the door. When I sat with her, years later, she did not talk about the gunman much. She talked about the radios.

That detail has stayed with me longer than almost anything else in my years on the crime beat. Carla is a composite of several survivors I have interviewed, but every one of them said some version of the same thing. The attacker hurt them once. The response hurt them for the rest of their lives, because it taught them that the people paid to come would not come, or would come wrong, or would spend the following years explaining why nobody could be blamed.

Americans tend to imagine government failure as absence: the call that goes unanswered, the agency that never shows up. The worse failures are the ones where government arrives in force and still makes the outcome deadlier, then turns its energy toward protecting itself. Those are the cases I want to walk through, because they share a pattern, and patterns can be broken. The pattern has three parts, hesitation, catastrophic choices and institutional fear, and each one leaves a wound in the public long after the cameras leave and the crime scene tape comes down.

Hesitation and Inaction

On May 24, 2022, an eighteen-year-old gunman entered Robb Elementary School in Uvalde, Texas, and killed nineteen children and two teachers. Officers were inside the building within minutes. The gunman was not confronted and killed until seventy-seven minutes after the first officers arrived. By the end of the standoff, the school and the streets around it had drawn several hundred officers from local, county, state and federal agencies. Hundreds of armed, trained, equipped people stood within yards of classrooms where children were calling 911 and whispering for help.

The Justice Department's Critical Incident Review, released in January 2024 and running to roughly six hundred pages, named the single most critical failure plainly. Officers treated an active shooter as a barricaded suspect, a situation that supposedly allows time to wait, negotiate and gather equipment. Active-shooter doctrine in this country has said the opposite since Columbine: the first officers on scene go in, because every minute costs lives. The report found no clear incident commander, no urgency in establishing command, and a cascade of confused and inaccurate public statements afterward that deepened the families' suffering.

Hesitation is rarely a single person's cowardice, though cowardice can be part of it. It grows from training that is never rehearsed, radios that do not talk to each other, and an unspoken rule that nobody moves without permission from someone who outranks them. At Uvalde, each new agency that arrived assumed someone else was in charge. Each officer who waited could point to another officer who was also waiting. Responsibility spread so thin across the crowd that it vanished.

Uvalde was not the first time. In February 2018, when a former student opened fire at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland, Florida, the armed sheriff's deputy assigned to the campus took a position outside the building and stayed there while seventeen people were killed. Prosecutors charged him with child neglect and other counts, and a jury acquitted him in 2023. The verdict settled the narrow question of criminal liability. It left untouched the question every parent asked, which was why the one person paid to stand between a gunman and the students did not go in.

I have sat in courtrooms like the ones in Florida and Texas, and the defense in these cases is always some version of the same argument: the officer lacked information, the situation was chaotic, the training was unclear. Some of that is true. All of it is an indictment of the agencies that employed them. An organization that cannot tell its own armed officers, in plain language, that they are expected to move toward gunfire has failed before the first shot is ever fired.

The legal aftermath has been just as diffuse. A Texas grand jury indicted the former school district police chief and one school officer on charges of child endangerment. In January 2026, a jury acquitted that officer, Adrian Gonzales, on all counts. The former chief has pleaded not guilty, and his trial has been tentatively set for 2027. Whatever happens in that courtroom, the deeper truth is already on the record. The system that should have stopped a teenager with a rifle had the people, the weapons and the time, and it froze.

Catastrophic Decision-Making

If hesitation is one face of failure, the other is the bold decision made in a fog of anger and pride. On May 13, 1985, after a long and bitter conflict with the radical group MOVE, Philadelphia police dropped an explosive device from a helicopter onto the group's row house on Osage Avenue. The fire that followed was allowed to burn. Eleven people died inside, five of them children. Sixty-one homes in a working, mostly Black neighborhood were destroyed.

A commission appointed by the city reviewed the operation and called the plan reckless, ill-conceived and hastily approved, and described the dropping of a bomb on an occupied row house as unconscionable. A grand jury later declined to bring criminal charges. The city did not formally apologize until 2020, thirty-five years after the fire. The homeowners who lost everything on Osage Avenue spent decades fighting over shoddy replacement houses built by the city, a second injury delivered by the same government that caused the first.

Waco followed eight years later. In February 1993, a federal raid on the Branch Davidian compound outside Waco, Texas, ended in a gunfight that killed four ATF agents and six Davidians and began a fifty-one-day siege. On April 19, the FBI moved in with armored vehicles and tear gas, and the compound burned. Roughly seventy-five people died that day, more than twenty of them children. A special counsel, former Senator John Danforth, concluded in 2000 that government agents did not start the fire and that the Davidians bore responsibility for the deaths, and he also documented years of official nondisclosure that had poisoned public trust.

Both cases can be argued in good faith. MOVE had engaged in armed confrontation with police before, and the Branch Davidians killed federal agents. I do not pretend either situation offered easy options. My point is narrower. In each, the officials in charge reached for the most dramatic tool available, under pressure to end a standoff that had become an embarrassment, and the people who paid were children and neighbors who had no say in the matter.

Institutional Fear

Watch what agencies do after a disaster and you learn what they fear most. It is rarely the next gunman. It is the deposition, the headline, the budget hearing, the lawsuit. In Uvalde, officials released early accounts that praised the response and then had to retract them as video and records surfaced.
