Families spent months fighting for basic information about how their children died. That instinct to control the story is not unique to Texas. It is the default setting of American public institutions under pressure.

The fear is understandable on a human level. Commanders know that anything they say in the first hours will be quoted in a lawsuit for the next decade, so they say little, or they say something reassuring that turns out to be false. City attorneys advise silence. Union contracts slow discipline. Each of those choices protects someone, and none of them protects the public. Over time, an agency trained to minimize exposure loses the habit of telling the truth even when the truth would help it.

In 2026, the disappearance of Nancy Guthrie showed how that fear plays out even in an open case. Guthrie, the eighty-four-year-old mother of NBC "Today" host Savannah Guthrie, vanished from her home near Tucson, Arizona, at the beginning of February, and investigators said they believed she had been taken against her will. No suspect had been named as of the latest reporting I could verify, and the case remains unresolved. What became public was a dispute over handling. The Pima County sheriff acknowledged he would have preserved the scene longer and called in the FBI sooner, while the FBI director publicly accused local officials of shutting federal investigators out early, a charge the sheriff disputed.

I make no judgment about who is right in that dispute, and I make none about any person's guilt. The lesson sits elsewhere. When a family's worst week becomes a fight between agencies over credit and control, the public sees institutions protecting turf. A recall effort against the sheriff was launched and later fell short of the signatures it needed. However that political story ends, a family is still waiting, and every leaked squabble makes the next family less willing to trust a sheriff's promise that everything possible is being done.

Public Trauma

Trauma from a failed response does not stay with the immediate victims. It spreads. Parents across the country watched the Uvalde footage of officers checking their phones and using a hand sanitizer dispenser in the hallway while children were inside. Many concluded that if the worst happened at their own child's school, nobody would come in. That belief changes behavior. It drives families toward private security, toward arming themselves, toward withdrawing from public schools, and toward a cynicism that no press conference can undo.

Denise, a community leader I have known for years in a mid-sized Southern city, runs a neighborhood association that once met in a school gym. After Uvalde, attendance at her safety meetings tripled, and the questions changed. People no longer asked how to work with the police. They asked how to survive without them. Denise is a composite of organizers I have interviewed since 2022, but her experience is common. A single televised failure can undo a decade of patient trust-building on the other side of the country.

The Osage Avenue fire did something similar to Philadelphia. A generation of residents grew up knowing that their city once bombed a neighborhood and then let it burn. That knowledge did not fade when the commission issued its report. It became part of how people in West Philadelphia judged every promise from city hall, every new police initiative, every redevelopment plan. Trauma is a civic memory, and civic memory has a very long half-life.

Carla felt it in her own body. For two years after the warehouse shooting, she could not sit with her back to a door, and she flinched at the sound of police radios in grocery store parking lots. Her therapist told her that what haunted her was not only fear but betrayal, the specific wound of being abandoned by people whose whole job was to arrive. Survivors of crime can heal from many things. Betrayal by the institution that promised protection is among the hardest, because the promise is repeated every day on every patrol car door.

The Collapse of Confidence

Each failed response leaves an invoice. Uvalde produced lawsuits, firings, resignations and a national argument about whether police can be relied on at all. Waco became a rallying point for anti-government extremists. Timothy McVeigh chose the second anniversary of the fire to bomb the federal building in Oklahoma City in 1995. That is the most terrible example of how a failed response can echo, and it shows why the stakes go far beyond any single tragedy.

Confidence matters in practical ways. People who believe help will not come stop calling for it, stop reporting threats, and stop cooperating with investigations. Taxpayers who watch agencies spend lavishly on armored vehicles and tactical gear, then fail at the one moment that equipment was meant for, start voting against public safety budgets of every kind. The damage lands on good officers along with bad ones, and on the communities that most need a functioning response.

There is also a quieter cost. Every failure that ends without a public reckoning teaches the next commander that hesitation is survivable. When a review lands years later and nobody in authority loses a job, the lesson inside the institution is clear: protect the department, wait out the anger, issue a statement about lessons learned. The public reads the same events and draws the opposite conclusion, that the government will not tell the truth about itself. Two groups watching the same tragedy walk away believing completely different things, and the gap between them is where confidence dies.

What a Real Fix Looks Like

The first fix is rehearsal. Every school district and every police agency that might respond to it should run joint active-shooter drills at least once a year, on site, with radios that work across agencies and a single, named incident commander for every scenario. The standard Uvalde ignored already exists and is taught nationally through programs such as the ALERRT training center at Texas State University. Legislatures can tie state safety grants to proof that drills happened and that after-action reports were filed.

Automatic transparency comes next. Cities and states should require that body camera footage, dispatch logs and a preliminary timeline of any mass-casualty response be released within a fixed window, with narrow exceptions for an active prosecution. Families should never have to sue to learn how their children died. Independent review should follow every major failure, conducted by someone outside the chain of command, published in full.

Accountability, third, has to reach the decision-makers. Grand juries and prosecutors cannot carry that load alone, and the uneven results of criminal cases show why. Police chiefs, mayors and city councils can adopt written duty-to-act policies with real employment consequences, and state licensing boards for police officers can revoke certifications when an officer refuses to engage. Citizens can show up to budget hearings and ask a simple question: when did this department last rehearse the worst day, and where is the report?

Survivors deserve the last fix. States should fund trauma care, lost wages and long-term counseling for every person caught in a failed response, without forcing them to prove fault first. Carla waited years for a settlement that came with a confidentiality clause. A government that cannot always protect people can at least stop making them fight it afterward, and it can let them speak freely about what happened.

The takeaway

Government does not only fail by staying away. It fails by freezing, by reaching for the biggest weapon, and by hiding afterward, and each of those failures leaves a trauma that outlasts the crime. Demand rehearsals, demand records and demand that someone with a name is in charge before the worst day comes.

Chapter 17: How Enforcement Aligns With Criminal Outcomes

In 2014, by a widely cited comparison of federal records, the government took more property from Americans through asset forfeiture than burglars stole from them. The forfeiture funds held by the Justice and Treasury departments took in billions that year, while the FBI's tally of burglary losses came in lower. Analysts have argued over the details ever since, and some of that money did come from real criminals. The comparison still lands with force, because it should be impossible. The institution created to stop people from taking what is yours ended up taking more of it than the thieves did.

I have spent years on the crime beat watching that kind of inversion happen in smaller, quieter ways. A man loses his car, not because he was convicted, but because a deputy found cash in the glovebox. A woman goes to jail, not because she hurt anyone, but because she could not pay a fine for an overgrown lawn. When enforcement starts producing the same results crime produces, losses, fear, broken families and lost jobs, the people on the receiving end stop seeing much difference between the two.

That is the argument here, and it is not a claim that every officer is a thief. Most police officers and court clerks I have met are doing the job as it was handed to them. The problem is the way the job was built. Incentives shape behavior, and the incentives inside American enforcement too often reward the very outcomes the system claims to be fighting.

Structural Incentives

Start with money, because money explains more than any training manual. Under civil forfeiture laws, police can seize cash, cars and homes they suspect are connected to crime without charging the owner with anything. In many states the seizing agency keeps much of the proceeds, and a federal program called equitable sharing has long let local departments route seizures through federal agencies and receive a large share back. The department that decides whether to take your property is the department that gets to spend it.

Philadelphia showed how far that logic can travel. For years the city's district attorney's office used civil forfeiture against homes and cars, sometimes when a relative of the owner had been caught selling small amounts of drugs from the property. Owners who had committed no crime were pulled into a courtroom run largely by prosecutors, where cases could drag on through repeated hearings. A class action lawsuit challenged the program, and the city agreed in 2018 to sweeping limits that ended most real estate forfeitures. Before that settlement, the proceeds had helped fund the very office that brought the cases.

Federal policy has swung back and forth on this. In 2015, Attorney General Eric Holder sharply restricted the practice of federal adoption, in which local police hand a seizure to federal agencies and receive most of it back. Two years later, Attorney General Jeff Sessions restored it. The swing tells you that the incentive is a policy choice, not a law of nature, and that it can be changed by people who decide to change it.

Tyson Timbs learned what that means. After his father died, he used life insurance money to buy a Land Rover for roughly $42,000. He later used it to transport heroin, pleaded guilty to drug charges, and the state of Indiana moved to take the car. The maximum fine for his offense was a small fraction of the vehicle's value. In Timbs v. Indiana in 2019, the Supreme Court held unanimously in its judgment that the Eighth Amendment's ban on excessive fines applies to state and local governments, not only to Washington. The ruling matters because it gave every owner a constitutional argument against forfeitures grossly out of proportion to the offense.

Quotas and productivity numbers work the same way. Many departments deny having formal quotas, yet officers across the country have described evaluations that count stops, tickets and arrests. A count is easy to measure. Safety is not. When an officer's career depends on the number of citations written, the rational move is to write a great many small ones in places where it is easy, and that rarely means the places where violent crime happens.

There is a deeper structural problem underneath the money. Prosecutors are judged by conviction rates, and conviction rates rise when cases are easy. Jails are funded per bed, so empty beds look like waste. Private probation companies in some states earn fees from the people they supervise. Bail bond businesses profit when bail is high. Each of these actors is responding sensibly to the rules in front of it, and the sum of those sensible responses is a machine that rewards volume over justice.

Misaligned Priorities

Here is the result of those incentives in one blunt contrast. Nationally, police clear only somewhere around six in ten murders, a far lower share than the 1960s, and the clearance rates for rapes, robberies and burglaries are lower still. Yet the same agencies conduct millions of traffic stops every year, many for equipment problems and expired registrations. The serious, hard cases go cold while the easy, low-level enforcement piles up, because the easy cases generate numbers and revenue and the hard ones generate overtime.

A homeowner whose house was broken into understands this in her gut. She files a report, waits for a call back, and hears nothing for months. Then she drives to the grocery store and sees two patrol cars parked behind a pickup with a cracked taillight. Nobody has to explain the department's priorities to her. She can see them on the street. When I hear officers complain that the public does not appreciate them, I think of people like her, who appreciate the police perfectly well and simply notice where they are spending their time.

Ferguson, Missouri, became the textbook example. In March 2015, the Justice Department released its investigation of the Ferguson Police Department after the killing of Michael Brown. Investigators found that the city had made revenue a central goal of policing. City officials pressed the police chief to increase ticket writing, and fines and fees grew into a major slice of the city budget, budgeted at nearly a quarter of general revenue in one recent year. The report described a municipal court that functioned less as a neutral judge than as a collection agency for the city.

The numbers were staggering for a town of about twenty-one thousand people. In fiscal 2013, the court issued arrest warrants to roughly nine thousand people, covering tens of thousands of offenses, most of them minor violations like parking tickets, traffic infractions and housing code problems. The Justice Department also found that Black residents bore the burden of this system far out of proportion to their share of the population. A police department that was busy writing tickets to fill a budget was not a department focused on solving the crimes that frightened residents.

Harm Through Enforcement

Marcus, a taxpayer I came to know while reporting on court debt, never committed a violent act in his life. He is a composite drawn from several men and women whose stories I have heard in county courthouse hallways, and every part of his story happened to someone. It started with a speeding ticket he could not pay on time. Late fees doubled it. A missed court date, scheduled during a shift he could not leave, produced a warrant. The warrant produced an arrest at a routine traffic stop and two nights in jail.

By the time Marcus walked out, he had lost his warehouse job for missing work. His license was suspended for the unpaid balance. To keep earning anything, he drove anyway, and the next stop turned a suspended license into a new criminal charge. None of this made his neighborhood safer. It made Marcus poorer, angrier, and more likely to end up in the same system that had started the spiral. He described the experience to me as being robbed twice, first by the court, then by everything that followed.

The hardest moment, he told me, came at his daughter's school. He had promised to pick her up on a Friday, and he could not drive there legally, so he walked forty minutes in the rain and arrived late. She was waiting on the curb with a teacher. Nobody in that system meant to humiliate a father in front of his child. It happened anyway, because each piece of the machine did its job without asking what the whole thing was doing to the people inside it.

Debt-related license suspensions are not rare. National estimates from reform advocates put the number of Americans with licenses suspended for unpaid fines and fees in the millions. Supporters argue that suspensions force people to pay. Studies in several states have found they do little to improve collections, while they make it harder for people to get to the jobs that would let them pay. A policy that blocks repayment in the name of collecting money has stopped serving any purpose except punishment.

Criminalization of Poverty

The cruelest version of this alignment happens before anyone is convicted. Kalief Browder was sixteen in 2010 when he was arrested in the Bronx, accused of stealing a backpack. Bail was set at an amount his family could not raise, and he spent about three years on Rikers Island waiting for a trial that never came, much of it in solitary confinement, according to widely reported accounts. The charges were eventually dropped. He was released in 2013, and in 2015, at twenty-two, he took his own life. New York City later settled a lawsuit brought by his family.

His case became a symbol because it was so stark, but the mechanism behind it is ordinary. Cash bail means a person with money goes home to prepare a defense, keep a job and care for children, while a person without money sits in a cell for the same accusation. Many plead guilty simply to get out, even when they are innocent or the evidence is weak. In that arrangement, poverty itself becomes the thing being punished.

The Supreme Court saw this problem decades ago. In Bearden v. Georgia in 1983, the Court reviewed the case of a man whose probation was revoked because he lost his job and could not pay his fine and restitution. The Court held that a judge cannot automatically jail someone for failing to pay without first asking whether the person tried in good faith and whether alternatives to prison would work. Jailing a person solely because he is poor, the Court reasoned, is fundamentally unfair.

Bearden remains good law. Yet the Ferguson investigation, decades later, found a court that routinely issued warrants and jailed people over unpaid fines without the kind of ability-to-pay inquiry the Supreme Court required. A constitutional protection exists on paper and fails in the courtrooms where poor people actually stand. Ask a public defender in almost any American county, and you will hear the same story about how rarely those hearings happen as the law intends.

The Inversion of Justice

Put all of this together and the picture becomes hard to look at. Enforcement takes property from people who have not been convicted. It jails people for being poor. It suspends the licenses people need to work and then charges them for driving to work. It spends its energy on minor violations while murders and rapes go unsolved. The victims of real crime wait for justice that never arrives, while ordinary people are drained by a system that looks a great deal like the predators it was built to stop.

A survivor whose burglary is never investigated pays twice in that system. She loses her property to a thief, and then she watches the same department spend its afternoons writing tickets on the boulevard. A taxpayer funds both the theft investigation that never happens and the court debt machinery that pushes his neighbors into crisis. The money flows toward what is easy to count, and the harm flows toward whoever has the least power to resist.

The damage to legitimacy may be the greatest cost of all. People who have been shaken down by a court over a parking ticket do not volunteer as witnesses when a shooting happens on their block. They have learned that contact with the system is dangerous, expensive and unpredictable. Every unjust fine buys a little revenue today and sells off a piece of the cooperation that solves serious crimes tomorrow.

Good faith exists on the other side of this argument. Fines can deter dangerous driving. Forfeiture can strip drug cartels of real profits. Bail can keep a genuinely dangerous person off the street. I accept all of that. My charge is that we built these tools without limits, tied them to agency budgets, and then acted surprised when they were used for revenue instead of safety. A tool with a profit motive attached will eventually be used for profit.

How to Realign Enforcement

The first fix is to cut the money link. Legislatures can require that forfeiture proceeds go to a state general fund or to victim compensation, not to the agency that made the seizure. New Mexico in 2015 and Nebraska in 2016 went further and required a criminal conviction before most property can be forfeited, and Maine followed with similar reforms in 2021. Cities can cap the share of their budgets that comes from fines and fees. Missouri did this after Ferguson, tightening the limit on how much municipalities could draw from traffic revenue.

Next, stop suspending driver's licenses for debt. A growing list of states, from California to Mississippi to Virginia, have ended or restricted debt-related suspensions in recent years, often with support from both progressive and conservative groups. City councils and county courts can also eliminate administrative fees that fund the court itself. San Francisco became one of the first counties to wipe out a range of criminal justice fees in 2018, after finding they were costly to collect and fell hardest on people who could not pay.

Courts, third, must enforce Bearden. State supreme courts and judicial councils can require a written ability-to-pay finding before any person is jailed or has a license suspended for nonpayment, and they can offer community service or payment plans as real alternatives. Pretrial reforms can replace money bail with risk-based release decisions, so that freedom depends on danger rather than wealth. Police chiefs, finally, can change what they measure, rewarding detectives for clearing serious crimes and officers for reducing harm rather than for raw counts of tickets and stops.

Citizens have a part to play that costs nothing but attention. Pull your city's budget and find the line for fines, forfeitures and court fees. If it is growing faster than the population, ask your council why. Attend a session of municipal court and watch who stands before the judge and how many are asked whether they can pay. Those two small acts of reporting, done by ordinary people, have started reform movements in more than one American town.

The takeaway

When enforcement profits from the people it polices, it starts to resemble the crime it claims to fight. Break the money link, end debt-based license suspensions, and hold courts to the rule that no one goes to jail simply for being poor.

Chapter 18: The Collapse of Public Trust

Why does nobody talk? Anyone who has watched the evening news after a daytime shooting has asked it. A man is killed on a crowded corner at four in the afternoon, a dozen people are standing within sight, and a week later the police are still pleading for witnesses. The question usually comes with a judgment attached, that the neighborhood is protecting a killer or simply does not care.

I have stood on those corners after the tape came down. I have knocked on the doors and sat on the porches, and I can tell you the judgment is almost always wrong. People in high-crime neighborhoods care more about violence than anyone, because they live beside it. They do not stay quiet out of loyalty to the shooter. They stay quiet because they have concluded, often from hard personal experience, that talking to the police will not make them safer and may make them less safe.

That is the answer to the question, and it reverses the usual story. Silence is not the cause of unsolved crime in many American neighborhoods. It is the result of a broken relationship between residents and the institutions meant to protect them. Witnesses who do not trust police do not talk, and when they do not talk, cases go cold, shooters stay free, and the next shooting becomes more likely.

Broken Promises

Tanya's brother was shot in the parking lot of a corner store on a summer evening. She is a composite of several survivors I have interviewed over the years, and her story is assembled from theirs, but none of the details is unusual. Detectives came to the hospital and promised her they would find the person responsible. They gave her a card. For the first two weeks, they called back. After that, the calls stopped, and her own calls went to voicemail.

Months later, Tanya learned from a neighbor that at least three people had seen the shooting. None of them had come forward. When she asked one of them why, the answer was simple and devastating. The woman had once called the police about a man threatening her nephew, and the officers who arrived treated her nephew as the suspect. She had decided she would never again invite police into her family's life. Tanya understood. That was the worst part, she told me. She understood exactly why the witness stayed silent, and her brother's killer was still out there.

Promises of that kind are broken across the country every day, and the numbers reflect it. Nationally, police clear somewhere around six in ten murders, according to FBI data in recent years, down from more than ninety percent in the 1960s. In some big cities the share is far lower. For nonfatal shootings, the clearance rate in many departments is lower still. Each unsolved case is a broken promise to a family, and the neighbors see every one of them.

Some of that decline has honest explanations. Murders today are more often committed by strangers or near-strangers with guns, at a distance, than the domestic killings that once made up a larger share of cases. Detective units in many cities are understaffed and buried under caseloads. None of that comforts a sister standing in a hospital hallway. What she hears, when the calls stop, is that her brother's life did not matter enough to finish the job.

Chicago offers the most vivid example of how a broken promise becomes a collapse. On October 20, 2014, Officer Jason Van Dyke shot seventeen-year-old Laquan McDonald sixteen times on a city street. Early official accounts portrayed the teenager as a threat to officers. A dashcam video told a different story. The city fought its release for more than a year, until a judge ordered it made public in November 2015. The footage showed McDonald walking away from police when he was shot.

The release changed Chicago. Van Dyke was charged with first-degree murder the same day the video came out, and in October 2018 a jury convicted him of second-degree murder and sixteen counts of aggravated battery with a firearm. Mayor Rahm Emanuel fired the police superintendent, saying publicly that trust in the department had eroded. Three officers were later charged with conspiring to cover up what happened, and a judge acquitted them in 2019. The Justice Department, after an investigation it opened in the wake of the shooting, found in 2017 that Chicago police had engaged in a pattern of excessive force, and the city entered a court-supervised consent decree.

For residents of the South and West Sides, the lesson was not merely that one officer had committed a crime on a cold night on Pulaski Road. It was that the city had known what the video showed for more than a year and had tried to keep it hidden. That is what destroys trust: not only misconduct, but the institutional effort to bury it.

National surveys tell the same story at a larger scale. Gallup's long-running measure of public confidence in institutions found confidence in police falling to around half of Americans or below in the early 2020s, the lowest levels in the history of that question, with far lower confidence among Black Americans. A drop of that size does not come from one video. It comes from years of cases like McDonald's, each one confirming what many residents already suspected about how the system protects itself.

Community Fear

Fear in a neighborhood with low trust runs in two directions at once. Residents fear the shooters, who live among them and know where their children walk to school. They also fear the police, or at least fear what police contact can bring, from a hostile stop to an arrest over an unrelated warrant to the loss of a public housing lease if a relative gets swept up. When both sources of power feel dangerous, silence becomes the safest choice a rational person can make.

The fear of retaliation is real, and it is not imagined by cowards. Witnesses in violent cases are sometimes threatened, assaulted or killed. Prosecutors in many cities admit privately that they cannot promise protection, because witness relocation money is scarce and often runs out after a few weeks in a motel. A grandmother asked to testify against a gang member is being asked to bet her family's safety on a system that has already disappointed her once.

There is also a quieter fear, of being disbelieved or blamed, and it falls hardest on the people who have already been hurt once. Survivors of sexual assault, domestic violence and robbery have long described police interviews that felt like interrogations. That experience travels by word of mouth. A single humiliating encounter, told around a kitchen table, can keep an entire extended family from calling for help for a generation.

Homeowners feel the fear in yet another way. A retired man who has lived on the same block for forty years watches a vacant house become a drug spot. He knows the faces of the people who come and go. He also knows that if he calls, the officers may knock on his door in uniform in full view of the street, and everyone will know who called. In a city where anonymous tip lines feel anything but anonymous, the safest homeowner is the one who closes the blinds. That is a terrible thing for a government to teach its taxpayers.

The phrase people reach for in these neighborhoods is "no snitching," and outsiders often treat it as a criminal code imposed by gangs. Some of it is exactly that, enforced with threats. Much of it is something else, a folk response to decades of experience in which cooperating with police brought trouble and rarely brought justice. Telling residents to abandon it, without changing the conditions that created it, is asking people to take a risk the institutions themselves will not take.

The Erosion of Cooperation

Researchers have tried to measure what happens to cooperation when trust breaks. One widely discussed study examined 911 calls in Milwaukee after local news coverage, in 2006, of the brutal beating of a Black man named Frank Jude by off-duty police officers. The authors found that crime-related calls dropped noticeably in the following months, especially in Black neighborhoods, while calls about traffic accidents did not. A later reanalysis questioned how large and lasting the effect was, so I hold the precise numbers loosely. The direction of the finding matches everything I have seen on the street.

The broader research on legitimacy points the same way. Scholars of procedural justice have found over decades that people are more likely to obey the law and help police when they believe officers treat them fairly, explain their decisions and listen before acting. The finding holds across races and income levels. It suggests that cooperation is not something police can demand. It is something they earn through how they behave in ordinary encounters, long before any crime happens.

Cooperation also erodes when people see that talking produces nothing. If a resident reports drug dealing on her block five times and nothing changes, she stops reporting. If a witness gives a statement and the case is never charged, she tells her friends it was a waste of time and a risk. Every investigation that dies quietly teaches the community that the effort is not worth it.

I have watched this erosion happen in real time on stories I covered. A neighborhood rallies after a child is hit by a stray bullet. Residents fill a church basement, the commander promises results, and tips pour in for a few weeks. Then the case stalls, the commander is transferred, and nobody explains what happened. The next time a child is hurt, the church basement is half empty. Nothing about the residents changed in between. They simply learned.

Trust as Crime Prevention

Most public safety debates treat trust as a soft value, something nice to have once crime is under control. That view has the relationship backward. Trust is a crime-fighting tool, possibly the most important one police have. Detectives solve homicides mainly because someone tells them who did it. Without witnesses, forensic evidence often cannot carry a case, and without cases, deterrence collapses.

Think about what an unsolved shooting does next. The victim's friends know or suspect who pulled the trigger. If the police do not make an arrest, some of them will take matters into their own hands. Retaliation produces another victim, another set of grieving friends, another unsolved case. Low clearance rates and cycles of retaliatory violence feed each other. A city that cannot solve its shootings is, in effect, outsourcing justice to the street.

Tanya lived that fear for a year. Her brother's friends came to the house after the funeral and said they knew who did it. She begged them to go to the detectives instead of handling it themselves. One of them laughed, not cruelly, and asked her what the detectives had done for her so far. She had no answer. She spent the following months terrified that the next funeral would be one of theirs, or one of the other side's, and that her brother's death would become the first link in a chain.

Taxpayers foot the bill for that failure in ways they rarely connect. Every unsolved shooting adds emergency room costs, lost wages, and police overtime spent on the next incident. Businesses close or refuse to open, and the jobs they would have offered go somewhere else. Homeowners watch their property values sink. The money spent rebuilding trust is small next to the money spent on the consequences of losing it.

The good faith here deserves its own sentence. Many officers know all of this and work hard at it. They coach Little League, attend block parties, return calls, and knock on doors on their own time to tell a mother that her son's case has not been forgotten. Their efforts are real, and they are undermined every time a department hides a video, protects a bad officer or lets a case go cold without telling the family why.

Rebuilding Trust

Rebuilding starts with telling the truth quickly. Cities and states can require that video of police shootings and serious uses of force be released within a short, fixed window, as Chicago now does under a policy adopted after the McDonald case. A department that releases bad footage on its own, rather than under court order, sends a message no public relations campaign can match: we will not hide from you.

The second step is to treat investigations as promises with deadlines. Departments can assign victim liaisons who call families on a fixed schedule, even when the news is that there is no news. They can publish clearance rates by neighborhood and by crime type every year, so residents can see whether the effort is equal. Several cities have created dedicated nonfatal shooting units, on the theory that solving the shootings people survive will prevent the ones they do not.

Third, protect the people who come forward. Legislatures can fund real witness protection at the state level, with housing assistance that lasts as long as the danger does, not just a week in a motel. Prosecutors can expand programs that let witnesses give statements and testimony with greater safety, and they can prosecute witness intimidation as the serious crime it is.

Anonymous reporting deserves real investment as well. Text-based tip lines, run by civilian organizations rather than police dispatch, give residents a way to share what they know without a patrol car pulling up to the door. Several cities have paired such lines with reward funds raised by local businesses. The point is to lower the price of honesty until ordinary people can afford to pay it.

Fourth, change what happens in ordinary encounters. Police chiefs can train and evaluate officers on procedural justice, and they can stop using minor stops as fishing expeditions that sour residents on every later contact. Camden, New Jersey, offers one example of what a reset can look like. In 2013 the city dissolved its police department and replaced it with a county-run force that emphasized community policing, foot patrols and de-escalation. Violent crime and homicides have since fallen dramatically. Experts debate how much of that decline the new approach deserves credit for, but residents describe a different relationship with officers than the one they had before.

Citizens have a role too. Community leaders can host listening sessions where residents speak directly with commanders, and they can insist that those sessions produce written commitments that are checked a year later. Survivors like Tanya can push city councils to fund the liaison and witness programs that would have helped her family. Trust is rebuilt one kept promise at a time, and every resident who sees a promise kept becomes a witness to the change.

Tanya's case is still open. She keeps the detective's card on her refrigerator, and she still calls. What she wants now is modest: a phone call every month, an honest account of where things stand, and some sign that her brother's name means something to the people paid to find his killer. If a department can give every family that much, it will have started the slow work of earning back the neighborhood.

The takeaway

Silence in a frightened neighborhood is not indifference; it is the price of broken promises. Release the video, keep calling the family, protect the witness, and publish the results, and people will start talking again.

Chapter 19: The Predictability of Crime — And Our Refusal to Act

On January 5, 2018, someone close to a nineteen-year-old in South Florida called the FBI's public tip line. The caller described his guns, his erratic behavior, his disturbing posts online and his desire to kill people, and raised the possibility that he might attack a school. On February 14, 2018, that young man walked into Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and killed seventeen people. Between those two dates sat forty days, a federal agency with a clear protocol for threats to life, and a tip that was never passed to the field office that could have acted on it.

The FBI admitted as much two days after the shooting, saying its own protocols had not been followed. That admission is rare. The pattern behind it is not. On one date, the warning arrives, specific and urgent. On a later date, the warning comes true. The weeks in between belong to institutions that knew enough to act and did not.

Highland Park, Illinois, follows the same shape. In 2019, police were called to the home of a young man twice, once after a suicide attempt and once after a relative reported that he had threatened to kill everyone, and officers removed a collection of knives. Police filed a report with the state flagging him as a danger. Months later, his father sponsored his application for a state firearm owner's card. On July 4, 2022, he fired into a holiday parade crowd and killed seven people. In 2025 he pleaded guilty and was sentenced to life in prison. His father pleaded guilty in 2023 to misdemeanor reckless conduct over the card application. Two moments, three years apart, and a chain of paperwork in between that nobody pulled.

Known Patterns

The individual warnings are only half the story. The larger truth is that crime in general is astonishingly predictable, and that has been known for decades. Criminologists who mapped crime at the level of individual street blocks found that roughly half of all crime in a city tends to occur on about four or five percent of its street segments. The pattern has turned up in Seattle, in Tel Aviv, in Bogota and in city after city in between. Researchers call it the law of crime concentration, and while scholars still argue about how universal it is, the basic finding has been replicated again and again.

Those hot spots are also stubborn. Studies following street segments over many years have found that the blocks with the most crime tend to stay that way, year after year, unless something changes. A homeowner on one of those blocks does not need a research paper to know it. She can tell you which corner, which hour and which empty lot.

Violence concentrates among people as well as places. Analyses in several cities, often done for violence-reduction programs, have found that a small network of individuals, frequently connected to particular groups, account for a large share of shootings, both as shooters and as victims. Those individuals are often already known to police, probation officers and outreach workers. They have been shot at before. They have friends who were killed. The next round of violence is, to a remarkable degree, already on someone's list.

Time follows patterns too. Shootings in most American cities rise in the summer months, cluster on weekend nights, and spike around certain holidays. Retaliation tends to follow within days of an earlier shooting, often close to where the first one happened. Police analysts know these rhythms. Emergency room doctors know them. Funeral directors know them. The calendar of violence is printed in advance, and the city's response is still too often scheduled after the fact.

Ray, a homeowner whose house sits two doors from one of those corners, put it to me plainly. He is a composite of several longtime residents I have interviewed on blocks like his, but his frustration is word for word what I hear everywhere. He has called about the same corner for eleven years. He can tell you that trouble starts after dark on the first warm Friday in spring. He has told the city, the precinct and his council member. Every year, he said, they act surprised.

He showed me a shoebox of his own records, dates scrawled on the backs of envelopes, plate numbers, times of shots fired. He has been robbed once and had a bullet pass through his garage door. His insurance premium went up twice. He told me he does not need a miracle from the police. He needs somebody to read the shoebox. That a retired taxpayer can predict his block's violence more reliably than the agency his taxes fund is not his failure. It is theirs.

Ignored Warnings

Warnings about individual attackers often arrive in more than one channel at once. After Parkland, a state commission appointed in Florida spent months reconstructing what was known about the shooter beforehand. It documented a long trail of concerns raised with the school, with local law enforcement and with mental health services, and it concluded that the systems in place did not share information or act on it. The commission's work made clear that the tragedy was not a bolt from the blue. It was the endpoint of a long record that no one owned.

The same story repeats in cases with far less attention. Domestic violence homicides very often follow earlier calls to police, prior protective orders, threats with a weapon, or attempts at strangulation, which research on intimate partner homicide has identified as a strong warning sign. Advocates and prosecutors can often name the cases most likely to end in a killing. Too often the warning sits in a file because the agency that holds it has no duty, no staff or no procedure to act.

There is an honest difficulty here, and I want to name it. Most people who make threats never carry them out, and most young men with guns and grievances never commit a mass shooting. Police cannot arrest people for being troubled. Civil liberties matter, and a system that rounded up everyone who fit a profile would create injustices of its own. The answer is not to act on every worry. It is to build processes that take credible, specific warnings seriously and act through lawful tools that already exist.

Parkland and Highland Park had those tools within reach. The FBI had a written protocol for a threat to life, and the tip met it. Illinois had a law allowing officers to ask a judge to keep firearms from a person shown to be dangerous, and police had already flagged the young man to the state as a danger, yet he was approved for a firearm card a few months later. In neither case did the warning fail for lack of law. It failed for lack of follow-through, the unglamorous work of someone picking up a file and deciding it was theirs.

Political Paralysis

If the patterns are known and the warnings arrive, why does action fail? Part of the answer is political. Prevention does not produce a ribbon-cutting. When a program works, the result is a shooting that does not happen, and nobody holds a press conference for a funeral that was never needed. Politicians are rewarded for visible responses, the new unit, the crackdown, the arrest, not for quiet success.

Oakland, California, shows what that does to a program that works. Starting around 2012, the city adopted Ceasefire, a focused-deterrence strategy that identifies the small groups driving gun violence, calls their members to face-to-face meetings with police, community leaders and service providers, and offers both help and a clear warning about consequences. An independent evaluation credited the strategy with a substantial drop in gun homicides, roughly a third, over the following years. Then, according to a later city audit, Oakland gradually walked away from the strategy, notably around 2020. Homicides climbed sharply in the years that followed.

Boston lived through the same cycle a generation earlier. In the mid-1990s, a partnership of police, probation officers, clergy and researchers built Operation Ceasefire around the small number of gang members driving youth homicide, and youth killings fell dramatically in what the press called the Boston Miracle. Within a few years, the partnership frayed as leaders moved on and attention drifted, and violence rose again. The strategy did not stop working. The city stopped doing it. The failure was political and administrative, and in both cities families paid for it.

Budgets reinforce the paralysis. Prevention money usually comes in short grants that expire just as programs mature. Elected officials change every few years, each wanting a signature initiative of their own. Police leadership turns over even faster. A strategy that needs five years of steady commitment rarely gets two. In that churn, the knowledge of where and among whom violence will happen gets lost, rebuilt and lost again.

Institutional Denial

Denial shows up in quieter forms as well. Agencies treat each attack as unforeseeable because foreseeability creates liability. If a department admits that it knew a corner was dangerous or that a person posed a threat, it opens the door to lawsuits and political blame. It is safer, institutionally, to call each incident a tragedy no one could have predicted. That language comforts officials, and it insults the families who were warning them all along.

I have heard the phrase "no one could have seen this coming" at more press conferences than I can count, and in many of those cases I later found records showing that someone did see it coming and said so in writing. The phrase is not a description. It is a legal strategy dressed up as grief. The moment an official uses it, a reporter's job is to go find the earlier warning, and in my experience, it is usually there.

Denial also lives in the data. Many departments still publish crime statistics in ways that hide concentration, reporting citywide totals instead of block-level maps, or releasing figures months late. Ray has never seen a public map of his own block's violent incidents. The precinct has one. The gap between what the agency knows and what it shares lets everyone pretend the problem is vague, general and hard to locate, when it is specific enough to fit on an index card.

There is cultural denial too, and it reaches well beyond any police department. Americans like to believe that violence is random, because randomness absolves us. If a shooting could have happened anywhere to anyone, nobody had to do anything differently. The research says otherwise. Violence clusters by place, by time and by network, and that is good news, because what clusters can be targeted.

Families of victims pay twice for that denial. They lose someone, and then they are told the loss was a random act of fate. Several Parkland parents spent years pushing for public records and accountability precisely because they refused to accept that story. Their persistence, more than any official review, forced the country to see the missed warnings for what they were.

Preventable Violence

Once you accept that much violence is predictable, a great deal of it becomes preventable, and the tools are not exotic. Some of the strongest evidence in modern criminology supports policing focused on small hot spots rather than spread thinly across a city. Randomized experiments, beginning with a well-known study in Minneapolis in the 1990s, found that concentrating patrols at the highest-crime addresses reduced crime there without simply pushing it around the corner.

Changing the places themselves works too. In Philadelphia, researchers ran a randomized trial in which vacant lots were cleaned, planted with grass and trees and fenced. Shootings around the treated lots fell measurably compared with untreated ones, with the strongest effects reported in the poorest neighborhoods, and residents nearby said they felt far safer going outside. The cost was modest, a cleanup and routine mowing, a rounding error in a police budget. Fixing abandoned houses produced similar results in related studies. Those results did not depend on a single arrest.

Prevention can also reach the people most at risk. Focused-deterrence programs like Oakland's, when sustained, have shown reductions in group-involved shootings in several cities. Hospital-based violence intervention programs meet gunshot victims at the bedside, the moment when retaliation is most likely, and connect them with mentors and services. Street outreach workers who know the networks can sometimes cool a dispute before it becomes a shooting. None of this is magic, and results vary by city and by how well a program is run. The evidence still beats doing nothing while waiting for the next call.

Ray's block got a taste of this once. A few summers back, a city crew cleaned the vacant lot beside the corner, fixed two streetlights and boarded the abandoned house next door. An outreach worker started showing up on Friday nights. The corner was quiet that summer, quieter than he could remember. Then the grant ended, the outreach worker moved to another neighborhood, and the lot grew over again. By the next spring, the shoebox was filling up.

What Leaders Can Actually Do

Legislatures can give warnings a lawful path to action. Extreme risk protection order laws, often called red flag laws, let family members or police ask a judge to temporarily remove firearms from a person shown to pose a danger, with due process for the person affected. Florida passed one within weeks of the Parkland shooting. Illinois already had such a law in place in 2019, yet no order was sought in the Highland Park case, according to widely reported accounts. A law only works if agencies are trained and expected to use it, so states should pair these laws with clear protocols and regular audits of how often they are invoked.

City councils can fund prevention for the long haul. That means multiyear budgets for focused deterrence, violence interruption and hospital programs, with independent evaluation written into the contract so a program cannot be quietly abandoned without a public accounting. It also means investing in place-based fixes, from vacant-lot greening to better lighting to boarding and repairing abandoned buildings, on the blocks where the maps say violence concentrates.

Police chiefs can share what they know. Departments can publish block-level maps of violent crime on a regular schedule and brief residents like Ray on what is being done about their corners. Threat-assessment teams that bring together schools, police and mental health professionals, an approach Florida and many districts adopted after Parkland, can make sure a warning that arrives in one office does not die there. Tip lines can be audited, so that every credible report of a threat to life is logged and reviewed.

Citizens, finally, can demand the map. Ask your council member which blocks in your district produce the most shootings, what is being done there, and how anyone will know whether it worked. Ray has started bringing a printed list of dates and incidents to every community meeting. It is a small act. It is also the beginning of the record that institutions keep failing to build for themselves.

The takeaway

Much of the violence that shocks us was announced in advance, by a tip, a pattern or a corner that never changed. Treat warnings as duties, fund the programs that work for long enough to work, and make the map of danger public.

Chapter 20: America's Punishment Culture and the Endless Losing Wars on Crime

"So which war are we in now?" Luis asked, snapping the cape off a customer and shaking the hair onto the floor. The television above the mirrors was running a press conference, a mayor at a podium flanked by officers, promising a crackdown on something. Luis has cut hair on the same corner for more than thirty years and runs a free summer program for neighborhood kids out of the back room. He has heard the speech before. "When I was a kid it was drugs," he said. "Then gangs. Now it's people sleeping outside. Nobody ever tells you who won."

Luis is a composite of several community leaders I have known, but his question is the one I hear most often in barbershops, church halls and union halls around the country. It is a better question than most politicians ask. America declares wars on social problems, fights them with police and prisons, loses them, and then funds them forever. The losses are visible in every neighborhood like his. The accountability is invisible. Nobody resigns when a war fails. Nobody audits the strategy. The next official simply declares a new one.

I have covered the front lines of several of these wars, and I want to make an argument that may sound extreme at first. The failure to win them is bad. The fact that no one answers for losing them may be worse than the crime they were meant to stop, because a government that cannot be held accountable for failure will keep failing, at any price, indefinitely.

The Birth of Punishment Culture

Every culture punishes. What sets the United States apart is how heavily it leans on punishment as the answer to almost everything. In the early 1970s, American prisons and jails held a few hundred thousand people. By the late 2000s, the total had climbed past two million, giving the country one of the highest incarceration rates in the world. Crime rose and fell during those decades. Incarceration climbed almost without pause, for reasons that had as much to do with politics as with crime.

The turn came out of real fear. Crime did rise sharply in the 1960s and 1970s, and voters in cities and suburbs demanded action. Politicians in both parties learned that promising toughness won elections and that any talk of causes or treatment could be painted as weakness. Sentencing laws grew longer. Discretion moved from judges to prosecutors. Parole was abolished or narrowed in many states. Each step was defensible on its own terms. Together they built a machine that ran on one fuel.

In June 1971, President Nixon declared drug abuse public enemy number one, and two years later New York passed the Rockefeller drug laws, named for Governor Nelson Rockefeller. They imposed mandatory prison terms of fifteen years to life for selling or possessing relatively small amounts of narcotics, with little room for judges to consider circumstances. Other states and Washington followed with mandatory minimums of their own. The war metaphor stuck, and with it the assumption that the right response to any social problem was an enemy, an army and a victory parade that never came.

The "War on Drugs" — A Case Study in Failure

Measure the drug war by its own goals and the verdict is hard to escape. The stated aim was to reduce drug use and the harms that come with it. Fifty years and, by widely cited estimates, hundreds of billions to more than a trillion dollars later, illegal drugs remain widely available, often cheaper and more potent than before. Overdose deaths climbed past one hundred thousand a year in the early 2020s, a toll beyond anything the drug warriors of the 1970s imagined, before beginning to fall more recently.

Meanwhile the costs landed on the people least able to bear them. Millions of arrests for possession produced criminal records that blocked jobs, housing and student aid. Enforcement fell disproportionately on Black and Latino neighborhoods, even though national surveys have long found drug use at broadly similar rates across racial groups. Families lost parents to long sentences for nonviolent offenses. The arrests did not dry up demand. They created vacancies in the trade that new sellers filled within days.

New York eventually admitted the Rockefeller laws had failed and rolled back much of them in reforms in 2004 and 2009. California voters, after years of overcrowding, also changed course. Under the state's three strikes law, passed in 1994, Gary Ewing received a sentence of twenty-five years to life for stealing three golf clubs from a pro shop, given his prior serious felony convictions. In Ewing v. California in 2003, the Supreme Court upheld the sentence by a five-to-four vote, holding that it did not violate the Eighth Amendment ban on cruel and unusual punishment. Legislatures, the Court reasoned, could choose to incapacitate repeat offenders.

The bill for that philosophy came due in court. In Brown v. Plata in 2011, the Supreme Court, again five to four, upheld an order requiring California to cut its prison population by tens of thousands. The prisons had been running at close to double their design capacity, and the Court found that overcrowding was causing medical and mental health care so poor that it violated the Constitution. A year later, California voters passed Proposition 36, narrowing three strikes so that most life terms required a serious or violent third offense. The war had not been won. It had simply become unaffordable and unconstitutional.

The "War on Gangs" — Reactive, Not Strategic

The gang war followed the same script. In 1988, California passed its Street Terrorism Enforcement and Prevention Act, adding penalties for gang-related crime, and states and cities across the country built gang units, gang enhancements and gang databases. The strategy was mostly reactive. A shooting would spark a crackdown, the crackdown would sweep up dozens of young men, and within months the same streets would produce new conflicts and new names.

Gang databases show how little thought went into the tools. In 2016, California's state auditor examined CalGang, the statewide gang database, and found serious flaws, including forty-two people entered as gang members before their first birthday and records kept far beyond the legal limits. Agencies could not justify many of the entries the auditor reviewed. Being listed could follow a young person into court, into sentencing and into job searches, often without his knowledge. The state passed reforms after the audit, which tells you the problem was real.

Gang violence is a real danger, and I have sat with too many mothers of murdered teenagers to pretend otherwise. The failure of the gang war was not that it took violence seriously. It was that it treated every young man in a neighborhood as a suspect while neglecting the strategies that actually reduce group violence,, the focused and sustained partnerships between police, outreach workers and community leaders that have cut group shootings where cities stuck with them. Reaction replaced strategy, and arrests replaced results.

The "War on Homelessness" — Criminalizing Poverty

The newest war is aimed at people with nowhere to live. Cities across the country have passed bans on sleeping, camping or sitting in public spaces, enforced with tickets, sweeps and jail. In City of Grants Pass v. Johnson in 2024, the Supreme Court ruled six to three that enforcing such camping bans does not violate the Eighth Amendment, even against people who have no shelter available. The decision overturned an earlier Ninth Circuit rule and freed cities across the West to punish public sleeping.

Grants Pass settled a legal question. It did not settle whether punishment works. A ticket for sleeping outside cannot be paid by someone with no income. A night in jail ends with the person back on the same street, now with a record that makes housing and work harder to find. Sweeps destroy tents, identification documents and medications, and the people move a few blocks away. Homeowners and business owners have every right to want sidewalks clear and parks safe. The question is whether punishment delivers that, and the evidence so far suggests it mostly moves people around.

California shows the opposite failure, spending without accountability. A 2024 report by the state auditor found that California spent roughly twenty-four billion dollars on homelessness programs over five years and had not consistently tracked whether that money improved outcomes. Billions of dollars, poor measurement, no clear answer about what worked. Whether a government reaches for handcuffs or checkbooks, the pattern is the same when nobody is required to show results.

Why These Wars Cannot Be Won

These wars fail for a common reason. Each targets a symptom while ignoring a market or a cause. Drug demand persists, so arresting sellers simply opens jobs for new ones. Gangs form around young men with few prospects and long memories of violence, so sweeping them up without changing either produces more gangs. Homelessness grows out of housing costs, addiction and mental illness, so jailing homeless people treats the visible part of the problem and leaves the engine running.

A war also needs an enemy, and the enemy in these wars turns out to be fellow citizens. Once a problem is framed as a war, every failure can be blamed on not fighting hard enough. Rising overdoses become an argument for more arrests. A new shooting becomes an argument for a bigger gang unit. More tents become an argument for more sweeps. The framing makes defeat impossible to acknowledge, because the only acceptable response to losing a war is to escalate it.

That is how punishment replaces thought. It offers an instant, visible answer that requires no diagnosis. A legislator who votes for a longer sentence can describe the vote in a single sentence on a campaign flyer. A legislator who votes for addiction treatment, supportive housing or violence intervention has to explain evidence, timelines and uncertain results. Punishment wins the argument because it is simpler, not because it works. Over decades, entire institutions stop asking whether a policy reduces harm and ask only whether it punishes enough.

Unaccountability and the Failure Loop

Here is the loop. A crisis arrives. Leaders declare war and pour money into enforcement. Results fail to arrive, but nobody measures them carefully, because enforcement budgets are rarely tied to outcomes and audits are rare. The agencies that received the money argue they need more. Politicians who funded the war cannot admit it failed without admitting their own judgment was wrong. So the war is renewed, often with a larger budget, and the cycle starts again.

Huge budgets with no results and no audits are not an accident. Police and corrections spending in most states and cities is approved line by line, year after year, with little requirement to show that the money reduced crime. Failure gets re-funded because the people who would have to admit it are the same people who approve the next budget, and because the constituencies that benefit, from prison builders to unions to contractors, show up at every hearing while the people harmed rarely do.

The costs are staggering in money alone. The country spends tens of billions of dollars a year on corrections. Every dollar spent warehousing a nonviolent offender is a dollar not spent on detectives to solve murders, on schools, on treatment or on the prevention programs that actually reduce violence. Taxpayers pay that bill without ever seeing the invoice for what the money failed to buy.

California's three strikes era makes the arithmetic concrete. Every man serving twenty-five years to life for a petty theft cost the state tens of thousands of dollars a year to house, and the cost climbs as prisoners age and need medical care. Multiply that across thousands of life terms for nonviolent third strikes and the price of a single slogan runs into billions over the decades. Those dollars bought very little public safety that shorter sentences and supervision would not have bought more cheaply.

The human costs are larger. Children grow up with parents in prison. Neighborhoods lose working-age men to cells and return them years later with records and few prospects. Victims of serious crime wait for justice while the system spends its energy on possession arrests and camping tickets. Communities learn that government arrives mainly to punish, which corrodes the trust that solving real crime depends on.

I have watched the same official stand at a podium twice, a decade apart, announcing the same crackdown on the same problem in nearly the same words, with no mention of what happened to the first one. Nobody in the room asked. The press release went out, the cameras moved on, and the budget line grew. That moment, repeated in city halls across the country, is the failure loop in its purest form.

Why This Is Bigger Than Crime

My argument, then, is that the problem is bigger than crime. Crime harms its victims directly and terribly. A government that cannot be held accountable for failure harms everyone, quietly and continuously, and it disables the very tools a democracy uses to correct its mistakes. When no one answers for losing a war, the voters lose their power to change course. Officials learn that failure carries no penalty. Citizens learn that their votes and their taxes change nothing. A country that accepts that bargain will eventually accept almost anything.

The danger is not abstract. An unaccountable government grows comfortable with secrecy, because secrecy protects failing programs from scrutiny. It grows comfortable with spending, because spending signals action. It grows comfortable with punishing its weakest citizens, because they rarely vote, lobby or sue. Luis put it more simply than I can. "They don't fight the problem," he told me. "They fight us, and then they send us the bill."

How to End the Losing Wars

The first fix is to make every war prove itself. Legislatures can attach sunset clauses to major criminal justice programs and sentencing enhancements, so that they expire unless independent evaluators show they reduced harm. They can require outcome audits by state auditors or inspectors general, published in full, before any enforcement budget is renewed. California's own homelessness audit is a model of the question to ask, and every state should ask it of its prisons, gang units and drug task forces.

Next, move money to what works. Texas, under conservative leadership in 2007, chose to invest in treatment and probation alternatives rather than build new prisons it had been told it would need, and the state was able to close prisons in the years after. Houston, working across government and nonprofits, moved tens of thousands of homeless people into housing through a housing-first approach and cut its homeless population substantially over roughly a decade. Those results came from strategy, not slogans.

Honest experimentation comes third. Portugal decriminalized personal drug possession in 2001 and paired it with treatment, and has avoided the explosion in overdose deaths that some predicted. Oregon tried decriminalization in 2020 without building the treatment system first, then partly reversed it in 2024 after public frustration. Both lessons matter. Reform succeeds when it is planned, funded and measured, and it fails when it becomes a new slogan of its own.

Citizens carry the last fix. Ask your city council and your legislators a question every budget hearing should answer: what did last year's enforcement money achieve, and how do you know? Community leaders like Luis can bring that question to every candidate forum. Taxpayers can refuse to accept the language of war from any politician who cannot name what victory would look like.

The takeaway

America keeps declaring wars on crime that it cannot win and never has to explain losing. End the cycle by tying every enforcement dollar to measured results, shifting money to strategies that work, and refusing to re-elect anyone who cannot say what a war has achieved.

Chapter 21: Drunk Driving: The Preventable Epidemic America Refuses to Solve

It was a little after one in the morning, and the parking lot behind the bar was full of people fishing for their keys. I was standing beside an unmarked car with a Beverly Hills detective who had worked traffic and vice for years, and he was counting under his breath. A couple laughing too loudly. A man in a good suit who needed two tries to find his door handle. A young woman who sat in her driver's seat for a full minute with her forehead against the wheel, then started the engine. Nobody stopped any of them. Nobody, at that hour, was assigned to.

A Beverly Hills Detective's Estimate

When the lot had mostly emptied, I asked him how many of those drivers he thought were over the legal limit. He did not hesitate. In his estimate, roughly eight of every ten people who walk out of a busy bar at closing time are too impaired to drive legally. That is one experienced officer's judgment from years of watching the same doors, not a measured statistic, and I report it that way. Still, I have never met a bartender, a bouncer, or a patrol officer who told me he was wildly wrong.

What struck me that night was not the number. It was the geography. Every one of those drivers was visible, standing in one place, before the danger began. The bar knew who they were, because it had just served them. The parking lot had one exit. If you set out to design a choke point where impaired drivers could be identified and diverted with almost no effort, you could hardly do better than the door of a bar at last call.

The detective was not a crusader. He was tired, practical, and a little embarrassed by his own job. He told me that he could spend an entire shift chasing one weaving sedan through residential streets, file the arrest, and lose the rest of the night to booking, while the bar he had been watching kept pouring. To him, the absurdity was obvious. We pay an armed officer to wait at the far end of the risk, and we pay nobody at all to stand at the near end, where a single word and a phone call could end it.

Why We Wait Until They Are on the Road

America's answer to drunk driving is built around the moment after that door, not the moment at it. We pull people over once they are weaving. We set up checkpoints on highways. We arrest, test, book, and prosecute. The Supreme Court blessed sobriety checkpoints in Michigan Department of State Police v. Sitz in 1990, holding that the brief stop of every motorist at a roadblock was a reasonable intrusion given the scale of the danger. That ruling matters, because it shows the justices were willing to inconvenience the sober majority to catch the impaired few. They simply drew the line on the asphalt.

The law of testing reinforced that roadside focus. In Birchfield v. North Dakota in 2016, the Court held that police may require a breath test without a warrant after a drunk-driving arrest, but not a blood test, and that states cannot make it a crime to refuse the blood draw. It was a sensible privacy ruling. It also tells you where the entire legal machine is pointed. Every doctrine, every budget line, every training hour assumes the impaired driver is already behind the wheel and moving. By then, prevention has failed, and what remains is detection, chase, and paperwork, much of it performed after someone is hurt.

That machine runs at enormous scale. Police make something on the order of a million impaired-driving arrests a year, according to national crime reporting, and each one consumes hours of patrol time, lab time, court time, and jail space. Those arrests are not worthless. Some of them interrupt a crash that was minutes away. But a system that catches a million people after they start the engine is also a confession that it has let millions more drive off untouched, and that it has never seriously tried to reach them before they left the curb.

Thousands of Preventable Deaths Every Year

Federal crash data put the toll at roughly twelve thousand to thirteen and a half thousand deaths a year in crashes involving a driver over the legal limit, close to a third of all traffic fatalities. I have stood at the edges of those scenes. The flares, the folded sheet, the officer who cannot quite look at the family arriving in their bathrobes. The number is so steady from year to year that it has stopped sounding like news. A preventable cause that kills a full stadium section every month should never sound like weather.

Dana knows what the number looks like up close. Her younger brother was driving home from a late shift when a pickup crossed the center line. The other driver had closed out a tab two hours earlier at a place half a mile away, and he had walked past a doorman and a bartender to get to his truck. Dana sat through every hearing. What she says to me, every time we talk, is that the system was furious at the man afterward and indifferent to him before. Two people saw him leave. Neither had any duty, any tool, or any reason to stop him.

The worst crash of this kind in American history makes the same point. On May 14, 1988, a pickup driven by Larry Mahoney, who had been drinking heavily, went the wrong way on Interstate 71 near Carrollton, Kentucky, and struck a church bus carrying children and chaperones home from an amusement park. The bus caught fire. Twenty-seven people died, most of them children. Mahoney's blood alcohol was reported at well over twice Kentucky's limit, and a jury convicted him of second-degree manslaughter. The punishment was real. It did not bring back a single passenger.

Carrollton changed some things. Bus safety rules were tightened, fuel tanks were protected, emergency exits were added. Parents of children killed on that bus became some of the fiercest anti-drunk-driving advocates in the country, and one mother who lost her daughter that night later led MADD nationally. Every reform that came out of that fire was aimed at the bus or at the sentence. Almost nothing was aimed at the question that still haunts me when I reread the coverage: where did a man drink that much, and who watched him leave?

The Simple Alternatives We Refuse to Try

Bar responsibility is the first and oldest lever. Most states have dram-shop laws that let victims sue a business for serving a visibly intoxicated customer who then causes harm. In practice they are weak deterrents, because proving what a server saw is hard, insurers settle quietly, and the lawsuits arrive years after the funeral. A dram-shop statute that bites only in civil court after a death is a funeral fund, not a safety program. The same principle, applied at the point of service with inspections and real license consequences, would change behavior overnight.

Mandatory key surrender is the second. Some bars and restaurants already run voluntary key-check programs, holding a patron's keys until the patron proves sober or leaves without the car. Making that a condition of a liquor license for establishments above a certain size would cost almost nothing. Bartenders I have interviewed say the hardest part of cutting someone off is that they have no backup. A house rule written into the license would give them one.

Proof of a safe ride home is the third: a designated driver, a paid ride, a hotel room, a friend's phone number on file. None of it requires new science. Server-training programs, which teach staff to spot intoxication and refuse service, already exist in many states and are mandatory in some, so the workforce is half-ready. It requires a bouncer with authority and an owner who knows the license depends on it.

Technology covers the rest. Ride-share apps have made a sober ride a two-minute tap in most cities, and some research has found drops in impaired crashes where those services expanded, though the evidence varies by city. Ignition interlocks, which require a clean breath sample before a car will start, are now authorized in some form in every state for convicted offenders, and federal safety researchers credit them with substantially reducing repeat offenses while installed. Congress went further in the 2021 infrastructure law, directing federal regulators to require impaired-driving prevention technology in new cars. The deadline for a final rule passed without one.

The Cultural Dead End

So why does none of this happen at scale? Part of the answer is that drinking is woven into American social life in a way few other risks are. We celebrate with it, mourn with it, close deals with it. Any proposal that touches the bar door is heard as an attack on that life, and the hospitality industry is very good at saying so. Lawmakers who would never vote against a seatbelt hesitate to vote against a happy hour. The bar owner is a constituent and a donor. The future victim is a stranger nobody has met yet.

Advertising does its part. Beer and liquor brands spend heavily to associate drinking with freedom, friendship, sport, and the open road, and then append a small line about drinking responsibly. I do not want to ban the ads. I want the country to notice the contradiction. An industry that profits from the last round has every incentive to keep the conversation focused on the individual driver's choices and away from the place where the drinks were sold, and for forty years it has largely succeeded.

There is also a stubborn myth that drunk driving is a character flaw rather than a predictable outcome of a system. We picture the reckless drunk, not the ordinary accountant who had four glasses of wine over dinner and felt fine. Candy Lightner founded Mothers Against Drunk Driving in 1980 after her thirteen-year-old daughter, Cari, was killed by a driver with prior drunk-driving arrests. MADD changed the country.
