It helped push the national drinking age to twenty-one and the legal limit down to 0.08 in every state. But the movement's greatest wins, by design, targeted punishment and the road.

How Punishment Culture Blocks Prevention

I do not fault grieving parents for wanting consequences. I have watched a sentencing where a mother read the names of everyone her son would never meet. The trouble is what our politics does with that grief. Punishment is easy to sell. A lawmaker can announce a tougher sentence on a courthouse step with the family behind him. A requirement that bars verify rides home produces no villain and no press conference, only a quieter Saturday night and a dead statistic nobody notices is missing.

Punishment also comes too late by its nature. It acts on people who have already driven, already crashed, already been caught. Most impaired drivers are never stopped on any given night, which means the threat of a harsh sentence is distant and abstract to a man fumbling for his keys at one in the morning. The certainty of being stopped at the door would deter far more than the severity of a sentence he does not expect to face. We have built our response around the least effective lever because it is the most satisfying one to pull.

The Cost of Inaction

The bill for all this lands on people who never set foot in the bar. Federal estimates put the economic cost of alcohol-impaired crashes in the tens of billions of dollars a year, and far more once lost quality of life is counted. That money is emergency rooms, disability payments, wrecked public property, court time, jail beds, and insurance premiums raised for every careful driver in the state. A taxpayer who has never had a drink is still paying for the bar's profit margin on the last round.

Some costs never show up in any federal table. A family business that closes because its founder is in a wheelchair. Children raised by grandparents. A survivor like Dana, who still pulls over and shakes when headlights drift toward her lane. When I add up those losses against the modest inconvenience of checking a ride or holding a set of keys, the arithmetic is not close. We are choosing the expensive, bloody option, and we are choosing it every weekend, in every state.

Insurers understand this better than legislators. They price the risk of impaired driving into every policy they write, which means a homeowner in a quiet suburb is quietly subsidizing the crash at the interchange near the stadium. Employers absorb the lost workdays. Hospitals absorb the uninsured trauma cases and pass the cost along. Nobody votes on any of it. It simply arrives, folded into premiums and taxes, the invisible tariff we pay for refusing to stop a drunk at the door.

A Blueprint for Ending Drunk-Driving Deaths

Start with the license. A state legislature can make a liquor license above a set capacity conditional on a key-check or safe-ride program, inspected the way kitchens are inspected, with escalating fines and suspension for repeat failures. Pair that with real dram-shop exposure and mandatory server training, which many states already require in some form. City councils can fund late-night ride vouchers in entertainment districts, a cost that is trivial next to one fatal crash. Several cities and universities have run free or discounted late-night ride programs, and they are cheap to pilot and easy to measure.

Lower the line. Utah dropped its limit to 0.05 at the end of 2018, the first state to do so, and a federal evaluation found its fatal crash rate fell afterward while the sky did not. Other states should follow. Police chiefs can shift some patrol time from random highway stops to visible presence near clusters of bars at closing time, where the risk is concentrated and predictable. Courts can require interlocks on first offenses, not only repeat ones, as a growing number of states now do. And Congress can insist that federal regulators finish the vehicle technology rule the 2021 law already demands.

None of this requires abandoning enforcement. Keep the checkpoints, keep the arrests, keep the interlocks for people who are caught. What changes is the order of operations. The cheapest life to save is the one that never gets into the car, and every dollar spent at the door relieves pressure on patrol, prosecutors, courts, and jails downstream. A police chief who can show a council that fewer officers are needed on the highway at two in the morning because the bars are doing their part has found that rare thing in public safety, a reform that pays for itself.

Citizens have a role too, and it starts small. Take the keys. Order the ride for the friend who insists he is fine. Ask your favorite bar whether it holds keys, and tell the owner you will choose places that do. Show up at a city council meeting when an entertainment district is being licensed and ask what the safe-ride plan is. These are not heroic acts. They are the ordinary habits of a country that has decided the bar door, not the hospital, is where this story should end.

The takeaway

Drunk driving is not a mystery or an accident of fate. The danger walks out of a known door at a known hour, and we wait until it is moving at sixty miles an hour to act. Move the intervention to the door, through licenses, keys, rides, and car technology, and thousands of families each year never learn the names of the people who would have killed their children.

Chapter 22: Rodney King, Breonna Taylor, and George Floyd: Lessons America Refused to Learn

The most important police reform law passed in response to the Rodney King beating was used, nearly three decades later, to write a plan for fixing the Minneapolis Police Department, and in 2025 the federal government asked a judge to throw that plan away. He did. Thirty-four years of outrage, three names known around the world, millions of people in the streets, and the country ended up close to where it began. I have covered all three of these moments. I want to explain how that happened, because it was not an accident.

The Scale of the Protests

Each of these deaths reached the public through a camera. In March 1991 a man named George Holliday filmed Los Angeles police officers beating Rodney King beside a freeway after a chase, and the footage ran on every network for weeks. In April 1992, a jury in Simi Valley acquitted the four officers charged in state court. Los Angeles burned for days. More than sixty people died, thousands were injured, and property damage ran past a billion dollars. A federal civil rights trial in 1993 later convicted two of the officers. By then the city had already learned what an acquittal could ignite.

The federal response to the unrest was military, not civic. The governor called in the National Guard, the president sent federal troops and federal officers, and order was restored within days. I remember the strange quiet afterward, the soldiers on corners in neighborhoods that had begged for years for a patrol car to answer a burglary call. The message residents took from that week was simple and bitter. When the city burned, help arrived in hours. When they had complained about the beatings, nothing had arrived at all.

Nothing prepared me for the summer of 2020, which dwarfed even that. George Floyd died on a Minneapolis street on May 25 while officer Derek Chauvin knelt on his neck for roughly nine and a half minutes, a scene recorded by a teenager on her phone. Within days there were marches in every state and in cities around the world. Polls taken that summer suggested that well over fifteen million Americans had joined a protest, which would make it one of the largest movements in the nation's history. Breonna Taylor's name, already months old, was carried on signs beside his.

That delay matters. Taylor was killed in March, and for weeks her death was a local story, covered by Louisville reporters and pressed by her family and their lawyers, while the national press looked elsewhere. It took a second death, on video, in another city, to make the country say her name. I have seen this pattern many times on the crime beat. A killing without footage is treated as an allegation. A killing with footage becomes a fact. That standard leaves every unfilmed victim waiting for a camera that never comes.

Marcus remembers the size of it more than anything. He runs a neighborhood association in a city that never makes the national news, and that June he helped organize a march that drew more people than his block club had members in twenty years. Retired schoolteachers, high school athletes, a few off-duty firefighters. They walked to city hall and read a list of demands into a microphone. He kept the list. Most of it, he tells me, is still a list. There was a civilian review board with subpoena power, a public database of officer discipline, a crisis team for mental health calls, and a promise that the next police contract would be negotiated in the open. The city created the crisis team. Everything else was referred to a committee.

National Outrage

The anger in 1992 and 2020 was not only about violence. It was about the verdicts, the reports, and the silence that came after. In the King case, the Christopher Commission, appointed after the beating, found that a relatively small group of Los Angeles officers drew a hugely disproportionate share of excessive-force complaints, and that the department rarely disciplined them. The commission also documented racist and violent messages officers had typed into patrol car computers. Its report was blunt enough to help push Chief Daryl Gates into retirement. The rank and file stayed largely the same.

Breonna Taylor's case turned the anger toward process itself. In March 2020 Louisville officers forced their way into her apartment after midnight on a narcotics warrant. Her boyfriend, Kenneth Walker, believing intruders were breaking in, fired once and struck an officer. Police answered with dozens of rounds, and Taylor, a twenty-six-year-old emergency medical technician, was killed in her hallway. No drugs were found. A state grand jury charged only one officer, Brett Hankison, and only for shots that went into a neighbor's apartment. A state jury acquitted him of that charge in 2022.

The federal government later charged several officers, including over the falsified paperwork behind the warrant. Hankison's first federal trial ended in a hung jury. In 2024 a second jury convicted him of violating Taylor's civil rights through excessive force, for firing blindly through a covered window and door. In 2025, the Justice Department asked the judge to give him essentially no prison time, and she refused, sentencing him to thirty-three months. He appealed. That sequence, a police killing, a single officer convicted of a narrow count years later, and his own prosecutors arguing for leniency, is what outrage looks like when it has nowhere to go.

Floyd's case produced the clearest legal result of the three. In April 2021 a Hennepin County jury convicted Chauvin of second-degree murder, third-degree murder, and second-degree manslaughter, and he was sentenced to twenty-two and a half years. He later pleaded guilty to federal civil rights charges, and the three other officers at the scene were convicted federally as well. I sat with people outside that courthouse who wept when the verdict came. Several told me the same thing, almost word for word. They had not expected it.

Institutional Deafness

After each of these moments, institutions did what they do best. They formed a commission. They issued a report. They promised training. Then they waited. Police unions negotiated contracts that kept disciplinary records sealed or purged and gave officers long delays before interviews. Arbitrators reinstated officers fired for misconduct. Prosecutors who worked beside police every day declined to charge them. None of this required a conspiracy. It required only that the people with power outlast the people with grievances, and in America the people with power usually have pensions and the people with grievances usually have jobs to get back to.

Even the basic facts stayed hidden. After 2020, the country still had no complete national count of people killed or seriously injured by police. The FBI launched a voluntary use-of-force data collection in 2019, and for years so few agencies participated that the bureau could not publish much of it. Newspapers and independent researchers built their own databases from news reports because the government would not. When a nation cannot say how often something happens, it cannot say whether reform is working, and the institutions resisting reform know that perfectly well.

I have heard the good-faith version of this argument from chiefs I respect. They say most officers never fire their weapons, that viral videos are a sliver of millions of daily encounters, that sweeping federal mandates can ignore local conditions. Some of that is true. Most officers do get through careers without a scandal. But the deafness I am describing is not about the average officer. It is about the system's refusal to identify and remove the small number of officers the Christopher Commission found in 1991, and that Minneapolis and Louisville investigators found again three decades later.

Both cities were investigated after 2020. In 2023 the Justice Department reported that Minneapolis police had engaged in a pattern of excessive force and racial discrimination, and it issued similar findings about Louisville. A Minnesota state agency had already found a pattern of race discrimination in the Minneapolis department, and the city signed a court-enforceable agreement with the state. Consent decrees with the federal government followed in the last days of one administration. In May 2025, under the next, the Justice Department moved to dismiss both, and the Minneapolis judge dismissed that case with prejudice.

The Opportunity for Reform

It is worth remembering how close the country came. After King, Congress gave the Justice Department power in the 1994 crime bill to sue police departments for patterns of unconstitutional conduct, the tool later used in cities from Los Angeles to Chicago. Los Angeles itself operated under a federal consent decree after the Rampart corruption scandal, and by the time a judge lifted it in 2013, outside studies found that public approval of the department had risen and that force incidents had declined. Oversight can work when somebody stays long enough to enforce it.

Los Angeles voters did their part as well. In 1992 they approved a charter amendment, drawn from the Christopher Commission's recommendations, that ended the chief's lifetime civil service protection and limited future chiefs to fixed terms, while strengthening civilian control of the department. It was not glamorous. It did not trend. It simply changed who answered to whom, and that kind of structural change is precisely what outlives a news cycle. The commission understood something the country keeps forgetting: a reform that depends on the goodwill of the person it constrains is not a reform at all.

The George Floyd Justice in Policing Act, passed by the House in 2021, would have limited qualified immunity, banned chokeholds and many no-knock warrants at the federal level, and created a national registry of police misconduct. Bipartisan Senate negotiations stretched for months and collapsed that fall over qualified immunity and other provisions. A narrower executive order in 2022 required federal agencies to adopt some of those standards and to build a misconduct database. In January 2025, that order was rescinded. The federal opportunity, for now, is gone.

States and cities moved further than Washington, and they deserve credit. Louisville banned no-knock warrants within weeks of Taylor's death, in an ordinance that carries her name, and the city paid her family twelve million dollars in a settlement that also included reform commitments. Several states restricted chokeholds, expanded their power to strip certification from officers fired for misconduct, or opened disciplinary records to the public. Minneapolis said it would carry out the reforms in its rejected decree anyway, and its state agreement remains in force. Those are real gains. They are also scattered, uneven, and easy to reverse.

Why Nothing Changed

The honest answer is that outrage has a half-life and institutions do not. A protest movement is built from volunteers, and volunteers go home. A police union, a prosecutor's office, a city attorney defending lawsuits, and a legislature with a dozen other priorities are permanent. They can wait three years for a trial, five years for an appeal, and one election for a change of administration. Every reform that depended on sustained pressure from the street faded when the street emptied. Every reform written into law with an enforcement mechanism has survived longer.

I watched this play out at Marcus's city council a year after his march. The chamber that had overflowed in June held eleven people in the following spring, most of them city staff. The contract vote took four minutes. A union lawyer spoke, a council member thanked the officers for their service, and the agreement passed with the same disciplinary protections it had carried for a decade. Marcus was one of the eleven. He was the only resident who stood up to speak, and he was given two minutes.

There is a second answer, and it is uncomfortable. Many Americans watched these videos and concluded that the problem was a few bad men rather than the rules that protected them. Convict Chauvin, and the system has worked. Fire the officer, and the case is closed. That instinct lets everyone else off the hook, including the arbitrator who reinstates, the supervisor who ignores the complaint file, and the council that signs the contract. Marcus put it to me plainly. The city punished one man, he said, and then asked the neighborhood to stop talking about it.

The third answer is money and fear. When crime rose in many cities after 2020, the political appetite for police reform collapsed, and leaders who had promised change began promising more officers instead. Those two goals were never really opposites. A department that removes its worst officers and earns its residents' trust solves more crimes, not fewer. But in a frightened city, reform sounds like weakness, and the moment for it was allowed to pass, the same way it passed in 1992.

Making Reform Stick

The lesson of these three cases is that reform must be built to outlast the news cycle. State legislatures can do that by writing the core rules into law rather than policy: limits on no-knock warrants, bans on chokeholds except where deadly force is justified, duty-to-intervene requirements, and public access to sustained misconduct findings. They can create independent statewide prosecutors for police killings, so the local prosecutor never has to charge a colleague. Several states have moved in that direction, assigning police-involved deaths to the attorney general's office.

Congress can still act without waiting for a grand bargain. A national, mandatory registry of officers decertified for misconduct would stop the familiar pattern of an officer fired in one county and hired in the next. Tying federal policing grants to complete use-of-force reporting would finally produce the numbers the country lacks. Neither idea requires settling the bitter fight over qualified immunity, and both have drawn support from police leaders as well as reform advocates. The obstacle has never been the policy. It has been the willingness to finish the job after the cameras leave.

City councils hold the second lever, and it is the police contract. Councils can refuse to sign agreements that purge disciplinary records, delay interviews, or send firings to arbitrators who routinely reinstate. They can fund early-warning systems that flag officers with clusters of complaints, the very group the Christopher Commission identified. Police chiefs who want to clean house can ask for exactly those tools, and the best ones already do. Courts can make settlement payouts public and require cities to report how often the same officers appear in them.

Citizens like Marcus are the third lever. Keep the list of demands, and turn it into a scorecard. Attend the contract hearing, which happens on a weekday afternoon when nobody is watching. Run for the civilian oversight board, or demand that one with subpoena power be created. Vote in the prosecutor's race, which decides more about police accountability than any presidential election. The street got the country's attention three times. Only the boring rooms where contracts and budgets are written will make the change permanent.

The takeaway

Rodney King, Breonna Taylor, and George Floyd each gave America a moment to fix what was broken, and each time the institutions simply waited for the anger to fade. Reform that survives is reform written into law, contracts, and budgets, with someone permanent assigned to enforce it, so the next outrage finds a system already changed. The protests proved the country can care. What it has never yet proved is that it can keep caring after the crowd goes home.

Chapter 23: Racism in Law Enforcement and the Destruction of Public Trust

Why would anyone refuse to help the police catch a killer on their own street? I have heard that question from detectives, from suburban readers, from cable hosts who think the answer is obvious. Witnesses go quiet, they say, because of a street code, because of fear of retaliation, because people in rough neighborhoods do not care. Some of that is real. Retaliation is real. But in my years on the crime beat, the most common reason I have heard is simpler, and it has nothing to do with gangs.

Loretta owns a brick house with a deep porch on a corner she has lived on for thirty-one years. She has called the police more times than she can count. Twice, officers who arrived treated her son, home from college, as the suspect on his own front steps. One of those times he spent forty minutes in the back of a cruiser while they ran his name. When a young man was shot dead across the street last spring, Loretta saw the car and the shooter. She told no one in uniform.

That is the answer. People stop helping police when police have taught them that contact is dangerous. Bias in law enforcement does not only wrong the person who is stopped, searched, or beaten. It destroys the cooperation that solves crime, and the bill for that destruction comes due in unsolved murders, in every neighborhood where it has happened, for everyone who lives there.

Historical Roots

The suspicion Loretta carries did not begin with her son. In much of the South, early policing grew alongside slave patrols, armed groups charged with catching people who escaped bondage and suppressing rebellion. Historians have traced how, after emancipation, Black Codes and vagrancy laws turned ordinary life into a crime, and local police enforced them. Through the Jim Crow decades, officers enforced segregation and often stood aside during lynchings. In 1965, state troopers beat peaceful marchers on the Edmund Pettus Bridge in Selma in front of news cameras. That is not ancient history. Many people who watched it are still alive.

Northern cities were never as innocent as they liked to believe. Black families who moved north during the Great Migration found segregated housing, enforced by restrictive covenants, real estate practices, and sometimes by mobs, and they often found police who treated their arrival as a public order problem. Riots in Detroit, Harlem, and elsewhere in the twentieth century frequently began with a police encounter that residents saw as one insult too many. Generations of grandparents passed those memories down. When a young officer today wonders why a polite request is met with a cold stare, part of the answer is that family memory runs long.

The federal government studied the problem and named it plainly more than half a century ago. After the urban uprisings of 1967, President Lyndon Johnson appointed the Kerner Commission to explain why American cities were burning. Its 1968 report pointed to white racism, segregation, and poverty, and it identified aggressive policing and the deep hostility between Black residents and the police as a recurring spark. The commission warned that the country was drifting toward two separate and unequal societies. Johnson, unhappy with its conclusions, largely shelved it.

Modern Manifestations

What the Kerner Commission described in 1968, the Justice Department kept finding afterward. In 2015 it reported that Ferguson, Missouri, ran its police and municipal court as a revenue machine, and that Black residents bore a starkly disproportionate share of stops, citations, arrests, and force. In 2016, investigators found Baltimore officers had made huge numbers of unconstitutional stops concentrated in Black neighborhoods. Chicago, Minneapolis, and Louisville drew similar findings. These were not activist pamphlets. They were federal reports built on the departments' own records, and they read like a single document written over and over in different cities.

The traffic stop is where most Americans meet a police officer, and the data there are consistent. Researchers at Stanford who gathered records on tens of millions of stops across the country found that Black drivers were stopped more often relative to their share of the population, and searched more often on weaker evidence, with searches turning up contraband less often than searches of white drivers. They also found that the share of Black drivers stopped tended to fall after dark, when officers could less easily see a driver's race. A disparity that shrinks when the lights go out is hard to explain any other way.

New York ran the largest experiment of all. At its peak in 2011, the city's police made several hundred thousand stop-and-frisk encounters in a single year, the great majority of them involving Black and Latino residents, and roughly nine in ten ended with no arrest or summons. In Floyd v. City of New York in 2013, a federal judge held that the department's practices violated the Fourth Amendment's protection against unreasonable searches and the Fourteenth Amendment's guarantee of equal protection. The ruling matters for a reason beyond the law. Stops fell dramatically afterward, and the city's crime kept falling for years.

Then there are the cases that are impossible to forget. On February 23, 2020, Ahmaud Arbery, a twenty-five-year-old Black man, was jogging through a neighborhood near Brunswick, Georgia, when three white men chased him down in pickup trucks. Travis McMichael shot him with a shotgun. His father, Gregory McMichael, was a former investigator for the local district attorney's office. For more than two months, no one was arrested. Only after video of the killing surfaced publicly and the state's bureau of investigation took over were the men charged.

A jury convicted all three men of murder in 2021, and all three received life sentences. A federal jury later convicted them of hate crimes. The local district attorney, Jackie Johnson, was indicted in 2021 on charges that she had used her office to shield the McMichaels. In 2025 a judge acquitted her of obstruction, saying prosecutors had offered no evidence she directed police not to arrest, and dismissed the remaining charge over defects in the indictment. Whatever one concludes about her, the official record shows a killing that went unanswered until a video forced the issue.

Community Harm

What does that history do to a neighborhood? It teaches residents to calculate every encounter. Parents in Black families across the country give their children a version of the same talk, how to keep their hands visible, how to say yes sir, how to survive a traffic stop. I have heard it in kitchens, in church basements, from fathers who are themselves police officers. A child who learns at nine that the people paid to protect him might hurt him does not grow up dialing 911 with confidence. He grows up deciding, case by case, whether the risk of calling is worth it.

Loretta's son is the kind of young man every city says it wants to keep. He studies engineering, coaches his little cousins, and comes home every summer to cut his grandmother's grass. The second time he was detained on his own steps, an officer told him he matched a description. The description, Loretta later learned, was a young Black man in a dark hoodie. He was wearing a gray one. Nobody apologized. When she filed a complaint, she received a form letter months later saying it had been reviewed. She keeps that letter in a drawer beside the deed to her house.

The journalist Jill Leovy, in her book about homicide in Los Angeles, described Black neighborhoods as both over-policed and under-protected. The phrase has stuck with me because I see it everywhere I report. Officers crowd the corners for minor stops, drug sweeps, and warrant checks, while serious crimes against Black victims go unsolved at lower rates. Residents feel watched and abandoned at the same time. Loretta put it more bluntly. The police come fast when they want somebody, she told me. They come slow when somebody needs them.

Good officers suffer too, and fairness requires saying so. I have known Black and white patrol officers who worked hard to earn trust in tough neighborhoods, coached ball teams, showed up at funerals, and knew every grandmother on their beat by name. They are often the first to say what bias costs them. One bad stop by someone else on the shift can undo a year of their work, and the department rarely treats that as a loss worth measuring.

The Cost of Bias

Lawsuits are the most visible bill. Big-city governments pay out enormous sums every year to settle police misconduct claims, and a meaningful share of those claims involve stops, searches, and force that fall heavily on minority residents. Taxpayers cover that money, often from the same budgets that would otherwise fund schools, parks, and the detective units that solve murders. Consent decrees and federal monitors add tens of millions more. A city that tolerates biased policing is not saving money. It is borrowing against its own future, at interest.

Homeowners pay a separate price. When residents lose faith that crime will be solved and that their children will be treated fairly, the families with options leave. The neighborhood loses the teachers, nurses, and small-business owners who anchor a block. Property values sag, the tax base shrinks, and the city has less to spend on the services that might have kept them. Loretta has watched four of her neighbors sell in the last decade. Every one of them, she says, mentioned the shootings, and more than one mentioned the police.

The less visible bill is in the case files. Nationally, roughly half of homicides end in an arrest, and in many cities the rate is far lower when the victim is Black. Investigations that reporters have pieced together from police records found clusters of neighborhoods where most killings go unsolved year after year. Every unsolved shooting leaves a shooter free, a family without an answer, and a neighborhood that has learned one more time that calling the police does not produce justice. That lesson compounds. It is the most expensive kind of debt a city can carry.

Officers themselves pay a cost that rarely makes the budget hearings. Detectives I have ridden with describe knocking on dozens of doors after a shooting and hearing the same nothing at every one. They know what is behind the silence. Some of them are furious about it, at the residents and at colleagues whose behavior earned it. Morale suffers, burnout rises, and good investigators leave for suburban departments where witnesses still answer the door. A department that tolerates bias ends up losing its best people along with its public's trust.

Trust Lost, Crime Increased

The link between lost trust and rising crime is not speculation. A widely cited study of Milwaukee found that after the public learned of the beating of a Black man named Frank Jude by off-duty officers, calls to 911 from Black neighborhoods dropped sharply for months. Other researchers later questioned the size of that effect, and the debate is worth having honestly. But the basic finding fits what I have seen in city after city. When a community's trust is broken publicly, the phones go quiet, and the people who benefit from that silence are the ones holding guns.

Witnesses are the foundation of almost every solved violent crime. Detectives need someone to say what they saw, to identify a car, to testify months later in a courtroom across from the defendant's family. Without that cooperation, cases collapse, and shooters learn that they can act with little risk. Retaliatory violence fills the gap the courts leave, because people who do not trust the police settle things themselves. The cycle is brutally simple. Bias drives away witnesses, missing witnesses produce unsolved crimes, and unsolved crimes produce more crime. Loretta's silence is a link in that chain.

I want to be fair to the departments here. Some witness silence comes from fear of the shooter, not distrust of the police, and no reform will erase that entirely. But the two fears feed each other. A resident who believes the police will protect her identity, take her seriously, and treat her son decently is far more willing to risk retaliation than one who expects to be ignored or suspected. Trust does not make witnesses brave. It gives them a reason to believe that bravery will be worth something.

Rebuilding Trust That Solves Crimes

Trust can be rebuilt, and some places have shown how. After the 2001 police shooting of an unarmed Black teenager, Timothy Thomas, set off days of unrest in Cincinnati, the city, the police union, and community plaintiffs signed a collaborative agreement that committed the department to problem-solving policing, data reporting, and outside review. It was imperfect and it took years, but it is widely credited with reducing force and improving relations. The lesson is that every party, including the union, signed it. Reform imposed on a department rarely lasts as long as reform it helped write.

New York's experience after the stop-and-frisk ruling points the same way. Pulling back from mass stops did not unleash the crime wave its defenders predicted, which suggests the stops were costing trust without buying much safety. A city that wants to know which of its tactics actually prevent violence has to be willing to stop the ones that do not, even when they are popular with the officers who use them and with the residents who never feel them.

Legislatures can make the disparities visible and enforceable. California and Connecticut, among other states, require agencies to collect and publish detailed traffic stop data by race, which lets the public see which departments and which officers are outliers. Philadelphia passed a law limiting stops for minor equipment violations like a single broken taillight, and Virginia restricted several low-level pretextual stops. Those stops produce few seizures and enormous resentment. Ending them frees officers for serious crime and removes thousands of hostile encounters a year.

Police chiefs can treat bias as a performance failure, not a public relations problem. That means early-warning systems that flag officers whose stops or force reports diverge sharply from their peers, supervisors who act on those flags, and promotions tied to clearance rates rather than ticket counts. Prosecutors can refuse to bring cases built on illegal stops. City councils can fund victim services and witness protection, because a witness who feels safe is a witness who testifies. And residents like Loretta can be invited into the work, through civilian oversight boards with real authority, not hearings designed to let people vent and go home.

The takeaway

Racism in policing is not only an injustice to the people it targets. It is a public safety failure, because it drives away the witnesses every solved case depends on, and it leaves killers free in the neighborhoods that need protection most. Measure the bias, end the stops that produce it, reward officers who earn trust, and the phones in places like Loretta's corner will start ringing again.

Chapter 24: How Law Enforcement Eroded the First Amendment — and Nearly All the Others

In August 1963, about a quarter of a million people gathered on the National Mall for the March on Washington. Police officers in ordinary uniforms directed traffic and stood along the edges of the crowd. Fifty-one years later, in August 2014, a few hundred people gathered on a suburban street in Ferguson, Missouri, after a police officer shot and killed an eighteen-year-old named Michael Brown. They were met by armored vehicles, officers in camouflage aiming rifles from the tops of trucks, and clouds of tear gas. The protest was smaller by a factor of hundreds. The response looked like a war.

Militarized Policing

The distance between those two summers was not an accident of local temperament. It was policy. In 1963 most American police departments owned patrol cars, revolvers, and nightsticks. By 2014 many owned mine-resistant vehicles built for Iraq and Afghanistan, military rifles, night-vision gear, and grenade launchers for chemical munitions. Much of that equipment came free from the Pentagon through the 1033 program, which Congress expanded in the 1990s to transfer surplus military property to local law enforcement. Over the years it moved billions of dollars of gear into towns that had never seen a tank outside a parade.

In 1963 the march had a permit, a plan, and a police force that treated the crowd as citizens to be protected. The street in Ferguson had a police force that treated the crowd as a hostile population to be controlled. Two reporters working in a McDonald's nearby were arrested in those first days. Officers pointed weapons at people with their hands raised. Television viewers watched images that looked exactly like the foreign conflicts the equipment had been built for. The tools had changed, and the mindset had come with them.

Presidents have argued about this ever since. After Ferguson, one administration restricted transfers of the most military items. The next rescinded those limits. Another restored many of them by executive order in 2022, and in 2025 the order was revoked again and a new one directed agencies to expand the flow of military equipment to local police. The equipment comes with training cultures that teach officers to see themselves as soldiers. Researchers who have tracked SWAT deployments found they grew from a few thousand a year in the early 1980s to many tens of thousands, most of them to serve drug search warrants rather than to stop hostage-takers or active shooters.

Does any of it make the public safer? The best evidence says not much. A study published in 2018 in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences examined SWAT deployments in Maryland and found no detectable reduction in violent crime or gains in officer safety from more militarized policing, while showing that images of militarized officers lowered public trust and support for police funding. In 1963, the officers on the Mall understood something those numbers confirm. A crowd treated as citizens tends to behave like citizens. A crowd treated as an enemy remembers it for a generation.

Suppression of Protest

Rick has spent his retirement reading things other people skip. He kept books for a small manufacturer for thirty years, and now he reads his city's budget line by line, the way some men read box scores. A few years ago he noticed a new category of spending, settlement payments for lawsuits over the city's handling of protests in 2020. He had not marched. He had no strong feelings about the marchers. He simply wanted to know why he, a taxpayer, was paying for decisions made by officers who would never see the bill.

Rick's city was hardly alone. In 2020, police across the country answered racial justice protests with tear gas, pepper balls, rubber bullets, and mass arrests, often against crowds that were overwhelmingly peaceful. In Denver, a federal jury in 2022 awarded fourteen million dollars to protesters who said officers had used excessive force and violated their free speech rights. New York City agreed in 2023 to pay millions to settle a class action over its tactics, including trapping marchers between lines of officers before arresting them. Portland paid to settle a suit over its crowd-control weapons and accepted a court injunction limiting their use.

Federal agents joined the crackdown. In Portland that summer, officers from federal agencies, some without clear identification, pulled protesters into unmarked vehicles and used tear gas and impact munitions on crowds, prompting lawsuits from protesters, journalists, and legal observers. Press-freedom groups documented hundreds of assaults on journalists nationwide in 2020, many by police, along with well over a hundred arrests of reporters doing their jobs. I have covered protests for most of my career. I had never before seen so many colleagues carrying gas masks and helmets just to report on their own cities.

The suppression did not end when the crowds went home. Departments in several cities monitored activists' social media accounts, photographed marchers, and in some places ran images through facial recognition systems, practices that civil liberties groups uncovered through public records requests and lawsuits. A person who knows that attending a rally may land her face in a police database thinks twice about attending the next one. That hesitation is precisely the chilling effect the First Amendment was written to prevent, and it costs the government nothing to produce.

None of this means police face an easy task at a protest. Some 2020 demonstrations turned violent, businesses burned, and officers were injured. A commander who must protect lives and property in a chaotic crowd deserves some understanding. But the documented pattern was not careful targeting of people throwing rocks. It was force applied to whole crowds, to medics, to reporters, and to people standing still. When a city answers peaceful speech with chemical weapons, it is not keeping order. It is teaching citizens that the right to assemble is something the police permit.

Speech, Assembly, and Search

The First Amendment promises the right to speak, to publish, and to assemble peaceably. In practice, those rights now depend heavily on how a commander decides to classify a crowd. Once police declare an unlawful assembly, often through a loudspeaker few people can hear, everyone present becomes subject to dispersal orders, arrest, and force. Protest zones are fenced and permitted. Journalists are told to leave with everyone else. The right has not been repealed. It has simply been converted into a privilege granted at an officer's discretion and revoked the same way.

Search and seizure protections have fared worse. Its protection against unreasonable searches and seizures has been worn down by decades of doctrine that gives officers wide latitude to stop drivers for any minor violation, ask for consent to search, and seize property they suspect is tied to crime. Civil asset forfeiture lets agencies take cash and cars without convicting anyone. In one case out of Fresno, California, a federal appeals court in 2019 granted officers qualified immunity against a claim that they had stolen a large sum of cash and rare coins they had seized under a warrant, reasoning that no prior ruling had clearly established such a theft was unconstitutional.

Due process erodes in the same way. People arrested at protests are often held overnight and released without charges, their records marked, their phones sometimes searched. Officers who lie in reports face little risk when the cases are dropped before trial. The Constitution's protections were written to be enforced against the government. When the agents of government can violate them with little chance of consequence, the protections still exist on paper, but they stop doing the work they were written to do.

The Court has shown it can draw firm lines when it chooses. In Tennessee v. Garner in 1985, it held that police may not use deadly force to stop a fleeing suspect unless the officer has probable cause to believe the suspect poses a significant threat of death or serious injury. The case involved a fifteen-year-old boy shot in the back of the head while running from a burglary with a purse and a few dollars. That ruling changed police practice across the country. It proves the Constitution can still restrain force, if judges are willing to say clearly what it forbids.

Violence Against Citizens

The legal standard for police force tells the story. In Graham v. Connor in 1989, the Supreme Court held that excessive-force claims must be judged by whether an officer's actions were objectively reasonable from the perspective of a reasonable officer on the scene, allowing for split-second judgments in tense situations. The case began with Dethorne Graham, a diabetic man having an insulin reaction, who was thrown onto the hood of a car and injured. The standard is defensible in principle. In practice, courts and juries have read it so generously that almost any force can be framed as reasonable after the fact.

Blood was spilled long before Ferguson. On May 4, 1970, Ohio National Guard troops fired into a crowd of students protesting the Vietnam War at Kent State University, killing four and wounding nine. Some of the dead had been walking to class. Eleven days later, police fired on students at Jackson State College in Mississippi, killing two. Federal charges against several Kent State guardsmen were dismissed, and a civil suit was settled in 1979 with a modest payment and a statement of regret. No one went to prison for any of it.

Fifty years later, the images had changed but the pattern had not. In Buffalo in June 2020, officers clearing a square shoved a seventy-five-year-old protester named Martin Gugino, who fell backward and struck his head, bleeding on the pavement as the line of officers walked past. Two officers were charged with assault, and a grand jury declined to indict them. I watched that video many times. What stayed with me was not the push. It was the dozens of officers who stepped around him.

The violence reaches into homes as well as streets. In 2014, a SWAT team in Habersham County, Georgia, serving a drug warrant before dawn, threw a flash-bang grenade into a room where a toddler was sleeping in a playpen. The child, Bounkham Phonesavanh, was gravely burned and injured. The man police were seeking was not there. A federal grand jury indicted the deputy who sought the warrant, and a jury acquitted her. Local authorities later paid a settlement to the family. The raid tactic that maimed a baby remained legal, and in most of the country it remains routine.

The Absence of Consequences

Why does so little change? The answer lies largely in qualified immunity, a doctrine the Supreme Court built rather than one Congress wrote. In Harlow v. Fitzgerald in 1982, the Court held that government officials are shielded from civil damages unless they violate clearly established rights that a reasonable person would have known. Lower courts have since demanded that victims find a prior case with nearly identical facts. If no court has yet ruled that a specific act of force or theft was unconstitutional, the officer often walks away, and the next victim faces the same empty shelf.

The doctrine's roots run back to Pierson v. Ray in 1967, when the Court shielded police who had arrested Freedom Riders in Mississippi under a law later found unconstitutional, reasoning that officers acting in good faith should not be punished for enforcing a statute they believed valid. That argument had some force. But the modern version protects not only honest mistakes about unsettled law, it protects conduct any reasonable person would recognize as wrong. In 2020 the Supreme Court declined to take up a batch of petitions asking it to reconsider the doctrine, leaving the work to legislatures.

Criminal charges are rarer still. Prosecutors rely on police for every other case they bring, and grand juries hear the facts as prosecutors present them. Officers are seldom charged, and when they are, convictions are uncommon. Discipline is often softened by union contracts and arbitration. What remains is the civil settlement, paid by taxpayers like Rick, almost never by the officer. Rick added up his city's protest payouts over several years. The total, he told me, would have paid for a new branch library. Not one officer contributed a dollar.

Restoring Rights by Law

States do not have to wait for the Supreme Court. In 2020 Colorado passed a law letting people sue officers in state court for violating state constitutional rights, barring qualified immunity as a defense, and making officers personally liable for a small share of damages when they act in bad faith. New Mexico created a similar state civil rights claim in 2021, and New York City adopted a local right against unreasonable searches without the immunity shield. Other legislatures can copy those laws, and taxpayers should demand that they do. Colorado's law has not bankrupted its officers or emptied its departments, which answers the warning that such statutes would drive police out of the profession.

Protest policing can be written into law too. Several states and cities have restricted the use of tear gas and impact munitions against crowds, and California passed a law protecting journalists from being arrested or blocked for covering protests and from being targeted with crowd-control weapons. Courts can enforce injunctions like Portland's when departments stray, and federal judges have shown they are willing to hold cities in contempt. City councils can require dispersal orders to be audible and recorded, body cameras to be on during every crowd operation, and officers to wear visible identification at all times.

Militarization can be reversed at the local level regardless of who sits in the White House. City councils can require public approval before their police accept military equipment, a policy several cities have already adopted, and they can return gear they do not need. Police chiefs can train crowd commanders in de-escalation and reserve tactical units for genuine threats. Congress can attach conditions to the 1033 program that survive changes of administration. And citizens can do what Rick does: read the budget, total the settlements, and show up at the council meeting to ask who approved the tactics that produced them.

Rick did exactly that last year. He stood at the lectern with a single sheet of paper listing every protest settlement by date and amount, and he asked the council to require that future payouts be reported publicly alongside the names of the units involved. The motion did not pass the first time. It came back the next session with two cosponsors, and it passed. That is how rights come back: not in a single landmark ruling, but in a hundred small rules that make abuse visible and expensive to the people who can stop it.

The takeaway

Rights that can be suspended at an officer's discretion have stopped being rights. Militarized equipment, generous force standards, and qualified immunity have turned the First and Fourth Amendments into permissions, while taxpayers absorb the cost of every violation. States and cities can restore them now with laws that let citizens sue, limit force against crowds, and keep military gear off American streets.

Chapter 25: Judges: Out of Touch, Unaccountable, and Often Corrupt

"He didn't even look up," Teresa was saying, stirring a cup of coffee she never drank. "I read my statement, the whole thing, every word I practiced in the car. And he was writing something the whole time. When I finished, he said thank you, ma'am, and gave the man the exact deal the lawyers had worked out a month before." We were sitting in a diner across from the county courthouse, an hour after the sentencing. Teresa had been attacked in a parking garage two years earlier. She had expected the hearing to be the end of something. It felt, she said, like being dismissed by a stranger.

Judicial Isolation

I have sat in courtrooms like that one more times than I can count, and I have seen what Teresa saw. A judge on the bench is the most powerful person most citizens will ever face in public life. He can take their liberty, their children, their home, or their money, and he does it from behind a raised desk, in a robe, in a room built to signal that the rest of us are guests. Many judges are thoughtful and humane. But the role itself breeds isolation, and isolation breeds a particular kind of blindness.

Judges rarely live in the neighborhoods their sentences shape. Many come from prosecutors' offices or corporate firms, and some have not set foot in a public housing complex, a detox ward, or a juvenile lockup since law school, if ever. They hear the world described in briefs and motions and plea agreements. Their days are crowded dockets, a few minutes per case, the same lawyers appearing again and again. Over time, the people in front of them stop being individuals. They become file numbers, and the victim reading a statement becomes one more procedural step before the deal closes.

Almost no judge learns what happens after he rules. A sentence goes out the door, and the judge moves to the next file. Nobody reports back that the man released on a low bond reoffended within a week, or that the teenager sent away for a year came home angrier and less employable than he left. Doctors track outcomes. Teachers see test scores. Most trial judges work without any systematic feedback on whether their decisions made the community safer or more dangerous. Few professions with that much power get that little information about their results.

Teresa is not naive about any of this. She knows most cases end in plea bargains and that judges are buried in work. What wounded her was the sense that her suffering was ceremonial, that the outcome had been settled by professionals before she ever opened her mouth. That feeling is common among the survivors I interview. They come to court expecting a referee. They meet an administrator. The institution designed to give them a hearing often seems built mainly to move cases along.

Bias and Blindness

Isolation becomes bias when a judge's experience quietly shapes whom he believes. In 2016 a California judge, Aaron Persky, sentenced Brock Turner, a former Stanford University swimmer convicted of three felony counts of sexual assault, to six months in county jail, citing the severe impact a prison term would have on him. Outrage spread across the country, and the victim's courtroom statement was read aloud by millions. In 2018 voters in Santa Clara County recalled Persky, the first California judge removed that way in more than eighty years. His defenders argued he had followed a probation recommendation, and that judges must be free to make unpopular rulings. That argument deserves a fair hearing. It is also exactly why the case mattered.

The worry many voters expressed was not that one sentence was lenient. It was that the reasoning seemed to weigh a young athlete's future more heavily than the woman he had assaulted, and that a defendant from a different background might never have received the same sympathy. Studies of federal sentencing have found persistent gaps by race, even after accounting for offense and criminal history, with Black men receiving longer sentences on average than white men. Researchers argue about the causes. The pattern itself is not seriously disputed, and judges are the ones holding the pen.

Blindness also runs through the way courts handle the poor. Judges set bail amounts that a middle-class defendant could pay from a checking account and a working parent cannot raise at all, sending people to jail for days or weeks before any finding of guilt. They impose fines and fees that compound into warrants. A judge who has never had to choose between rent and a court payment may not see that a modest fine is, for some families, a sentence of its own. Nothing in the job requires him to find out.

Corruption and Power

Then there is outright corruption, and no case shows it more starkly than what happened in Luzerne County, Pennsylvania. For years, juvenile court judge Mark Ciavarella sent children to detention for minor offenses, often in hearings that lasted minutes, often after the children had waived their right to a lawyer. One girl had mocked a school administrator on a social media page. Others had been in schoolyard scuffles or caught with small amounts of stolen property. Many were sent to for-profit juvenile centers. Ciavarella and another judge, Michael Conahan, had taken millions of dollars in payments connected to the construction and operation of those very facilities.

The two men had the power to make the arrangement work. Conahan, as the county's president judge, helped steer county business toward the private center while the older county-run facility was shut down. Ciavarella supplied the bodies. Parents who protested were often ignored, and some of their children were led out of the courtroom in handcuffs while they watched. For years, lawyers, probation officers, and court staff saw the pattern and said nothing loud enough to stop it. That silence, more than the greed of two men, is what lets corruption in a courthouse survive.

Everything came apart in 2008 and 2009, after advocates at a Philadelphia legal nonprofit began asking why so many Luzerne children were appearing in court without counsel. The Pennsylvania Supreme Court vacated thousands of juvenile adjudications. Conahan pleaded guilty to federal charges and received a sentence of more than seventeen years. A jury convicted Ciavarella of racketeering and related crimes in 2011, and he was sentenced to twenty-eight years in federal prison. In 2022, a federal judge ordered the two men to pay more than two hundred million dollars in damages to hundreds of the people they had harmed as children.

Corruption is not always a bribe in an envelope. In Caperton v. A.T. Massey Coal Co. in 2009, the Supreme Court confronted a West Virginia case in which a coal company had lost a jury verdict of roughly fifty million dollars. While the appeal was pending, the company's chief executive spent about three million dollars supporting the election of a lawyer named Brent Benjamin to the state's highest court. Benjamin won, refused to step aside, and cast the deciding vote to throw out the verdict. The Supreme Court held, five to four, that the Constitution's guarantee of due process required his recusal because the risk of actual bias was too high.

Caperton matters because it was so rare. The justices said themselves that the facts were extreme. Below that extreme, judges usually decide for themselves whether they are too conflicted to hear a case, and they usually conclude they are not. Campaign money flows freely into state judicial elections, often from the law firms and businesses that will appear before the winners. Most of it is legal. Very little of it is ever tested in court.

The Human Cost of Bad Rulings

Corrupt judges are not only a scandal. They are a crime against particular people. The Supreme Court recognized that in Bracy v. Gramley in 1997. William Bracy had been convicted and sentenced to death before Thomas Maloney, a Chicago judge later convicted of taking bribes to fix murder cases. Bracy had not bribed anyone. He argued that Maloney came down hard on defendants who did not pay, partly to deflect suspicion from the ones who did. The Court unanimously held that Bracy had shown enough to be entitled to discovery to prove that his judge had been biased against him. A corrupt judge, in other words, endangers even the defendants who never offered him a dime.

The children of Luzerne County show the cost over a lifetime. Some spent months away from their families for offenses that would have drawn a warning anywhere else. They lost school years, missed graduations, and came home branded as delinquents. Several have said publicly that the experience pushed them toward the very trouble it was supposed to prevent. A record vacated years later does not return a lost adolescence. Taxpayers paid for every one of those detention beds, and they paid again when some of those children grew into adults the system had already failed.

Bad rulings that involve no corruption at all can be just as permanent. In Stump v. Sparkman in 1978, the Supreme Court considered an Indiana judge who had approved a mother's petition to have her fifteen-year-old daughter sterilized. There was no hearing, no lawyer for the girl, and no notice to her. She was told she was having her appendix removed. She learned the truth years later, after marrying and trying to have children. The Court held that the judge was absolutely immune from her lawsuit because approving the petition was a judicial act within his general jurisdiction. She was left with no remedy at all.

Teresa's case involved no corruption and no constitutional scandal, and its cost was still real. The man who attacked her was released on probation with a short list of conditions. Within the year he violated two of them, and a different judge extended his supervision without a hearing she was told about. She moved apartments twice. She stopped parking in garages. She told me the attack took her sense of safety for a season. The courthouse, she said, took her belief that anyone in power was paying attention.

Why Judges Face No Oversight

Stump v. Sparkman shows how deep the shield runs. Judges enjoy absolute immunity from civil suits for their judicial acts, even acts done in bad faith, on the theory that fear of lawsuits would cripple independent decisions. The theory has merit. A judge who rules against a powerful litigant should not be sued into silence. But absolute immunity means that the main check left is discipline, and judicial discipline in America is weak by design. State conduct commissions are run largely by judges and lawyers, operate mostly in secret, and remove only a tiny number of judges each year.

Elections were supposed to be the answer for state judges, and most state judges do face voters in some form. In practice, judicial races are low-information contests. Many voters skip them entirely, and those who do vote often know nothing about the candidates beyond a name and a party. In retention elections, where voters simply decide whether to keep a sitting judge, the incumbent nearly always wins. Persky's recall was remarkable precisely because it was so unusual. A system that removes a judge only after a national firestorm is not oversight. It is luck.

Federal judges face even less. They serve for life and can be removed only by impeachment, which has happened to only a handful in the nation's history. Complaints filed under federal judicial conduct rules are reviewed by other judges and overwhelmingly dismissed. In 2021 a major newspaper investigation found that well over a hundred federal judges had presided over cases involving companies in which they or their families owned stock, violating a basic ethics law. Congress responded in 2022 with a law requiring federal judges to disclose their stock trades online. The justices of the Supreme Court adopted their first formal ethics code in 2023, with no mechanism to enforce it.

The result is a branch of government that polices itself, in private, with almost no outside view. Teresa asked me at that diner who she could complain to. I told her the truth. She could file a complaint with the state judicial conduct board. It would almost certainly be dismissed, and she would probably never be told why. She laughed, not happily, and said that the man who attacked her had more oversight on probation than the judge who sentenced him.

Making Judges Answer

Legislatures can start by opening the books. States can require courts to publish sentencing, bail, and plea data by judge, so voters and lawmakers can see who is an outlier. Colorado, Utah, and Arizona already publish formal evaluations of judges before retention elections, drawing on surveys of lawyers, jurors, and court staff, and other states can copy those systems. Recusal decisions should not rest with the judge whose impartiality is challenged. A different judge should decide, as several reformers and bar groups have urged, and campaign contributions above a modest threshold should trigger automatic disclosure in every case involving the donor.

Discipline needs teeth and daylight. State conduct commissions should include more members of the public, publish the outcome of every complaint, and report annually on how many judges were investigated and what happened. Pennsylvania, after Luzerne County, tightened its rules so children cannot waive a lawyer without first consulting one, a reform every state should match. Congress can create an inspector general for the federal courts and give the Supreme Court's ethics code an enforcement process that someone other than the justices runs.

Citizens hold a lever too, and it is attention. Court-watch programs, in which trained volunteers sit in courtrooms and record what judges do, have exposed bail practices and courtroom conduct in New Orleans and other cities, and they are cheap to run. Community leaders can organize one in any county with a courthouse. Voters can learn the names of the judges on their ballot before election day instead of skipping that section. And survivors like Teresa can bring a friend to the hearing, take notes, and send them to the local paper. Judges change behavior when they know someone is watching.

Good judges should welcome all of this, and many do. I have known trial judges who visit jails and treatment centers on their own time, who read every victim letter, and who explain their sentences in plain language from the bench. Some judicial training programs already take new judges inside prisons so they can see what a sentence means. Making those habits standard rather than personal virtues would cost almost nothing. The best people in robes have nothing to fear from transparency. It is the worst ones who depend on the dark.

The takeaway

Judges decide the most consequential outcomes in the justice system with the least supervision of anyone in it. Kids for cash, bought elections, and immune sterilizations prove what that freedom can produce. Publish their data, take recusal out of their hands, give discipline real teeth, and sit in their courtrooms, and the people in robes will start answering to the people they serve.

Chapter 26: The Media's Collapse: How Journalism Witnessed and Facilitated America's Slide Into Crime

Denise sits in the third row of a city council chamber in a small river town, a legal pad on her knee and a cold coffee at her feet. She runs the neighborhood association on the east side, where three houses on one block have burned in eighteen months and nobody has been charged. On the agenda tonight is a no-bid contract for the police department's new records system. The press table at the front of the room is empty. It has been empty for years. The weekly paper that used to send a reporter to every meeting folded, and the nearest daily now covers this town from forty miles away, when it covers it at all.

I have sat in rooms like that one more times than I can count, and I can tell you what happens when the press table goes dark. The contract passes without a question. The fires stay a rumor. The only record of the evening is Denise's handwriting. In my years on the crime beat I watched the American press shrink from a watchdog with teeth to a camera crew that arrives after the shooting, films the tape and the candles, and leaves before anyone asks why the same corner keeps producing the same funerals. The press did not cause America's crime problem. It watched the slide, covered the blood, and stopped exposing the systems that made the blood predictable.

The Death of Investigative Journalism

Consider the numbers, which are brutal and come from the most careful counters in the business. Northwestern University's Medill School has tracked local news for two decades, and its 2025 report found that the country has gone from roughly 7,300 newspapers in 2005 to fewer than 4,500. Newspaper employment has fallen by more than three-quarters over the same stretch. More than two hundred counties now have no local news source at all, and well over a thousand more are down to a single one. Taken together, something like fifty million Americans live with little or no local reporting.

Behind those figures are people I knew. The courthouse reporter who could read a docket like a weather map. The editor who let a reporter spend four months on one story because the story mattered. Investigative work is slow, expensive and legally risky, and it was the first thing cut when classified ads moved online and hedge funds bought the chains. A crime story written from a police press release costs an afternoon. A story proving that a sheriff's office has been losing evidence for a decade costs a salary, a lawyer and a year of nerve.

What that work could do is not theoretical. In 2002 the Boston Globe's Spotlight team documented how the Archdiocese of Boston had moved abusive priests from parish to parish for decades and settled with victims in secret. The series won the Pulitzer Prize, drove Cardinal Bernard Law to resign within the year, and set off investigations across the country and the world. It took a small team months of court fights to unseal records. That kind of team is precisely what most American newsrooms no longer have.

Into the vacuum stepped the institutions themselves. Police departments now run polished social media accounts with more followers than the local paper ever had. Prosecutors post mugshots and conviction announcements. Sheriffs film their own drug seizures with dramatic music. None of that is illegal, and some of it is useful public information. It is also advertising, written by the people whose performance the public most needs to judge. When the agency becomes the main source of news about the agency, the only questions that get asked are the ones it chooses to answer, and the stories that would embarrass it simply never appear.

The Globe's own people have been honest that pieces of the story had reached the paper years earlier and were not pursued. I respect that candor, because it names the real failure. Institutions are rarely exposed by a lucky leak. They are exposed when somebody is paid to keep pulling on a thread after everyone else has gone home, and that somebody has largely been laid off.

Crime Coverage as Entertainment

While the watchdog starved, the spectacle grew fat. Cable news discovered in the 1990s that a single crime, cast with a sympathetic victim and a suspect the audience could hate, could fill twenty hours of airtime a day for months. The 1996 killing of six-year-old JonBenét Ramsey in Boulder, Colorado, became the template. Pageant footage of a little girl played on a loop, tabloids and respectable outlets alike pointed at her parents, and the coverage hardened into a national verdict. In 2008 the Boulder district attorney publicly cleared the family, citing DNA evidence, and apologized to them. No network that spent a decade implying otherwise ever matched that apology with equal airtime.

The Casey Anthony trial, which I come back to elsewhere, ran on the same engine. So did a hundred lesser cases whose names have faded. The format rewards emotion, a villain and a cliffhanger. It does not reward the question a working reporter is supposed to ask, which is what failed before the crime and who was responsible for preventing it.

I watched that machinery spin up again in 2026, when Nancy Guthrie, the mother of NBC anchor Savannah Guthrie, disappeared from her home near Tucson, Arizona, on the first of February. The Pima County sheriff treated it as a likely abduction, and the FBI released doorbell-camera images of an armed, masked figure at her front door. As of my most recent sources in October, she had not been found and no one had been charged in connection with her disappearance. Her family deserves every hour of that attention. What troubles me is how few families ever receive one.

The entertainment model has since migrated to podcasts and streaming documentaries, and some of that work has been excellent, reopening wrongful convictions that official channels ignored. Much of it is something else: amateurs naming suspects on the strength of a hunch, flooding tip lines with noise, and turning a grieving family's driveway into a tourist stop. I have seen detectives spend days running down theories that started in a comment thread. The audience feels involved. The investigation slows down, and the systemic question underneath, why so many cases in this country go unsolved at all, gets no episode.

How Media Normalized Violence

A media circus has an opposite, and it is not silence. It is the brief. Four paragraphs, a block number, a time stamp, and the phrase "police are investigating." On any weekend in a big American city there are several of them, and they read like the weather. I wrote plenty myself as a young reporter. They taught me, and they taught readers, that a shooting in certain neighborhoods is a condition rather than an event.

Repetition without context does that. When the same corner produces its fifth homicide in two years and the story is still four paragraphs long, the coverage is telling the public that this is normal for those people in that place. Nobody calls the city's landlord registry to see who owns the vacant house where the shooter hid. Nobody checks whether the last four cases were ever cleared. The violence becomes background noise, and background noise does not move a city council.

Local television sharpened the habit. The old newsroom rule that if it bleeds, it leads was never written down anywhere, but every producer I worked beside knew it by heart. Crime footage is cheap to gather and easy to understand in ninety seconds. A story about why the county's juvenile diversion program lost its funding takes a week and has no pictures. So the evening broadcast fills with flashing lights and crime-scene tape from across the metro area, and viewers come away convinced that danger is everywhere and explained nowhere. Fear without explanation does not produce smarter policy. It produces louder demands for whatever looks tough on the next campaign mailer.

Television compounded it with decades of entertainment that cast police as heroes and suspects as props. The reality show "Cops" ran for more than thirty years before its network cut it loose in 2020, amid the protests after George Floyd's murder. Shows like it trained a generation to see crime as a chase that ends in handcuffs, the end of the story rather than a symptom with a history.

The Failure to Expose Systemic Crime

Most of the crime that shapes American life does not happen in a single moment that a camera can catch. It happens in budgets, in contracts, in evidence rooms and in charging decisions. A crime lab that falsifies results for years. A police union contract that erases disciplinary records after a set period. A jail medical vendor whose patients keep dying. These are stories about documents, and documents do not bleed.

Covering them requires reporters who know the beat well enough to notice what is missing. In 2011 a young reporter at the Harrisburg Patriot-News, Sara Ganim, broke the news that a grand jury was investigating Jerry Sandusky, the former Penn State assistant football coach, for child sexual abuse. Her paper won the Pulitzer for it. The detail that stays with me is that national sports media had been inside that stadium for years and missed it entirely. A local reporter on a local beat found what the big outfits were too close, or too comfortable, to see.

When the local beat disappears, the systems behind crime are left alone with their own press releases. Researchers studying municipal finance have even found that after a local paper closes, government borrowing costs tend to rise, a sign that lenders price in the loss of scrutiny. If bond markets can sense the absence of a watchdog, so can anyone thinking about stealing from a public budget.

The Public Left Blind

Ask an ordinary American whether crime is going up, and the answer for most of the past thirty years has been yes, nearly regardless of what the numbers were doing. Gallup has asked the question for decades, and majorities have often said crime was rising during long stretches when it was falling. That gap is not stupidity. It is what happens when people get their sense of danger from vivid stories rather than steady reporting.

The blindness cuts both ways. People overestimate random street violence and underestimate the harms with no camera crew attached: the domestic homicide that followed six ignored protective-order violations, the fraud that drained a retiree's savings, the abuse inside an institution that was trusted to protect children. A public that cannot see a problem cannot vote on it, fund a fix for it, or demand a resignation over it.

Denise felt that blindness in her own town. After the third fire on her block she called the regional paper. A reporter was kind and took notes and said the paper would look into it. Nothing ran. When she pulled the property records herself, she found all three houses had changed hands through the same limited-liability company within a year of burning, each one insured. She took the file to the fire marshal and the police chief and got polite nods. What she wanted was not a favor from them. She wanted one reporter with the time to ask the questions out loud, in print, where people who vote could read them.

Gabby Petito's case in 2021 showed the blind spot in a single season. The young woman's disappearance on a cross-country trip drew saturation coverage, and tips from the public helped investigators locate her remains. The same autumn, reporters pointed to a state task force study in Wyoming, where her remains were found, counting roughly 710 Indigenous people reported missing there between 2011 and 2020. The study found that Indigenous homicide victims were far less likely than white victims to receive any newspaper coverage at all, and more likely to be described in negative terms when they did. Attention is a resource, and the press hands it out according to who looks like its audience.

Media's Role in Institutional Protection

My profession's hardest truth is that the press has often protected the very institutions it should have watched. It did not always do it out of malice. It did it out of dependence. A crime reporter who angers the police chief stops getting the early call on a big arrest. An editor who runs a damning series about the district attorney may find the office suddenly slow to return calls. Access is the currency of the beat, and institutions know how to spend it.

What results is a style of coverage that repeats the official account first and checks it later, if ever.
