Early police statements after use-of-force incidents have repeatedly turned out to be wrong once video surfaced, yet they routinely become the first headline, the one most people read. Correction rarely travels as far as the original claim. I have watched a family spend years trying to undo a single sentence from a department spokesman that ran unchallenged on the evening news the night their son died. The spokesman moved on to another job. The sentence stayed in every search result with the young man's name.

Ownership adds another layer. When a newspaper or station belongs to a company that also courts government contracts, licenses or tax breaks, nobody needs to send a memo telling reporters to go easy. People learn which stories get a long leash and which get questions about sourcing and length. I have known good editors who swore they never killed a story for business reasons and meant it. They also never assigned the ones that might have cost the company a friend in the statehouse.

The law gives reporters real protection, and that makes the timidity harder to excuse. In New York Times v. Sullivan in 1964, the Supreme Court held that a public official cannot win a libel suit over coverage of official conduct without proving actual malice, meaning the publisher knew a statement was false or recklessly disregarded the truth. The ruling exists so that the press can be wrong in good faith while chasing the powerful.

Protection for sources is thinner. In Branzburg v. Hayes in 1972, the Court held that the First Amendment does not excuse reporters from testifying before a grand jury, which is why a clerk or a deputy who risks a career to talk still has reason to fear. Many states have passed their own shield laws, but Congress has debated a federal one for years without enacting it. Between broad protection from libel and patchy protection for sources, the press has more room to fight than it uses, and the people inside institutions who want to tell the truth have less cover than they deserve.

The Cost of Silence and How to Break It

Silence has a price, and taxpayers pay it in contracts nobody read, in cases nobody followed, and in neighborhoods where Denise and people like her are left to keep the only record. Every dollar a public official can move without a reporter watching is a dollar more likely to go missing. Every unsolved case that never makes the paper is one more reason for a shooter to believe the next one will go unsolved too. The good news is that the cure is known, cheap by government standards, and already being tried in places that decided an empty press table was a public-safety problem.

Start with money for reporting that stays independent of the officials it covers. New Jersey created the Civic Information Consortium in 2018, a state-funded nonprofit that makes grants to local news projects through an arm's-length board. California reached an agreement with Google in 2024 to put money into local journalism. New York and Illinois have both passed tax credits that help news organizations keep local reporters on payroll. Nationally, Report for America has placed hundreds of young journalists in understaffed newsrooms since 2017, with communities sharing the cost. None of these is perfect, and each needs firewalls between funder and editor. Each beats an empty press table.

Next, a city council can do something tonight without waiting on any legislature. It can stream and archive every public meeting, post contracts above a modest threshold online before the vote, and publish police data on clearance rates, use of force and complaints in a form anyone can download. That turns every resident into a potential watchdog. State legislatures can tighten public-records laws with real deadlines and real penalties for agencies that stall, and they can pass shield laws where none exist.

Editors have work to do as well. Treat a police statement as a claim to be checked, not a fact to be printed. Follow every homicide to its outcome, so the public learns which cases were solved and which were dropped. And citizens can do the plainest thing of all: pay for local news, show up at meetings, and file the records request yourself. Denise did, and her legal pad became the first real account of those fires.

The takeaway

Journalism did not pull the trigger, but it walked away from the room where the decisions were made and kept its cameras on the crime scene. Bring the reporters back to the council chamber, the courthouse and the budget office, fund them in ways that keep them free, and the systems behind America's violence will have to answer to someone again.

Chapter 27: Who Are the Most Destructive Criminals — and Why Don't You Know Their Names?

American employers take more money out of their workers' pockets through minimum-wage violations alone than all the robbers, burglars and car thieves in the country take from their victims. That is not a slogan. It is the conclusion of a 2017 analysis by the Economic Policy Institute, which estimated, from survey data in the ten largest states, that minimum-wage violations cost workers something like fifteen billion dollars a year nationally. Set that beside the FBI's own estimates of what all robberies, burglaries, larcenies and vehicle thefts cost their victims, and the paycheck thieves come out ahead.

Nobody gets a mugshot for it. No local station leads with it. In my years on the crime beat I covered hundreds of armed robberies and not one press conference about a restaurant chain that shaved hours off timecards, because no such press conference was ever held. The crimes that cost Americans the most are committed by people and institutions that are almost never booked, and the reason you do not know their names is not an accident. It is how the system was built.

Institutional Crime

Begin with a bank most Americans have walked into. For years, employees at Wells Fargo opened accounts that customers never asked for, millions of them, to hit sales targets that management set and enforced. Customers were charged fees on accounts they did not know existed, and some saw their credit damaged. In 2016 regulators and the city of Los Angeles fined the bank a combined 185 million dollars. In 2020 the bank agreed to pay three billion dollars to resolve criminal and civil investigations by the Justice Department and the Securities and Exchange Commission, and admitted it had falsified records and harmed customers.

The structure of that resolution is what I want readers to notice. The criminal case was handled with a deferred prosecution agreement, which means the company paid and promised to behave and was not convicted. The former chief executive was fined and barred from banking by a federal regulator. A senior executive who ran the retail division later pleaded guilty to obstructing a bank examination and received probation. Thousands of low-level employees lost their jobs. The people at the top of the pressure machine lost money and titles, and nobody who designed it went to prison.

Enron is the older lesson. The Houston energy company used accounting tricks and off-the-books partnerships to hide debt and inflate profits until it collapsed into bankruptcy at the end of 2001. Employees who had been encouraged to hold company stock in their retirement accounts watched those savings evaporate. Its accounting firm, Arthur Andersen, was convicted in 2002 of obstruction for shredding documents. In Arthur Andersen LLP v. United States in 2005, the Supreme Court unanimously overturned that conviction because the jury instructions had been too broad. By then the firm had already collapsed and tens of thousands of jobs were gone.

Hidden Offenders

Carol spent twenty-two years as a clerk in a regional warehouse before she retired, and she still keeps a shoebox of pay stubs in her hall closet. For most of those years, she says, the shift ran fifteen or twenty minutes past the clock, every night, because the supervisor wanted the trucks loaded before anyone punched out. She did the math once at her kitchen table and stopped, because the number made her sick. Nobody from the company was ever charged. Carol is a taxpayer, too, and she points out that every worker underpaid that way is a household more likely to need public help, which she helps fund.

That is the shape of hidden crime. It is spread thin across thousands of victims, each loss small enough to shrug off and large enough to matter. The offender is often a company, not a person, and the paperwork is designed to make the theft look like a scheduling mistake. A worker who complains risks the job. A worker who stays quiet loses an hour a week, every week, for years. When Carol finally asked her supervisor about the unpaid minutes, she was moved to the overnight shift within a month. She never asked again, and neither did anyone who watched it happen.

Bernie Madoff showed the other shape: concentration. A former chairman of the Nasdaq stock market, he ran what prosecutors called the largest Ponzi scheme in history, paying old investors with new investors' money for decades. When it collapsed in December 2008, account statements showed tens of billions of dollars that did not exist. He pleaded guilty in 2009 and was sentenced to 150 years in prison, where he died. His victims included retirees, charities and pension funds, people who had trusted the reputation the way Carol trusted the time clock.

Sam Bankman-Fried brought the pattern into the digital age. His cryptocurrency exchange, FTX, collapsed in November 2022, and federal prosecutors showed that customer deposits, roughly eight billion dollars of them, had been diverted to his trading firm and spent on investments, political donations and real estate. A Manhattan jury convicted him in November 2023 of fraud and conspiracy, and in 2024 he was sentenced to twenty-five years. He had been a fixture of magazine covers and congressional hearings only months before.

Investors, celebrities and politicians had lined up to be seen with him, and his exchange paid for stadium naming rights and television commercials. The respectability was not incidental to the fraud. It was the fraud's best protection, because few people wanted to be the one who questioned a young billionaire everyone else was praising, and the regulators who might have asked were still arguing over who had jurisdiction. Madoff had worn the same armor a generation earlier. A booking photo would have warned people. A magazine cover did the opposite.

Power Structures That Shield Harm

What protects these offenders is not one villain in Washington. It is a set of structures that make them hard to see and harder to punish. The first is the regulator who looks away. After Madoff's arrest, the SEC's own inspector general reported in 2009 that the agency had received credible, detailed warnings about him over many years, including from a financial analyst named Harry Markopolos who laid out why the returns were mathematically implausible. Examiners looked and missed it. The cop on that beat was standing on the corner when the robbery happened and did not turn around.

I have no doubt that most of the examiners involved were honest people doing work they found tedious and underfunded. That is part of the problem. Regulatory agencies are staffed far below the size of the industries they police, examiners often lack the specialized training their targets can buy, and a career at the agency can be a stepping stone to a far better paid job at the very firms under review. None of that requires a bribe. It requires only a culture in which asking the hard question feels like more trouble than it is worth.

The second is the law itself, written and interpreted in ways that narrow what counts as a crime at the top. Jeffrey Skilling, Enron's former chief executive, was convicted in 2006 of fraud and conspiracy. In Skilling v. United States in 2010, the Supreme Court held that the federal honest-services fraud statute covers only bribery and kickback schemes, not the broader self-dealing prosecutors had alleged. Part of his conviction had to be reconsidered, his sentence was later reduced, and he was released in 2019. I do not quarrel with a court that insists criminal laws be clear. I do quarrel with a Congress that has left them that unclear for the people with the most power to exploit the gaps.

Money, used lawfully, is the third shield. Large companies can hire former prosecutors to negotiate with current ones. They can settle without admitting wrongdoing. They can spread a case across years until the public forgets it. A teenager caught with a stolen phone has a public defender with too many files. A bank caught with millions of fake accounts has a law firm with a floor of associates. The two cases are not processed by the same justice system in any meaningful sense, even when they share a courthouse.

Bankruptcy court became the newest shield. Purdue Pharma, the maker of OxyContin, pleaded guilty to federal criminal charges over its marketing of the drug in 2007 and again in 2020, yet the family that owned it sought to use the company's bankruptcy to win permanent immunity from civil lawsuits by opioid victims, without filing for bankruptcy themselves. In Harrington v. Purdue Pharma in 2024, the Supreme Court held that the bankruptcy code does not authorize that kind of release for non-debtors over the objection of the people whose claims are being extinguished. It took years of litigation by victims and a handful of states to reach that answer, and the fact that the plan got so far tells you how much the system was willing to bend.

Why the Public Never Sees Them

Partly, the reasons are mechanical. National crime statistics are built around offenses that police report, such as assaults, burglaries and thefts, and they say little about corporate fraud, wage theft or environmental crime. If a harm is not counted, it does not appear in the charts politicians wave at campaign events, and it does not shape the budgets that decide which crimes get investigators.

Coverage follows the counting. Street crime arrives with a location, a time, a victim and a photograph. Corporate crime arrives as a regulatory filing on a Friday afternoon, written in language meant to discourage reading. Even when reporters chase it, the story is often framed as business news, filed beside quarterly earnings, as though stealing from customers were a strategy decision rather than a crime.

There is also the matter of faces. A booking photo turns an offender into a person the public can remember and fear. Executives who settle with regulators do not have booking photos. Their companies issue statements about "regrettable practices" and "lessons learned," and the people who made the decisions slide into other boardrooms. Ask a hundred people to name a carjacker from the local news and many can. Ask them to name the executives behind the last major corporate settlement and almost none can, though the corporate case may have taken more money from more households than every carjacking in the state.

Carol has noticed the difference in her own living room. The evening news, she says, knows the name of every teenager arrested for stealing a car in the county. She has never once heard it mention a company fined for stealing wages, though she knows from her own pay stubs that such companies exist a short drive from her house. When she asked a local reporter about it at a community forum, the answer was honest and depressing: the police department sends out arrest reports every morning, and the labor department sends out nothing anyone would call news.

The Scale of Invisible Crime

Put the numbers side by side and the picture inverts. I do not claim precision here, because nobody keeps the ledger that would make precision possible, and that absence is itself a scandal. One Ponzi scheme erased tens of billions in reported wealth. One exchange lost billions in customer money. One bank paid billions to settle a fraud built into its sales system. Minimum-wage theft alone runs into the billions every year. Most of that money came out of ordinary households, the same people told to fear the stranger in the parking lot. By any reasonable reading of the FBI's own loss figures, a single fraud on the scale of Madoff's took more than years of street robberies across the entire country.

None of that makes street crime trivial. A mugging can take a life, and a burglary can destroy a family's sense of safety for years. I have sat with those victims and I take their harm seriously. The point is proportion. A country that pours almost all of its enforcement attention into the crimes that are easiest to photograph will keep losing the most money, and often the most lives, to the crimes that are hardest to see. Unsafe workplaces, falsified safety reports and fraud against the elderly kill and impoverish people quietly, and quietly is exactly the problem.

Fraud against older Americans alone now runs into the billions of dollars a year in losses reported to the FBI, and investigators believe most victims never report at all, out of embarrassment or because they do not know where to go. Add tax evasion by the wealthy, which federal estimates put among the largest drains on the public treasury, and the costs that ordinary taxpayers like Carol absorb climb higher still. Every dollar not paid by someone who cheated is a dollar charged to someone who did not. Invisible crime is not a victimless abstraction. It is a bill, mailed to people who never saw the theft.

Bringing the Powerful Into the Light

Fixing this does not require a revolution, only the will to treat theft as theft at every altitude. Start with the paycheck. California made intentional wage theft over a set threshold prosecutable as grand theft beginning in 2022, and New York changed its larceny law in 2023 to cover wage theft explicitly. Other legislatures can copy those statutes, fund labor inspectors in proportion to the size of the workforce, and let local prosecutors build dedicated wage-theft units. Some district attorneys already have. A city council can require that any company receiving a public contract or tax break certify its payroll compliance and lose the benefit if it lies.

Next, end the habit of resolving corporate crime with no individual accountability. Prosecutors can insist that deferred prosecution agreements name responsible executives and claw back their bonuses, and judges reviewing those deals can refuse to rubber-stamp ones that let every decision-maker walk. Congress can write fraud statutes clear enough to survive the scrutiny the Supreme Court applied in Skilling, so that judges are not left to narrow vague laws after the fact. Regulators can treat repeat offenders as repeat offenders, raising penalties each time a company returns to the same misconduct instead of negotiating a fresh fine as though the slate were clean.

Count it, too. The FBI and the Bureau of Justice Statistics could publish an annual national estimate of corporate and white-collar crime alongside the street-crime figures, so that the public sees both columns. Expand whistleblower programs that already work. The SEC's program, created by the Dodd-Frank law in 2010, has paid large awards to insiders who report fraud, and the tips have produced major cases. And citizens can do their part: keep your pay stubs, report violations to your state labor department, and ask every candidate for district attorney one question, which is what they will do about the thieves in suits.

The takeaway

America's most destructive criminals rarely wear masks. They sign memos, set quotas and settle cases. They hire the best lawyers in the country and count on a public that is looking the other way. Count their crimes in the national statistics, put individual names on the charges, give insiders a safe way to report, and the public will finally see where its money and its safety actually go.

Chapter 28: Why Law Enforcement Is Often Entangled With Organized Crime

If everybody knows the names of the crime families, the cartels and the big street crews, why do they never seem to go away? Police chiefs hold press conferences over tables stacked with seized cash. Federal agents announce indictments with charts of nicknames and photographs. A few years later the same organizations, or their heirs, are running the same rackets on the same corners. Most Americans have half-asked that question while watching the evening news and moved on.

I have spent years asking it out loud, in courtrooms, in squad rooms, and on front steps in neighborhoods that never got an answer. The usual explanations are that organized crime is too rich, too violent, too well lawyered. All true, and none of it sufficient. Rich, violent groups lose all the time when the state is determined to beat them.

Here is the answer, plainly. Organized crime survives because the agencies meant to stop it are vulnerable to it, and too often they are entangled with it, through bribes, through informant deals that turn into partnerships, and through a quieter kind of cooperation that comes from simply looking away. Most officers are honest, and many of the people who exposed corruption were officers themselves. The system they work inside is built with doors that the dishonest ones, and the criminals who recruit them, know how to open.

Structural Vulnerabilities

Start with the arithmetic. A narcotics detective earns a public salary. The people he investigates handle cash in amounts that can buy a house in an afternoon. He works in plain clothes, often without a supervisor present, with wide discretion over whom to stop, what to seize and what to write down. His most valuable sources are criminals themselves. No honest management consultant would design a job that way if the goal were to resist temptation.

New York learned this decades ago. In 1970, after officer Frank Serpico and a colleague took their complaints about widespread payoffs to the press, Mayor John Lindsay created what became known as the Knapp Commission. Serpico had been shot in the face during a Brooklyn drug raid in 1971, and many people, including Serpico himself, came to believe his fellow officers had failed to back him up. The commission's 1972 report found corruption woven through the department, and it gave the country a vocabulary: "grass-eaters," who passively accepted gratuities, and "meat-eaters," who aggressively hunted for money.

The lesson did not stick. Twenty years later another body, the Mollen Commission, found a different and worse pattern in some precincts: small crews of officers who did not just take bribes but robbed drug dealers, sold drugs and protected the trade. The commission was formed after the 1992 arrest of officer Michael Dowd, who was later convicted in a drug-trafficking scheme, and its 1994 report concluded that the department's own internal investigators had too often preferred not to find corruption, because finding it was bad for the department's image. The structure had not changed. Only the size of the money had, and the crack trade of the 1980s had made that money larger than any precinct payoff the Knapp investigators had ever documented.

Corruption Pathways

There are three main roads from a badge to a criminal enterprise. The first is the oldest, the straight payoff, cash in exchange for protection, tips or silence. The second is the informant relationship that rots from the inside. The third is the officer who becomes the enterprise. Each road starts with a small step that can be explained away: a free meal, a favor for a source, a seizure counted a little short. I have interviewed officers who were caught on the third road, and nearly every one of them described the first step as something everyone did. None of them could say exactly when the small favors became the job itself.

The second road runs straight through Boston. James "Whitey" Bulger led the Winter Hill Gang while serving for years as a prized FBI informant, and his handler, agent John Connolly, protected him. Connolly was convicted in federal court in 2002 of racketeering for shielding Bulger's crew and leaking the identities of people cooperating against them. In 2008 a Florida jury convicted him of second-degree murder for his role in the 1982 killing of a gambling executive, after evidence showed he had tipped off Bulger's associates that the man might talk. An appeals court upheld that conviction in 2015.

Nor did the damage stop with one corrupt agent. Bulger, warned before his 1995 indictment, fled and spent sixteen years as a fugitive before his arrest in California in 2011. A federal jury convicted him in 2013 of racketeering that included his role in eleven murders. For much of the time he was killing people, the most powerful law enforcement agency in the country was treating him as a valued source, and its paperwork made him harder, not easier, to catch. Families in South Boston who had lost sons and brothers learned in open court that the government had known far more than it ever told them.

The third road leads to two retired New York detectives, Louis Eppolito and Stephen Caracappa. In 2006 a Brooklyn federal jury found that while on the job they had worked for the Lucchese crime family, passing along confidential information and taking part in killings. A judge threw out the racketeering conviction on statute-of-limitations grounds, and a federal appeals court reinstated it in 2008. Both men were sentenced to life. They were not grass-eaters. They were hit men with pensions.

That road also runs across borders. Genaro García Luna was Mexico's top public security official, the man the United States treated as a partner in the drug war. In February 2023 a jury in Brooklyn convicted him of taking millions of dollars in bribes from the Sinaloa cartel in exchange for protection and intelligence, and in 2024 he was sentenced to more than thirty-eight years in prison. He has maintained his innocence. The verdict means that the official at the center of a joint anti-cartel effort was, by the jury's finding, working for the cartel.

Institutional Blind Spots

Corruption at that scale depends on institutions that do not want to see it. The FBI's handling of informants in Boston is the clearest case on record. In Limone v. United States, decided in 2007, Judge Nancy Gertner of the federal court in Massachusetts found that Boston agents knew a mob hitman they were protecting as a witness had lied when he named four men in a 1965 murder. The agents withheld evidence that could have cleared them. Two of the men died in prison. The other two served more than three decades before they were exonerated. The court awarded the men and their families about 101.7 million dollars, and an appeals court upheld the judgment in 2009.

The Supreme Court had already laid down the rule those agents broke. In Giglio v. United States in 1972, the Court held that prosecutors must disclose promises of leniency made to a government witness, because a jury cannot judge a witness's credibility without knowing what he was promised. The rule exists precisely because deals with criminals are dangerous. When agencies treat their informants as assets to be protected at any cost, the rule becomes paper.

Justice Department officials tightened their informant guidelines after the Boston scandal, and the department's own inspector general went back a few years later to see whether the FBI was following them. The 2005 review found one or more violations in nearly nine of every ten informant files it examined, including cases in which agents failed to tell prosecutors that their informants had committed serious crimes. I credit the inspector general for looking, and for publishing what he found. The finding still tells you how hard it is to change a culture in which an agent's career depends on keeping a source happy, and in which the source knows it.

Blind spots also come from how success is measured. A department rewarded for arrests and seizures will celebrate a table of cash and drugs and ask few questions about the informant who delivered it, or about the officers who keep delivering suspiciously convenient cases. Internal affairs units often report to the same chain of command they are supposed to investigate. Officers who report misconduct, as Serpico learned, can find themselves isolated, transferred or worse.

Cooperation Through Neglect

Not every entanglement involves a bribe. Some of it is a decision, made over and over, to leave certain crimes alone. Rosa has owned her small brick house for nineteen years, and for the last three a house across the street has run what everyone on the block calls a "store," with cars idling at all hours and young men on the porch. She has called the police more times than she can count. Patrol cars come, sit for ten minutes, and leave. Twice she was told, quietly, that "it's being handled." Nothing ever changed except the value of her house, which an appraiser told her last spring had dropped sharply.

Rosa will probably never learn why. Sometimes the answer is a long investigation that needs the spot left open. Sometimes it is an informant being protected. Sometimes it is simple lack of interest in a neighborhood that does not vote in large numbers. From her front window, all three look identical, and that is the point. Neglect and protection produce the same result for the homeowner across the street.

The pattern scales up. Illegal gambling, unlicensed massage businesses fronting for trafficking, and chop shops can run openly for years in some cities because enforcement is treated as low priority, a decision that organized groups notice and exploit. Wherever the state signals that a market is tolerated, someone organized will move in to control it. Tolerance creates territory, and territory creates the disputes over who controls it, which is where the shootings come from.

I have watched the same cycle in more than one city. The same city that ignored the illegal market for years announces a crackdown on the violence the market produced, arrests the shooters, and leaves the market itself running. Residents who called for years watch the crackdown on television and recognize none of the people who actually profit. They know the block. The block knows them. The decision-makers know neither, and the next crew moves into the house before the news trucks have left.

Crime Ecosystems

Organized crime is not a gang. It is an ecosystem, and the most important part of it is often not on the street at all. Drug money has to be moved, hidden and spent, and that requires banks, lawyers, accountants and real estate agents. In 2012 HSBC agreed to pay about 1.9 billion dollars and entered a deferred prosecution agreement after federal authorities found it had failed to stop cartel money from flowing through its accounts. In 2024 TD Bank pleaded guilty to federal charges over failures in its anti-money-laundering program and agreed to pay roughly three billion dollars in penalties.

Those cases show where the leverage is. A street dealer is replaceable within hours. A compliant bank, a corrupt customs officer or a detective selling case files is far harder to replace. Yet enforcement budgets and headlines still flow mostly toward the street level, where arrests are easy to count and the organization barely notices the loss. Financial investigations are slow and require accountants rather than tactical teams. They rarely produce a photograph. A chief who wants to show results before the next budget hearing knows which kind of case fills the table at the press conference, and which kind may not pay off until after he has retired.

That imbalance is itself a form of entanglement. It lets the ecosystem shed its most visible members while the people who make it profitable stay untouched. Prosecutors and police leaders get their press conference, and the organization gets a cost of doing business it can easily afford. The young men arrested on the corner are often barely adults, recruited from the same neighborhoods that suffer the violence. They go to prison. The money they handled keeps moving through accounts in buildings with doormen, and the people who own those accounts rarely see a courtroom.

Breaking the Ties

Workable remedies are known, and several have been tested in the city that needed them most. After the Mollen report, New York created a standing Commission to Combat Police Corruption to monitor the department's own anti-corruption efforts from outside. Every large city can create an independent inspector general for its police, with subpoena power, its own investigators, and a duty to publish findings, so that internal affairs is no longer grading its own homework.

Informant programs need hard rules with outside review. Legislatures can require that every long-term informant relationship be documented, reviewed by prosecutors on a fixed schedule, and closed when the informant is found committing serious violent crimes. Courts can enforce Giglio strictly and sanction offices that hide deals. Police chiefs can rotate officers out of narcotics and vice units on a set schedule, require partners on cash seizures, and run integrity tests, the tactic New York adopted after Knapp, in which supervisors present officers with planted opportunities to steal.

Follow the money with the same energy used on the corner. Fund financial-crimes units, require officers in high-risk assignments to file financial disclosures, and prosecute the bankers and lawyers who launder cartel money as individuals, not just as institutions paying fines. And citizens like Rosa can push for something simple: a written response, within a set time, to every repeated complaint about the same address, with the case escalated outside the precinct when nothing changes.

Protect the honest officers, too, because they are the best early warning system any department has. Serpico was forced to go outside his own chain of command and then to the press before anyone listened. A state law can give officers who report corruption the same protection from retaliation that whistleblowers in other public jobs receive, along with a confidential channel to the inspector general rather than to the supervisors they may be reporting. A department that punishes the officer who speaks up is telling every corrupt officer that the code of silence still holds. One that promotes the honest witness is sending the opposite message, and organized crime hears it.

The takeaway

Criminal enterprises last because they learn to live inside the institutions sent to destroy them, through payoffs, through informant deals that become partnerships, and through neglect that leaves whole markets alone. Put independent watchdogs over police, put strict rules on informants, chase the money past the street corner, and the rackets lose the protection they have always counted on. Homeowners like Rosa will notice the difference first.

Chapter 29: How Institutions Shield Powerful Offenders From Accountability

For decades the paperwork looked the same. A complaint arrived at a diocesan office in Pennsylvania, sometimes from a parent, sometimes from a grown man who had finally found the words. Church officials met, wrote careful memos in euphemism, and sent the accused priest for "treatment" or to a new parish where nobody knew him. The family was urged to keep quiet for the good of the church. The police were not called. The file went into a cabinet, and the cabinet was locked.

Then, in August 2018, the cabinet opened. Pennsylvania's attorney general stood at a podium and released a grand jury report of nearly nine hundred pages. It named more than three hundred priests in six of the state's eight Catholic dioceses, identified more than a thousand child victims, and said the grand jury believed the true number was in the thousands. The same institutions that had spent generations managing the secret were now hearing their own memos read aloud on national television.

Religious Institutions

I covered the aftermath of that report, and what struck me hardest was not the scale. It was the method. The grand jury described a playbook that repeated across dioceses and decades: use soft words like "boundary issues" instead of rape, send abusers to church-run evaluation centers, give them housing and pensions, move them when rumors spread, and above all avoid scandal. The report found that church leaders had prioritized the institution's reputation over children, and it accused senior officials of a systematic cover-up.

Contrast the two moments again and the cost becomes plain. In the era of the locked cabinet, the abuse was preventable. A single call to police after the first credible complaint would have stopped many priests before they reached their second or third parish. By 2018, almost none of it could be prosecuted. Statutes of limitations had run out on nearly every case, which is exactly what delay is designed to accomplish. The grand jury recommended that Pennsylvania open a temporary window for old civil claims, and lawmakers there have repeatedly failed to finish the job.

The Boston Globe's Spotlight reporting had exposed the same pattern in Massachusetts in 2002, and in the years since, attorneys general and grand juries in other states have documented it again. I give credit where it is due: many dioceses now report allegations to civil authorities, run background checks and keep review boards that include lay people. Those reforms came because the cabinets were forced open, not because the institution opened them.

Entertainment and Sports Industries

Fame builds its own locked cabinet, and money keeps it shut. For years, the Hollywood producer Harvey Weinstein was the subject of whispers that never became headlines. In October 2017 reporting by The New York Times and The New Yorker laid out allegations from many women, along with the machinery that protected him: settlements, nondisclosure agreements, and employees who feared for their careers.

His company collapsed within months. A New York jury convicted him in 2020, but the state's highest court overturned that conviction in 2024, ruling that the trial judge had improperly allowed testimony about allegations that were not charged. At a 2025 retrial, a jury convicted him on one count of a criminal sexual act, acquitted him on another, and deadlocked on a rape count. He was separately convicted of rape in California in 2022. Whatever the final count in New York, the larger record is not in dispute: for years, people around him knew enough to worry, and the industry's lawyers, publicists and executives treated the problem as a reputation issue to be managed rather than a crime to be reported.

Sean Combs, the music mogul, followed a different path through the courts. A 2023 lawsuit by his former partner Cassie Ventura was settled within a day of filing, and in 2024 CNN aired a 2016 hotel security video showing him assaulting her in a hallway. Federal prosecutors charged him with racketeering conspiracy and sex trafficking. In July 2025 a Manhattan jury acquitted him of those most serious counts and convicted him on two counts of transporting people across state lines for prostitution. In October 2025 he was sentenced to fifty months in prison and fined half a million dollars, and he has appealed. The acquittals deserve respect. So does the judge's point at sentencing that a substantial term was needed to show that violence against women carries real consequences.

Elite sport proved just as protective. Larry Nassar, a team doctor for USA Gymnastics and a physician at Michigan State University, abused athletes for years while complaints went nowhere. When survivors finally spoke publicly, more than 150 women and girls gave statements at his sentencing hearing in Michigan in 2018, and he was sentenced to decades in prison, on top of a federal sentence for child pornography.

The money that followed measured the institutional failure. Michigan State agreed to pay half a billion dollars to survivors, and USA Gymnastics and the U.S. Olympic and Paralympic Committee later agreed to a settlement of hundreds of millions more. A 2021 report by the Justice Department's inspector general found that FBI officials had seriously mishandled the allegations after they were reported in 2015, and that Nassar abused more athletes while the bureau delayed. In 2024 the Justice Department agreed to pay about 139 million dollars to settle claims by survivors over the FBI's failures. Every institution in that chain, the federation, the university and the bureau, had a chance to stop him, and each one found a reason to wait.

Trafficking Networks

Jeffrey Epstein shows what happens when wealth meets a prosecutor willing to deal. In 2008, after police in Palm Beach had gathered evidence from many underage girls, the federal prosecutor's office in Miami, led by Alexander Acosta, signed a non-prosecution agreement with Epstein. He pleaded guilty to two state prostitution charges, one involving a minor, and served about thirteen months in a county jail, much of it on work release. The agreement also protected potential co-conspirators. His victims were not told the deal was coming.

A federal judge ruled in 2019 that prosecutors had violated the Crime Victims' Rights Act by keeping the victims in the dark. That ruling, and the Miami Herald's 2018 investigation by Julie K. Brown, helped bring federal sex trafficking charges against Epstein in New York in July 2019. He died in jail a month later, and the medical examiner ruled it a suicide. Acosta, by then the Secretary of Labor, resigned. A 2020 Justice Department review concluded he had exercised poor judgment rather than committing professional misconduct. His longtime associate Ghislaine Maxwell was convicted of sex trafficking in 2021 and sentenced to twenty years.

The lesson reaches past one man. Trafficking networks depend on people who could stop them and do not: the employee who books the travel, the lawyer who drafts the settlement, the prosecutor who decides a powerful defendant deserves a gentler path. The victims are almost always young, poor or both, which makes them easy to doubt. Every institution that lets a powerful man buy his way out sends the same message to the girls he targeted.

Youth Homes and Orphanages

Marcus was fourteen when a judge sent him to a state youth facility after a string of fights at school. He remembers the smell of floor wax and the night a staff member came into his room. When he told a counselor the next week, he was written up for lying and lost his phone call home. Thirty years later he sat at a long table in a state capitol hearing room, a grown man in a pressed shirt, and read a statement into the record. His hands shook. His voice did not. He told the committee the worst part was not the abuse. It was learning that the adults in charge had chosen the institution over him.

Marcus's story is a composite, and it matches a pattern documented in real places. In New Hampshire, more than a thousand former residents of the state's Youth Development Center have sued, alleging physical and sexual abuse stretching back decades. In 2024 a jury awarded one of them, David Meehan, thirty-eight million dollars. The state then argued that a statutory cap limited the award to 475,000 dollars, a dispute that went to the state Supreme Court and remained there as of my most recent sources. The state was fighting the very man it had failed to protect, using taxpayer money to shrink the bill for abuse that happened in a taxpayer-funded building.

Pennsylvania offers another case. In 2019 a Philadelphia Inquirer investigation reported that staff at the Glen Mills Schools, one of the oldest reform schools in the country, had beaten boys and threatened them into silence. Within weeks the state pulled the remaining students and revoked the school's licenses. Glen Mills had operated for well over a century and had been praised for its sports teams and its sprawling campus. Judges and probation offices from several states kept sending boys there while complaints circulated, because the reputation was easier to believe than the boys.

Youth organizations outside the justice system have told the same story. The Boy Scouts of America filed for bankruptcy in 2020, and more than 82,000 people filed abuse claims. A bankruptcy court approved a settlement trust of about 2.46 billion dollars in 2022. For decades, the organization had kept confidential files on suspected abusers without telling the public. It used them to keep known offenders out of scouting, which was a real if limited protection, but it did not routinely send those names to police or warn the communities where the men went next. A list that keeps a predator out of one troop while leaving him free to coach a team across town protects the organization, not the children.

Prison Systems

If children in custody are vulnerable, adults in prison are nearly invisible. At the federal women's prison in Dublin, California, abuse by staff was so widespread that inmates and staff called it "the rape club." The prison's former warden, Ray Garcia, was convicted by a jury in 2022 of sexually abusing women in his custody and sentenced to seventy months. Several other officers have been convicted or have pleaded guilty. In 2024 the Bureau of Prisons emptied the facility and later announced it would close for good, an admission that the institution could not be repaired.

The women who reported abuse at Dublin described a system built to punish them for speaking. Some said they were sent to solitary confinement or threatened with transfers far from their families after they came forward, and the officers they accused often supervised the very housing units where they lived. An incarcerated woman cannot quit, move out or call a lawyer on her own schedule. Her abuser controls her food, her mail and her disciplinary record. When the institution's first instinct is to protect its staff, a complaint becomes a risk only the victim pays for.

A 2022 bipartisan Senate investigation found that federal prison employees had sexually abused women in custody at most of the federal facilities that held women over the preceding decade, and that the Bureau of Prisons often failed to detect or prevent it. That record exists despite a law written to stop it. Congress passed the Prison Rape Elimination Act unanimously in 2003, and national standards followed in 2012. The law requires data collection, audits and zero-tolerance policies. Audits that look only at paperwork can pass a prison where the abusers write the paperwork.

The Constitution sets a floor. In Farmer v. Brennan in 1994, the Supreme Court held that prison officials violate the Eighth Amendment when they are deliberately indifferent to a substantial risk of serious harm to an inmate, and the case arose from the assault of a transgender prisoner. The ruling matters because it puts the duty on the institution, not only on the individual abuser. It also sets a high bar: a prisoner must show that officials actually knew of the risk and disregarded it, which is why so many lawsuits founder. Institutions learn quickly that the safest course, legally, is not to know.

Breaking the Shield

Every one of these institutions protected itself the same way: it kept the complaint inside. The first fix is to force it outside. Legislatures can require that any allegation of sexual abuse by an adult in a position of authority, whether in a church, a team, a youth facility or a prison, be reported directly to police and an outside agency, with criminal penalties for supervisors who bury it.

Time is the abuser's best friend, so take it away. Many states have extended or eliminated criminal statutes of limitations for child sexual abuse and opened temporary windows for old civil claims. New York's Child Victims Act, passed in 2019, opened such a window, and thousands of survivors filed claims. Pennsylvania's legislature should finish the work its grand jury asked for.

Critics warn that reopened windows can bankrupt dioceses and youth groups, and some have filed for bankruptcy as a result. That risk is real, but it is the price of decades of concealment, and courts can manage it through trusts that pay survivors fairly. A survivor who was fourteen when it happened should not lose the right to be heard because an institution was skilled at running out the clock.

Restrict secrecy. In the cases above, the abuse was hidden by paper as much as by fear: settlements, confidentiality clauses and sealed files. Laws can bar nondisclosure agreements from silencing victims of sexual abuse, and Congress passed the Speak Out Act in 2022 to limit pre-dispute nondisclosure clauses in sexual assault and harassment cases. Prosecutors can adopt written policies, so that no plea deal for a sex crime is signed without notice to the victims, as federal law already requires in federal cases. Epstein's victims had to win that point in court years after the fact.

Put independent eyes inside closed institutions. Self-policing failed in every system above, often because the people receiving complaints worked beside the people accused. Prison and youth facility oversight should include unannounced inspections by an outside inspector general with authority to interview residents in private. Lawmakers can fund it, and citizens can demand it at budget hearings, the way survivors like Marcus did when they finally got a seat at the table.

The takeaway

Institutions protect abusers because exposure threatens the institution. Take the decision away from them: mandatory outside reporting, no clock running out on child victims, no secret deals, and independent inspectors behind every locked door. The powerful will always have lawyers and friends. Survivors need rules that do not depend on anyone's courage inside the institution, and the cabinet stays open only if the law keeps it open.

Chapter 30: The Offenders We Don't Picture: Abuse by Educators

"So when I finally told my buddies," Marcus said, stirring a coffee he never drank, "the first thing one of them said was, 'Lucky you.'" He laughed when he said it, the way men laugh at something that still has teeth. He was forty-one, a union electrician, a father of two, and he had asked to meet me at a diner off the highway because he did not want his kids to overhear. When he was fifteen, a teacher at his high school had started keeping him after class. She was twenty-eight. By spring she was driving him to her apartment. He told no adult for twenty-five years.

What struck me was not the abuse itself. In my years on the crime beat I have heard worse, sitting across from people in kitchens, waiting rooms and courthouse hallways. What struck me was how completely the story had been stripped of its weight by everyone around him. His friends congratulated him. A girlfriend in his twenties called it every boy's fantasy. The one counselor he tried to tell, years later, changed the subject. Marcus had been robbed twice: once by the woman who used him, and again by a culture that could not see a crime when the offender was a woman and the victim was a boy.

Abuse in Trusted Professions

Ask a room of parents to picture a child predator and most of them will describe the same figure: a man, a stranger, someone lurking at the edge of a park or behind a screen. The image is comforting precisely because it is foreign. It puts the danger outside the circle of people we trust. The reality, documented by decades of child-protection research, is that most children who are sexually abused know the person who abuses them. The offender is a relative, a family friend, a coach, a youth pastor, a scout leader, a teacher. Access is the raw material of the crime, and trusted professions supply access by design.

Schools are the largest of those professions. Tens of millions of American children spend roughly seven hours a day, nine months a year, in the care of adults who are not their parents. Most of those adults are decent, overworked people who would throw themselves in front of a bus for their students. I have met hundreds of them, and I believe that. Yet the very structure that makes teaching valuable makes it attractive to the small number of people who want to exploit children: private conversations, after-school help, field trips, text threads, the emotional authority of the adult who grades you and writes your college recommendation.

The churches learned this lesson the hard way, and so did youth sports. The public now understands, at least in broad strokes, that a priest or a gymnastics doctor can be a predator. Schools have largely escaped that reckoning. When a teacher is arrested, the story runs as a local item, a single bad apple, and then the news cycle moves on. Nobody tallies the pattern. Nobody asks how many districts quietly handled the same thing the same way, which is how an institution keeps a scandal from ever becoming one.

What the Research on Educator Misconduct Shows

The most important document on this subject was written more than twenty years ago, and most Americans have never heard of it. In 2004, Charol Shakeshaft, a researcher at Hofstra University, delivered a report to the U.S. Department of Education, which Congress had requested under the No Child Left Behind Act. Her assignment was to gather what was known about educator sexual misconduct. Her central finding was that almost nothing was known, because almost no one had looked.

To estimate the scale, Shakeshaft reanalyzed national survey data collected for the American Association of University Women from students in grades eight through eleven. Close to one student in ten reported having experienced some form of sexual misconduct from a teacher, coach or other school employee at some point in school, ranging from sexual comments and exposure to unwanted touching and intercourse. The figure rests on a single survey of a couple of thousand students, and critics were right to say it needed replication. The point stands anyway: even a fraction of that estimate would describe millions of children.

Her report drew immediate fire. Teachers' organizations worried it would smear an honorable profession, and some commentators accused her of manufacturing a panic. I understand the instinct to protect teachers; I share it. But the honest response to a weak data set is to build a strong one, and that is the part the country skipped. Two decades later there is still no national system that counts educator sexual misconduct the way we count school shootings or bus accidents. We have better data on cafeteria sales than on the adults who abuse students.

What the research did establish, across the studies Shakeshaft reviewed, is a pattern. Offenders tend to be well liked. They are often decorated teachers, popular coaches, the adult students seek out. They groom not only the child but the surrounding adults, building a reputation that makes later complaints sound absurd. And the misconduct usually begins with boundary violations that colleagues notice and excuse: the closed door, the special favors, the late-night messages, the student who is always in that one classroom at lunch.

The phone in every student's pocket has made grooming faster and quieter. A generation ago an abuser needed a closed classroom; now a private channel opens the moment a teacher and a student exchange numbers or follow each other on social media. Messages arrive at midnight, compliments become confidences, confidences become secrets, and the secret becomes the leash. Prosecutors I have spoken with describe case files thick with screenshots of conversations that began with homework help. Parents rarely see any of it, because the whole point of the channel is that they cannot. A district that has no rule about private messaging between staff and students has, in effect, left a side door unlocked.

The Female Offender Blind Spot

Most educators who abuse students are men. I want to be plain about that so that nothing here is mistaken for a claim that women are the bigger danger. They are not. The argument is narrower and, I think, more troubling: women who abuse students are recognized less, reported less, and punished less, and the boys they target are often not treated as victims at all. In the survey data Shakeshaft reanalyzed, women made up a substantial minority of the offenders students described, something like four in ten. Other studies she reviewed put the female share much lower. The spread itself tells you how little anyone had bothered to measure.

That claim is about recognition, not about numbers. A crime can be relatively rare and still badly under-recognized, and when it is, the people who commit it get extra room to work. The female teacher who sends a fourteen-year-old boy flirtatious messages is less likely to draw a colleague's suspicion than a male teacher doing the same thing with a girl. If she is caught, she is more likely to be described as having had an "affair" or a "relationship" with the student, language no newsroom would tolerate in the reverse case. Words matter here. A child cannot have an affair with his teacher. He can only be abused by one.

Two cases, one in Washington and one in Florida, taught the country how to misread these crimes. In 1997, Mary Kay Letourneau, a thirty-four-year-old elementary teacher in the Seattle suburb of Burien, pleaded guilty to two counts of second-degree child rape involving a boy who had been her student. He was twelve when the abuse began. A judge suspended most of her sentence on the condition of treatment; within weeks of her release she was found with the boy again, and the full prison term of more than seven years was imposed. She later married him. Much of the coverage, then and for years after, described the case as a forbidden romance.

Debra Lafave was a twenty-three-year-old middle school teacher in the Tampa area when she was arrested in 2004 for sexual activity with a fourteen-year-old student. In 2005 she pleaded guilty to lewd and lascivious battery and received three years of house arrest and seven years of probation, with no prison time. Her lawyer argued publicly that a young, attractive woman would face unusual danger behind bars, and a great deal of the national conversation centered on her looks. A second set of charges in another county was eventually dropped after a judge rejected the plea deal and prosecutors decided the boy should be spared testifying at a trial.

Reverse the sexes in either case and imagine the coverage. A thirty-four-year-old male teacher who raped a twelve-year-old girl would not be the subject of a romantic television movie. A male teacher's lawyer who argued his client was too handsome for prison would be laughed out of the courtroom and into the history books. I have sat in courtrooms where men convicted of comparable conduct with students received prison terms measured in decades. I am not arguing those men deserved less. I am arguing that the law is supposed to measure the harm to the child, and the child's harm does not shrink because the adult wore a skirt.

Researchers who study sentencing have found the same tilt across the system: women tend to receive lighter sentences than men for similar offenses, and the gap appears in sexual crimes as well. Some of that difference reflects real factors, such as criminal history. Some of it reflects a story the culture tells itself about who can hurt a child. That story reaches the police officer deciding whether a complaint is worth pursuing and the prosecutor deciding what charge to file. It reaches the jury. It reaches the school principal who hears a rumor about a young female teacher and decides it is probably a crush.

The deepest damage lands on boys. Research on male survivors of childhood sexual abuse finds that they often take years longer than girls to disclose, if they disclose at all, and that the harms are real and familiar: depression, substance abuse, trouble with intimacy, a corrosive confusion about what happened to them. A boy abused by a woman is told, in a hundred small ways, that he was lucky, that he must have wanted it, that real men do not complain. Shakeshaft's data suggested that boys made up a large share of the students who reported misconduct, which ought to have shocked the country. It barely registered.

Marcus put it to me in a way I have never forgotten. He said that if a man had done to him what that teacher did, he would at least have had a word for it. Because it was a woman, he had a joke instead. He spent his twenties drinking, wrecked two relationships, and could not explain to himself why the memory made his chest go tight. When he finally saw a therapist, the first thing she said was that what happened to him was a crime. He told me he sat in his truck afterward and cried for an hour.

"Passing the Trash" - How Schools Move Offenders On

The phrase is ugly because the practice is ugly. "Passing the trash" describes what happens when a school district learns that an employee has sexually abused or harassed a student and, instead of reporting the employee to police and to the state licensing board, negotiates a quiet exit. The teacher resigns. The district agrees to seal the file, or to give a neutral reference, or even a positive one. The teacher applies somewhere else, often in another district or another state, and the new employer has no idea. Shakeshaft's work helped popularize the term because she found the practice was common, not exceptional.

Every party to that bargain gets something it wants except the children. The district avoids a lawsuit, a union grievance, bad press, and the cost of a contested firing. The teacher avoids prosecution. The administrators avoid being the people who let it happen on their watch. And the next school inherits a predator with a clean record and a letter of recommendation. I have read personnel files in which an administrator described a man as an asset to any school community while a separation agreement in the same folder recorded the misconduct he was being paid to go away over.

In 2016, a USA Today Network investigation found that the national clearinghouse states use to share information on disciplined teachers was missing thousands of records, including hundreds of educators whose licenses had been revoked over allegations of sexual or physical abuse. Reporting to the clearinghouse was voluntary in practice, and some states reported little. The reporters found teachers who had lost credentials in one place and gone on to work with children in another. The database was not a firewall. It was a sieve with a government seal.

Congress finally acted in the Every Student Succeeds Act of 2015. A provision now codified as section 8546 of the federal education law requires states that take federal education money to have laws or policies forbidding any school employee from helping a person get a new job when the employee knows, or has probable cause to believe, that person sexually abused a minor. It was a real step. But a federal study published in 2019 found that only a handful of states had fully complied, and most had no plans to pass new legislation, with some claiming their existing laws were already enough. A ban that is not enforced is a press release.

Institutional Protection and Public Blindness

The legal system has made it easier for schools to look away. In Gebser v. Lago Vista Independent School District, decided in 1998, the Supreme Court considered a Texas case in which a high school teacher had a sexual relationship with a student; it came to light only when a police officer happened upon the two of them. The student sued the district under Title IX, the federal law against sex discrimination in education. By a five-to-four vote, the Court held that a district can be made to pay damages for a teacher's abuse only if an official with authority to stop it had actual knowledge of the misconduct and responded with deliberate indifference.

The majority had reasons I can respect. Title IX is a funding law, and the justices did not want districts held liable for misconduct they had no way of knowing about. The dissenters warned of the consequence, and they were right. A rule that rewards actual knowledge and punishes only deliberate indifference gives every administrator an incentive not to know. Do not ask about the closed door. Do not write down the rumor. Do not open the anonymous note. Ignorance becomes a legal strategy, and in too many districts it became policy.

Public blindness does the rest. Parents want to believe their children's school is safe, so they resist the idea that a beloved teacher could be dangerous. Colleagues do not want to accuse a friend, and they fear being wrong. Unions, doing their job of protecting members, push for due process that sometimes stretches into protection. School boards, often volunteers, are told by their lawyers to say nothing. And when the offender is a woman, every one of those filters runs stronger, because the accusation sounds so unlikely to everyone hearing it.

I watched that blindness operate in the way communities talk after an arrest. When the accused is a man, neighbors ask how they missed it. When the accused is a young woman, they ask whether the boy is telling the truth, whether he bragged, whether he started it. Comment sections fill with jokes. Talk-radio hosts wish they had had teachers like that. Each of those reactions tells the next boy in the next school exactly what will happen if he speaks up, and most of them draw the obvious conclusion and keep quiet.

I want to credit the good faith here, because it exists. Most administrators who handled a case quietly told themselves they were protecting the student's privacy, the families' wishes, or a staff member's right to a fair process. Some of them were genuinely terrified of a false accusation ruining an innocent career, which does happen and is its own injustice. But good intentions running through a broken system still produce the same outcome: the offender keeps working with children, and the next victim pays for everyone's discretion.

The Cost of Ignoring Patterns

The price of that discretion is paid in several currencies. The first is the child's life. Survivors of childhood sexual abuse face higher rates of depression, anxiety, addiction, self-harm and suicide, along with lost education and lost earnings. A student who is being abused by a teacher often stops going to that class, then to school. Grades fall. Some drop out. The adult who was supposed to open doors for them has nailed them shut, and the student is the one blamed for failing.

The second currency is money, and it comes from taxpayers. When an abuse case finally breaks open, districts face lawsuits that can run into millions of dollars per victim, and the settlements are paid out of insurance pools, reserves and, ultimately, public budgets. Some states have extended or reopened the window for adult survivors to sue, which has brought waves of old claims against public school districts. Every dollar spent paying for a cover-up is a dollar not spent on a reading specialist or a school counselor. The quiet exit that saved a district a lawsuit in one decade buys a far larger one in the next.

The third currency is trust, and it may be the most expensive. Every time a community learns that a school knew and did nothing, parents lose faith in the institution that is supposed to be the backbone of a neighborhood. Good teachers pay that price too. They are suspected, monitored and second-guessed because their predecessors were protected. The profession that should be most respected becomes the one parents watch most nervously.

The pattern is the crime's best friend. One complaint looks like a misunderstanding. Two look like a coincidence. Only when someone lines up the complaints across years, schools and states does the shape of a predator appear. Our system is built to keep those complaints apart: separate personnel files, sealed agreements, state databases that do not talk to each other, and a court rule that rewards not knowing. If you designed a machine to hide serial offenders inside schools, it would look a great deal like what we have.

Screening and Reporting Reforms That Work

The fixes are known, they are mostly cheap, and some states are already using them. Start with hiring. Pennsylvania's Act 168 of 2014, often called its pass-the-trash law, requires schools to complete an employment history review before hiring anyone who will have direct contact with children. Applicants must disclose whether they were ever investigated for abuse or sexual misconduct, disciplined for it, or pushed out while such allegations were pending, and former employers must answer. Confidentiality agreements that hide such conduct are void. Every legislature that has not copied that model should, and should add penalties for districts that lie.

Close the database gaps next. Every state should be required, not invited, to report every license revocation and surrender tied to sexual misconduct to the national clearinghouse within a set number of days, and every district should check it before every hire, including substitutes, coaches and contractors. After the USA Today investigation, the organization that runs the clearinghouse moved to audit its records and open access to local districts. That is a start. Congress could make continued federal education funding depend on proof of full reporting, the same lever it already holds over section 8546.

Enforce the federal ban that already exists. The Department of Education can audit states' compliance with section 8546, publish the results by name, and withhold funds from states that ignore it. Legislatures can make it a crime for an administrator to help an abuser obtain a new job, and some have. A superintendent who signs a secret separation agreement with a known abuser should face the same consequences as any other accessory, and should lose the license that let them run a school.

Change the reporting culture inside buildings. Every state already makes teachers mandated reporters of suspected child abuse, but training is often a slideshow once a year. Strong districts train staff to recognize grooming, not just abuse, and adopt written boundary policies: no private social media messaging with students, no rides home without permission, doors with windows, two adults on overnight trips. Those rules protect honest teachers from false suspicion as much as they protect children. They also give colleagues something concrete to report, which is far easier than accusing a friend of a crime.

Finally, teach the whole picture, including the part we would rather skip. Prevention programs for students should tell boys, in plain language, that an adult woman who touches them sexually is committing a crime, that it is not luck and not their fault, and that they will be believed. Police and prosecutors should be trained that female offenders exist and that a boy's victimization is not a punchline. Citizens can do their part at the school board meeting: ask whether your district checks the clearinghouse, whether it has ever signed a confidentiality agreement with an employee accused of misconduct, and what its boundary policy says. Then ask for it in writing.

Marcus went to his own children's school board last year and asked those questions. Nobody on the board knew whether the district checked the clearinghouse for coaches and substitutes. Three months later it did, and the board adopted a written boundary policy covering texting, rides and closed doors. He told me it was the first time in twenty-five years that what happened to him had produced anything useful. That is the shape of the fix: not outrage that fades with the news cycle, but ordinary citizens asking specific questions until the rules change.

The takeaway

The predator we picture is a stranger; the one we miss is the trusted adult, and sometimes she is a woman. Demand employment history checks, full clearinghouse reporting, and real enforcement of the federal pass-the-trash ban in your state, and believe boys when they tell you.

Chapter 31: Law Enforcement Does Not Investigate Telephone Crimes

The phone rang in Dolores's kitchen a little after nine on a Tuesday morning, while the kettle was still ticking as it cooled. The voice on the line was young, frightened and crying, and it said, "Grandma, it's me. I'm in trouble." Then a calm man took the phone and introduced himself as a public defender. Her grandson had been in a car accident, he said, a pregnant woman was hurt, and there was a gag order, so she must not tell anyone, not even his parents. Bail was nine thousand dollars. A courier would come to her house to collect it in cash.

Dolores was seventy-eight, a retired school secretary, a widow who still mowed her own lawn. She went to the bank, withdrew the money, and handed an envelope to a polite young man in a hooded sweatshirt on her own front porch. The next day the same lawyer called back: the woman had lost the baby, and bail had gone up. She went to the bank again. By the time her son stopped by that weekend and asked why she looked so pale, she had lost most of the savings her husband left her. Her grandson had been at college the whole time, asleep in his dorm.

Her son drove her to the police station. A patrol officer took the report kindly and gave her a case number. Then he told her, in so many words, that there was almost nothing anyone could do. The callers were probably overseas. The money was gone. She should call her bank, report it to a federal website, and be careful next time. Nobody ever called her back. In my years on the crime beat I have heard some version of that conversation more times than I can count, and I have come to believe it is one of the most quietly devastating sentences in American policing.

The Rise of Phone-Based Fraud

The telephone scam is old. The con artist with a smooth voice has been around as long as there have been phones. What changed in the last two decades is the industrial scale. Cheap internet calling made it possible to dial millions of American numbers from anywhere on earth for almost nothing. Spoofing tools let a caller make any number appear on a victim's screen, including the local sheriff's office or the victim's own bank. Data breaches and people-search websites hand scammers names, addresses and relatives. Fraud stopped being a hustle and became an export business.

The scripts evolved with the tools. Callers now impersonate Social Security officials, Medicare, utility companies, a bank's fraud department, Amazon, a sheriff's deputy collecting a fine for missed jury duty. Some ask for remote access to a victim's computer; others walk victims step by step to a cryptocurrency machine at a gas station. The newest twist uses cloned voices. A few seconds of audio from a social media video can be enough for software to imitate a grandson or daughter well enough to fool a frightened parent. The con has not changed. The costume has gotten better.

No case shows the shape of that business more clearly than the fake IRS calls. In October 2016, the Justice Department charged sixty-one people and companies in the United States and India in what it called a first-of-its-kind takedown of call centers in Ahmedabad, India. Callers there posed as IRS or immigration officers and threatened Americans with arrest, deportation or seizure of their homes unless they paid immediately, often with prepaid cards. The government said more than fifteen thousand people had lost hundreds of millions of dollars. A network of runners inside the United States bought prepaid cards with victims' money and laundered the proceeds.

That case is worth remembering because it worked. In 2018, a federal judge in Houston sentenced more than twenty defendants, some to as long as twenty years in prison. The prosecution took years, required cooperation between the Treasury Department's tax inspectors, Homeland Security and the Justice Department, and followed the money from Indian call floors to American strip malls. It proved that phone fraud can be solved. It also proved what solving it costs, which is exactly why almost no local department ever tries.

The grandparent scam that hit Dolores runs on the same model. In early 2025, a federal grand jury in Vermont indicted twenty-five Canadian nationals, most from the Montreal area, on charges of running call centers that targeted elderly Americans in more than forty states. Prosecutors alleged the scheme took more than twenty-one million dollars from older people who believed they were bailing out a grandchild. The defendants are presumed innocent unless proven guilty. The allegations themselves read like a script: a panicked young voice, a fake lawyer, a gag order, a courier at the door.

Why Agencies Lack Tools and Training

Walk into the detective bureau of a typical American police department and ask who works phone fraud. You will usually get a blank look. Most departments are small; a large share of American agencies employ fewer than twenty-five sworn officers. They have detectives for burglary, assault and narcotics, and maybe one person who handles everything with a financial component. That detective is buried under check fraud and stolen credit cards from local businesses. A call from Quebec or Gujarat to a widow's landline is, practically speaking, beyond her reach.

The obstacles are real, and I want to give the officers their due. Phone fraud crosses jurisdictions in a single call. The evidence sits with telecommunications carriers, internet calling providers, gift card companies, banks and cryptocurrency exchanges, each of which demands its own subpoena or warrant and answers on its own schedule. The suspects are often outside the country. A department that spends a hundred hours on a case may never arrest anyone. A national police research group warned years ago that crime was moving onto phones and computers faster than investigative training was moving with it, and that federal, state and local agencies would have to work together in new ways.

The trouble is that most agencies heard that warning and changed nothing. Police training academies still devote a sliver of their hours to financial crime and almost none to tracing calls, payment rails or money mules. Departments are measured on arrests and clearance rates, and a phone scam case drags both numbers down. Federal agencies, for their part, typically take cases only above a high dollar threshold or connected to a larger ring. Between the local officer who cannot and the federal agent who will not, there is a gap wide enough to drive a courier's car through.

I asked a small-town police chief once why his department did not even try to trace the calls. He was not defensive. He told me he had nine officers to cover three shifts, a drug problem, a rash of car break-ins and a city council that judged him by how fast patrol cars showed up. A phone fraud case would take one of his people off the street for weeks and probably end with a dead end in another country. He was making a rational choice inside an irrational system, and his town's grandmothers were paying for it.

Courts have narrowed some of the private tools too. In Facebook v.
