Duguid, decided in 2021, the Supreme Court unanimously read the federal autodialer ban in the Telephone Consumer Protection Act narrowly, holding that it covers only equipment that stores or produces numbers using a random or sequential number generator. The case involved unwanted text alerts, not scams, and the Court's reading of the statute was a fair one. The practical effect, though, was to shrink one of the private lawsuits consumers had used against mass callers, leaving more of the job to regulators and police who were already overwhelmed.

The Scale of Unreported Losses

Every year the Federal Trade Commission publishes what Americans report losing to fraud, and every year the number sets a record. Reported losses passed twelve billion dollars in 2024, and coverage of the 2025 figures put them higher still. Phone calls are not the most common way scammers first make contact anymore, because texts and social media have overtaken them, but the FTC has repeatedly found that the people who lose money after a phone call tend to lose more per person than people contacted any other way. A voice persuades in a way an email does not.

Those reported numbers are the floor, not the ceiling. Most fraud victims never report at all. Some do not know where to go. Some are too ashamed. Some, like Dolores after her first visit to the station, conclude that reporting is pointless. The FTC itself has estimated that the true losses to older Americans alone, once unreported fraud is accounted for, run into the tens of billions of dollars a year. If burglary took that much from American households, it would be a national emergency with its own task forces and its own campaign slogans.

The shame deserves its own sentence, because scammers count on it. Victims of phone fraud are often treated, even by their own families, as foolish rather than robbed. The professionals running these call floors use scripts refined over thousands of calls, with supervisors, quotas and rehearsed answers to every objection. A retired engineer or a former bank officer can fall for them as easily as anyone, and many do. Calling a victim gullible is like calling a burglary victim careless for owning a door. It protects nobody and keeps the next victim quiet.

The losses are not just money. I have interviewed older people who stopped answering the phone entirely, cutting themselves off from their own families. I have met adult children who took over their parents' finances after a scam and watched their parents lose independence and dignity in the same month. Doctors who study older adults link financial exploitation to depression, declining health and earlier moves into care. When a fraud ring cleans out a retirement account, the bill eventually reaches taxpayers too, through Medicaid nursing home costs and public benefits for someone who used to be self-sufficient.

Vulnerable Populations Targeted

Scammers do not dial at random for long. They buy lists, and the lists are sorted by vulnerability. Older adults are prime targets because they are more likely to have savings, own a home, answer a landline and be raised to be polite to a stranger. Immigrants are targeted with calls impersonating immigration authorities or consulates, because the fear of deportation shuts down skepticism. Small business owners get calls threatening to cut off their electricity within the hour. Each script is engineered to produce one emotion strong enough to override judgment: fear, love or shame.

Families in crisis are targeted most cruelly of all. After Nancy Guthrie, the mother of NBC "Today" co-host Savannah Guthrie, disappeared from her home near Tucson in February 2026, ransom notes demanding cryptocurrency were sent to news outlets, and the family pleaded publicly for her safe return. Within days, according to federal court records, a California man sent two family members text messages through an internet calling account that posed as a ransom demand and asked whether the bitcoin had been sent. He was charged, pleaded guilty in July 2026 to telecommunications harassment counts and was sentenced to probation. The complaint said his texts were not connected to the earlier ransom notes. Her disappearance, as of the most recent reporting I have seen, remains unsolved.

What that case shows is how fast a predator can reach a family in its worst hour, and how easy it is to hide behind a phone number. It also shows what happens when investigators take a telephone crime seriously: the FBI traced the messages through an email account to a house and made an arrest. The Guthrie family had the world's attention. A widow in a small town who gets the same kind of call does not, and that difference, not the technology, decides whether anyone follows the trail.

Building a Modern Response

The first fix is the simplest: stop sending victims away with a case number. Every police department should have a written protocol for phone fraud that includes calling the victim's bank immediately to freeze transfers, logging the scammer's phone numbers and payment details, and routing the report to the FTC, the FBI's internet crime center and the state attorney general the same day. Speed matters more than anything else. Money that has not yet left a bank or a gift card account can sometimes be stopped. Money that sat for a week is gone.

Second, states should build regional fraud units instead of expecting every small department to grow its own expert. A county or state task force with a few trained investigators, a prosecutor and an analyst can see patterns that no single town ever will: the same courier, the same spoofed number, the same mule account. Several states and large counties already run elder financial abuse teams that bring police, prosecutors, social workers and bankers to the same table, and the Justice Department runs a national elder fraud hotline that helps victims file and connects them to services. Legislatures should fund these units by the losses they prevent, not the arrests they make.

Third, put pressure on the choke points the scammers cannot avoid. Congress passed the TRACED Act in 2019, and the Federal Communications Commission used it to require major carriers to adopt the STIR/SHAKEN call-authentication framework by mid-2021, so that spoofed calls are easier to flag and block. It has helped, though scammers have shifted to texts and to smaller carriers that lag behind. Regulators should keep closing those gaps and hold providers accountable for carrying fraudulent traffic. Gift card sellers, banks and cryptocurrency kiosks should be required to warn and pause large, unusual transactions by older customers, and some retailers and banks already train cashiers and tellers to step in.

Fourth, make the courier the weak link. The grandparent scam depends on someone showing up at a front door to collect cash. Local police can catch that person, and departments that set up controlled pickups with cooperative victims have made arrests that unravel whole rings. Every courier arrested is a phone, a contact list and a chain of payments that leads upward. Prosecutors should charge them seriously, and federal partners should treat local arrests as the start of a case rather than the end of one.

Fifth, measure what matters. A city council cannot fix what it does not count. Every department should publish how many fraud reports it receives each year, how many were referred onward, how much money was frozen or recovered, and how many arrests followed. Those numbers will look bad at first. Good. Bad numbers create budgets, and a published count turns a victim's private humiliation into a public problem that someone in city hall is responsible for solving.

Finally, the most effective defense is cheap and belongs to every citizen. Families can agree on a code word that any relative in real trouble will know. Older parents can be told, plainly and repeatedly, that no court, government agency or lawyer collects bail with gift cards, cryptocurrency or a courier at the door. Churches, senior centers and libraries can run short scam-awareness sessions, and many already do. City councils can require that every fraud report get a follow-up call within a week. Dolores now runs one of those sessions at her senior center. She tells the room what happened to her without flinching, and she ends the same way every time: hang up, call your family, then call the police and insist they write it down.

The takeaway

Phone fraud is solvable when someone bothers to chase it. Demand a real fraud protocol from your police department, push your state to fund a regional fraud unit, and set a family code word tonight.

Chapter 32: Law Enforcement Does Not Investigate Cybercrime

Americans now report losing several times more money to crimes committed through computers and phones than they lose to burglary and robbery combined, and the police department in a typical American town has no one assigned to investigate any of it. That sentence sounds like an exaggeration. It is not. In 2025, the FBI's Internet Crime Complaint Center logged more than a million complaints for the first time in its history, with reported losses of roughly twenty-one billion dollars. Federal estimates of what burglars and robbers take run to a small fraction of that.

Those are only the losses people bothered to report to a federal website. The FBI itself says many victims never do. And yet, if your bank account is drained tonight by someone who tricked you online, the most likely response from your local police department will be a report, a case number and advice to call your bank. I have watched that ritual play out for years. It is the bureaucratic equivalent of a shrug, and it is the standard American response to what is, by dollars, one of the largest crime waves of our time.

Ray learned that the hard way, twice. He is a retired machinist who has lived in the same brick ranch house for thirty-four years and pays his property taxes the week the bill arrives. One spring, his county government's computer network was locked by ransomware. For weeks, residents could not pay water bills online, the courthouse delayed hearings, and the 911 center fell back on paper cards. The county eventually spent millions restoring systems and buying protection it should have had already. Ray's tax bill went up the following year. Nobody was ever arrested.

Cybercrime as a Major Crime Category

The numbers have been climbing for a decade, and the climb is steep. Reported losses to the FBI's complaint center rose by roughly a quarter from 2024 to 2025 alone. Investment fraud, much of it run through fake cryptocurrency platforms, accounted for the largest share, around eight and a half billion dollars. Business email compromise, in which criminals impersonate a boss or a vendor and redirect payments, took billions more. Cryptocurrency figured in more than half of all reported losses. Americans sixty and older reported losing well over seven billion dollars, more than any other age group.

Ransomware is the form most likely to make the evening news, because it shuts things down. In May 2021, a criminal group known as DarkSide broke into Colonial Pipeline's computer systems, and the company halted the pipeline that carries a large share of the fuel used on the East Coast. Gas lines formed and stations ran dry across several states. Colonial paid a ransom of about seventy-five bitcoin, worth around four and a half million dollars at the time. A month later the Justice Department announced it had traced the payment and seized most of it from a single cryptocurrency wallet.

That seizure matters, and I will return to it, because it shows what is possible. But Colonial was a national emergency involving critical infrastructure, and it drew the full attention of the FBI and the White House. Every week, smaller victims are hit with the same tools: hospitals, school districts, county governments like Ray's, dental offices, family manufacturers. Most never get a federal agent. Many quietly pay. The criminals reinvest the proceeds in better tools, and the cycle runs again.

Ransomware has become a franchise business. Developers build the malicious software and rent it to affiliates, who break into networks and split the ransom with the developers. Brokers sell stolen passwords and network access to whoever pays. Negotiators handle the extortion. The whole operation runs like a company with a customer service desk, because that is what it is. When a county board in a small state pays a ransom, it is not buying back its files from a lone hacker. It is paying an invoice to an industry that will use the money to hit the next county.

Undertrained and Underequipped Agencies

The typical American police department was built to answer calls in a defined geographic area. Cybercrime has no geography. A victim in Ohio can be robbed by a crew in Eastern Europe using servers in three other countries and a money mule in Florida, all in an afternoon. The local detective assigned to the case, if there is one, usually lacks the training to read server logs, follow money across cryptocurrency exchanges, or write the legal requests that providers in other states and countries will honor. Most departments do not have a digital forensics lab. Many share one with the county or the state and wait months for results.

A national police research group warned in 2018 that while traditional crime measures had been falling, new crimes enabled by computers were proliferating, and that police training and investigative practice had not caught up. Police leaders I have interviewed say the same thing in plainer words: their officers are trained to handle a bar fight, not a bitcoin wallet. Academy curricula still spend weeks on firearms and driving and only hours, if that, on digital crime. Officers who do acquire cyber skills are often hired away by private companies that pay far more.

Federal agencies fill part of the gap, but only part. The FBI, the Secret Service and Homeland Security Investigations all run cyber units and task forces, and they do impressive work on the largest cases. They also triage hard. A loss of a few thousand dollars, even when it is a family's entire savings, rarely meets the threshold for federal attention unless it connects to a larger ring. The victim falls into the space between a local agency that cannot investigate and a federal agency that will not.

The law has its own gaps. In Van Buren v. United States, decided in 2021, the Supreme Court considered the case of a Georgia police sergeant who was paid to run a license-plate search in a law enforcement database he was authorized to use. The Court held, six to three, that the federal Computer Fraud and Abuse Act's ban on exceeding authorized access applies to people who enter parts of a system that are off-limits to them, not to people who misuse information they are entitled to see.

The reading protected ordinary workers from being prosecuted for breaking an employer's computer-use policy, a legitimate concern. It also meant that prosecutors facing insider abuse have to reach for other laws, and it is one more reminder that the nation's main anti-hacking statute was written in the 1980s, before most Americans had heard of the internet, and has been patched rather than rebuilt ever since.

Equipment is the other half of the problem. Digital forensic tools that extract data from phones and computers are expensive, licenses must be renewed every year, and the software has to be updated constantly to keep up with new devices. A small department may own one outdated kit or none. Evidence that should be examined in days sits on a shelf for months while a regional lab works through its backlog, which in the meantime has filled with phones from homicide and child exploitation cases that understandably go first. A fraud victim's laptop goes to the back of the line and usually stays there.

Why Cybercrime Is Ignored

Some of the neglect is about structure, and some of it is about incentives. Police departments are judged by response times, arrests and clearance rates. A cyber case takes months, involves out-of-state subpoenas, and very often ends without an arrest because the suspect is beyond reach. From the narrow perspective of a department's statistics, investigating cybercrime looks like a losing bet. A chief who assigns three officers to cyber cases can expect criticism for slower patrol response long before anyone praises the frauds they disrupted.

Part of it is cultural. Many people inside and outside law enforcement still treat online fraud as something that happens to careless people. The victim clicked the link. The victim believed the romance. The victim sent the money. That attitude would be unthinkable for a mugging. Nobody asks a robbery victim why they walked down the street. Yet I have watched officers lecture fraud victims about their gullibility while taking the report, which teaches victims that reporting is a humiliation and keeps the crimes out of the official count.

There is also a visibility problem, and it feeds everything else. Burglaries produce broken windows. Assaults produce emergency room visits. Cybercrime produces a bank statement that a victim may hide from their own children. Because it is invisible, it does not drive local politics. City council members hear about car break-ins at every meeting; they rarely hear about the retiree who lost two hundred thousand dollars to a fake investment platform. Officials respond to what they hear about. Criminals have learned to commit the crime nobody hears about.

I do not think the officers who shrug are bad people. Most of them sincerely want to help and know they cannot. Some have taken the same report from the same kind of victim fifty times and watched every one of those cases go nowhere. The failure is not individual laziness. It is a system that built its budgets, training and statistics around the crimes of 1975 and has refused to rebuild them around the crimes of today, while the money Americans lose keeps flowing out of the country by the billions.

The Explosion of Digital Harm

The most destructive cyber fraud in America now goes by a crude name: pig butchering. The scam begins with a friendly message, often a supposed wrong number or a match on a dating app. Over weeks or months the scammer builds trust, sometimes romance, then introduces an investment opportunity in cryptocurrency on a slick platform that shows enormous returns. The returns are fiction. When the victim has been fattened with confidence and has invested everything, the platform freezes the account and demands more money in fake taxes or fees. Then it disappears.

The scale of the industry behind it is staggering. In October 2025, federal prosecutors in Brooklyn indicted Chen Zhi, the chairman of Cambodia's Prince Group, on wire fraud and money laundering conspiracy charges, alleging that his company ran forced-labor compounds where trafficked workers were made to run these scams on victims worldwide. The government filed to forfeit roughly 127,000 bitcoin, valued at about fifteen billion dollars at the time, the largest forfeiture action in the Justice Department's history. The Treasury Department sanctioned the company. The charges are allegations, and he is presumed innocent unless proven guilty. The case shows that the scam on a stranger's phone can be the front end of an organized enterprise on the scale of a cartel.

The damage can reach an entire community through one person. Shan Hanes was the chief executive of Heartland Tri-State Bank in Elkhart, Kansas, a small community bank serving farmers and families in a corner of the state where everyone knows the banker. In 2023, caught in a pig-butchering scheme, he wired roughly forty-seven million dollars of the bank's money to cryptocurrency wallets controlled by the scammers. The bank failed, and federal regulators closed it. Hanes pleaded guilty to embezzlement by a bank officer, and in 2024 a federal judge sentenced him to more than twenty-four years in prison. Investors lost their stakes, and a town lost its bank.

Every new technology adds a weapon. Artificial intelligence now lets criminals clone voices, write flawless phishing emails in any language and generate fake video. The FBI's complaint center began tracking AI-related complaints for the first time in its 2025 report. Sextortion schemes target teenagers, threatening to share intimate images unless they pay, and federal officials have linked such schemes to a number of teen suicides. Each of these crimes has a victim as real as anyone ever robbed at gunpoint, and most of them will never see a detective.

Ray's neighbor, a widow named Joan, lost a little over ninety thousand dollars to a man she met on a social media site who said he was an engineer working on an offshore oil platform. They talked every day for five months. He showed her screenshots of his cryptocurrency gains and helped her open an account on the same platform. When she tried to withdraw money for a new roof, the platform demanded a fee. When she called the sheriff's office, a deputy told her she should have known better. She has not told her children. Apart from me, Ray is the only person who knows, and he brought me to her only because he thought somebody should write it down.

A Blueprint for Cyber Enforcement

The Colonial Pipeline seizure proves the first point: cyber criminals can be traced and their money taken back. Bitcoin is not anonymous; every transaction sits on a public ledger, and investigators with the right training and tools can follow it. What the country lacks is not possibility but capacity. Every state should build a statewide cybercrime unit, staffed by investigators, analysts and dedicated prosecutors, that takes cases from local departments too small to work them. Several states have built cyber units inside their state police or attorney general's office. They should be the rule, not the exception.

Training must reach the patrol officer, because the patrol officer takes the report. The Secret Service already runs the National Computer Forensics Institute in Hoover, Alabama, which trains state and local officers, prosecutors and judges in digital investigations at no cost to their agencies. Congress should expand it so that every department of meaningful size has at least one trained officer, and states should require basic cyber training in every academy. A first responder who knows to collect the scammer's wallet address, phone number and payment records on day one can make a case possible that would otherwise die.

Speed is the most valuable tool, and the cheapest. The FBI's Recovery Asset Team works with banks to freeze fraudulent transfers when victims report quickly, and it has recovered substantial sums in cases reported within the first day or two. Every police department should be required to forward fraud reports to the FBI's complaint center and to the victim's bank immediately, not when someone gets around to it. Banks and cryptocurrency exchanges should be required to hold suspicious large transfers from older customers long enough for a human being to call and ask a question.

Local governments must defend themselves before they need the police at all. The federal Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency offers free vulnerability scanning and guidance to state and local governments, and many counties have never used it. A county board that has not required multifactor authentication, tested backups and a ransomware response plan is gambling with taxpayers' money. Ray now sits on his county's citizen budget committee, and his first question every year is whether the systems are backed up and tested. It is the right question, and every resident should be asking it.

Finally, count it and publish it. City councils and state legislatures should require police departments to report cybercrime complaints, referrals, recoveries and arrests every year, side by side with burglary and robbery. Once the numbers sit next to each other, the mismatch between where the losses are and where the officers are becomes impossible to ignore. Budgets follow what gets measured.

Citizens have a role that goes beyond voting. Report every loss, even a small one, because reports are the raw material investigators use to connect cases and the evidence legislators need to fund units. Talk openly with parents and grandparents about pig butchering and sextortion, the way earlier generations talked about strangers with candy. Ask your bank what it does to stop suspicious transfers, and move your money if the answer is nothing. Shame is the scammer's best protection. Conversation takes it away.

The takeaway

One of the biggest crimes by dollars is the one police barely touch. Report every cyber loss fast to your bank and the FBI's complaint center, and press your state for a funded cybercrime unit and your county for tested backups.

Chapter 33: Fraudsters, Embezzlers, and Abusers of Vulnerable Populations

Why does a teenager who steals a car stereo seem to draw more police attention than the woman who quietly drains a town of fifty million dollars, or the guardian a judge appoints to protect an old man and who then empties his bank account? Most people have felt the shape of that question without ever quite asking it. The answer is not that the law is weaker for one than the other. On paper, the embezzler and the exploiter face serious prison time. The difference is who is looking.

Street crime announces itself. A broken window, a stolen car, a body on a sidewalk all demand a response, and the response is what police departments are built to deliver. Crimes against the vulnerable do the opposite. They are designed to be invisible, committed by people with keys, signatures and the trust of their victims, against victims who often cannot or will not complain: the elderly widow with dementia, the foster child, the immigrant family afraid of any official, the town that never imagined its own bookkeeper was the thief.

So the answer is this. These offenders are rarely targeted because the system waits for complaints, and their victims are the people least able to make one. That is not an accident of nature. It is a choice about where to put investigators, and it can be changed. In my years covering crime I have come to think that the people who steal from the defenseless are among the most destructive offenders in the country, and among the least pursued.

Crimes Against Seniors

April Parks ran a professional guardianship company in Las Vegas. Courts in Clark County appointed her to manage the lives and money of older people judged unable to manage their own, and for years she was one of the busiest guardians in the system. Prosecutors later showed that her company had overbilled wards and siphoned money from the accounts of the people she had been appointed to protect, hundreds of them in all. Some of her wards had been removed from their homes over the objections of their families. In 2019 she was sentenced to sixteen to forty years in prison in what Nevada's attorney general called the largest elder exploitation case in the state's history.

The Parks case did not come to light because the court system caught it. It came to light because relatives of her wards complained loudly and repeatedly, journalists dug into the records, and a national magazine investigation laid out how a guardian could legally take control of an elderly stranger's life with a few court filings. The judges who appointed her had approved her bills. The system meant to protect the vulnerable had handed them over, and then had trouble believing the families who said something was wrong.

Guardianship abuse is only one corner of the problem. Older Americans lose money to adult children who treat a parent's account as an early inheritance, to caregivers who pocket checks, to contractors who take payment for roofs they never replace, and to the phone and online scams that swallow billions every year. Federal complaint data show Americans sixty and older reporting far larger losses to fraud than any other age group. Most elder financial abuse is never reported at all. Victims are ashamed, or dependent on the abuser, or unable to understand what was taken.

I have sat with families in probate courtrooms and watched them learn, line by line from a ledger, how a parent's life savings disappeared. The anger in those rooms is different from the anger after a burglary. A burglar is a stranger. The person who drained Mom's account was usually invited in: a son, a home aide, a financial adviser, a court-appointed guardian with an official seal on her paperwork. The betrayal compounds the loss. Older victims of financial abuse often decline quickly afterward, losing not just money but the independence that money bought, and the cost of their care shifts to Medicaid and the taxpayers who fund it.

Congress has acted, in a halting way. The Elder Justice Act, passed in 2010 as part of the Affordable Care Act, was the first comprehensive federal law aimed at elder abuse, though much of it has never been fully funded. The Elder Abuse Prevention and Prosecution Act of 2017 required the Justice Department to designate elder justice coordinators in every federal judicial district. The Senior Safe Act of 2018 protected bank and brokerage employees who report suspected exploitation in good faith. These were real steps. They have not produced anything like the cop on the beat for the elderly that the scale of the losses demands.

Crimes Against Children

Children are robbed too, and sometimes by the institutions charged with caring for them. A child's clean credit history is gold to an identity thief, and the theft can go undiscovered until the child applies for a student loan or a first apartment and learns that someone has been borrowing in their name for years. Often the thief is a relative. Children in foster care are especially exposed, because their personal information passes through many hands and no parent is watching their credit.

The organizations built around children are tempting targets for embezzlers, too. Youth sports leagues, school parent groups and booster clubs handle cash from fundraisers, concessions and registration fees, usually with a single volunteer treasurer and no audit. When money disappears, the children lose the field trip, the uniforms or the season. Many of these cases end with a quiet repayment plan instead of a police report, because the parents running the group do not want to prosecute a neighbor. The thief learns that stealing from children carries almost no risk, and sometimes moves on to the next volunteer job.

One of the quietest takings is entirely legal. Many foster children are entitled to Social Security survivor or disability benefits, and for decades state agencies have applied for those benefits on the children's behalf and then kept the money to reimburse the state for the cost of foster care. In Washington State Department of Social and Health Services v. Guardianship Estate of Keffeler, decided in 2003, the Supreme Court unanimously held that the practice did not violate the federal law protecting benefits from creditors. The Court read the statute fairly; the problem is what the statute allowed. A 2021 investigation by NPR and the Marshall Project found states collecting well over a hundred million dollars a year this way, often without telling the children.

I want to be careful here, because the officials who run foster care systems are not embezzlers, and many are doing hard work with too little money. But a system that takes a dead parent's benefits from an orphan to pay its own bills, and leaves that child to age out at eighteen with nothing, has confused protection with ownership. Maryland changed its law so that a growing share of a foster youth's benefits is saved for them in trust as they approach adulthood, and a few other states have moved in the same direction. Every state should follow.

Crimes Against Minority Communities

Teresa runs a community center in a working-class neighborhood where most of the families speak Spanish at home. She keeps a folder in her desk that she calls the graveyard. It holds receipts, contracts and business cards from people who promised her neighbors legal status: a "notario" who charged a family five thousand dollars to file immigration papers that were never filed, a storefront "consultant" who submitted an application that triggered a deportation case, a man who sold fake work permits out of a van. She showed me the folder the way some people show a photo album, one ruined life to a page.

Notario fraud works because of a language trap. In much of Latin America, a notario público is a trained legal professional with real authority. In the United States, a notary public is someone licensed to witness signatures, with no power to give legal advice. Fraudsters exploit the gap, advertising as notarios to families who believe they are hiring a lawyer. Many states have made it illegal for notaries to advertise with that term or to offer immigration services, and the Federal Trade Commission and immigration authorities have warned about the practice for years. Enforcement remains thin, because victims fear that going to any government office will expose them.

That fear is the engine of affinity fraud, the scheme that travels through trust inside a community: a church, an ethnic association, a circle of friends who share a language. The fraudster is often a member of the group, which makes the betrayal deeper and the silence longer. Investment frauds have swept through immigrant congregations, churches of every denomination and tight-knit ethnic communities across the country, and federal regulators have repeatedly warned that affinity fraud targets the very loyalty that holds such communities together. When the victims are poor, foreign-born or distrustful of police for good historical reasons, the offender can work for years.

Teresa told me about a grandmother who came to the center after losing her savings to a notario who also took her original birth certificate and passport and never returned them. The woman had waited three years to come forward. She thought the police would ask about her status before they asked about her money. When Teresa walked her into the local precinct, the detective who took the report was kind and did his job, but the notario's storefront had already closed and reopened under a new name two neighborhoods over. Nobody had connected the complaints because nobody was looking for a pattern.

Embezzlement and White-Collar Theft

Rita Crundwell was the comptroller of Dixon, Illinois, a town of about fifteen thousand people best known as Ronald Reagan's boyhood home. For roughly two decades she controlled the city's money, and during that time she stole more than fifty-three million dollars, funneling public funds through a secret bank account to build one of the country's most celebrated quarter horse breeding operations. While she bought horses, farms, a luxury motor home and jewelry, Dixon cut staff, delayed road repairs and told residents there was no money. The theft was discovered only when a city clerk, filling in while Crundwell was away, came across a bank statement she did not recognize and took it to the mayor.

Crundwell pleaded guilty to federal wire fraud in 2012, and in 2013 a federal judge sentenced her to nearly twenty years in prison. Her case is often described as the largest municipal fraud in American history. The city later recovered about forty million dollars in a settlement with its outside auditors and its bank, which it had sued for failing to catch the theft. Think about that. For years, professional auditors signed off on books that hid a theft larger than the town's annual budget, and the city council accepted their reports.

Embezzlement on a smaller scale happens every day in churches, nonprofits, school booster clubs, homeowners associations, medical practices and family businesses. The treasurer who has run the books for twenty years and never takes a vacation is a cliché among fraud examiners for a reason. These cases are often handled quietly: the thief repays part of the money, resigns and moves on, because the organization fears embarrassment more than it wants justice. The Supreme Court made clear in Kousisis v. United States in 2025 that federal wire fraud does not require proof that the victim suffered an economic loss, only that the defendant used material lies to obtain money or property. The law is strong. The will to use it is not.

Why These Offenders Are Rarely Targeted

The first reason is structural. Fraud and embezzlement cases are complex, paper-heavy and slow, and most local police departments have few detectives trained in forensic accounting. A single embezzlement case can require months of reviewing bank records. Departments are measured on arrests and response times, and an investigator who spends a year on one financial case produces a single arrest on paper. Prosecutors face the same pressure. Elder abuse cases are hard to try when the key witness has dementia or dies before trial.

Second, the victims are disbelieved. Older people are dismissed as confused. Children are not asked. Immigrants are afraid to come forward. Small towns and nonprofits are too embarrassed. When Parks's wards and their families complained, they were treated for years as difficult relatives rather than crime victims. When Crundwell's colleagues noticed her lifestyle, they assumed the horses paid for themselves. Trust is the tool these offenders use, and our habit of deferring to people with credentials and titles does the rest.

Third, nobody owns the problem. Adult protective services agencies investigate abuse of vulnerable adults but usually cannot arrest anyone. Police can arrest but often see the matter as civil. Probate courts oversee guardians but rarely audit them. Child welfare agencies protect children from their parents but not from the agencies themselves. Each institution can point to another, and the gaps between them are exactly where these offenders live.

The cost of that neglect lands on taxpayers in ways they rarely trace back to its source. Dixon's residents paid for Crundwell's horses through years of deferred repairs and lost services. Medicaid absorbs the nursing home bills of seniors whose savings were stolen. Public benefits support immigrant families who were defrauded out of the chance to work legally. Every embezzled dollar from a public budget is a dollar raised again in taxes or cut from a service. When we decline to prioritize these crimes, we do not save money. We pay for them twice, once to the thief and again to clean up after them.

Prevention Through Prioritization

The fix begins with a decision: treat crimes against the vulnerable as a priority category, the way many prosecutors now treat domestic violence. Every district attorney's office of meaningful size should have a dedicated unit for elder abuse and financial exploitation, with prosecutors who know how to try cases with fragile witnesses and investigators who can read bank records. Many large counties already run elder abuse forensic centers, which bring police, prosecutors, adult protective services, doctors and financial experts together to review cases as a team; Orange County, California, was among the first, and the model has spread to other counties.

Guardianship needs real oversight. Courts should require background checks and licensing for professional guardians, audit their accounts every year, and give wards and their families a lawyer and a voice before rights are taken away. After the Parks scandal, Nevada adopted reforms that include stronger court oversight and a right to counsel for people facing guardianship. Every state legislature should study its own system before its own scandal arrives.

Protect children's money and identities. States should stop taking foster children's benefits to pay for their own care, as Maryland has moved to do, and save that money for the children. Congress can require it nationally. Child welfare agencies should check the credit of every foster youth and repair any fraud before the child ages out, and parents can freeze a child's credit with the major bureaus for free, which blocks most new-account fraud.

For minority and immigrant communities, enforcement has to be paired with trust. State attorneys general should prosecute notario fraud aggressively and publicize the convictions in the languages of the affected communities. Immigration legal clinics, bar associations and community centers like Teresa's can run free consultations so families have a legitimate alternative to the storefront. Police should make clear, in writing and in practice, that reporting a fraud will not lead to questions about a victim's own immigration status.

Finally, every organization that handles other people's money should adopt the controls fraud examiners have recommended for decades: two signatures on large checks, separation of the person who writes checks from the person who reconciles accounts, mandatory vacations, surprise audits and a tip line. City councils should demand them in public budgets and read the audits, not just accept them. Dixon learned that lesson at a cost of fifty-three million dollars. Your town can learn it for the price of asking a question at the next meeting.

The takeaway

People who prey on the old, the young and the outsider count on our looking away. Demand dedicated elder abuse prosecutors, audited guardians, protected foster benefits and two-signature controls wherever public money moves.

Chapter 34: Government Transparency and the Cost of Secrecy

In the summer of 2010, the people of Bell, California, a working-class city of about 37,000 just southeast of downtown Los Angeles, opened their newspaper and learned what their city manager earned. The number was roughly $800,000 a year in salary alone, about twice what the President of the United States is paid, and his full compensation package ran higher still. Fourteen years later, in April 2024, the people of the entire state opened their newspapers and learned something that should have frightened them more. Nobody could say what roughly $24 billion in homelessness spending had bought.

Those two mornings sit at opposite ends of a scale. Bell was a small city looted by a few officials who kept the books dark. The homelessness audit described something larger and, in its way, more troubling: no one stealing on the record, no single villain, only a government that spent tens of billions of dollars and never built the instruments needed to know whether the money worked. I have covered both kinds of failure in my years on the crime beat. I have come to believe they are the same failure at different sizes.

The California Homelessness Funding Failure

California's State Auditor released the report in April 2024, and its core finding was plain. Across five fiscal years, from 2018 through 2023, the state spent about $24 billion on homelessness and housing programs. The agency responsible for coordinating that effort had stopped consistently tracking spending and outcomes, and the auditor said the state lacked current, reliable information about what its programs cost and what they achieved. Meanwhile the homeless population kept climbing in many of the places the money was meant to reach.

Numbers from the street tell the rest. Federal point-in-time counts put California's homeless population at roughly 180,000 people by 2023, the largest of any state by a wide margin, and a majority of them were living unsheltered: in tents, in cars, in doorways. The state was spending more each year and losing ground. A business with that record would at least know which of its divisions was bleeding. California, by its own auditor's account, did not.

Back in 2010, Bell's residents had a villain to point at. The Los Angeles Times exposed the salaries, prosecutors charged the city manager, Robert Rizzo, and several council members, and Rizzo eventually pleaded no contest to dozens of counts that included misappropriation of public funds. In April 2014 a judge sentenced him to twelve years in state prison and ordered him to repay nearly $9 million. In 2024, by contrast, there was no one to charge, because the absence of tracking is not a crime. That is precisely the problem. A system that measures nothing cannot be shown to have failed, and a system that cannot be shown to have failed cannot be fixed.

I spent a long afternoon once in a Bell storefront office with a retired machinist who had lived in the city for forty years. He told me he had never once looked at a city budget, because he assumed somebody was watching. That assumption is the most expensive one a citizen can make. In Bell, nobody was watching. In Sacramento, the people paid to watch had stopped keeping the ledger. The scale changed between 2010 and 2024. The blindness did not.

Tens of Billions Spent, Results Never Tracked

Consider Denise, a composite of the taxpayers I have interviewed across the state. She is a dental hygienist in her fifties who rents a two-bedroom apartment and pays her state income tax without complaint. Every morning she walks past the same encampment on the way to her bus stop. It has been there, in one shape or another, for six years. She has read about the billions. She asked me the question I have heard in a dozen kitchens: where did it go? I could not give her an answer. Neither, according to its own auditor, could the state.

The auditor's office did examine five specific programs that together received about $13.7 billion. Two, including the conversion of hotels and motels into housing, looked likely to be cost-effective. For the others, which together accounted for billions more, the data was too thin to judge at all. The auditor also reviewed San Jose and San Diego and found that neither had effectively tracked its own homelessness revenue and spending. A household that ran its finances this way would be in bankruptcy court. A business would face its shareholders. A government simply asked for the next appropriation.

One detail stays with me: the date. The coordinating council, the state body created to see the whole picture, stopped consistently tracking spending and outcomes in 2021, according to the audit. The money did not stop in 2021. It kept arriving, in some of the largest homelessness budgets in American history, while the one office positioned to tell the public what it bought went quiet. No law forced it to keep counting, so it did not. That is not an accident of bureaucracy. It is a structure that lets everyone avoid the question.

Los Angeles offers a sharper picture of what darkness costs. In 2016 the city's voters approved Proposition HHH, a $1.2 billion bond to build supportive housing. Audits by the city controller's office later found that per-unit costs on many projects were running near $600,000, with some projected well above $700,000. The city's housing department disputed parts of that analysis, and honest people can argue about land prices and labor costs. What no one should accept is that it took repeated audits and sustained reporting to pry the numbers loose in the first place.

Why No One Is Being Held Accountable

Accountability needs three things: a promise, a measurement, and a consequence. California's homelessness system made the promise loudly. It skipped the measurement. Without the second, the third never arrives. Every agency, nonprofit, county and city in the chain can point to another link. The state funds the counties, the counties contract with providers, the providers report what they are asked to report, and nobody is asked to report whether a human being slept indoors a year later.

I want to credit the good faith of the people inside this system, because I have met many of them. Caseworkers who drive clients to appointments on their own time. Program directors who have watched three funding streams arrive in one year with three different reporting forms. Most of them are not hiding anything. They are drowning in a structure that rewards spending fast and punishes the slow, unglamorous work of counting.

Good faith does not excuse the design, though. Legislators who approve billions without requiring outcome data have made a choice, and so have the governors who sign those budgets. They know that a measured program can be cut and an unmeasured one rarely is. Opacity protects everyone with a budget line. It protects no one sleeping on concrete, and it does nothing for Denise.

Imagine the same rules applied to a private charity. A foundation that took in billions, could not say how many people it helped, and stopped filing its outcome reports would lose its donors in a season, and its board might face the state attorney general. Government agencies face no donor revolt, because taxpayers cannot withhold their gift. That is exactly why the obligation to measure has to be written into law rather than left to the goodwill of whoever runs the program this year.

The Link Between Secrecy and Corruption

Here is the thesis I have come to hold after decades of watching public money disappear: a government that is not fully transparent is almost always corrupt, whether or not anyone has yet been caught. Not every official in the dark is a thief. But the dark is where thieves go to work, and they always find it. Secrecy is not a side effect of corruption. It is the precondition.

Bell proved the mechanism. Rizzo's salary did not rise to $800,000 in a single vote. It climbed for years through contracts and compensation arrangements that were never presented clearly to the public, in a city where few residents attended council meetings and local news coverage had thinned. When the judge sentenced him, she pointed to the absence of checks and balances in the city's government. Strip away the specifics and that sentence describes every scandal I have ever covered.

The council was in on it, too. Members of a part-time city council in a poor city were drawing pay close to six figures through seats on boards that barely met, and most of the former council members charged alongside Rizzo were convicted. Ordinary residents had no idea, because nothing in the public paperwork made the scale plain. The scheme did not require brilliance. It required only that no one outside the room ever see the numbers, and for years no one did.

Los Angeles proved it again, at a larger scale. José Huizar sat on the city council and chaired its powerful planning and land-use committee, the body that decides which downtown towers get built. Federal prosecutors showed that from roughly 2013 to 2017 he ran a scheme worth about $1.5 million in bribes and benefits from developers who needed his votes. He pleaded guilty in January 2023 to racketeering conspiracy and tax evasion, and in January 2024 a federal judge sentenced him to thirteen years in prison. The deals happened in private meetings, casino trips and cash handoffs, all far from any public record.

Huizar was not a minor functionary. He was one of fifteen people governing the second-largest city in the country, during the very years when Los Angeles was declaring homelessness an emergency and asking voters for billions to build housing. The same planning decisions that determine how much a supportive-housing unit costs, and whether it gets built at all, ran through his committee. I do not claim his crimes touched homelessness dollars; the case was about private development. My point is narrower and worse: the machinery the public was asked to trust with its housing money was, at its center, for sale.

Notice the pattern. In both cases, the corruption lived precisely where the public could not see. Large discretionary decisions, weak disclosure rules, and complicated paperwork that no ordinary resident would ever read. When outsiders finally forced the records open, through a newspaper investigation in Bell and a federal probe in Los Angeles, the rot was obvious in months. It had been hidden for years.

Homelessness spending is especially vulnerable to this kind of rot, and anyone who has covered public contracting knows why. The money moves fast, under emergency declarations that loosen bidding rules. It flows through hundreds of contractors, many of them small, many of them new. The people it serves rarely file complaints, and when they do, few listen. Every one of those conditions is a gift to the dishonest. When the books are also dark, the only thing standing between public money and private pockets is the personal integrity of every person in the chain. That is not a system. It is a prayer.

Public Trust Destroyed

Damage outlasts the defendants. After Bell, residents faced a city with drained reserves, inflated property taxes that had to be refunded, and a council chamber full of new faces who had to prove, from scratch, that they were not the same. A whole generation of Bell residents learned that their government had treated them as marks. That lesson does not expire when a sentence begins.

Trust works like a savings account that takes decades to fill and one headline to empty. When people believe their taxes are stolen or squandered, they stop voting for bonds, stop attending hearings, and stop calling city hall. Some of them stop calling the police, too, which is where government secrecy turns into a crime problem. A witness who assumes the whole system is rigged does not walk into a station to give a statement. A neighbor who thinks the council is bought does not report the drug house on the corner.

Criminologists have studied this for decades under the name legal cynicism, the belief that the law and its institutions are not on your side. Researchers working in cities across the country have found that neighborhoods where that belief runs deep tend to suffer more violence, partly because residents settle disputes themselves rather than trusting the system to do it. Corruption in city hall feeds that cynicism directly. People do not draw a neat line between the councilman taking bribes and the detective asking for their help. To them, it is all the government, and the government has lied.

Denise put it to me bluntly. She said she no longer believes any number a politician gives her, good or bad. When a mayor announces that homelessness is down, she assumes it is spin. When a sheriff says crime is falling, she assumes the statistics were massaged. That is what an unmeasured government produces: citizens who will not believe even the truth, because they have been given so little of it.

She is not alone, and she is not paranoid. National surveys have tracked a long slide in the share of Americans who say they trust government to do what is right most of the time, down from a large majority in the early 1960s to a small minority today. Every Bell, every Huizar, every unexplained billion pushes that number lower. And the cost of low trust is paid in the neighborhoods that most need a working government: places where people have the least money to buy private alternatives to public safety, public housing and public schools.

Transparency as Crime Prevention

Nothing about the cure is mysterious, and parts of it already exist. Start with spending. Ohio built an online checkbook years ago that lets anyone search state expenditures line by line, and dozens of governments have followed with similar portals. California could require every dollar of homelessness money, from the state down to the individual provider contract, to report into a single public database with outcomes attached: people housed, people still housed after twelve months, cost per person. If a program cannot report, it stops receiving new money until it can.

Then add independent watchdogs with teeth. Inspectors general who report to no one they are investigating, with subpoena power over contractors who take public money and a legal duty to publish what they find. Several large cities and counties already have such offices for police and procurement. Homelessness programs, now among the largest discretionary budgets in California, deserve the same scrutiny, and the auditor's recommendations, which included restoring statewide tracking, should be treated as a floor rather than a suggestion.

Courts can force the doors open, and they have. In 2014, in Kalven v. City of Chicago, an Illinois appellate court held that police misconduct complaint files and lists of officers with repeated complaints were public records under the state's freedom of information law. The journalist who brought the case went on to help publish those records in a searchable public database. California's own legislature passed SB 1421 in 2018, opening records of serious police misconduct and uses of force that had been sealed for decades. Both changes came from a simple principle: what the public pays for, the public may see.

Legislatures can write outcome tracking into the law itself, rather than leaving it to agencies that have every reason to skip it. Every major appropriation should carry a measurement clause, an independent auditor with power to examine contractors, and an automatic public report. Strengthen public-records laws with real deadlines and penalties for agencies that stall, because a records law that lets a city take a year to answer a request is a records law in name only.

City councils can do the small things that would have stopped Bell. Post every employment contract and compensation package online, in plain language, before the vote. Require that any raise for a senior official be read aloud and published in the agenda. Pay attention to the cities where the local newspaper has died, because those are where the next Bell is growing.

Citizens have the last word. Show up. File the records request. Ask your county for the outcome data on the program your property tax is funding, and publish the answer if you get one and the refusal if you do not. Every scandal I have described from California's recent history ended the same way: someone outside the government insisted on seeing inside it.

Support the institutions that do this work for you. Bell was not exposed by a regulator. It was exposed by reporters reading salary records that the city had been slow to release. Local newsrooms have been shrinking for two decades, and the counties and small cities left without one are exactly where officials can do what Bell's officials did. A subscription to a local paper, a donation to a nonprofit newsroom, or a seat at the monthly council meeting is a crime-prevention measure, and a cheap one.

The takeaway

Secrecy is where corruption lives and where public trust dies, and a government that spends billions without measuring results has already failed its citizens whether or not anyone is stealing. Demand the receipts, write tracking into the law, and treat every closed door in city hall as the first warning sign of crime.

Chapter 35: Rehearsing Crime: Violent Video Games and the First Amendment

"No, you don't shoot him yet," the boy said, leaning across the folding table. "You pull him out of the car first, then you take the car, then the cops come, and that's when it gets good." He was thirteen, sitting with two friends in the back room of a neighborhood boxing gym, waiting for practice and talking the way kids talk about a movie they love. Marcus, the man who runs the gym, was taping hands a few feet away. He looked over at me and raised an eyebrow. Neither of us said anything. There was nothing illegal in the room, and nothing unusual.

Marcus is a composite of the community leaders I have spent time with over the years: men and women who run gyms, church basements and after-school programs on donated money and their own stubbornness. He has buried more of his former kids than any coach should. He does not believe a video game pulled the trigger in any of those deaths, and neither do I. But he asked me a question that afternoon that I have never been able to shake. If those boys spend three hours a night practicing carjackings, he said, what exactly are they getting good at?

Violent Entertainment as Cultural Training

Every culture trains its young through stories. The tales a society tells over and over, and the roles it invites children to play, become the rehearsal space for adult life. For most of human history those stories were heard or watched. What is new about the modern video game is that the player does not watch the crime. The player commits it, again and again, with a controller in his hands, rewarded with points, money, and status for each success.

The military understood the value of that kind of rehearsal long before the gaming industry did. Armies have used simulators for generations to train soldiers in everything from marksmanship to convoy ambushes, because repetition under realistic conditions builds reflexes. The U.S. Army went further in 2002 and released its own free video game, America's Army, as a recruiting tool. A former Army officer and psychologist, Dave Grossman, built a public career arguing that violent games condition children to kill in the same way military training conditions soldiers. Many researchers think he overstates the case badly. The underlying intuition, that practice shapes people, is hard to dismiss entirely.

My argument is not that games manufacture killers. It is cultural, and I want to state it plainly. A nation that hands its children tens of millions of hours of interactive crime, scored and rewarded, is teaching something about what is exciting, what is normal and what earns respect. We would never accept a school curriculum built around stealing cars and shooting police officers. We accept it as entertainment without a second thought.

Think about what the culture rewards elsewhere. Children who practice the piano for an hour a day learn the piano. Children who drill free throws get better at free throws. Every coach in America, Marcus included, rests his whole method on the idea that repetition changes people. It is strange to insist that repetition matters in every area of human learning except the one where the content is robbery and murder. I do not think it is the gun that the practice delivers. I think it is the script: the instant escalation, the contempt for victims, the joke made out of a dead body.

Crime Simulation in Video Games

No product embodies that more than Grand Theft Auto. The series began in 1997 as a top-down game about stealing cars, and with each release it became richer, more realistic and more popular. Grand Theft Auto V, released in 2013, has shipped well over 150 million copies and stands among the most commercially successful entertainment products ever made, in any medium. Its online mode has kept millions of players returning for more than a decade.

The game is brilliantly made. I want to say that clearly, because the people who build these worlds are gifted artists, and the satire running through the series is often sharp. But the core loop is crime. Players rob stores, steal cars, run over pedestrians, fight police, and in one notorious mission participate in an extended torture scene. The game carries an M rating, for players seventeen and older, from the industry's own ratings board. Any parent, teacher or coach can tell you how much that rating keeps it away from thirteen-year-olds.

Marcus has his own measure. He watches how his kids talk about the game, not whether they play it. The language of the street arrives already formatted, he says: what you do when someone disrespects you, how you handle a witness, what a police chase feels like. None of it is real. All of it is practiced. And practice, in his gym, is the whole point of everything.

The online world adds a layer the earlier games never had. In the shared online version of the game, players do not only rob computer-controlled shopkeepers; they rob, ambush and humiliate one another, in real time, with real people on the other end of the headset. Respect is earned by domination and lost by hesitation. Many adults have no idea what that social world looks like, because they have never logged in. The kids in Marcus's back room live in it for hours, and the status rules they absorb there are not so different from the ones that get young men killed on actual corners.

What the Research Shows, and What It Doesn't

Honesty requires me to say what science has and has not found, because the evidence does not back the loudest version of the argument. In 2015 the American Psychological Association reviewed the research and concluded that violent video game play is linked to increases in aggressive behavior, aggressive thinking and desensitization, along with decreases in empathy. It stated, in the same breath, that the evidence was insufficient to link games to criminal violence.

In 2020 the APA revisited the question, partly because politicians and commentators had been citing its earlier findings to blame mass shootings on games. Its updated resolution kept the finding of a small but reliable association with aggressive outcomes, like yelling and shoving, and warned plainly against attributing violent crime to video games. Some researchers think even the small association is overstated; a meta-analysis that reexamined the data found the effects close to negligible. The scientific mainstream, in short, does not believe games cause the shootings and stabbings I have spent my career covering.

Desensitization is the finding I take most seriously, because it fits what I have seen. A number of laboratory studies have reported that heavy players of violent games show blunted physiological reactions to images of real violence. The effect is modest and contested. It also matches something I have watched in courtrooms and at crime scenes over many years: young people describing a shooting in the flat vocabulary of a game, as if the dead were respawning somewhere offstage. That is not proof of anything. It is a reason to keep asking.

The crime statistics point the same way. Violent video games exploded in popularity from the mid-1990s on, and over that same period juvenile violent crime fell steeply from its early-1990s peak. Countries such as Japan and South Korea, where young people play enormous amounts of video games, have far lower violent crime than the United States. If games were a powerful cause of violence, those numbers would look very different.

So I make my case knowing it cannot rest on causation. It rests on something research is poorly built to measure: the slow, cumulative work of a culture's entertainment on its sense of what is normal. Laboratory studies measure what happens in an afternoon. No one can run a controlled experiment on a generation. The absence of proof that games cause crime is not proof that a steady diet of rehearsed crime is good for anyone, and parents like the ones who sit in Marcus's bleachers already know that.

The Columbine-era panic did enormous damage to this conversation. After the 1999 massacre in Colorado, commentators seized on the shooters' fondness for the game Doom as if it explained the killings. Families of students killed in a 1997 school shooting in Paducah, Kentucky, sued game and media companies, and a federal appeals court dismissed the claims in 2002. The blame was too easy, and the courts and researchers were right to reject it. The lesson Americans took away, unfortunately, was that the entire subject was off limits.

The First Amendment Paradox

That brings us to the courts. In 2005 California passed a law barring the sale or rental of violent video games to minors without parental involvement, with civil fines for retailers who broke it. The industry sued, and in 2011, in Brown v. Entertainment Merchants Association, the Supreme Court struck the law down by a vote of seven to two. Writing for the majority, Justice Antonin Scalia held that video games are protected speech, like books, plays and films, and that the state had not shown the compelling evidence of harm needed to restrict them for minors.

Scalia's opinion pointed out that children's literature has always been violent. Grimm's fairy tales feature cannibalism and mutilation. Classic novels assigned in high school include murder. The state, he wrote in effect, could not invent a new category of unprotected speech simply because the medium was new and interactive. As a matter of free-speech law, the reasoning is strong, and I have relied on that same First Amendment for my whole working life.

The dissents deserve a hearing too. Justice Stephen Breyer pointed out the strangeness of a legal order that allows a state to bar the sale of a magazine with a nude image to a thirteen-year-old, yet protects the sale to that same child of a game in which he binds, gags, tortures and kills a woman. Justice Clarence Thomas argued that the founding generation never understood free speech to include a right to speak to children without going through their parents. Justice Samuel Alito, who agreed the California law was too vague, wrote separately to warn that interactive games might be meaningfully different from books.

Here is the paradox. Protected speech is not consequence-free speech. The First Amendment rightly keeps government from deciding what we may say, read or play. It does not tell parents, schools, retailers or the industry itself that they must be indifferent to what children consume. Brown settled the legal question. It did not settle the moral one, and we have behaved ever since as if it had.

Even the majority left a door open, though few noticed. Scalia's opinion turned largely on the state's failure to prove harm with solid evidence, and on a law the Court found both too broad and too narrow, since it targeted games while ignoring cartoons and films with similar content. Alito's concurrence went further, suggesting that the striking realism of modern games and the active role of the player might one day justify a carefully drawn law. The Court did not say society must look away. It said government had not yet made its case.

Cultural Addiction to Violent Media

Scale is what turns a private habit into a public question. Surveys find that a large majority of American teenagers play video games, and many boys play for hours a day. Violent titles reliably rank among the best sellers year after year. The World Health Organization added gaming disorder to its international classification of diseases, describing a pattern in which gaming crowds out other interests and continues despite harm. Most players never reach that point. Some do.

Marcus sees the edges of that problem in his own gym. Boys show up to six o'clock practice with red eyes because they played until three in the morning. Grades slip. Parents, worn out from second jobs, are relieved that the kid is home and quiet, and they do not ask what is on the screen. None of those boys is a criminal. Several of them are lonely, and the game is the place where they feel competent, powerful and admired. The danger is not that the game makes them violent. It is that the game becomes the only place they feel like someone.

Adults are hardly better. The most watched television dramas of recent decades have been built on murder, drug empires and organized crime. Streaming services compete on graphic violence. True crime, the genre I have worked in, is consumed by millions as relaxation. We have become a country that unwinds by watching people get hurt. I do not exempt myself from that sentence.

Violence on screens was not invented by game designers, and the films and shows adults binge are just as saturated. What games add is participation, and participation at a scale no other medium has reached. A long novel takes ten hours. A blockbuster film takes two. A popular open-world crime game can absorb hundreds of hours from a single player, and the most devoted rack up thousands. Multiply that by tens of millions of players, many of them children, and you have the largest rehearsal of criminal conduct in human history, carried out in bedrooms with the door closed.

Why America Cannot Confront Harmful Entertainment

Three obstacles stand in the way: money, politics and embarrassment. Video games are now a larger industry than film and recorded music, and the companies at the top are among the most profitable in entertainment. They fund lobbying and legal work that make any regulation an uphill fight. The industry has also been smart. After Senate hearings in 1993 over games like Mortal Kombat and Night Trap, it created its own ratings board, the ESRB, in 1994, heading off federal regulation with self-regulation.

Politics poisoned the rest. Every time an elected official blames video games after a mass shooting, the topic becomes a dodge, a way to avoid talking about guns, mental health care or school security. After the Parkland shooting in 2018, the White House held a meeting with game executives, and critics across the spectrum called it a distraction. They were not wrong. But because the issue has been used so cynically, serious people now treat any concern about violent entertainment as either a moral panic or a talking point.

And there is embarrassment. Adults who grew up on these games do not want to be the scolds their parents were. I understand that. I was raised being told that rock music and comic books would ruin me. The fear of sounding old has become its own form of censorship, of the most comfortable kind: we simply decline to raise the question.

Does Anybody Care? The National Indifference to Cultural Violence

I have asked parents, teachers, coaches and police officers whether they think about what their kids are rehearsing. Almost all of them say yes. Very few have done anything about it. The ratings are on the box, the parental controls are in the console, and the conversation never happens. Indifference is not ignorance. It is a choice made a thousand times a night in living rooms across the country.

Marcus is one of the few who acts. He keeps a television in the gym's back room, and on Fridays he plays sports and old fight films there instead of letting the boys load up their phones. He talks with them about the game they love, asking what they think a real carjacking does to the man pulled from the car. He has sat with that man's family. Most of his kids have never thought about it, and the look on their faces when they do is the most hopeful thing I have seen in that gym.

What Parents, Platforms and Lawmakers Can Do

Fixes that work do not require censorship. Start at the register. A Federal Trade Commission undercover shopper survey released in 2013 found that retailers refused to sell M-rated games to unaccompanied young teens in roughly seven of every eight attempts, a sharp improvement over earlier years. That proves voluntary enforcement can work when someone is checking. Digital storefronts, where most games are now bought, should be held to the same standard, with age verification that means something and regular public audits of how often minors get through.

Parents have tools most never touch. Every major console and phone lets an adult block games by rating, set time limits and see what a child plays. Use them. Better still, play the game with your child for an hour and talk about it, the way Marcus does. A conversation about what the game rewards, and what the same act costs in real life, does more than any ban.

Lawmakers can do what Brown leaves room for. Fund independent research, free of industry money and political spin, on long-term effects of heavy interactive violence, especially on children already exposed to real violence at home or on the street. Back media literacy programs in schools that teach young people to read entertainment the way they would read an advertisement. And fund the gyms, rec centers and mentoring programs that give kids something better to do for three hours a night. The gaming industry can do its part by investing in design that rewards creativity, cooperation and consequence, as many acclaimed games already do.

The takeaway

Violent games do not turn children into criminals, and the research says so. But a culture that lets its children rehearse crime for thousands of hours, and refuses even to talk about it, has chosen comfort over responsibility. Keep the First Amendment, and use the freedom it protects to choose better.

Chapter 36: Crime Bankrupts Cities, Destroys Property Values, and Shatters Lives

Ruth keeps the original sale contract for her house in a plastic sleeve in the kitchen drawer, under the takeout menus. She bought the brick two-story with her late husband for what was then a serious amount of money, and she shows the paper to visitors the way other people show wedding photos. Then she shows them the most recent offer she received, from an investor who mails the whole block every spring. It is less than a third of what they paid, decades ago, before inflation. Three of the houses on her street are boarded. One burned last winter. She sleeps with the hall light on.

She is a composite, built from homeowners I have sat with in older industrial cities across the Midwest and the Northeast. Her numbers vary from city to city. Her story does not. She did everything she was told a responsible American should do: worked, saved, bought a house, paid it off, paid her taxes. Crime took the value of that house the way a flood takes a basement, slowly and then all at once, and no insurance policy covers it.

The Economic Cost of Crime

We talk about crime as a moral catastrophe, and it is one. It is also a financial catastrophe that most budget debates ignore. Economists who try to add up the full bill, counting medical care, lost wages, stolen and damaged property, police, courts and prisons, private security, and the pain and lost quality of life of victims, arrive at figures in the trillions of dollars a year nationwide. Even the narrower government bill is staggering. State and local governments spend well over a hundred billion dollars a year on policing alone, and tens of billions more on corrections and courts.

Most of those costs never appear on a crime report. A shooting is one line in a police log. Behind it sit an emergency room trauma team, months of surgery and rehabilitation often paid by public insurance, a victim who cannot work, a family that moves, and a business on the corner that closes early and then closes for good. Researchers who have studied gun violence alone put its annual national cost in the hundreds of billions once the full chain is counted. Taxpayers cover a large share of it, whether or not they live anywhere near a shooting.

I learned this lesson at a hospital, not a police station. Years ago a trauma surgeon walked me through the cost of a single gunshot wound to the abdomen: the helicopter or ambulance, the operating room, the intensive care unit, the follow-up surgeries, the colostomy supplies, the physical therapy. The total ran well into six figures for one patient, and she told me most of her gunshot patients were uninsured or on public coverage. Then she pointed at the board of names waiting for beds. A city's crime rate shows up in its hospital budget, its Medicaid spending and its disability rolls long after the headlines are gone.

How Crime Depopulates Communities

People leave. That is the simplest and most devastating economic effect of crime, and it has been measured.
