A widely cited economic study from the late 1990s found that, across large American cities, each additional reported crime was associated with roughly one resident leaving the city, and that the people most likely to go were families with children and college-educated households, the very taxpayers a city most needs to keep.

Detroit is the most painful example in American history. At its peak around 1950 the city had roughly 1.8 million residents. By 2010 the census counted just over 700,000, and the decline continued for more than a decade after that. Crime was not the only reason. The collapse of auto manufacturing, racial discrimination in housing and lending, highways that gutted neighborhoods, and decades of political failure all played their parts. But for years Detroit ranked among the most violent big cities in the country, and every family I interviewed there who had left named fear as one of the reasons, often the first one.

Departure feeds on itself. When a family leaves a block, a house sits empty. Empty houses attract stripping, squatting, drug use and arson. Neighbors see it, and the next family starts looking. The tax base shrinks, so the city cuts services, so the streetlights go dark and the police take longer to come, so more families leave. Ruth has watched every step of that cycle from her front window, and she can name the families in order.

The people who leave also take their voices. A family that moves to the suburbs stops voting in city elections, stops attending the parent-teacher meeting, stops calling the councilman about the vacant lot. Their church thins out. Their block club loses a member. Political power in the city shifts toward the people least able to pressure it, and the services that keep a neighborhood livable become easier to cut. Depopulation is not just an economic loss. It is a slow disenfranchisement of the people who remain.

Property Value Collapse

For most American families, a home is the largest asset they will ever own, and crime attacks that asset directly. Economists have found that home prices respond quickly to changes in local danger. One well-known study found that when a registered sex offender moved onto a block, the value of the nearest homes dropped measurably, by several percent, within months. Other research has linked surges in gun violence to lower home values and slower appreciation in the affected neighborhoods. Buyers do not need a statistic. They look at the bars on the windows and drive on.

The damage is not evenly shared. In wealthier areas a crime wave dents prices and recovers. In poorer neighborhoods it can wipe out the only wealth a family has, and with it the money a grandmother might have left to her grandchildren or borrowed against to start a business. When Ruth's house lost most of its value, her family did not just lose a number on paper. They lost the inheritance that was meant to lift the next generation, and that kind of loss compounds across decades.

Falling values also feed back into crime. When a house is worth less than the cost of repairing its roof, owners stop maintaining it, and landlords who buy cheap often rent to anyone and fix nothing. Lenders retreat from the neighborhood. Insurance premiums rise or policies vanish. The block becomes the kind of place where a drug crew can rent a house for cash with no questions asked. A collapsing housing market is not only the result of crime. It becomes one of its engines.

Commercial property follows the same curve. In recent years a string of national retailers closed stores in some high-theft urban districts and pointed to shoplifting and organized retail crime as a reason. I want to be careful here, because the data turned out to be weaker than the headlines. Walgreens closed several San Francisco stores in 2021 citing theft, and in early 2023 its chief financial officer conceded that the company may have overstated the problem. The National Retail Federation retracted a widely cited claim that organized theft accounted for nearly half of the industry's losses after reporters found errors in the figure.

The honest conclusion is not that retail theft is imaginary. Store employees I have interviewed describe it vividly, and many cities saw real increases. It is that companies close stores for many reasons, including weak sales, online competition and overexpansion, and theft is the reason that plays best in public. Both things can be true. A neighborhood that loses its only pharmacy is harmed either way, and residents who must ride two buses to fill a prescription do not care which line on the corporate spreadsheet drove the decision.

The Burden on Local Governments

Crime hits a city budget from two sides at once. It drives up spending, because police, emergency response, jails, courts, and services for victims all cost more when crime rises. It drives down revenue, because shrinking populations and falling property values mean smaller tax collections. Cities with high crime are squeezed between those two forces until something breaks, and the first things cut are usually the libraries, recreation centers and summer jobs that keep teenagers busy.

Detroit broke. On July 18, 2013, the city filed for Chapter 9 bankruptcy, with long-term obligations estimated at roughly $18 billion, the largest municipal bankruptcy in American history. The emergency manager's filings painted a portrait of a city that could no longer deliver basic services. Many of its streetlights did not work. Police response times to urgent calls, which he described as close to an hour, became a symbol of the collapse, although later reporting found the true average was somewhat shorter and that the department's measurement was inconsistent. Either way, a resident calling for help in Detroit in 2013 waited far longer than any family should.

In December 2013, federal bankruptcy judge Steven Rhodes ruled that Detroit was eligible for bankruptcy protection, and he held that pensions owed to city workers could be reduced in the process, despite protections in the Michigan constitution. That holding was a warning to every city worker and retiree in America: a promise made by a failing city is only as good as the city. The case ended with what became known as the grand bargain, in which foundations, the state, and the Detroit Institute of Arts raised hundreds of millions of dollars to soften pension cuts and keep the museum's collection from being sold.

The human side of that ruling is easy to forget. Retired police officers, firefighters, sanitation workers and clerks, many in their seventies, had planned their lives around those pensions. Some saw monthly checks reduced; others lost health benefits they had been promised for decades. Crime did not cause Detroit's bankruptcy single-handedly. But a city that had spent years losing residents to fear, and spending ever more on emergency services for those who stayed, had no cushion left when the bill came due.

Crime as Long-Term Municipal Debt

The bill for crime does not arrive all at once. It is paid over decades, often with interest. Cities that settle police misconduct lawsuits, a cost of bad policing rather than crime itself, sometimes borrow the money and pay it back over years. Chicago is the clearest case. In 2014 the city borrowed close to $200 million to cover police misconduct settlements and judgments, and local reporters calculated that interest on those thirty-year bonds would eventually cost taxpayers more than the original borrowing. The habit continued. The city's 2026 budget called for borrowing hundreds of millions more for the same purpose. Children in Chicago schools today will be paying, through their future taxes, for beatings and wrongful convictions that happened before they were born.

Prisons are the clearest long-term liability. A sentence handed down today can obligate taxpayers to decades of spending on housing, guards, and, increasingly, medical care for aging inmates. Pension and health obligations for police and corrections officers stretch even further. None of those costs is unreasonable on its own. Together they become fixed charges that crowd out schools, parks, libraries and road repair, the very investments that might have prevented the crime in the first place.

The deepest debt is the one no ledger records. A neighborhood that loses its families, its businesses, and its trust takes a generation or more to rebuild, if it rebuilds at all. Every year of high crime is a loan taken out against that neighborhood's future, and the interest is paid by children who were not yet born when the shooting started.

The Human Cost: Trauma, Fear, and Migration

None of these numbers capture what Ruth carries. She no longer walks to the corner store after dark. She keeps her late husband's car in the garage because a neighbor's was stolen twice. When her granddaughter visits, they stay inside. Ruth has not been robbed or assaulted. She is still a victim of crime, every single day, and nobody counts her.

Children pay the heaviest share. Researchers have found that exposure to violence in a neighborhood, even when a child is not directly hurt, is associated with lower test scores, poorer sleep and higher levels of anxiety. Teachers in high-crime districts describe students who cannot concentrate the morning after gunfire. Those children carry the effects into adulthood, often into lower earnings and worse health, and some carry it into violence of their own.

Then comes migration, the decision that empties cities. The families who leave are rarely villains. They are parents doing what any parent would do, getting their children to a place where they can sleep. But every departure takes a taxpayer, a churchgoer, a coach, a customer and a witness away from the neighborhood that needed them. Those who stay are disproportionately the elderly, the poor and the stuck, which is to say the people with the least power to demand that the city change course.

Survivors of violent crime often move whether they want to or not. A woman whose home was broken into while she slept may never sleep there again. A family whose son was shot on the corner may not be able to walk past that corner. I have interviewed dozens of people who sold their homes at a loss after a violent crime simply because they could not stand to stay. Their departure shows up in census figures as a statistic. In their lives it is a second trauma, layered on top of the first.

Why Cities Cannot Recover Without Prevention

Cities do not escape this spiral with tax incentives or new stadiums. They escape it by making neighborhoods safe enough that people stay. Camden, New Jersey, is the strongest example I know. In 2012 the city recorded 67 homicides, a record, in a population of under 80,000, and its police department was crippled by budget cuts and labor costs. In 2013 the city disbanded its police department and handed policing to a new county force, which put more officers on the street under new labor terms, emphasized community patrols, and rewrote its use-of-force rules. Homicides fell sharply over the following decade, to 17 in 2024 and reportedly lower still in 2025. Crime is still a problem in Camden, and the gains were uneven across neighborhoods, but the city's trajectory changed.

Detroit is also slowly rising. After bankruptcy it restored streetlights citywide, demolished thousands of abandoned houses, and invested in community violence intervention programs. Its population decline slowed sharply, and recent census estimates showed the city gaining residents for the first time in decades. Demolishing blight is not just cosmetic. Researchers in Philadelphia found that cleaning and greening vacant lots was followed by measurable drops in gun violence nearby, at a cost of a few hundred dollars per lot.

Prevention is also the only thing that pays for itself. Every family that stays keeps paying property tax. Every business that remains open collects sales tax and employs neighbors. Every child who grows up without exposure to violence is more likely to finish school, work and pay into the system rather than draw from it. Measured against the cost of a single homicide, counting investigation, prosecution, imprisonment, medical care and lost earnings, nearly any serious prevention program is a bargain.

City councils can build budgets around prevention instead of reaction. Fund vacant-property cleanup, streetlight repair and code enforcement before buying more squad cars. Support community violence intervention programs, which several cities have expanded with promising results. Count the full cost of crime in every budget debate, including lost property value and population, so voters see the real price of doing nothing. State legislatures can help by creating land banks that take control of abandoned properties quickly and by funding early childhood and youth programs in the neighborhoods that bleed most.

Homeowners like Ruth have more power than they think. Block clubs that report every abandoned house, every broken light and every illegal dumping site, and that show up at council meetings together, get results. I have seen a single organized block force a city to board, clean and resell a row of empty houses in under a year. Prevention is not a slogan. It is a street-by-street fight, and the people who live there are the best soldiers in it.

The takeaway

Crime drains money, people and hope from cities, and every family that flees leaves the next one poorer and less safe. Cities that recover do it by preventing crime at its roots, block by block, and by counting its full cost before the bill comes due.

Chapter 37: Law Enforcement Is Expensive — But Almost Worthless

In 2024, American police agencies solved fewer than one in six of the property crimes reported to them. That figure comes from the FBI's own national data, and it describes a good year. Burglaries were cleared at about fifteen percent. Car thefts were cleared at under ten. And those are only the crimes people bothered to report, which, according to the government's national victimization survey, is well under half of what actually happens.

Sit with that for a moment, because it is the most important fact about American policing and almost no one says it out loud. If you are burglarized, your odds of seeing anyone arrested are poor. If your car is stolen, they are worse. You will pay for the police either way, through your property taxes, your sales taxes and your state income tax, at a price that rivals what many cities spend on their schools. I have covered crime in America for a long time, and I have come to a conclusion that will anger many good officers: as a crime-solving institution, American law enforcement is expensive and almost worthless.

The Cost of Policing vs. the Value Delivered

State and local governments spent roughly $135 billion on police in 2021, according to analyses of census data by independent fiscal researchers. That figure has climbed steadily for decades, and it does not include federal law enforcement, private security, or the tens of billions more spent on jails, courts and prisons. In many big cities, the police department is the single largest line in the general fund.

What do taxpayers get for it? The usual answer is safety, and that answer is not entirely wrong. Research does suggest that more police on the street can deter some crime, especially violent crime, and I credit the officers who run toward danger when everyone else runs away. But deterrence is not the promise departments make. The promise is that if you are harmed, the system will find who did it. On that promise, measured by results, the value delivered is shockingly low.

Tom is a composite of the burglary survivors I have interviewed over the years. He is a retired bus mechanic who came home from a weekend at his daughter's house to find his back door kicked in, his late wife's jewelry gone, and his tools taken from the garage. He called the police. An officer arrived about two hours later, walked through the house, and handed him a case number for his insurance company. Nobody dusted for fingerprints. Nobody asked the neighbors whether their doorbell cameras had caught anything. Nobody called him again.

He was not angry at that officer, who was polite and plainly overwhelmed. He was angry at the arithmetic. He had paid property taxes on that house for thirty-one years. He added them up for me on a legal pad, then worked out the share that went to the police. Then he asked me what, exactly, he had bought. I did not have a good answer, and I have since put his question to police chiefs, mayors and council members. Most of them change the subject to response times.

Clearance Rates: How Few Crimes Are Solved

A crime is counted as cleared when police arrest someone and refer the case for prosecution, or when they identify the offender but cannot arrest for reasons beyond their control, such as the suspect's death. Clearance is not conviction. A cleared case can still collapse in court, which means the share of crimes that end with a guilty person held responsible is smaller still.

Murder is the crime police take most seriously, and even there the record is poor. In the mid-1960s, national data showed police clearing around ninety percent of homicides. By 2021 the rate had fallen to roughly half, the worst on record. It recovered to a little over sixty percent in 2024. That means that in a good recent year, about four in ten killers in America were never identified to the satisfaction of a prosecutor. In some large cities the rate has been far lower, and the unsolved cases are concentrated in the poorest neighborhoods.

The burden of that failure is not shared equally. Newspaper analyses of homicide data from dozens of large cities have found that killings of Black victims are noticeably less likely to end in an arrest than killings of white victims. The families I have sat with in those neighborhoods know the names of the men who killed their sons, or think they do, and they watch them walk the same streets for years. Every unsolved murder is a message to the next shooter that the odds are on his side.

Beyond murder, the picture is grimmer. Agencies cleared under half of all violent crimes in 2024. Rapes and robberies are solved at rates that would get any private company sued. Add the crimes that never get reported, and the chance that a given property crime ends in an arrest falls into the single digits. A burglar in most American cities can expect to commit many burglaries before he is ever caught.

Why Most Crimes Are Never Investigated

Most crimes are never solved because most crimes are never really investigated. Departments triage. A supervisor looks at a report and asks whether it has a named suspect, a witness, usable video, or physical evidence. If not, the case is often closed on paper the day it arrives, or it sits in a queue until it expires. Many cities now route minor thefts and burglaries to online reporting systems, where a resident types in what happened and receives a number in return. That number is a receipt, not an investigation.

Meanwhile, the evidence that could solve these crimes has never been more abundant. Doorbell cameras line residential streets. Phones record where nearly everyone has been. Stolen goods are resold online under accounts that can be traced. A determined investigator in 2026 has tools that detectives of the 1960s could not have imagined, and yet clearance rates for burglary and theft have barely moved. The problem is not a lack of evidence. It is a lack of anyone assigned to go and collect it.

The survey data shows that the public has noticed. When the government's national crime victimization survey asks people why they did not report a crime to the police, a common answer is that they believed the police would not or could not do anything. That belief is rational. The burglary victims I have interviewed were not cynical when they started. They became cynical after they called, waited and heard nothing.

The Collapse of Detective Work

Detective work is skilled labor, and in many departments it has been hollowed out. Detectives in big-city burglary and robbery units often carry caseloads that make real follow-up impossible. Homicide units, the elite of most departments, have struggled with staffing as experienced investigators retire and fewer officers want the job. Overtime budgets, crime lab backlogs and a shortage of trained digital examiners all slow cases further, while witnesses forget and evidence degrades.

Digital evidence has become the new bottleneck. A single homicide can involve dozens of phones, hours of video from businesses and homes, and data from social media accounts, each requiring warrants, extraction and analysis. Many departments have only a handful of examiners trained to do that work, and backlogs stretch for months. A case that could be cracked in a week by a well-staffed unit can sit until the trail goes cold. Departments that buy expensive equipment without hiring the people to use it have purchased ornaments.

Even the most closely watched cases expose the cracks. In 2008 investigators in Orange County, Florida, examined the family computer in the case of two-year-old Caylee Anthony. In 2012, more than a year after Casey Anthony was acquitted of murder in her daughter's death, the sheriff's office acknowledged that investigators had missed a Google search for fool-proof suffocation methods made on the day Caylee was last seen. They had pulled records from one web browser and overlooked another that held more than a thousand entries. No one can say who made the search or whether it would have changed the verdict. A basic step was missed in one of the most publicized investigations in the country.

The disappearance of Nancy Guthrie shows the same strain in a newer form. The 84-year-old mother of NBC anchor Savannah Guthrie was last seen at her home near Tucson, Arizona, on the night of January 31, 2026, and was reported missing the next day. The Pima County Sheriff's Department said it believed she had been taken against her will, the FBI joined the investigation, and investigators released doorbell camera images of a person at her door. DNA evidence moved from a private lab to the FBI's laboratory in Quantico. Months later, according to the most recent reports I could verify, no suspect had been publicly named and the case remained unresolved, while the county's board of supervisors publicly questioned the sheriff about his management of the department.

I make no judgment about who is responsible for her disappearance, and no one should. My point is about capacity. This was a case with a national spotlight, a famous family, a large reward and federal help. If such a case can stall for months, imagine the resources devoted to Tom's kicked-in back door, or to the young man shot on a corner whose name never makes the evening news.

The Myth of "Protect and Serve"

Painted on patrol cars across the country, the motto dates back to the Los Angeles Police Department in the 1950s. Most Americans believe it describes a legal duty, a promise that if they call, someone is obligated to come and to try. It does not. American courts have repeatedly held that police owe no constitutional or general legal duty to protect any particular person.

In 1975, in Washington, D.C., two women in a rooming house heard intruders attacking a third woman downstairs and called the police. Officers came, knocked, got no answer and left. The women called again. No one came. The intruders then held all three women for many hours, raping and beating them. In 1981, in Warren v. District of Columbia, the city's highest court ruled that their lawsuit could not proceed, holding that the government and its police have no general duty to provide protection to any individual citizen. Protection, the court reasoned, is owed to the public at large.

The Supreme Court went further in 2005. Jessica Gonzales of Castle Rock, Colorado, held a court order restraining her estranged husband, and the order told police to enforce it. One evening in 1999 he took their three daughters. She called the police repeatedly through the night, pleading with them to act. They did not. Hours later he drove to the police station and opened fire, and officers killed him. The three girls were found dead in his truck. In Town of Castle Rock v. Gonzales, the Court ruled seven to two that she had no constitutionally protected entitlement to enforcement of the order, because police retain discretion over how to enforce it.

I understand the legal logic. Courts fear an avalanche of lawsuits every time police fail to stop a crime, and no department can guard every citizen at every moment. But the public is never told about these rulings. Families make life decisions, about restraining orders, about whether to buy a gun, about whether to move, on the belief that the police are obligated to come. Legally, they are not. That gap between the motto and the law is one of the great unspoken deceptions in American civic life.

Public Disillusionment

People notice. National surveys of confidence in American institutions have shown trust in police sliding from the high levels of the early 2000s, with deep gaps by race, age and neighborhood. In the communities with the most violence, residents who call for help and receive none conclude, reasonably, that they are on their own. Some stop reporting. Some arm themselves. Some turn to the street's own justice, which is how one unsolved shooting becomes three.

Jessica Gonzales did not let her case end at the Supreme Court. She took it to the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights, which concluded in 2011 that the United States had violated her rights and those of her daughters by failing to protect them. The finding carried no power to force a payment, but it said plainly what American courts would not: a government that issues a protective order and then shrugs at its violation has failed the most basic test of public safety. Her story is known among advocates for domestic violence survivors. It should be known by every voter.

Tom did not buy a gun. He bought cameras, a steel door and a second lock, and he quit calling the police about the teenagers who cut through his yard at night. His neighbors did the same. The block is a little safer and a lot more alone. Multiply Tom by millions and you get a nation privately paying for the protection it already funds publicly, and losing the habit of cooperation that solves crimes.

Why Solving Crime Is No Longer a Priority

Policing has drifted toward the work that is easy to count. Departments measure arrests, stops, tickets and response times. Analyses of police call data in several cities have found that violent crime accounts for only a small share of what officers spend their time on, often in the single digits as a percentage of calls. Traffic, alarms, disturbances, mental health crises and paperwork fill the rest. None of that work is useless. Very little of it solves crimes.

The incentives point the wrong way. A patrol officer who makes a quick arrest for a minor drug offense produces a measurable statistic in an afternoon. A detective who spends three weeks building a burglary case may produce nothing at all. Budgets reward visible activity, overtime rewards court appearances, and politicians reward a drop in reported numbers, which can sometimes be achieved by discouraging reports rather than solving crimes.

That is not a hypothetical. In 2014 the Los Angeles Times found that the Los Angeles Police Department had misclassified many serious assaults as minor offenses over a period of years, making violent crime appear lower than it was. Similar problems with downgraded or unrecorded crimes have surfaced in other cities. When the scoreboard can be adjusted by the team keeping score, the score stops meaning anything, and the first casualty is the victim whose crime simply disappears from the record.

The Future of Accountability

Measurement comes first. City councils should require their police departments to publish clearance rates by crime type and by neighborhood, every quarter, alongside the budget. A department that receives a fifth of the general fund should have to tell taxpayers, in plain numbers, how many burglaries, shootings and rapes it solved. Tie a share of the budget to improving those numbers, not to arrests.

Police chiefs can start tomorrow without waiting for a council vote. Assign every reported burglary with any lead, a camera, a witness, a pawn record, to an investigator who must contact the victim within days. Tell victims, in writing, what was done on their case and why it was closed. Tom never learned whether anyone so much as read his report, and that silence did more damage to his faith in the system than the burglary itself. A department that respects the people who pay for it answers their calls twice: once when the crime happens, and again when the work is done.

Rebuild detective work deliberately. Hire and train more investigators, pay them to stay, and hire civilian analysts and digital forensics specialists rather than asking sworn officers to do everything. Get crime labs out from under police control. Houston did this after a long scandal over its police crime lab, creating an independent forensic science center that took over the work in 2014 and became a national model for transparency and reduced backlogs. Many states have passed laws requiring that every sexual assault kit be tested, and those laws have led to identifications of serial offenders that untested kits had hidden for years.

Camden, New Jersey, shows that a city can tear down a failing department and build a better one. In 2013, after a record number of homicides the year before, Camden dissolved its city police force and replaced it with a county-run department that put more officers on foot patrol, emphasized community relationships and adopted strict use-of-force rules. Homicides fell dramatically over the following decade. Camden's model is not perfect and not easily copied, but it proves that the existing system is a choice, not a law of nature.

Legislatures can close the gap that Warren and Castle Rock opened. States are free to write laws that make enforcement of protective orders mandatory and give victims a real remedy when police ignore them. Citizens can do their part by asking candidates for mayor, sheriff and district attorney one simple question: what is our clearance rate, and what will you do to raise it?

The takeaway

Americans pay more than a hundred billion dollars a year for local police and sheriffs who solve a small share of crimes and owe no legal duty to protect any one of us. Demand published clearance rates, rebuild real investigation, and judge every department by the crimes it solves, not the arrests it counts.

Chapter 38: AI Cameras: The Coming Surveillance Wave

Who is watching the street you live on right now? Most Americans have a vague sense that the answer is no longer "nobody." There is the doorbell camera across the way, the gray box on the light pole at the edge of the subdivision, the dark dome over the gas pump. What most people have not worked out is that many of those lenses are no longer just recording. They are reading, sorting, matching, and reporting, and the report goes somewhere you will never see.

I put the question to a homeowner I will call Teresa, a retired school nurse in a quiet Orange County cul-de-sac, after her car was broken into twice in one spring. She wanted cameras everywhere. She also wanted to know who would get to look at the footage of her driving to the pharmacy, to church, to her sister's house. She did not think those two wishes contradicted each other, and neither do I.

So here is the answer. The street you live on is increasingly watched by software, much of it bought by your city or your homeowners association, and it will keep spreading because it works well enough to sell. These cameras can prevent crime and solve it faster. Left without rules, they will also be abused, exactly the way police abused the last camera we were promised would fix them: the body camera. Both halves of that sentence are true, and the fight of the next decade is over which half wins.

How AI Cameras Can Stop Crime

An ordinary security camera is a witness with amnesia. It records everything and understands nothing, so a human has to sit and scrub through hours of footage after the damage is done. An AI camera changes the economics. Software can read a license plate at speed, flag a car reported stolen, recognize a vehicle by its make, color and dents, and alert a dispatcher in seconds. Newer systems search video by description, so an investigator can ask for every white pickup with a ladder rack that passed a given corner on a Tuesday night.

I have watched detectives work cases the old way, knocking on doors for days to find one neighbor with a working camera. The new way collapses that search into minutes. For a burglary victim, a carjacking victim, or a family waiting for word on a missing child, minutes matter more than any argument about civil liberties ever will. An Amber Alert plate that pings a reader on a freeway on-ramp is a child who might come home.

The research on traditional surveillance cameras is more modest than the marketing. Systematic reviews of decades of CCTV studies, many of them from Britain, find real but moderate reductions in crime, strongest in parking lots and residential areas and when someone is actively watching. That is the honest baseline. Cameras do not end crime. They raise the odds of being caught, and certainty of being caught deters more reliably than severity of punishment. Software that makes every camera smarter raises those odds further, which is why I believe the wave is coming whether we plan for it or not.

Early Adopters in Irvine, Newport Beach and Beyond

Orange County is a useful laboratory because it is wealthy, safe by national standards, and nervous anyway. In July 2024 the Newport Beach City Council approved a five-year contract worth roughly $1.4 million for 69 Flock Safety cameras placed at the city's inbound and outbound gateways. City officials said the cameras read plates and vehicle characteristics only, have no facial recognition, would be used for criminal investigations and hotlist alerts such as stolen cars and Amber Alerts, and would not be used to write traffic tickets.

Next door, Irvine has used license plate readers for years. Public records compiled by privacy researchers show the department scanning well over a hundred thousand plates in a single year, and by 2023 the city reported fourteen readers in operation while the council directed staff to plan more cameras in parks and public facilities, with video analytics software to search the footage. In July 2026 Irvine's mayor wrote to oppose a state bill, SB 1013, that would cap retention of non-hotlist plate data at thirty days and restrict sharing with federal agencies. The city credited plate readers with helping to drive down organized retail theft at its shopping centers, and its police chief stated the department does not share the data with federal immigration enforcement.

That letter is worth reading closely, because it captures the whole debate in two pages. The city wants long retention because crimes like sexual assault, domestic violence and trafficking are often reported weeks or months later, and a plate read placing a car near the scene can be the only corroboration a victim has. Privacy advocates want short retention because a database of everyone's movements is dangerous in the wrong hands. Both are describing real victims. I have sat with both kinds, the woman whose attacker was identified by a camera and the woman whose ex-husband used a badge to find her new address.

Beyond Orange County, the same company's cameras now sit in thousands of communities, often purchased by homeowners associations and shared with police through a vendor portal. Teresa's neighborhood was debating exactly that when I met her. The pitch was simple, the cost per household was small, and almost no one at the meeting asked who would be able to search the data from three states away.

The British Model

If you want to see where a camera-saturated society leads, look at the United Kingdom. Industry estimates from the British Security Industry Association put the number of closed-circuit cameras there in the millions, with the upper estimate working out to roughly one camera for every eleven people. Most are privately owned, in shops, offices and flats, but the effect on a pedestrian in central London is the same: you are almost never out of frame.

The British experience teaches two lessons. The first is that cameras alone did not deliver a crime-free country. They helped solve many cases, notably in terrorism investigations where footage traced bombers' movements, but evaluations found the deterrent effect uneven. The second lesson is more important. When British police began adding live facial recognition to the camera network, a man named Ed Bridges challenged South Wales Police in court. In 2020 the Court of Appeal ruled the force's use of automated facial recognition unlawful, finding the legal framework left too much discretion over where it could be used and who went on a watchlist, and that the force had not taken reasonable steps to check the software for racial or gender bias.

That ruling did not ban the technology. British police have since expanded live facial recognition under revised guidance. What it established is the principle I want Americans to steal: a court looked at a new surveillance tool, asked who decides how it is used, and found the answer was not good enough. Britain had a national debate, however imperfect. America is mostly skipping that step.

The Reckless Deployment Problem

Here the story turns. In January 2020 Detroit police arrested Robert Williams on his front lawn in front of his wife and young daughters, accused of stealing watches from a store. The evidence was a facial recognition match from blurry surveillance footage. It was wrong. He spent about thirty hours in custody before the case collapsed. In June 2024 the city settled his lawsuit for $300,000 and agreed that police would no longer arrest people based solely on facial recognition results, along with a review of earlier cases where the technology had been used.

Williams was not unlucky. He was the predictable result of a tool deployed with no rule saying a computer's guess is not evidence. And the plate-reader networks have produced their own scandal sheet. In May 2025 the independent outlet 404 Media reported that a sheriff's deputy in Johnson County, Texas, had searched a national network of tens of thousands of Flock cameras, including cameras in states where abortion is legal, logging the reason as a search for a woman who had an abortion. The sheriff said the purpose was her safety. Court records later reported by the same outlet showed investigators had also treated the matter as a death investigation and discussed whether she could be charged.

Then came the audits. In 2026 a Georgia sheriff's office reviewing its own camera searches ended up with seven cases of improper access, three firings and four resignations, and officers elsewhere in Georgia, Arizona and Wisconsin were fired or charged for allegedly using the system to track coworkers, relatives and acquaintances. Flock responded by cutting its default data retention from thirty days to seven and requiring case numbers for searches. Those are good changes. They came from a vendor under pressure, not from any law, which tells you how thin the guardrails were.

Body Cameras as a Warning

I have been here before. After Ferguson in 2014, body cameras were sold to the public as the great equalizer. Every encounter recorded, every lie exposed, every honest officer vindicated. Federal grants helped pay for the rollout, and departments bought the devices by the hundreds of thousands, along with the cloud storage contracts that never stop billing. A decade later the record is sobering, and it should be read aloud at every council meeting where AI cameras are on the agenda.

Cameras got switched off. In July 2017 a Minneapolis officer shot and killed Justine Damond, an Australian woman who had called 911 to report a possible assault behind her home. Neither officer at the scene had a body camera recording. The department responded by requiring activation on every call and traffic stop, yet critics pointed out the rule carried no clear penalty, and a later city audit found compliance problems persisted.

Footage got withheld. Chicago police shot Laquan McDonald sixteen times in October 2014. The dashboard camera video, which contradicted the official account, stayed out of public view for more than a year until a judge ordered its release in November 2015. The officer was later convicted of second-degree murder. The delay, not the camera, shaped the city's trust for years.

And the promised transformation of behavior largely failed to show up in the data. A large randomized trial in Washington, D.C., involving more than two thousand officers, found that body cameras produced no statistically significant change in use of force or civilian complaints. The camera worked as a recorder. It did not work as a conscience, because the people controlling the switch and the archive were the same people being recorded. Put that sentence on the wall before buying anything with "AI" in the brochure.

The Risk of Mass Surveillance

One plate read is trivial. A year of plate reads is a diary. It shows where you sleep, where you worship, which clinic you visit, which union hall, which lawyer, which bar, which friend's house you leave at two in the morning. The Supreme Court understood this in Carpenter v. United States in 2018, holding that police generally need a warrant to obtain extended historical cell-phone location records, because that kind of data gives the government an intimate window into a person's life that the Fourth Amendment protects.

Courts have not yet extended Carpenter to camera networks. In January 2026 a federal judge in Virginia ruled for the city of Norfolk in a lawsuit challenging its Flock cameras, concluding that photographing plates on public roads and querying the database was not a search, and noting that most courts to consider the question have reached the same answer. The plaintiffs said they would appeal. Until a higher court speaks, the law treats your accumulated movements on public roads as fair game.

Think about who sits at the other end of that login. Most officers will never misuse it. A few will, and the Georgia audits show that a few is not zero. For a domestic violence survivor who has moved across town to escape an abuser with a badge or a friend on the force, a network that logs every car on every road is not an abstraction. It is a map to her front door.

That is the danger in plain terms. A tool built to find stolen cars becomes, through drift and convenience, a tool that can find anyone. Nobody has to plan the abuse. It only takes a database, a login, and a bad week in somebody's personal life. Teresa put it better than any lawyer I have interviewed. She said she was fine being seen, but she was not fine being followed.

The Future of AI-Driven Policing

Pilot programs already show the next generation. Cameras that flag a person "loitering" or a crowd "forming." Drones dispatched to 911 calls ahead of patrol cars, streaming live video. Software that writes the first draft of a police report from body camera audio. Fusion centers that knit plate readers, private doorbell footage, social media and arrest records into one searchable screen. Each piece will be sold as a time saver, and some will save lives.

Some of it is already routine. Chula Vista, a city south of San Diego, began sending drones to emergency calls in 2018, and the model has since spread to departments across the country. A drone that reaches a reported shooting before an officer does can tell responders whether the man in the parking lot is holding a gun or a phone, and that information can keep a frightened officer from firing. The same drone, hovering over a protest or a backyard, is a camera no fence can stop. Every one of these tools carries both futures inside it.

I expect the price of this technology to fall and its reach to grow until a city without it looks negligent. That is how body cameras spread, and it is how plate readers spread. The question is not whether your community gets AI cameras. The question is whether your community writes the rules before the vendor does, and whether a survivor, a taxpayer and a homeowner like Teresa have any seat at that table.

Balancing Safety and Civil Rights

The fixes are not mysterious, and several have already been tested somewhere. Start with community control. Seattle, which strengthened its surveillance ordinance in 2017, and Oakland, which passed one in 2018, require police to bring every new surveillance technology to the council for public review, a written use policy, and annual reporting before and after purchase. Any city council can pass one in a season. Pair it with an independent auditor who reviews search logs, not just the policy binder.

Next, legislatures should set hard limits. Short retention for non-hit data, with an evidence process for investigations that need longer. California already restricts sharing plate data with out-of-state and federal agencies under its 2015 plate-reader law, and the state attorney general issued guidance in 2023 reinforcing that limit. Every state should require a case number and stated purpose for every search, publish aggregate search data, and make misuse a crime with real penalties. Adopt Detroit's settlement rule as statute everywhere: no arrest on a facial recognition match alone.

Police chiefs can fix the body camera failures now. Automatic activation tied to drawn weapons, sirens or car doors. Discipline for officers who fail to record. Release of critical-incident footage on a fixed clock, as California has required since a 2018 law set a forty-five-day default. And courts, when the next challenge arrives, should apply Carpenter's logic honestly: a searchable record of a person's movements over weeks is the same intimate window whether it comes from a cell tower or a camera on a pole.

Citizens have a job too. If your homeowners association is voting on cameras, ask four questions before you vote yes. Who can search the data, how long is it kept, who else can it be shared with, and who audits the searches. Teresa asked all four at her next meeting, reading them off an index card while her neighbors shifted in their folding chairs. The board did not have answers. They do now, in writing, posted on the association website, and the cameras went up anyway. That is what winning looks like.

The takeaway

AI cameras will spread because they help catch criminals, and I want them catching criminals. Body cameras taught us that a lens controlled by the people it watches protects no one. So go to the meeting, and demand the surveillance ordinance, the retention limit, the published audit and the warrant rule before the cameras go up, not after the first scandal.

Chapter 39: Robots Will Replace Police in Many Roles

Two traffic violations, twenty years apart, tell the whole story. The first happened to a man I will call Andre on a two-lane road at dusk, when he was twenty-two. A dead taillight. Blue lights in the mirror. An officer at his window with a hand on his holster, a second officer at the passenger side with a flashlight in his eyes, and a question about whether there was anything in the car he should know about. He spent forty minutes on the shoulder with his palms on the dashboard. He was not arrested. He has never forgotten it.

The second violation happened last spring. Andre, now in his forties, drove a few miles over the limit through a school zone. Nobody stopped him. Two weeks later an envelope arrived with a photograph of his license plate, a time stamp, a speed reading and a modest fine. He paid it online in three minutes. Nobody's hand went near a gun, and Andre slowed down in that zone for good.

The Rise of Autonomous Enforcement

Hold those two moments side by side, because the distance between them is where American policing is going. In the first, the state enforced a minor rule by sending two armed men to stand at a car window in the dark, each of them trained to assume the driver might try to kill him. In the second, the state enforced the same kind of rule with a camera, a database and the mail. The first encounter carried a small but real chance that someone would die. The second carried none.

I have covered traffic stops that ended in a funeral and I have covered speed camera programs that ended in angry letters to the editor. I prefer the letters. Automated enforcement is not science fiction. Red-light cameras have been running in American cities for decades. New York City's school-zone speed camera program, launched in 2014, has been credited by city transportation reports with large drops in speeding at camera locations and fewer injuries compared with streets without cameras. In 2023 California authorized a five-year speed camera pilot in six cities, including Los Angeles, San Francisco and Oakland.

That is the first stage of autonomous enforcement: machines that observe, record and bill. The second stage, already in pilot programs, puts the machine on wheels or wings. Security robots roll through parking garages. Drones answer 911 calls ahead of patrol cars. Software reads plates, sorts video and drafts reports. Andre's first stop happened in a world where an armed human was the only tool available. His second happened in a world where that is no longer true, and the gap between those two worlds is the opportunity I want this country to seize.

Robots for Traffic Stops

Traffic stops are the most common way Americans meet police. Tens of millions happen each year, on interstates and cul-de-sacs, at noon and at two in the morning. The overwhelming majority are about equipment, registration and speed, the kind of violation that a camera can document and a letter can resolve. Yet we still send the most expensive, most heavily armed public employee we have to handle them, one stop at a time.

Some cities have tried to pull the gun out of the equation even before the robots arrive. In 2021 Philadelphia's City Council passed its Driving Equality law, which took effect in March 2022 and barred police from stopping drivers solely for a short list of minor violations such as a single broken brake light, an item hanging from the mirror or an expired inspection sticker. Stops for those violations fell by roughly half in the first year, according to the bill's sponsor, though local reporting found the racial makeup of drivers stopped did not change. Berkeley's council voted in 2020 to pursue moving traffic enforcement to unarmed civilian staff, and ran straight into California law, which reserves enforcement of the vehicle code to sworn officers.

That legal wall is exactly why automation matters. A camera does not need to be sworn. A speed camera, a red-light camera, an automated registration reader or a camera that detects a driver holding a phone can do most of what a routine stop does, and the enforcement happens by mail. Where a human is genuinely needed, for a drunk driver weaving across lanes or a car linked to a violent felony, the police remain. Everything else can move to the machine.

Machines That Don't Carry Guns

In 2021 The New York Times published an investigation finding that over five years, police had killed more than four hundred drivers and passengers who were not wielding a gun or knife and were not being pursued for a violent crime. That is more than one a week. Very few of those killings produced a criminal conviction. Many began with a minor violation.

Philando Castile was one of those drivers. In July 2016 he was pulled over in a Minnesota suburb with his girlfriend and her four-year-old daughter in the car. He told the officer he was carrying a firearm, which he was licensed to carry. Within seconds the officer fired seven times. Castile died, his girlfriend streamed the aftermath live, and the officer was acquitted of manslaughter in 2017. I have watched that video more times than I want to admit, and what stays with me is how ordinary the first thirty seconds are. A man reaching for his license. A child in the back seat.

Tyre Nichols was stopped by officers from a Memphis street crime unit in January 2023, beaten after he ran, and died three days later. The police chief later said the department could not substantiate the reckless-driving claim that supposedly justified the stop, and the city disbanded the unit. Two officers pleaded guilty; the other three were acquitted of all state charges in 2025, after a federal jury had convicted them on lesser counts while acquitting them of the most serious ones. I keep the focus on the stop itself. The violence began with an armed unit deciding that a traffic matter required a confrontation.

I owe the reader honesty about the hardest counterexample. On July 7, 2016, in Dallas, a gunman ambushed officers at the end of a peaceful protest and killed five of them. After hours of negotiation, police sent a bomb-disposal robot carrying an explosive toward the wall where he was holed up and detonated it, killing him. It was the first known time an American police department used a robot to kill someone. So the claim that robots will not kill is false. A machine kills when people decide to make it kill.

That case actually sharpens my argument. The danger was never the machine. The danger is the decision to bring lethal force into an encounter. A camera on a pole that mails a speeding ticket carries no explosive and no gun, and no one can panic while holding it. Remove the gun from routine enforcement and the routine stops stop killing people.

Why Robots Will Be Trusted More Than Police

Trust is a strong word, and I will not pretend machines earn it automatically. The early record of security robots includes slapstick and worse. A Knightscope security robot rolled over a toddler's foot at a California shopping center in 2016, and another tumbled into a fountain in Washington, D.C., the next year. In New York the police department leased a Knightscope K5 to patrol the Times Square subway station in 2023 for a reported cost of about nine dollars an hour, and the pilot ended within months with the robot sitting in storage. Privacy advocates objected from day one that the machine could be fitted with facial recognition.

Speed cameras have their own critics, and some of them are right. Investigative reporting in Chicago found camera tickets and late fees fell hardest on drivers in poorer and majority-Black neighborhoods, piling debt onto people least able to pay, with some losing their licenses or filing for bankruptcy over fines that began as forty-dollar violations. A machine can be biased in where it is placed and cruel in how it fines. Trust has to be designed in, not assumed.

Here is why trust still shifts toward the machine. A camera does not decide that a man looks suspicious because of the neighborhood or the hour. It does not lose its temper, does not escalate because a driver asked a question, and does not file a report that contradicts its own footage. Its errors are visible, repeatable and fixable by policy. A human officer's worst moment is often invisible until a bystander's phone catches it.

Andre told me what he wanted from any enforcement system was simple. He wanted to know that a taillight would never be the last thing that happened to him. He did not care whether the thing enforcing the law had a badge or a lens. He cared whether it could hurt his son, who had just gotten his learner's permit and drives the same two-lane road at dusk.

Cost Savings

A sworn officer is one of the most expensive line items in any city budget. Salary, overtime, health insurance, training, vehicle, equipment and pension together run well past the base salary, and in many larger departments the fully loaded cost of one officer reaches well into six figures a year. Every hour that officer spends writing a ticket for an expired registration is an hour not spent on an unsolved shooting.

Automated systems cost a fraction of that per violation handled. A camera works around the clock, does not draw a pension, and can be relocated in a day. New York's subway robot was leased for about the hourly cost of a fast-food worker. Speed camera programs in many cities cover their own costs from fines. For a taxpayer, the arithmetic is straightforward: shift routine enforcement to machines, keep the officers for violent crime, and the city pays less for more.

There is a second saving hidden in the first. Analyses of patrol and 911 data in several cities have found that only a small share of officers' working time goes to violent crime, while a large share goes to traffic, noise complaints, alarms and paperwork. Moving traffic to machines frees the most expensive hours in the department for the cases that actually terrify residents, and it does so without hiring a single new officer.

I add one warning, because I have watched it happen. Cities fall in love with camera revenue. The moment a program is designed to raise money rather than to change behavior, it becomes a tax on the poor dressed up as safety. A good program measures success by falling violations and falling crashes, and it plows any surplus into street redesign, not the general fund.

Public Realization

Something else happens when machines take over routine enforcement. People see the old system for what it was. Once a broken taillight is handled by mail without incident, it becomes hard to explain why we ever sent armed officers to the window for it. Once a camera records a stop from start to finish with no switch to flip, the gap between the footage and the old police reports becomes obvious.

Andre described the moment it happened for him. He was reading the speed camera notice at his kitchen table, the photograph of his own bumper in one corner, and he started laughing, not because the fine was funny but because the whole transaction had been so calm. Then he stopped laughing. He thought about the forty minutes on the shoulder at twenty-two, his hands on the dashboard, and realized that the state had always had a safer option. It had simply never chosen it for people like him.

That realization is already under way. Every viral video of a stop gone wrong forces the same question. Why was that officer there at all? Philadelphia's reform, Berkeley's proposal and the speed camera programs in New York and California are all answers to that question. When the public finally sees routine enforcement done safely, cheaply and without blood, the old model will look the way bloodletting looks to a modern doctor: not malicious, just indefensible.

The Future of Robotic Policing

I expect a division of labor within a generation. Machines will handle speed, red lights, registration and parking, much of property-crime documentation, and the first look at many 911 calls through drones and remote cameras. Humans will handle violence, investigations, mental health crises alongside clinicians, and any situation that needs judgment, mercy or arrest. Patrol will shrink. Detective work should grow, and so should the clearance rates for murder and rape that have embarrassed American policing for decades. The armed officer at a car window for a minor violation will become rare, then strange, then history.

That future is not guaranteed to be better. Robots armed with tasers or weapons, robots fitted with facial recognition, robots deployed only in poor neighborhoods would reproduce the worst of the old system with less accountability. New York offers a preview of the whiplash. In 2021 the police department ended its lease of a four-legged robot from Boston Dynamics after a public outcry over its use in a public housing building, and two years later a new mayor brought the robot dogs back. Each swing came from a political mood, not a law.

San Francisco showed how fast the line can move and how fast the public can push back. In late November 2022 the Board of Supervisors approved a policy allowing police robots to use deadly force in limited situations. Police officials had argued the option would be reserved for extreme emergencies, the kind Dallas faced. After protests at City Hall and a flood of public criticism, the board reversed itself a week later and sent the question back to committee. I credit the department's good faith; nobody wants another officer killed by an entrenched gunman. But a decision about when a city may kill with a machine belongs to voters and their elected representatives in daylight, not to a line buried in an equipment policy.

Ethical and Legal Guardrails

The law gives police enormous discretion at the roadside. In Whren v. United States in 1996, the Supreme Court held that a stop is lawful if an officer has probable cause of any traffic violation, whatever the officer's real motive. That ruling is the legal engine of the pretextual stop, and it is why a dead taillight can become a drug search or worse. Legislatures cannot overrule the Constitution's floor, but they can raise the ceiling, as Philadelphia did, by making minor violations non-stoppable offenses enforced by mail. Every state should pass a version of that law and pair it with authority for cities to use automated enforcement.

Next, ban weaponized robots by statute. No robot, drone or remote vehicle should carry a firearm, taser or explosive except in a narrow, pre-authorized hostage or active-shooter protocol approved by an elected body and reported publicly within days of every use. Dallas shows why the exception exists, and it also shows why it must be written down before the next crisis rather than improvised during it. San Francisco's reversal is the model of public pressure; state law should make the result permanent.

Third, protect due process in automated tickets. Courts have generally upheld camera tickets as civil penalties; in 2009 the federal appeals court in Chicago rejected a constitutional challenge to that city's red-light cameras, but fairness requires a clear appeal path, a human reviewer for every contested ticket, income-based fines or payment plans, and placement decided by crash data rather than revenue projections. Publish the map of every camera and the data on who gets ticketed, by neighborhood.

Fourth, keep the data narrow. A speed camera should store violations, not every passing car. Robots and drones should carry no facial recognition without a warrant, and footage should be deleted on a fixed schedule unless it is evidence in a case. A city council can write all four rules into one ordinance, and a police chief willing to give up traffic duty can be its strongest advocate. Citizens can show up at the hearing and say, in plain words, that they would rather get a letter than a visit.

The takeaway

Armed traffic stops are a choice, not a necessity, and machines are about to prove it. Call your state legislator and ask for three things: a ban on stops for minor equipment violations, authority for automated enforcement with fair fines, and a statutory ban on robots that carry weapons. Then keep officers for the violence only humans can stop.

Chapter 40: A Blueprint for Prevention America Still Won't Use

"So what would you actually do?" Carol asked, and pushed the sugar across the table at me as if it were a dare. We were in a diner off the county road after a budget hearing where she had spent her three minutes at the microphone asking why the jail line item had grown again. Carol is a retired bookkeeper. She reads municipal budgets the way some people read mysteries, and she had heard enough complaints from me over the years. She wanted a list. Not a speech. A list she could carry into the next hearing.

I told her the truth, which is that the list already exists. It has existed for decades. Almost every item on it has been tested in an American city or a peer country, measured by researchers with no stake in the outcome, and found to cost less than the crime it prevents. What we lack is not knowledge. It is the willingness to spend money now on children, readers, addicts and neighborhoods instead of later on prisons, funerals and insurance claims. Carol wrote that sentence on her napkin. Then she said, "Fine. Prove it." Here is what I told her, widened out for everyone who pays the bill.

The conversation Carol and I had in that booth happens, in some form, in every town in America. A homeowner wonders why the break-ins keep coming. A survivor wonders why the man who hurt her was released without treatment. A pastor buries another teenager and asks the mayor what the plan is. The plan is the same in each case, and it has six parts. None of them is new. Each has been tried, measured and found to work somewhere, and each is ignored somewhere else because its payoff arrives after the next election.

Early Childhood Intervention

Start before the child can walk. The Nurse-Family Partnership sends trained nurses into the homes of low-income, first-time mothers from pregnancy through the child's second birthday. Randomized trials beginning in Elmira, New York, and later in Memphis and Denver tracked those families for years. In Elmira, nurse-visited mothers had fewer verified reports of child abuse and neglect, and by age fifteen their children had markedly fewer arrests than children in the comparison group. Few crime-prevention programs in American history can show evidence that strong.

The Perry Preschool study in Michigan, which followed a small group of children from the 1960s into middle age, pointed the same way: high-quality early education was associated with fewer arrests and higher earnings decades later, and economists have estimated the return to the public at several dollars for every dollar spent. Neither program is a miracle. Both are simply cheaper than what comes after a neglected childhood.

Carol's question was practical, so the answer is too. Every state can fund nurse home visiting through Medicaid, which many already allow, and expand it until every eligible first-time mother who wants a nurse gets one. Counties can screen infants and toddlers for lead exposure and developmental delay and act on what they find. Those are budget lines, not slogans.

Carol did the arithmetic on her napkin while I talked. A year of incarceration costs taxpayers tens of thousands of dollars per prisoner in most states, far more in some. A nurse visiting a family for two and a half years costs a fraction of one prison year. She circled the two figures and drew an arrow between them. Then she asked the question every taxpayer should ask a legislator: if the cheap option prevents the expensive one, why does the expensive one always get funded first? I did not have a good answer. Nobody in Sacramento, Albany or Austin has given one either.

Literacy Programs

A child who cannot read by the end of third grade is in trouble, and the trouble compounds. Up to that point children learn to read; after it they read to learn, and a child who cannot keep up starts to fall behind in every subject at once. Shame follows, then acting out, then suspension. Low literacy is strongly associated with dropping out, and dropping out is strongly associated with incarceration. Walk through any adult prison education program, as I have, and you will meet grown men sounding out words their own children are learning.

Mississippi shows this is fixable. In 2013 the state passed a literacy law that retrained teachers in the science of reading, put coaches into struggling schools, screened children early, and held back third graders who could not read at a basic level while giving them intensive help. Mississippi had long sat near the bottom of national reading rankings. Within a few years its fourth graders climbed to roughly the national average on the federal reading assessment, and the gains held in later rounds. It did not happen through a new building or a new slogan. It happened through phonics, training and persistence.

Any legislature can copy the core of that law, and dozens of states have since passed their own science-of-reading measures. Fund reading coaches, require evidence-based instruction, screen early, and put tutoring dollars where the screens find need. School boards can stop buying curricula that teach children to guess at words from pictures. For prisons, make literacy instruction a standard part of every sentence, because a man released unable to fill out a job application is being set up to come back, and every taxpayer will pay for his return.

Addiction Treatment

Drug dependence drives an enormous share of property crime, street violence and overdose deaths, and America still treats it mostly with handcuffs. Jail is where the cycle is easiest to break and where we most often waste the chance. The weeks after release are the deadliest period an addicted person faces, because tolerance drops behind bars and the drugs on the street do not wait.

Carol knew this from her own family. Her nephew went in and out of county jail for years on theft charges tied to his pills, each stay a few weeks long, each release a coin toss. Nobody in that jail ever offered him medication. Nobody walked him from the jail door to a clinic. He survived, finally, because a treatment program in a neighboring county had an open bed on the day he was ready. She told me his recovery was luck, and luck is a terrible public policy.

Rhode Island acted on that fact. In 2016 its Department of Corrections began screening incoming prisoners for opioid use disorder and offering all three approved medications, with links to treatment in the community after release. A study published in JAMA Psychiatry in 2018 found deaths from overdose among recently released prisoners fell by about sixty percent in the period studied, a decline that contributed to a drop in overdose deaths statewide. The program was not soft. It was a corrections department deciding that people leaving its custody should leave alive.

Portugal, which decriminalized personal possession of drugs in 2001 and shifted resources to treatment, is the international example most often cited. Its results have been debated and its system has strained in recent years under funding cuts, but it ended the practice of treating every user as a criminal without the collapse critics predicted. The American lesson is narrower and easier to adopt: medication for addiction in every jail and prison, treatment on demand in every county, and drug courts that measure recovery rather than punishment.

Technology-Driven Policing With Guardrails

I am not anti-police technology. I have argued for cameras, license plate readers, automated traffic enforcement and robots that take guns out of routine stops. Used well, they raise the certainty of being caught, which deters crime more reliably than harsh sentences, and they free officers for the violent cases that go unsolved in this country at shameful rates. A large share of American murders and a far larger share of rapes never end in an arrest, and survivors live with that silence for the rest of their lives. Technology that closes even part of that gap is worth buying.

Used badly, they become instruments of abuse. The blueprint therefore pairs every purchase with a rule. A public ordinance before any surveillance tool is bought, as Oakland and Seattle require. Short data retention with an evidence exception. A published log of searches and an independent auditor who reads it. No arrest on a facial recognition match alone. Body cameras that switch on automatically and footage released on a fixed clock. Technology without these guardrails is not prevention. It is a new way to repeat the old failures.

One more rule belongs here, because I have seen what happens without it. Every technology contract should include an independent evaluation paid for out of the purchase price, with results published whether the vendor likes them or not. Cities routinely renew surveillance and analytics contracts on the strength of a sales presentation and a handful of anecdotes. If a gunshot detection system or a predictive software package cannot show that it helps solve or prevent crime, the money should move to detectives, crime labs or the prevention programs above.

Transparency and Spending Accountability

Carol's specialty is the money, and the money is where prevention dies. In 2024 California's State Auditor reported that the state had spent roughly $24 billion on homelessness programs over five years without consistently tracking whether those programs worked. That is not a homelessness problem alone. It is the way most public safety spending works. Agencies report what they spent and how many people they processed, almost never what changed.

Prevention cannot compete in that environment, because its benefits show up years later in someone else's budget. The nurse visit pays off in the juvenile court; the reading coach pays off in the prison system; the treatment slot pays off in the emergency room and the property insurance market. A council that sees only this year's line items will always cut the program that saves money in ten years.

The fix is a public scorecard. Every city and county should publish, each year, spending on policing, jails, courts and prevention side by side, with outcomes attached: clearance rates for homicide and rape, repeat-offense rates, overdose deaths, third-grade reading scores. Require every public safety program over a set dollar amount to report results or lose its funding. Give the auditor subpoena power. Open contracts and settlement payments to public view. Government that hides its numbers is protecting someone, and it is rarely the taxpayer.

Carol has a short test she now applies at every hearing, and I recommend it to anyone who attends one. For each public safety request, she asks three questions into the microphone. What did this program accomplish last year, measured by something other than how much it spent? How do you know? What will you stop funding if it fails? Most departments cannot answer the first question. The silence that follows is more informative than any annual report, and it tends to make council members nervous enough to ask the same questions themselves the next time.

Community-Centered Prevention

The most dangerous few hundred people in a large city are usually known by name to police, outreach workers and their own neighbors. Boston's Operation Ceasefire in the 1990s built a strategy on that fact, combining a direct message to gang members with real offers of help and certain consequences for the next shooting, and youth homicides fell steeply. Chicago's Cure Violence model, started by an epidemiologist in 2000, treats shootings like a contagious disease and sends trusted outreach workers, many of them former gang members, to interrupt retaliation. Evaluations of Cure Violence sites have been mixed, with strong drops in some neighborhoods and little change in others, which tells me the model works when it is run with discipline and funded steadily.

Camden, New Jersey, recorded sixty-seven homicides in 2012, the most in the city's history. The next year the city dissolved its police department and replaced it with a county force that emphasized foot patrols, community relationships and de-escalation training. Violent crime has since fallen by more than half, and homicides have dropped far below their peak. Camden is not a utopia, and its reform involved breaking a union contract that critics still contest, but it shows that a broken department can be rebuilt around the community it serves.

Even the physical street matters. In a randomized trial in Philadelphia, researchers cleaned and greened hundreds of vacant lots, adding grass, a few trees and a low fence. Neighborhoods below the poverty line saw gun violence drop markedly near the treated lots, and later analysis found that even basic mowing and trash cleanup reduced shootings. Residents also reported feeling safer and going outside more. A city can do that with a work crew and a small budget, and a homeowner's block association can lobby for it in a single meeting.

What Each of Us Can Do

The plan works only if the people who pay for crime demand it. Survivors carry the most moral authority in any hearing room, and I have watched a single mother's testimony move a budget vote that a dozen experts could not. Victims' groups can push legislatures to fund trauma recovery centers, nurse home visiting and violence interruption, and they can testify that what they wanted was for the next family to be spared, not merely for someone to be punished.

Taxpayers like Carol can demand the public scorecard, read it, and show up when prevention line items are cut. Survivors can also press for trauma recovery centers, a model first developed at San Francisco General Hospital that offers victims counseling, legal help and case management in one place, because a survivor left alone with untreated trauma is a family put at risk of the next cycle.

Homeowners have direct leverage too. They can ask their homeowners association and city council to fund lot cleanup, street lighting and youth programs before buying another camera, and to adopt surveillance rules when they do buy one. Community leaders, pastors, coaches, union stewards, business owners, can host outreach workers, volunteer as reading tutors and sponsor summer jobs, which a randomized study of Chicago's summer jobs program, published in 2014, linked to substantially fewer violent-crime arrests among participating teenagers. Police chiefs can hand routine traffic to machines and put their best detectives on unsolved shootings. Judges can order treatment rather than another short jail stay. Legislators can write the scorecard into law so that it survives the next administration.

When we finished, Carol folded the napkin into her purse. At the next budget hearing she read it aloud, item by item, and asked the council which of the six they intended to fund. Two council members asked for copies. One of them called her a week later to ask where the Rhode Island study could be found. That is how prevention actually advances in America, not through a national commission but through one stubborn taxpayer at a time, and then a second, and then a room full of them. None of it requires a miracle. All of it requires a vote.

The takeaway

Nothing in the blueprint is secret: nurses for new mothers, reading taught properly, treatment behind bars, technology on a leash, budgets that report results and neighborhoods that intervene before the next shooting. Every piece costs less than the crime it prevents. Pick one item, find the budget line that funds it in your city, and show up at the hearing where it is decided.

Chapter 41: The Why Behind Violence — And Why America Keeps Burying It

The candles on the corner had burned down to wax puddles by the time Ray started stacking the folding chairs. He runs a youth boxing gym in a storefront two blocks away, and the boy whose photo was taped to the light pole had trained there for three years before he stopped coming.
