Ray did not need a detective to tell him why the boy died. He knew the boy's father had gone to prison when the boy was six. He knew the boy read at a fourth-grade level in tenth grade. He knew about the older cousin, the dispute that started online, the gun passed around the block like a borrowed jacket.

Ray told me he had warned three different adults in three different offices: a school counselor, a probation officer, a caseworker at the county. Each of them listened. Each of them wrote something down. None of them had the authority or the money to do anything with what they wrote. When the boy stopped coming to the gym, Ray drove past his house twice and once caught him on the porch, thinner, jumpy, promising he would be back on Monday. Monday came and went. Ray keeps the boy's old gloves on a hook by the door.

I have stood on corners like that one for most of my working life, and the scene rarely changes. The tape. The mother. The television truck that leaves before the candles are lit. And the community leader, someone like Ray, who knew it was coming and could not get anyone with a budget to listen. If there is one sentence that holds everything I have reported, it is this: American violence is not a mystery. We know why it happens. We have decided not to act on what we know.

The Roots We Refuse to See

Look back across the causes and you see the same few roots again and again. Children injured early, by abuse, neglect, lead, or a parent lost to prison or addiction, who carry that injury into adolescence untreated. Children who never learn to read and come to believe school has nothing for them. Addiction that turns ordinary people into thieves and dealers and turns neighborhoods into markets. Trauma passed down through families like a debt. Disputes between cliques and families that police misread as random violence, until the retaliation arrives.

None of these roots is hidden. They show up in school records, pediatric files, child welfare reports, probation histories. They show up in what Ray knew about one boy. What hides them is a culture that prefers a villain to a cause. A villain can be arrested, convicted and forgotten. A cause demands that we change something about ourselves, our budgets and our institutions.

Both political tribes have their own reasons for looking away. One side fears that talking about family breakdown, addiction or the behavior of offenders will sound like blaming the poor. The other fears that talking about poverty, trauma, police misconduct or failing schools will sound like excusing criminals. So each side talks past the evidence, and the evidence keeps piling up in the files. Naming a root cause is not an excuse. It is the first step toward a cure, and the people who suffer most from our silence are the victims who live on the blocks where the roots grow deepest.

I have watched this preference shape the national conversation for decades. The cases Americans remember are the spectacular ones, and the spectacle teaches the wrong lesson. In 2008 a two-year-old named Caylee Anthony disappeared in Florida, and her mother, Casey Anthony, did not report her missing for about a month. The child's remains were found in woods near the family home that December. Cable networks covered the 2011 trial as nightly drama.

The jury acquitted Casey Anthony of murder and the other charges related to Caylee's death, convicting her only of misdemeanor counts of lying to investigators, two of which were later overturned on appeal. Millions of Americans formed passionate opinions about one woman's guilt. Almost none of that coverage asked how many children in that same state were being harmed in homes where no one was watching, or what the child welfare system needed to catch them.

Several states responded by passing versions of a so-called Caylee's Law, making it a crime for a parent or caregiver to fail to report a missing child promptly. The impulse was understandable. The result was a narrow statute aimed at the last spectacle rather than an investment in the caseworkers, home visitors and pediatric screenings that might reach the next child before anyone needed to report her missing.

The Cost of Denial

Denial is expensive, and I want the taxpayers reading this to feel the bill. We pay for every root we refuse to treat, only later and at a markup. We pay for the prison bed instead of the nurse visit, the emergency room instead of the treatment slot, the funeral instead of the outreach worker. We pay in falling property values on blocks where shootings cluster, in insurance premiums, in businesses that close and the tax base that leaves with them.

Survivors pay the steepest price, and they pay it longest. I have interviewed mothers a decade after a son's murder who still cannot drive past the intersection, men who lost jobs because the panic attacks would not stop, children who stopped speaking for a year. Most never receive counseling or compensation, because the victim services system is underfunded, hard to navigate and often closed to anyone with a record of their own. A country that buries the causes of violence also buries its victims, and then wonders why the hurt turns into the next round of harm.

Inside policing, denial costs just as much. On May 25, 2020, a Minneapolis officer named Derek Chauvin knelt on George Floyd's neck for roughly nine minutes while Floyd said he could not breathe and bystanders begged for help. A teenager's phone video showed the country what the department's first public statement had described in bland language as a man dying after a medical incident. Chauvin was convicted in 2021 of murder and manslaughter and sentenced to more than twenty years in prison. The city had already agreed to pay Floyd's family $27 million to settle their civil lawsuit.

The protests that followed spread to hundreds of cities, and the damage from the unrest that accompanied some of them ran into the billions of dollars, much of it in neighborhoods that could least afford it. Everyone paid: the Floyd family first and most, then the small business owners, the residents who lost the pharmacy on the corner, the officers who did their jobs honestly and inherited a public's fury. Warning signs about the officer and the department existed long before that day: Chauvin had faced numerous prior complaints, and residents had testified for years about the department's conduct. Ignoring them was a choice, and America paid for it in every currency it has.

The Predictability of Crime

I want to be precise about what "predictable" means. It does not mean we can know which person will pull a trigger on which night. It means we can know, with uncomfortable accuracy, which blocks, which disputes, which households and which young people carry the highest risk, and we can know it months or years ahead. Shootings cluster in small networks of people. Domestic homicides are usually preceded by a history of abuse, threats and earlier police calls. Drunk-driving deaths, roughly twelve to thirteen thousand a year in this country, follow patterns of time, place and repeat offenders that every state highway agency can map.

The tragedy is how little we do with that knowledge. Nationally, a large share of murders, in recent years somewhere around four in ten, never end in an arrest, and the share of solved rapes and nonfatal shootings is far lower. Every unsolved shooting leaves a shooter free and a family convinced that the system does not care, and that conviction feeds the next retaliation. When people stop believing the police will solve a killing, some of them solve it themselves. Predictable crime that goes unsolved becomes predictable crime that multiplies.

Even the cases that look like lightning strikes teach the lesson of what we do and do not invest in. In February 2026 the family of Nancy Guthrie, the eighty-four-year-old mother of NBC News anchor Savannah Guthrie, reported her missing from her home in the Catalina Foothills outside Tucson, Arizona. The Pima County sheriff said investigators believed she had been taken against her will, and the FBI released doorbell camera images of a masked, gloved person tampering with the camera at her door. Fake ransom demands followed, and one man pleaded guilty to harassment for sending them. As of my most recent sources in October 2026, no one has been charged and she has not been found. I will not speculate about an open case.

What the Guthrie case shows the nation is how much attention, money and investigative power the system can mobilize when it chooses to. A national task force, a seven-figure reward, round-the-clock coverage. Thousands of Americans go missing every year without any of that, and their families wait in silence. Tribal leaders spent years pressing the federal government simply to count the Native American women who disappear, and it took an act of Congress in 2020, Savanna's Act, to require better data collection and coordination. Counting is not the same as caring, but a country that will not even count is a country that has decided not to see.

The system's capacity is not the problem. Its priorities are. If we brought a fraction of that urgency to the predictable cases, the domestic abuser who has violated three protective orders, the teenager already shot once, the corner where the last two killings happened, we would prevent violence on a scale no single investigation could match. A sheriff's office that can assemble a task force in a day can also assign a detective to every domestic violence case with a prior protective order. A federal government that can mobilize for one famous family can fund the crime labs whose backlogs leave rape kits untested for years.

Ray watched the Guthrie coverage on the television above the gym's front desk, and he said what many people on his block were thinking. He wished her family every answer they were searching for. He also wished someone would search that hard for the boys on his corner before they went missing in the other way, into a cell or a grave.

The Path Forward

Forward means carrying out the blueprint at every level of government. Congress can tie a portion of federal public safety grants to published outcomes, clearance rates for homicide and rape, repeat-violence rates, overdose deaths, and fund nurse home visiting, literacy coaching and medication for addiction in every jail. State legislatures can pass science-of-reading laws modeled on Mississippi's, make minor traffic violations non-stoppable offenses as Philadelphia did, require surveillance ordinances and data limits for every camera network, and open police misconduct records to the public.

City councils can fund violence interruption steadily instead of in one-year bursts, clean and green vacant lots, publish a public safety scorecard every year, and shift routine enforcement to machines so detectives can work the shootings. Police chiefs can measure themselves by cases solved, not arrests made. Judges can order treatment for addiction instead of another short jail stay. Prosecutors can treat repeat domestic violence as the homicide warning it is.

Citizens are not spectators in any of this. A homeowner can join the block association that lobbies for lighting and lot cleanup. A retiree can tutor a third grader one hour a week. A business owner can offer a summer job to a teenager the outreach workers vouch for. Each of these steps has been tried somewhere in America and found to work. None is waiting on a scientific discovery. Every one of them is waiting on a vote.

Ray put it more simply than any policy paper. He said the boy did not need a miracle. He needed one adult with a budget to act on what three adults with notebooks already knew. A reading tutor in fifth grade. Someone to meet him at the gate when his father came home from prison. An outreach worker who knew the cousin and could have talked the dispute down before the gun came out. Ray could name each missed chance in order, like stations on a train line, and every one of them cost less than the police overtime spent at the scene the night the boy died.

A Call for National Reckoning

Countries can face their failures. Germany built memorials and rewrote its textbooks. South Africa held a truth commission. America has never had a reckoning with the way it handles violence: the children it ignores, the victims it blames, the officers it shields, the money it spends without asking what it bought. We hold hearings after the spectacular cases and forget them by the next news cycle.

We have even been warned in writing. In 1968 the presidential commission led by Illinois governor Otto Kerner, appointed during the urban riots of 1967, concluded that the country was splitting into two unequal societies and called for sweeping investment in jobs, housing, schools and fairer policing. The report became a bestseller. Most of its recommendations were shelved. Half a century later, the same diagnosis could be written about the same neighborhoods, which tells me the problem was never a lack of knowledge. It was a lack of will, and will is something citizens can supply.

A real reckoning would start with the people who pay. Survivors at the center of the conversation, testifying not only about what was done to them but about what the system failed to do first. Taxpayers demanding receipts. Homeowners asking why their property values rise and fall with violence their government could have prevented. Community leaders like Ray given budgets, not just thank-you plaques.

That reckoning can begin locally, this year. Ask your city council for a public safety scorecard. Ask your state legislator where the reading coaches and the nurses are. Ask your sheriff what happens to an addicted person on release day. Ask your police chief what share of last year's murders were solved. Write the answers down, publish them, and come back the next year to ask again.

I have spent my career writing about the worst day in other people's lives. I am tired of describing the same day over and over, with only the names and the street signs changed. I am tired of watching good people like Ray stack folding chairs after a vigil that every adult in the room saw coming. The next one does not have to happen the way the last one did. We know why. We know what works. The only thing left to decide is whether we finally care enough to act, and whether we will do it before the candles are lit on our own corner.

The takeaway

Violence in America is not random; it grows from roots we can name and patterns we can map, and burying them has cost us lives, money and trust. The fixes are proven and cheaper than failure. Pick one question from the list above, ask it in public this month, and refuse to stop asking until someone with a budget answers.

Garry S. Howard writes practical, reader-first guides. If this book helped you, please consider leaving a review on Amazon — it is the best way to help other readers find it.
